The year was 1756, and the world outside the rolling hills of Virginia was beginning to hum with a restless energy that had nothing to do with harvest or planting. In the heart of this verdant land stood the estate of the Esmond family, a place where history seemed to breathe easier than in the crowded courts of London. It was here that young Harry Esmond found himself, though his spirit was already cast toward horizons far beyond the familiar boundaries of his home.

Harry was a man of few words but many thoughts, a quiet observer who often felt like a stranger in his own life. He walked through the gardens, the scent of jasmine and damp earth clinging to him, while the distant sound of horses' hooves echoed across the fields—a reminder that the peace he cherished was fragile. His mind drifted back to stories told by his father, tales of adventure and duty that now felt less like fiction and more like a prophecy waiting to unfold.

As evening approached, the sky turned shades of amber and violet, casting long shadows over the trees. Harry paused beneath an ancient oak, its branches stretching wide like arms welcoming the twilight. There was something about these moments—the stillness before the storm—that made him feel both small and significant all at once. He knew that soon enough, the calm would break, and he would be called upon to answer a summons he could neither ignore nor delay.

In those quiet hours, Harry reflected on the weight of lineage and legacy. To be born an Esmond was to carry within you the hopes and fears of generations past. Yet, despite the grandeur surrounding him, he remained acutely aware of his own uncertainties. Was he truly ready for whatever lay ahead? Could he stand tall when the winds of change swept through the colonies?

These questions lingered in his mind as night fell completely, wrapping the estate in darkness save for the flickering light of lanterns carried by servants returning from their duties. Somewhere in the distance, a dog barked, breaking the silence momentarily before settling back into solitude. For Harry, this was no ordinary evening; it was merely the prelude to events that would reshape not only his life but also the course of history itself.

And so, under the watchful gaze of the moon, Harry prepared himself—not just physically, but mentally—for the journey that awaited him. The road ahead would test every fiber of his being, challenging his courage, loyalty, and resolve. But even as doubt crept into his thoughts, there remained a spark of determination deep within him, a fire fueled by the knowledge that he belonged to something greater than himself.

Thus began the tale of *The Virginians*, a story woven with threads of love, loss, triumph, and tragedy—a saga set against the backdrop of a nation teetering on the brink of war.

Upon the library wall of one of America's most celebrated authors hang two crossed swords, relics worn by his kin during the Great War of Independence. One blade was gallantly drawn in the service of the Crown; the other, the weapon of a brave and honored republican soldier. The possessor of these harmless trophies has earned a name equally revered in his ancestral homeland and his own, where genius such as his is always welcomed in peace. This history serves as a reminder of those very swords in the historian's study at Boston. During the Revolutionary War, the subjects of this tale—native-born Americans and children of the Old Dominion—found themselves arrayed on opposing sides of a bitter quarrel. Yet, at its conclusion, they reunited peacefully as brethren should, their enduring love having survived the anger that once divided them.

In the wainscoted parlour of the Warringtons in England, the portrait of a colonel in scarlet hangs beside that of a general in blue and buff. A descendant of these brothers recently showed me these images, alongside numerous letters, books, and papers belonging to them. Within the Warrington family—and to distinguish them from other members of that respectable lineage—these figures have always been known simply as "The Virginians"; indeed, their memoirs bear this title. Both spent considerable time in Europe, living on the very verge of that Old World from which we are now drifting so swiftly. Familiar with myriad varieties of men and fortunes, their lives brought them into contact with personages found only in books, yet who seem vividly alive through the letters they penned. Reading these yellowed pages, scored with the boyish tears of disappointed passion, dutifully dispatched after grand balls and ceremonies, scribbled by campfires or from prison cells, one almost hears their voices. There is even one letter pierced by a bullet, much of its text blotted out with the blood of its bearer. Had it not been for the affectionate thrift of one devoted correspondent, these missives might never have been preserved.

From the very beginning, the mother kept all her sons' letters. Early among them, Henry, the younger twin, writes his love to his brother, then recovering from a sprain at his grandfather's estate, Castlewood, in Virginia, thanking his grandpapa for a horse he rides with his tutor. The final entry reads, "from my beloved son," reaching her mere hours before her death. This venerable lady never visited Europe save once, accompanying her parents during the reign of George the Second. She took refuge in Richmond when Castlewood was burned during the war and was thereafter known as Madam Esmond. She cared little for the name or family of Warrington, holding it in slight esteem compared to her own.

As the reader shall soon discover, the letters of the Virginians are far from exhaustive. They offer hints rather than full descriptions, indications and outlines primarily. Perhaps the narrator has mistaken certain details or filled in colors incorrectly; yet, poring over these documents, he has strived to imagine the writer's situation, the company he kept, and the conversations he overheard. Drawing figures as he fancied them and setting down dialogue as he believes he might have heard it, he has endeavored, to the best of his ability, to revivify bygone times and people. Whether this task has succeeded, and whether it brings profit or amusement to the reader, remains for the kind reader to determine.

One summer morning in 1756, during the reign of His Majesty King George the Second, the *Young Rachel*, a Virginian ship commanded by Master Edward Franks, ascended the Avon River on her joyful return from her annual voyage to the Potomac. Proceeding with the tide toward Bristol, she moored as close as possible to Trail's Wharf, her designated destination. Mr. Trail, a part-owner who could survey his vessel from his counting-house windows, immediately took a boat to approach her side. The owner of the *Young Rachel*, a large, grave man with gray hair and a demure demeanor, offered a welcoming handshake to Captain Franks, standing on the deck, and congratulated him on the swift and fortunate journey. Turning promptly to business, he inquired about the cargo and passengers, noting that gratitude to Heaven for its mercies should precede such inquiries.

Captain Franks, a cheerful man who loved a jest, replied, "We have, but yonder ugly negro boy fetching the trunks, and a passenger who has the state cabin to himself." Mr. Trail frowned as if Heaven itself had granted fewer mercies than desired. "Confound you, Franks, and your luck!" he exclaimed. "The *Duke William*, which arrived last week, brought fourteen, and she is not half our tonnage."

"And this passenger, who occupies the entire cabin, pays nothing," continued the Captain. "Swear now, it will do you good, Mr. Trail; indeed it will. I have tried the medicine."

"A passenger takes the whole cabin and pays not a penny?" Mr. Trail gasped. "Gracious mercy, are you a fool, Captain Franks?"

"Ask the passenger himself, for here he comes."

As the master spoke, a young man of approximately nineteen emerged from the hatchway. Clad in deep mourning, with a cloak draped over his shoulders and a sword tucked under his arm, he called out, "Gumbo, you idiot, why don't you fetch the baggage out of the cabin? Well, shipmate, our journey is ended. You will see all the little folks tonight whom you have been talking about. Give my love to Polly, Betty, and Little Tommy; do not forget my duty to Mrs. Franks. I thought yesterday the voyage would never conclude, and now that it is over, I am almost sorry. That little berth in my cabin looks remarkably comfortable now that I am leaving it."

Mr. Trail scowled at the unpaid passenger, offering only a barely perceptible nod. Just then, Captain Franks introduced him: "This here gentleman is Mr. Trail, sir, whose name you have undoubtedly heard of."

"It is pretty well known in Bristol, sir," Mr. Trail declared majestically.

"And this is Mr. Warrington," continued the Captain, "Madam Esmond Warrington's son, of Castlewood."

Instantly, the British merchant removed his hat, while the owner of the beaver cap began bowing prodigiously, as if addressing a crown prince. "Gracious powers, Mr. Warrington! This is a delight indeed! What a crowning mercy that your voyage should have been so prosperous! You must accept my boat to go ashore. Let me cordially and respectfully welcome you to England. Let me shake your hand as the son of my benefactress and patroness, Mrs. Esmond Warrington, whose name is known and honored on Bristol Exchange, I warrant. Isn't that so, Franks?"

"There's no sweeter tobacco comes from Virginia, and no better brand than the Three Castles," said Mr. Franks, producing a large brass tobacco box and stuffing a quid into his jolly mouth. "You don't know what a comfort it is, sir! You'll take to it, bless you, as you grow older. Won't he, Mr. Trail? I wish you had ten shiploads of it instead of one. You might have ten shiploads; I've told Madam Esmond so. I've ridden over her plantation; she treats me like a lord when I visit the house. She doesn't grudge me the finest wine, nor keeps me waiting in the counting room as some folks do. She is a real born lady, she is; and might have a thousand hogsheads as easily as her hundreds, if there were but hands enough."

"I have lately engaged in the Guinea trade," said Mr. Trail, speaking obsequiously, "and could supply her ladyship with any number of healthy young negroes before next fall."

"We are averse to purchasing negroes from Africa," the young gentleman replied coldly. "My grandfather and my mother have always objected to it, and I cannot bring myself to think of selling or buying the poor wretches."

"It is for their good, my dear young sir! For their temporal and spiritual good!" cried Mr. Trail. "We purchase these poor creatures solely for their benefit. Let me discuss this matter further at my own house. I can introduce you to a happy home, a Christian family, and the honest fare of a British merchant. Cannot I, Captain Franks?"

"Can't say," growled the Captain. "He never asked me to take a bite or sup at your table. He invited me to psalm-singing once and to hear Mr. Ward preach; I don't care for such entertainments."

Choosing to ignore this remark, Mr. Trail lowered his voice. "Business is business, my dear young sir. I know it is merely my duty—the duty of us all—to cultivate the fruits of the earth in their season. As the heir of Lady Esmond's estate—for I believe I am speaking to the heir of that great property—I would urge upon you, at the earliest opportunity, the propriety and duty of increasing the ample means with which Heaven has blessed you. As an honest factor, I could not do otherwise; as a prudent man, should I scruple to speak of what tends to your profit and mine? No, my dear Mr. George."

"My name is not George; my name is Henry," the young man said, turning his head away as tears filled his eyes.

"Gracious powers! What do you mean, sir?" Mr. Trail stammered. "Did

For a considerable time, Colonel Esmond's grandson stood ringing the bell at Castlewood, his ancestral home, before anyone inside seemed inclined to acknowledge his summons. When the servant finally emerged, he displayed little interest in the news that the visitor was a relation of the family. With the household away, John cared little for their kin; his eagerness was focused solely on returning to his card game with Thomas in the window-seat. Meanwhile, the housekeeper was busy preparing for the arrival of my lord and my lady, expecting them that very evening.

Only through persistent pleading did Harry manage to glimpse my lady's sitting-room and the picture gallery. There, indeed, hung portraits of his grandfather in a periwig and breastplate—the same image he knew from Virginia—and a likeness of his grandmother, Lady Castlewood, dressed in the fashion of Charles II's reign. Her neck was bare, her fair golden hair cascading in ringlets that Harry recalled seeing snow-white in memory. However, the sulky housekeeper soon drove him away, citing the imminent arrival of the family. "There is my lady the Countess, my lord, his brother, the young ladies, and the Baroness, who is to occupy the state bedroom," she announced. Harry, confused, asked who the Baroness was. It was revealed to be Baroness Bernstein, the aunt of the young ladies.

Defeated, Harry wrote his name on a scrap of paper from his pocket-book and left it on a hall table: *Henry Esmond Warrington, of Castlewood, in Virginia, arrived in England yesterday—staying at the Three Castles in the village.* The lackeys, rising from their cards to collect their wages, opened the door. Even Gumbo, the gatekeeper, abandoned his conversation with old Lockwood to take Harry's guinea, seemingly unaware of the significance of the gift. Reflecting on his brief visit to his fathers' home, Harry realized he had seen only the unselfish countenance of little Polly. Walking away, he felt a profound disappointment, struck dumb by the cold reception. He reasoned that if any of them had ridden to his house in Virginia, regardless of the master's presence, he would have been welcomed warmly. Instead, standing before his ancestors' hall, he had to beg for a dish of bacon and eggs at a country alehouse.

After dinner, Harry walked to the bridge and sat upon it, gazing toward the old house as the sun descended behind it. Rooks cawed their way home to the elm trees. His youthful imagination conjured scenes of ancestors: knights and huntsmen crossing the ford, cavaliers of King Charles's era, and my Lord Castlewood riding out with hawk and hound. These reveries were interrupted by a sharp pang of sorrow for his lost brother, the dear friend with whom he had once shared all joys and griefs. Lost in these melancholy thoughts, mixed with the rhythmic clinking of a nearby blacksmith's forge and the evening's ambient noise, Harry was startled by the sudden dash of two young men on horseback across the bridge.

One of them swore loudly, calling Harry a fool and telling him to clear the path. The other, believing he might have jostled the pedestrian, spurred his horse faster, urging his companion onward. Before Harry could recover from his surprise or anger, the pair raced up the hill toward the house. Moments later, two livery servants on horseback followed, scowling at the traveler with a distinctly British "Curse you, who are you?" Finally, a heavy coach-and-six lumbered over the bridge, drawn by four horses and carrying three ladies, two maids, and an armed man seated behind. Three pale, handsome faces looked out at Harry Warrington, ignoring the salute he offered upon recognizing the family arms. The gentleman behind the carriage glared haughtily. Harry felt utterly alone. He considered returning to Captain Franks; the *Rachel* and her tossing cabin seemed far cheerier than the stone bridge where he stood.

The innkeepers at the Three Castles did not recognize the name Warrington. They identified the passengers in the coach as my lady, her stepdaughter Lady Maria, and her daughter Lady Fanny. The young gentleman in the grey frock was Mr. William, and the powdered youth was my lord. It was the latter who had sworn the loudest, and the grey-frocked man who had nearly galloped Harry into the ditch.

Although the landlord of the Three Castles had shown Harry a bedroom, the young man had refused to unpack his trunks, hoping the great house would eventually invite him. Hours passed with no invitation. Forced to open his luggage and dress for bed, Harry waited in a mood of deep melancholy. Just as darkness fell, a second carriage with four horses crossed the bridge. A stout, high-colored lady with striking dark eyes stared intently at Mr. Warrington. This was the Baroness Bernstein, my lord's aunt. Soon, everyone had vanished behind the castle gates, leaving Harry to retire to his room with a cruel sense of neglect and loneliness. Unable to sleep, he was kept awake by the raucous noises, curses, and giggling emanating from the landlady's bar below.

Suddenly, Gumbo's voice was heard outside, protesting, "You cannot go in, sir—my master is asleep!" Yet, a shrill voice filled with oaths cut through the air. Harry recognized it immediately. Someone cursed Gumbo as a "stupid, negro woolly-pate" and shoved him aside, flooding the room with further expletives and ushering in a young gentleman.

"Beg your pardon, Cousin Warrington," cried the young blasphemer, "are you asleep? I beg forgiveness for riding over you on the bridge. I didn't know you... course, I shouldn't have done it—I thought it was a lawyer with a writ. Dressed in black, you know. Gad! I thought it was Nathan coming to nab me." Mr. William laughed incoherently, clearly intoxicated.

"You did me great honor to mistake me for a sheriff's officer, cousin," Harry replied gravely, sitting up in his tall nightcap.

"Gad! I thought it was Nathan and was going to send you souse into the river. But I ask your pardon. You see, I had been drinking at the Bell at Hexton, and the punch is excellent there. Hullo! You, Davis! A bowl of punch; d'you hear?"

"I have had my share for tonight, cousin, and I believe you have too," Harry continued, maintaining his dignified tone.

"You want me to go, Cousin What's-your-name, I see," Mr. William declared with mock seriousness. "You want me to go, and they want me to come, and I didn't want to come. I said I'd see him hanged first—that's exactly what I said. Why should I trouble myself to come down alone in the evening to look after a fellow I don't care a pin for? Exactly what I said. Exactly what Castlewood said. Why the devil should he go down? Castlewood says so, and so does my lady, but the Baroness wants you. It's all the Baroness's doing; if she speaks, it must be done. So you must just get up and come." Mr. Esmond delivered these words with rapid, indistinct speech, darting around the room as he talked. But the young Virginian was furious.

"I tell you what, cousin," Harry cried, "I won't move for the Countess, or the Baroness, or all the cousins in Castlewood combined."

Just then, the landlord entered with the bowl of punch Mr. William had ordered. The young gentleman in bed screamed fiercely at the host to throw the sot out of the room.

"Sot, you little tobacconist! Sot, you Cherokee!" shrieked Mr. William. "Jump out of bed, and I'll drive my sword through your body. Why didn't I do it today when I took you for a bailiff—a confounded pettifogging bum-bailiff!" He continued screeching oaths and incoherencies until the landlord, the drawer, the hostler, and the entire kitchen staff rushed in to drag him away. Afterward, Harry Warrington pulled his covers tight around him in sulky wrath, undoubtedly falling into a deep sleep shortly thereafter.

The following morning, the landlord was far more obsequious upon meeting his young guest, having now learned his name and status. Messengers had arrived from the castle the previous night to retrieve the young gentlemen. Poor Mr. William had returned in a wheelbarrow, apparently not unfamiliar with such a mode of transport. "He never remembers anything about it the next day," the landlord vowed. "He is of a truly kind nature, Mr. William. The men receive crowns and half-crowns from him claiming he beat them while drunk. He's the devil when tipsy, Mr. William, but when sober, he is the very kindest of young gentlemen."

While Harry awaited recognition from his kinsmen outside, events unfolded within Castlewood House. Upon arriving home, the family discovered the note bearing Harry's name. His appearance sparked a domestic council. My Lord Castlewood assumed this was the young man seen on the bridge; since they hadn't drowned him, they must invite him. He suggested sending a servant with a message. Lady Fanny argued it would be more civil if one of the brothers went personally, especially given their rude initial greeting. Lord Castlewood agreed, suggesting his brother William go.

However, Mr. William vehemently objected, stating he would

Colonel Henry Esmond, a veteran who had borne the rank of Colonel through the wars of Queen Anne's reign, found himself, at the conclusion of that turbulent era, embroiled in conspiracies aimed at restoring the Stuart line to the English throne. Fortunately for his safety, the nation ultimately embraced the House of Hanover. While many staunch opponents fled the realm, seeking exile across the seas, Colonel Esmond was advised by his friends to follow suit. Though he deeply regretted his role in the failed uprising, and recognizing that the new monarch, the Augustus Prince of Hanover, was a ruler of profound peaceability, his friends swiftly facilitated a reconciliation.

It is recorded that Colonel Esmond hailed from the distinguished English lineage of Castlewood, in the County of Hants. It was widely known that King James II and his son had once proffered the title of Marquis to both the Colonel and his father. Furthermore, the King had suggested that the Colonel might assume the Irish peerage inherited by his family, had it not been for a minor procedural oversight which the Colonel simply refused to rectify. Wearied by the ceaseless political strife of England and plagued by domestic troubles on the continent, the Colonel elected to settle in Virginia. There, he took possession of a substantial estate originally granted to his ancestor by King Charles I.

In this American haven, his daughter and grandsons were born, and eventually, his wife passed away. Upon her marriage, she had been the widow of the Colonel's cousin—the unfortunate Viscount Castlewood, who met his end in a duel with Lord Mohun near the twilight of King William's reign. True to tradition, the Colonel named his American residence "Castlewood," mirroring the ancestral home left behind in England. Indeed, every custom of Virginia was here lovingly modeled after English precedent. It remained a fiercely loyal colony; the Virginians proudly asserted that King Charles II had ruled over Virginia long before he ascended the throne in England. Both the English monarchy and the Church were revered with unwavering fidelity. The local gentry, allied with prominent English families, held their heads high above the Dutch merchants of New York and the mercantile Puritans of Pennsylvania and New England. Few places in the world were less inclined toward republicanism than this great province, which would later lead the historic revolt against the British Crown.

The Virginian gentry lived upon their vast estates in a manner almost feudal. Each property employed a multitude of hands—both purchased and indentured servants—who were entirely subject to the master's command. The land provided ample food, livestock, and game, while the great rivers teemed with fish. Ships laden with tobacco harvested from private wharves along the Potomac or James River sailed to London or Bristol, returning with English goods and manufactured items, the sole commodities the Virginian elite deemed worth cultivating. Hospitality was boundless; no stranger was ever turned away from their gates. The gentry traveled between homes in a state nearly indistinguishable from feudal homage.

At this time, the institution of slavery was not yet a subject of public debate. To own Black servants caused no offense among Virginian gentlemen, nor was the dominion exercised over the Negro race typically savage. Provisions were plentiful, and the enslaved population, though laboring hard, appeared content and rarely unhappy. One might have preached abolition to Madam Esmond of Castlewood with the same ease as advising her to let her horses roam free; she possessed no doubt that the whip and the corn-bag were essential tools for both beasts and men. Her father, possessing a skeptical mind on many matters, harbored private doubts, yet these never manifested as open dissent. Instead, he remained somewhat disaffected rather than rebellious. Once a participant in active life at home, he might formerly have craved its rewards, but in his later years, he showed little interest in them. Some unseen tragedy had cast a permanent melancholy over his existence. He was not unhappy—he was kind, affectionate, and even obsequious toward the women of his family, whom he rarely contradicted—but he suffered from a spiritual bankruptcy that his soul never fully recovered. He endured life rather than enjoying it, yet paradoxically, he was happiest in his final moments as he prepared to leave it.

Having lost his wife, his daughter assumed control of the Colonel's affairs. He surrendered his duties to her with total acquiescence; provided he retained his books and quiet, he desired nothing else. When guests arrived at Castlewood, he entertained them lavishly, displaying a pleasant, sarcastic wit. Yet, he felt no sorrow when they departed.

"My love, I shall not be sorry to go myself," he once told his daughter. "And you, though the most affectionate of daughters, will find comfort again in time. Why should I, who am so old, be romantic? You may, who are still a young creature." He spoke without intending all he said, for the lady he addressed was a pragmatic individual with little capacity for romance.

After fifteen years of prosperous residence on his great estate, the Colonel agreed to his daughter's ambitious plan to construct a mansion far grander and more enduring than the simple wooden dwelling in which he had resided, ensuring his heirs would possess a home befitting their noble name. Several neighbors had recently erected handsome residences; perhaps it was her ambition to secure higher social standing that drove this desire for improved quarters. Colonel Esmond, however, cared little for physical quarters or genealogical distinctions. Yet, his daughter held a lofty opinion of her lineage's merit and antiquity. In his serene, declining years, the Colonel grew exquisitely calm and good-natured, easily accommodating his child's peculiarities. He even assisted her with his considerable antiquarian knowledge and his proficiency in painting.

Knowledge of heraldry had once formed part of the education for most noble ladies and gentlemen. During her visit to Europe, Miss Esmond had diligently studied family histories and pedigrees, returning to Virginia armed with a trove of documents she accepted with grave authority, alongside the most edifying volumes then published in France and England concerning the noble science. These works satisfied her completely: the Esmonds were descended not only from Norman warriors who accompanied their victorious leader to England but also from native Englishmen of royal dignity. Two magnificent heraldic trees, painted by the Colonel's own hand, depicted the family sprouting from two illustrious sources: on one side, Emperor Charlemagne, rendered in plate armor with his imperial mantle and diadem; on the other, Queen Boudicca, portrayed in the light costume of an ancient British queen, wearing a prodigious golden crown, a fur-trimmed mantle, and a lovely, symmetrical figure tastefully tattooed with brilliant blue designs. From these two noble stocks, the family tree rose until it converged in the thirteenth century within the person of a fortunate Esmond who claimed descent from both.

Regarding the Warrington family into which she married, good Madam Rachel paid scant attention. She styled herself Esmond-Warrington but was universally addressed as "Madam Esmond of Castlewood" after assuming control of the domain following her father's death. It is feared that disputes over precedence occasionally troubled her temper. Although her father had possessed a Marquess's patent from King James—which he had burned and disowned—she frequently acted as if the document remained valid and in full force. She regarded the English Esmonds as inferior to her own branch and made no secret of asserting her superiority over the entire colonial aristocracy. Consequently, quarrels, angry words, and even occasional scuffles arose at the Governor's assemblies in Jamestown.

But why recall these petty squabbles? Are not the participants beyond the reach of conflict today, and has not the Republic ended such social inequalities? Before the establishment of Independence, no country was more aristocratic than Virginia. Thus, the Virginians whose history we chronicle were bred to hold the highest respect for home institutions, and the rightful king could not have found two more faithful subjects than the twin sons of Castlewood.

When the boys' grandfather died, their mother, in great state, proclaimed her eldest son, George, as the successor and heir to the estate. Harry, George's younger brother by merely thirty minutes, was constantly enjoined to respect his senior. The entire household was instructed to honor him, including the numerous Black servants, who formed a large and happy family, and the indentured servants from Europe, whose lot was managed as tolerably as possible under the Lady of Castlewood's governance. Within the whole family, there was scarcely a rebel except for Mrs. Esmond's faithful companion, Madam Mountain, and Harry's foster-mother—a devoted Black woman who could never comprehend why her child should not be placed ahead of his brother. She maintained that Harry was handsomer, stronger, and cleverer, though in truth, the twins bore little difference in beauty, strength, or stature.

In temperament, they were strikingly different, yet in appearance, they were so similar that, aside from the color of their hair, it would have been difficult to distinguish them. Lying in their beds, crowned with the vast ribboned nightcaps worn by our great ancestors, it was nearly impossible for anyone but a nurse or mother to tell them apart. Alike in form, they differed profoundly in spirit. The elder, George, was peaceful, studious, and silent; the younger, Harry, was warlike and noisy. Harry was slow to begin learning but quick once started, whereas George was diligent from the outset. No threat of the birch rod would provoke Harry to study in an idle mood, nor would it prevent George from assisting his brother. Harry possessed a strong military inclination; he drilled the little negroes on the estate, cane them like a corporal, and engaged in frequent boxing matches with them, showing no

Kind friends, hospitable neighbors, an ancient name, a vast estate, and a fortune sufficient for comfort—along with a household staff eager to serve, good health, affectionate children, and excellent provisions for food, drink, and learning—should not these constitute the hallmarks of a truly happy life? Madam Esmond Warrington possessed every one of these blessings. She recited them daily in her morning and evening prayers, scrupulous in her devotion, generous to the poor, and seemingly free of malice toward anyone.

One might imagine her enthroned within her principality of Castlewood, surrounded by the adoration of local gentry, the dutiful obedience of her sons, and the frantic scramble of servants to fulfill her slightest whim. The poor whites, grateful for her bounty, accepted her medical advice without question, while lesser nobles eagerly agreed with her opinions and lost games of backgammon to her. With such advantages, surely the "Little Princess Pocahontas," as she was known, ought to be envied. Yet, amidst her dominions, she was hardly content.

Her husband, cut off in early life, was perhaps fortunate to be gone. Had he lived longer, fierce quarrels would likely have ensued, leaving him either a tyrant or a henpecked fool—a fate common enough a century prior. The truth remained: Little Madam Esmond avoided men and women alike, preferring instead to dominate them. Those who obeyed became her best friends; those who resisted faced her relentless fury until submission was forced. While society publicly confessed its own sins on Sundays, none proclaimed this truth with greater resolve than the little lady herself. Though she may have erred as a mortal, she rarely admitted fault to herself or others.

In her youth, her father watched her despotic fits and haughty stubbornness with amused detachment. His humor, a quality she lacked, often baffled and subdued her. But once the Colonel passed away, no one remained capable of commanding her respect. Thus, I am relieved I did not reside in Westmorland County, Virginia, a hundred years ago under her rule. One wonders how happy anyone could possibly be there. Even in Paradise, a serpent existed; if Eve had been perfectly content, would she not have listened?

Madam Esmond had managed Castlewood since childhood. After the Colonel's death, she rigorously supervised the estate, dismissing his English factor to hire a new one, overseeing construction projects, planting crops, appointing overseers, and importing tutors. Despite her love for her father, she rejected many of his maxims. Why should she obey her parents, having done so dutifully all her life? Should not all children follow their elders to ensure longevity? The little queen ruled her domain, and her sons were merely her first subjects. Soon, she dropped the name Warrington, adopting solely the title of Madam Esmond. Her lineage was well-known; she proudly discussed the Marquis's title granted by King James to her ancestors. Although her father's generosity might have allowed him to elevate her half-brother, Lord Castlewood, and his line, she insisted that her sons, belonging to the elder branch, deserved superior treatment. Only Lord Fairfax held precedence over her in the colony. She demanded priority over Ladies of Lieutenant-Governors and Judges, yielding only to the Governor's wife as the sovereign's representative. Historical records detail several fierce battles she waged with colonial dignitaries' wives over matters of etiquette.

As for her husband's family, the Warringtons, they received little favor. Annually, formal letters were sent to them and to her noble kin in Hampshire. However, when a judge's wife, estranged from Madam Esmond, returned to England and encountered Lady Warrington in London, she recounted the Princess's disparaging remarks about her own relatives. Lady Warrington presumably shared this with Lady Castlewood, leading to an immediate cessation of correspondence. To Madam Esmond's shock and rage, the letters went unanswered, prompting her to stop writing as well. Consequently, she fell out with neighbors, relatives, and eventually, her own sons.

A significant rift formed early between the Queen and her crown prince, George, concerning the dismissal of Mr. Dempster, the boys' tutor and the late Colonel's secretary. During the Colonel's lifetime, Madam Esmond struggled to tolerate him, though Mr. Dempster barely endured her. Suspicious of books and fearing corrupt influences, she suspected he was a disguised Jesuit. Forced to flee, he built a cabin in the wilderness to teach and practice medicine among scattered settlers. Master George vowed never to abandon his old tutor and kept his promise. Harry, however, preferred fishing and sports to reading, maintaining a distance from the poor Dominie.

Another dispute arose following the deaths of an aunt and the Colonel, which entitled George Warrington to a legacy of six thousand pounds, held in trust by their mother and a London lawyer. Madam Esmond could not comprehend that she was merely a trustee, not the owner. Furious when the London agent refused to transfer the funds immediately, she screamed, "Is not all I possess my sons'? Would I not tear myself apart to serve them? With six thousand pounds, I could buy Mr. Boulter's estate and slaves, generating a thousand pounds annually and securing a splendid future for Harry!" Neither her young neighbor, Mr. Washington of Mount Vernon, nor the London lawyer could persuade her that the agent acted correctly. She insulted the lawyer as an insolent pettifogger deserving punishment for doubting an Esmond's honor.

Indeed, the Virginian Princess possessed a fiery temper. When George was asked to choose sides, he sided with Mr. Washington and the lawyer, stating he could not help himself. He did not desire the money and wished to give it to his mother if he could. But Madam Esmond cared nothing for reason. Emotion dictated her actions. Seeing a chance to enrich Harry, whose inheritance was meager compared to George's, she raged that London wretches would not assist him and that her own son refused to help. Tears, scorn, veiled insults, prolonged silence, bitter outbursts, and passionate pleas to heaven characterized her state of mind. One might wonder if modern women do not argue similarly; the book of female logic is indeed stained with tears, and justice in their courts often seems inflamed.

This conflict resolved Madam Esmond to save diligently for Harry, fulfilling her maternal duty despite the strain. Construction halted on the fine buildings the Colonel had begun, which included Dutch bricks shipped from New York and imported mantelpieces, cornices, glass, carpets, and upholstery from England. Books ceased purchase, and wine shipments stopped. She lamented the cost of a carriage brought from England, riding to church in it with a heavy heart, whispering to the boys beside her, "Harry, Harry! I wish I had saved that money for thee, my poor portionless child—three hundred and eighty guineas for Messieurs Hatchett!"

"You will provide plenty while you live, and George will provide plenty when you die," Harry replied cheerfully.

"Not unless he changes his nature, my dear," the lady retorted sharply, casting a grim look at her elder son. "Unless Heaven softens his heart and teaches him charity, for which I pray day and night. Does Mountain know? Do you not, Mountain?"

Mrs. Mountain, the widow of Ensign Mountain and Madam Esmond's companion and manager, occupied the fourth seat in the family coach. "Humph!" she remarked. "I know you disturb yourself constantly worrying about this legacy. I see no real need."

"Oh, no need!" cried the widow, rustling her silks. "Of course I have no need to worry, for my eldest is a disobedient son and unkind brother—he has an estate. My poor Harry, bless him, deserves only a mess of pottage."

George looked despairingly at his mother until his eyes filled with tears. "I wish you would bless me too, O my mother!" he exclaimed, bursting into weeping.

Instantly, Harry wrapped his arms around his brother's neck, kissing him repeatedly. "Never mind, George. I know whether you are a good brother or not. Ignore what she says; she doesn't mean it."

"I do mean it, child!" the mother shrieked. "Would to Heaven—"

"HOLD YOUR TONGUE, I SAY!" roared Harry. "It is shameful to speak thus to him, ma'am."

"And so it is, Harry," agreed Mrs. Mountain, shaking his hand. "You have never spoken truer words in your life."

"Mrs. Mountain, do you dare set my children against me?" the widow cried. "From this very day—"

"Turn me and my child into the street?" Mrs. Mountain challenged calmly. "Do. That would be a fitting revenge for the English lawyer withholding the boy's money. Find another companion willing to tell you black is white and flatter you. It is not my way, madam. When shall I leave? I shall not stay long packing. I brought little to Castlewood House and shall take little away."

"Hush! The bells are ringing for church, Mountain. Let us try to compose ourselves," the widow said softly, looking with profound affection—at one, perhaps both—of her children. George lowered his head, while Harry moved closer during the sermon, resting his arm protectively around his brother's neck.

Harry continued his tale in his own lively style, interspersed with youthful exclamations and answers to his listener's questions. The elderly lady seemed tireless in her attention. Her amiable hostess and daughters frequently invited her to ride, walk, drink

Harry Warrington recounted the simple tale of his recent adventures to his newly discovered relative. As he spoke, Madame Bernstein, a woman possessing a sharp wit and a profound understanding of the world, quietly formed her judgment on the characters and events described. While her verdict was not entirely favorable, one must remember that humanity is flawed and life rarely offers constant pleasure or profit. The court-lady recoiled at the very idea of her American cousin's rustic existence; such a life seemed tedious to anyone bred in the city. Yet, little Madame Warrington knew nothing else and was content with her lot, just as she was with herself. Just because we are epicures does not mean the humble farmer Hodge is miserable with his bread and bacon. Madame Warrington led a life of duty that might seem monotonous to others, but it brought her genuine satisfaction. She was a brisk woman of business, overseeing every affair of her vast estate. Not a pie was baked at Castlewood without her little finger in the dough. She directed the maids in their spinning, supervised the kitchen staff, rode out on her pony to inspect the fields, and watched over the overseers and enslaved workers tending the tobacco and corn crops. If a slave fell ill, she would visit his quarters in any weather, administering remedies with great resolve. She kept a book of old-fashioned recipes, operated a distillery for waters and elixirs, and maintained a medicine chest that terrified her neighbors, who feared falling sick lest she descend upon them with her potions and pills.

Centuries prior, Virginia held few towns; the homes of the gentry were merely small villages inhabited by themselves and their dependents. Rachel Esmond ruled Castlewood like a miniature queen, while neighboring "princes" governed their own domains. Many of these lords were somewhat impoverished potentates, living comfortably yet roughly, surrounded by servants in ragged liveries. They kept open houses, welcoming every stranger, and indulged in field sports befitting their noble lineage. The widow of Castlewood matched her neighbors in hospitality but surpassed them in economy. Several likely men might have sought to marry her and raise her sons, yet none seemed worthy of a lady of her exalted birth. Rumors circulated that the Duke of Cumberland might be appointed viceroy, or even king, of America. Madame Warrington's gossipers laughed, suggesting she waited for him. With grave dignity, however, she noted that royalty itself had once offered alliances to the Esmond family.

Under her command served an officer's widow, previously mentioned, who had been Madame Esmond's school companion at Kensington Academy, just as her late husband had been the regimental friend of the late Mr. Warrington. When English girls at the academy mocked the little American stranger and ridiculed the princely airs she adopted from childhood, Fanny Parker had defended and befriended her. Both women married ensigns in Kingsley's Regiment and developed a tender attachment, referring to one another as "my Fanny" and "my Rachel" in their letters. Tragically, Fanny's husband died in poverty, leaving her with an infant and no provision. Later, Captain Franks brought Mrs. Mountain aboard the *Young Rachel* to Virginia.

Castlewood House had ample space, and Mrs. Mountain quickly enlivened the household. She carded games with the mistress, assisted the eldest boy with music, entertained guests, managed guest chambers, and oversaw linens. A kind, brisk, and jovial widow, she attracted the attention of several unmarried gentlemen in the colony, yet she refused to remarry, citing that her first husband, Mr. Mountain, had amicably spent her fortune and his own. Even his funeral expenses had been covered by her remaining trinkets. As long as Madame Warrington kept her, Mrs. Mountain preferred a home without a husband to any offer made in Virginia. Though the two ladies quarreled frequently, they loved each other deeply, making up their differences only to fall out again later. When either boy fell ill, they competed in maternal care. During the Colonel's final illness, Mrs. Mountain's cheerfulness and kindness were greatly valued by him—a memory Madame Warrington cherished above all others. Consequently, whenever Captain Franks asked if she intended to return with him, she declined, promising to stay another year.

When suitors approached Madame Warrington, she received their attentions kindly, often asking if they sought Mrs. Mountain. She would intervene on behalf of the younger woman, praising Fanny's English lineage and suitability for any gentleman. If a suitor declared his affection for the daughter of the Marquis of Esmond rather than her dependent, Madame Warrington would curtsy gravely, express her regret at the misunderstanding, and remind him that she lived for her people and sons, showing no intention of changing her station. Like Queen Elizabeth, a sensible woman of business who inspired both terror and love, the little Virginian princess had her favorites, accepted their flattery, grew weary of them, and treated them with cruelty or kindness as her whims dictated. Compliments were graciously received as her due. This foible was so well-known that wags exploited it. Rattling Jack Firebrace of Henrico County enjoyed free quarters for months, winning favor by reciting stolen verses. Tom Humbold of Spotsylvania wagered fifty hogsheads against five that he could persuade her to institute an order of knighthood, and he won.

The elder boy observed these eccentricities with private rage. From an early age, he revolted against the flattery paid to his mother, attempting to expose it with juvenile satire. Thus, his mother would remark seriously, "The Esmonds have always been of a jealous disposition; my poor boy takes after his parents." George despised Jack Firebrace and Tom Humbold alike, whereas Harry delighted in sporting with them—fowling, fishing, cockfighting, and enjoying the rural pleasures.

One winter, following the dismissal of their first tutor, Madame Esmond took the boys to Williamsburg for education. There, they attended the famous Mr. Whitfield, whose arrival revitalized the colony's spiritual life. Unlike neighboring provinces, Virginia was a Church of England colony where clergymen were state-paid and held glebe lands. Lacking bishops, the colonists imported ministers from England, often receiving noblemen's hangers-on or insolvent parsons seeking livelihoods. Naturally, these were not always the most eloquent pastors. No wonder Mr. Whitfield's powerful voice stirred souls that the harmless Reverend Broadbent could not awaken. Initially, the boys were as excited as their mother, singing hymns and listening with fervor. Had Mr. Whitfield stayed longer, Harry and George might have traded their epaulettes for black clerical coats. The simple boys shared their experiences, constantly watching for the sacred "call" that thrilled Protestant England.

However, Mr. Whitfield could not remain indefinitely with this small congregation. His mission was to enlighten the entire benighted church, from east to west, awakening slumbering sinners. Nevertheless, he comforted the widow with precious letters and promised to send a tutor capable of teaching profane learning alongside spiritual strength.

Soon, a chosen vessel arrived from England. Young Mr. Ward possessed a voice as loud as Mr. Whitfield's and could speak almost as fluently and at length. Nights and evenings echoed with his exhortations; domestic negroes crept to the doors to listen, and servants crowded the porch windows to hear him. He exerted the greatest influence over the "black sheep" of the Castlewood flock. These woolly lambs were deeply moved by his words, and when he led the hymns, a negro chorus rose within the house audible across the Potomac—a sound unheard in the Colonel's time, for that gentleman distrusted all cassocks and preferred debating theology only over backgammon. Where charity funds were needed, no one was more ready, and the good, easy Virginian clergyman, who loved backgammon dearly, believed the Colonel's charity covered his other shortcomings.

Mr. Ward was handsome, and his preaching pleased Madame Esmond immediately, satisfying her as much as Mr. Whitfield's had. Women of a century past were credulous, eager to admire and believe, prone to imagining infinite excellence in objects of their devotion. For weeks, perhaps months, Madame Esmond never tired of Mr. Ward's glib voice and voluble commonplaces. True to her nature, she insisted her neighbors attend, ordering them to be converted. Her favorite, the young Mr. Washington, she pressed repeatedly to stay at Castlewood and benefit from the spiritual advantages available. Yet, Mr. Washington had pressing business elsewhere and invariably ordered his horse away when Mr. Ward's services began. Furthermore, true to the nature of boys toward their pedagogue, the twins quickly grew tired and rebellious under the new teacher.

They found him a poor scholar, a dull fellow, and ill-mannered. George knew more Latin and Greek than his master, catching him in perpetual blunders and false accents. Harry, permitted greater liberties, mimicked Ward's eating and speaking mannerisms until Mrs. Mountain, Madame Esmond, and even little Fanny Mountain were forced to laugh. Madame Esmond might have exposed him as a vulgar quack had not her sons opposed him, a stance she countered with indomitable will. "What matters whether he possesses more or less of profane learning?" she asked. "In that which is most precious, Mr. W. is able to teach us all. What if his manners are slightly rough? Heaven does not choose its elect from among the great and wealthy. I wish you knew one book as well as Mr. Ward does. It is your wicked pride—the pride of all Esmonds—that prevents you from listening.

Following the departure of her discredited spiritual adviser, Madam Esmond and her son appeared to reach a fragile reconciliation. Yet, though George never voiced the grievance against his mother, the weight of their estrangement pressed heavily upon the boy's mind. Soon after these domestic upheavals, he fell violently ill. During his feverish delirium, his mind wandered, and he would shriek in terror, "Broken! Broken! It never, never can be mended!" His mother sat vigil beside his tossing form on the midnight bed, her heart heavy with silent dread. Her medical skills proved futile; the malady worsened despite the array of nostrums she drew from her closet and administered to her household.

Desperate, she faced further humiliation. One day, she rode through the deep snow on her pony to the humble dwelling of Mr. Dempster, imploring him to aid her suffering child. "I shall bury my resentment, madam," he promised gently, echoing her own past sacrifice of pride. "Please God, I may be time enough to help my dear young pupil!" He gathered his lancets and medicines, summoned his sole Negro servant, shuttered his lonely hut, and hastened back to Castlewood. For several days, it seemed inevitable that poor Harry would inherit the estate, but Mr. Dempster's skill triumphed. The fever broke, the intermittent attacks subsided, and George was restored to near-health.

Doctors recommended a change of air, perhaps even a voyage to England, but the widow, having quarreled with her children's relatives there, confessed with contrition that she had acted too hastily. Instead, a journey north and east was decided upon. The two young gentlemen, accompanied by Mr. Dempster as their tutor and two servants, sailed to New York and ascended the magnificent Hudson River to Albany. There, they were welcomed by the province's leading gentry before traveling into the French territories, where their letters of introduction secured them warm hospitality among the French nobility.

Harry threw himself into the wilds, camping with the Indigenous peoples, trading furs, and hunting bears. George, however, possessed no taste for field sports and his constitution remained delicate. Consequently, he became a particular favorite with the French ladies, who rarely encountered young Englishmen fluent in the language. George mastered the *bel air* so thoroughly that he could pass for a Frenchman. He danced the minuet with elegance, learned the latest imported French airs, and played them beautifully on his violin. Though his voice had recently broken, shifting from treble to bass—much to the envy of Harry, who was away on a hunt—he even engaged in an affair of honor with a young ensign of the Regiment of Auvergne, the Chevalier de la Jabotière. George "pinked" the ensign in the shoulder, swearing an eternal friendship thereafter. Madame de Mouchy, the Superintendent's wife, declared that any mother was blessed to have such a son and penned a glowing letter to Madam Esmond regarding George's conduct.

Mr. Whitfield, had he known of the widow's elation at her son's prowess, might not have been entirely pleased. When the lads returned home after ten delightful months, their mother marveled at their growth and refinement. George had matured so significantly that he nearly equaled his younger brother in stature. The boys were often indistinguishable, save for their hair: George wore his raven locks loose, while Harry tied his lighter tresses with a ribbon, finding the powdered wig ceremony too cumbersome for country life.

Those familiar with the earlier chapters know that Mr. George Esmond was of a jealous and suspicious nature, yet generous, gentle, and incapable of deceit. Though too magnanimous to seek revenge, he was almost unable to forgive injuries. He departed home with no goodwill toward an honorable gentleman whose name would later become legendary: George Washington. Upon his return, George held no softer opinion of his mother's and grandfather's friend. Washington, though barely of age, carried the demeanor of a man decades older. Possessing an extraordinary simplicity and gravity, he had managed his mother's and family's affairs since childhood, earning the trust of friends and the local gentry more readily than men twice his senior.

Mrs. Mountain, Madam Esmond's devoted companion and a woman of a jealous, match-making disposition, frequently corresponded with the boys during their travels. She believed everyone sought to marry everyone else. Scarcely an unmarried man visited Castlewood without Mrs. Mountain imagining he eyed the mistress of the mansion. She was convinced the detested Mr. Ward courted the widow, whom she suspected reciprocated. Regarding Mr. Washington, she noted his desire for marriage and assumed a shrewd young gentleman would seek a wealthy bride. Ignoring the fifteen-year age gap between the Major and Madam Esmond, she reasoned that such unions were common in their family; Lady Mountair's mother had married a colonel decades her senior, only to henpeck him until death. Mrs. Mountain, a petite woman barely five feet tall even in high heels, contrasted herself with Mr. Washington, a towering six-foot-two giant. "Great tall men always marry little chits of women," she deduced logically. Therefore, Mr. Washington must be courting the widow.

She shared these sagacious theories with her "boy," George, who begged her, for heaven's sake, to silence her. She claimed she could control her tongue but not her eyes, recounting a hundred incidents that confirmed her suspicions. Had she whispered these doubts to Harry? George asked sternly. No. "George is my boy; Harry is his mother's boy," she insisted. "Besides, if I spoke to him, he'd tell your mother instantly. Poor Harry cannot keep a secret, and then there would be a dreadful quarrel between Madam and me!"

"I beg you to keep this quiet, Mountain," George commanded with dignity. "Or you and I shall quarrel. You must not mention such absurd suspicions to me or anyone else."

Absurd? Why absurd? Mr. Washington was constantly in the widow's company. His name was perpetually on her lips; she cited his virtues as models for her sons, consulted him on every estate decision, and never bought a horse or sold a barrel of tobacco without his approval. At Castlewood stood a room officially designated "Mr. Washington's Room." "He actually leaves his clothes and portmanteau there when he departs," Mrs. Mountain lamented. "One day will come when he won't go away!"

Meanwhile, George adopted a frigid courtesy toward his mother's favorite, which chafed the honest gentleman, though he dared not remonstrate. Occasionally, George would offer a stinging sarcasm, breaking through barriers as easily as he tore through brambles on hunting expeditions with Harry. While George retreated to his bookroom to study mathematics, French, and Latin, sulking in increasing isolation, Harry roamed the woods with sporting companions—acquaintances, alas, not always as reputable as Mr. Washington.

When Mr. Washington finally visited Castlewood, his peculiar tenderness toward the mistress and the special cordiality she displayed nearly drove George Warrington to open rupture. However, this visit was merely a farewell. Major Washington was embarking on a perilous journey to the western Virginia frontier and beyond. The French had begun incursions into British territory, alarming governments in Virginia and Pennsylvania. Settlers had been driven from their homes by armed French forces, prompting British governors to halt these invasions. Under the convenient legal maxim that coastal possession granted rights to inland territory stretching to the Pacific, the British charters defined colonies only north-to-south, leaving them open to the west. The French, possessing colonies to the north and south, aimed to connect them via the Mississippi and St. Lawrence, threatening the British possessions.

Though peace was signed between Britain and France in 1748, the colonial dispute remained unresolved, waiting for a moment when one power could enforce its claims. By 1753, the issue erupted on the Ohio River, where British and French settlers collided. Beyond these Europeans were the Native Americans and land proprietors, yet the diplomats of St. James's and Versailles treated the conflict as purely European, ignoring the indigenous claimants while utilizing their tomahawks as tools for whichever side served their interests.

The Ohio Company, holding grants from Virginia along the river, found their settlements invaded by French detachments who forcibly ejected the Britons. Seeking protection, they turned to Mr. Dinwiddie, Lieutenant-Governor of Virginia, who resolved to send an ambassador to demand the French cease hostilities. Young Mr. Washington eagerly seized this opportunity for distinction, volunteering to leave his home and rural pursuits to deliver the Governor's message to the French commander at Fort Le Boeuf. Accompanied by a guide, an interpreter, and a few attendants, the intrepid envoy traveled from Williamsburg to Lake Erie in the autumn of 1753. The French commander's reply was blunt: his orders were to hold the post and drive all Englishmen away. The French openly declared their intent to possess the Ohio.

Armed with this harsh rejection, the messenger from Virginia retraced his steps through danger and hardship, navigating lonely forests and frozen rivers by compass, camping by forest fires in the snow. Harry Warrington cursed his bad fortune, having been absent at a cockfight when he might have witnessed such noble sport. Upon his return, conducting the expedition with heroic energy and simplicity, Major Washington became even more beloved by the lady of Castlewood. She hailed him as a model for her sons.

"Ah, Harry!" she would exclaim. "Think of you, with your cock-fighting and races,

Surely no man can claim more sympathy than one endowed with bravery, youth, good looks, and misfortune. Madam Esmond might have borne twenty sons, yet she possessed every right to admire her young soldier. Her attention toward Mr. Washington was now undivided; she raved of him and praised him in every assembly. She eagerly highlighted his sterling virtues to her sons, drawing sharp contrasts between his noble character and Harry's penchant for pleasure—whom she dismissed as "the wild boy!"—and George's listless contemplation of books.

George, however, was not swayed by his mother's extravagant encomiums. Instead, the jealous demon within him festered until he became a torment to himself and everyone around him. He offered jests so profound that his simple-minded mother failed to grasp their meaning, sitting bewildered amidst his sarcasms, unable to comprehend his moody, saturnine humor.

Meanwhile, public events were unfolding that would soon dictate the fate of their humble household. The quarrel between the French and English in North America had evolved from a provincial dispute into a national conflict. Reinforcements from France had already reached Canada, and English troops were anticipated in Virginia.

"Alas! my dear friend!" wrote Madame la Presidente de Mouchy from Quebec to her young correspondent, George Warrington. "How contrary is destiny to us! I see you leaving the embrace of an adored mother to throw yourself into the arms of Bellona. I foresee you returning wounded from combat. I hesitate almost to wish victory to our lilies when I behold you serving under the banners of the Leopard. There are enmities the heart does not recognize—ours remain at peace amidst the tumult. All here love and salute you, as well as Monsieur the Bear-Hunter, your brother (that cold Hippolyte who preferred the chase to the soft conversation of our ladies!). Your friend, your enemy, the Chevalier de la Jabotière, burns to meet his generous rival on the field of Mars. M. Du Quesne spoke of you last night at supper. M. Du Quesne, my husband, sends affectionate greetings to his young friend, accompanied by those of your sincere Presidente de Mouchy."

Indeed, the banner of the Leopard was unfurled to the wind, rallying numerous King's soldiers. It was resolved to wrest from the French all conquests made upon British dominions. Two regiments were raised and funded directly by the King in America, while a fleet carrying two additional regiments was dispatched from home under an experienced commander.

In February 1755, Commodore Keppel, aboard the famous *Centurion*—the same vessel in which Anson had circumnavigated the globe—anchored in Hampton Roads with two ships of war. Onboard were General Braddock, his staff, and a portion of his troops. Appointed by the Duke, the General awaited reinforcements. A century prior, the Duke of Cumberland had been hailed as "The Duke par excellence" in England; though not quite so renowned, he was certainly no worse than the caricatures drawn by his enemies. A fleet of transports swiftly followed, laden with stores, men, and money.

General Braddock landed his forces at Alexandria on the Potomac River and proceeded to Annapolis, Maryland, where he convened the colonial governors. Urging unity, he exhorted them to mobilize their respective provinces for the common cause. The arrival of the General and his small army sparked immense excitement throughout the provinces, nowhere more intense than at Castlewood.

Harry immediately departed to witness the troops encamped at Alexandria. The sight of their lines delighted him, as did the inspiring music of fifes and drums. He quickly befriended the officers of both regiments, longing to join the expedition. He became a welcome guest at their mess. Madam Esmond was pleased that her sons could enjoy the company of fashionable gentlemen from England. She believed their association would refine them, noting that these English officers were vastly superior to the horse-racing and cock-fighting Virginian squires, the lawyers, pettifoggers, and "toad-eaters" she encountered at the lieutenant-governor's table.

Madam Esmond possessed a keen eye for detecting flatterers in others' households. She was particularly satirical regarding the small circle of officials in Williamsburg, lacking patience for their etiquette and squabbles over precedence. Regarding the King's officers, both Harry and his elder brother smiled at their mother's compliments concerning the elegance and propriety of the camp's gentlemen. Yet, had the good lady known the truth—if she could have heard their raucous jokes and songs over wine and punch, or witnessed the disreputable state of many as they staggered to their lodgings—she would hardly have recommended their company so readily.

Men and officers swaggered through the countryside, frightening peaceful farmers and villagers with their riotous behavior. The General raged against his troops' disorder and the provincials' "traitorous niggardliness." The soldiers treated the land almost as a conquered territory, scorning the locals and insulting the wives of their Indian allies who had traveled to join the English. Consequently, the General was forced to bar Indian women from his camp. Amazed and outraged, their husbands withdrew, and within months, their invaluable services were lost to him.

Had Madam Esmond heard some of the stories circulating about the camp's gentlemen, she might have hesitated, but she rejected them outright. After all, soldiers were soldiers; those officers who visited Castlewood on her son's invitation were, in fact, most polite gentlemen. She received them graciously, offering them the finest sporting opportunities the country provided. Soon, the General himself sent polite messages to Mistress of Castlewood. Citing his father's service under the glorious Marlborough and the enduring respect for Colonel Esmond's name in England, he requested permission to visit Castlewood and pay his respects to the daughter of such a meritorious officer.

If Madam Esmond had understood the true motive behind General Braddock's politeness, her charms might not have been so easily won. The Commander-in-Chief held levees at Alexandria, attended by the local gentry. Among those presenting themselves were the twin brothers of Castlewood, mounted on their best horses, wearing their latest London suits, escorted by their two negro boys in smart liveries.

Braddock was sulky and irritable with the provincial gentry, barely acknowledging the young gentlemen. At dinner, he casually asked his aide-de-camp who the "young Squire Gawkeys" in blue, gold, and red waistcoats were. Mr. Dinwiddie, the Lieutenant-Governor of Virginia, along with the agent from Pennsylvania and a few others, were dining with his Excellency.

"Oh!" exclaimed Mr. Dinwiddie. "Those are the sons of the Princess Pocahontas."

With a tremendous oath, the General retorted, "Who the deuce was she?"

Dinwiddie, who harbored no love for her due to her previous imperiousness, launched into a disrespectful and ridiculous account of Madam Esmond, mocking her pomposity and immense pretensions. He entertained General Braddock with anecdotes until his Excellency fell asleep. Upon waking, Dinwiddie had departed, but the Philadelphia gentleman remained, deeply engaged in conversation with the officers.

The General resumed the topic, speaking of Madam Esmond in curt, disrespectful terms typical of soldiers of that era. He demanded again the name of the "old fool" Dinwiddie had mentioned, breaking into expressions of contempt for the gentry and the country at large. Then, Mr. Franklin of Philadelphia interjected. He repeated the widow's name but presented a vastly different view of her character, demonstrating extensive knowledge of her, her father, and her estate—as he did with every subject under discussion. He explained that Madam Esmond possessed cattle, horses, and ample stores that would prove highly useful at this critical juncture, strongly recommending that the General conciliate her.

Already convinced that Mr. Franklin was a shrewd and intelligent man, the General graciously ordered an aide-de-camp to invite the two young men to dine the following day. They arrived promptly; the General was pleasant and good-natured, and the members of his family made much of them. Behaving with the modesty and breeding befitting their station, the twins returned home delighted. Nor was their mother less pleased at the civilities shown to her boys.

In response to Braddock's message, Madam Esmond penned a note in her finest style, acknowledging his politeness and requesting that he specify a time for her to receive him at Castlewood.

The arrival of the army and the looming campaign became the constant topic of conversation within the Castlewood household. To embark on the campaign was Harry's dearest wish. He dreamed only of war and battle, spending his time with officers in Williamsburg, scouring and polishing every gun and sword in the house, reviving childhood amusements, and drilling the negroes.

His mother, possessing a gallant spirit herself, knew that the time had come for one of her sons to leave her and serve the King. She dared not decide which of them the lot would fall upon. She admired and respected the elder, yet she felt she loved the younger boy with all the passion of her heart. Though eager for war, Harry too hesitated to broach the subject nearest his heart. Whenever he ventured to mention it to George, the older brother's countenance wore an ominous look. Harry held a feudal attachment for his elder sibling, worshipping him with extravagant regard and yielding to him in all things. Thus, seeing George's grave preoccupation with military matters,

On the day appointed for Madame Esmond's entertainment to the General, the house of Castlewood was set out with the greatest splendor. Madame Esmond arrayed herself in a dress far more magnificent than she was accustomed to wear. Indeed, she wished to render every honor to her guest and to make the entertainment—which, in reality, was a sad affair to her—as pleasant as possible for her company.

The General's new aide-de-camp was the first to arrive. The widow received him in the covered gallery before the house. He dismounted at the steps, and his servants led away his horses to the well-known quarters. No young gentleman in the colony was better mounted or a better horseman than Mr. Washington. For a while before the Major retired to divest himself of his riding-boots, he and his hostess paced the gallery in conversation. She had much to say to him; she had to hear from him a confirmation of his own appointment as aide-de-camp to General Braddock, and to speak of her son's approaching departure.

Negro servants bearing the dishes for the approaching feast passed perpetually as they talked. They descended the steps down to the rough lawn in front of the house and paced a while in the shade. Mr. Washington announced his Excellency's speedy approach, accompanied by Mr. Franklin of Pennsylvania in his coach. "This Mr. Franklin had been a common printer's boy," Mrs. Esmond had heard; "a pretty pass things were coming to when such persons rode in the coach of the Commander-in-Chief!"

"Mr. Washington said a more shrewd and sensible gentleman never rode in coach or walked on foot," Mrs. Esmond noted internally. She thought the Major was too liberally disposed toward this gentleman; but Mr. Washington stoutly maintained against the widow that the printer was a most ingenious, useful, and meritorious man.

"I am glad, at least, that, as my boy is going to make the campaign, he will not be with tradesmen, but with gentlemen, with gentlemen of honor and fashion," said Madame Esmond in her most stately manner.

Mr. Washington had seen the gentlemen of honor and fashion over their cups, and perhaps thought that all their sayings and doings were not precisely such as would tend to instruct or edify a young man on his entrance into life; but he wisely chose to tell no tales out of school. He said that Harry and George, now that they were coming into the world, must take their share of good and bad, and hear what both sorts had to say.

"To be with a veteran officer of the finest army in the world," faltered the widow; "with gentlemen who have been bred in the midst of the Court; with friends of his Royal Highness, the Duke..."

"The widow's friend only inclined his head. He did not choose to allow his countenance to depart from its usual handsome gravity."

"And with you, dear Colonel Washington, by whom my father always set such store. You don't know how much he trusted in you. You will take care of my boy, sir, will not you? You are but five years older, yet I trust to you more than to his seniors; my father always told the children—I always bade them—to look up to Mr. Washington."

"You know I would have done anything to win Colonel Esmond's favor, Madam. How much would I not venture to merit his daughter's?" The gentleman bowed with not too ill a grace. The lady blushed and dropped one of the lowest curtsies. (Madame Esmond's curtsy was considered unrivaled over the whole province.)

"Mr. Washington," she said, "will be always sure of a mother's affection, whilst he gives so much of his to her children." And so saying, she gave him her hand, which he kissed with profound politeness.

The little lady presently re-entered her mansion, leaning upon the tall young officer's arm. Here they were joined by George, who came to them, accurately powdered and richly attired, saluting his parent and his friend alike with low and respectful bows. Nowadays, a young man walks into his mother's room with hobnailed high-lows and a wideawake on his head; and instead of making her a bow, puffs a cigar into her face. But George, though he made the lowest possible bow to Mr. Washington and his mother, was by no means in good humor with either of them. A polite smile played round the lower part of his countenance, whilst watchfulness and wrath glared out from the two upper windows.

What had been said or done? Nothing that might not have been performed or uttered before the most decent, polite, or pious company. Why then should Madame Esmond continue to blush, and the brave Colonel to look somewhat red as he shook his young friend's hand?

The Colonel asked Mr. George if he had had good sport.

"No," said George, curtly.

"Have you?" And then he looked at the picture of his father, which hung in the parlor.

The Colonel, not a talkative man ordinarily, straightway entered into a long description of his sport. He described where he had been in the morning, what woods he had hunted with the King's officers, how many birds they had shot, and what game they had brought down. Though not a jocular man ordinarily, the Colonel made a long description of Mr. Braddock's heavy person and great boots as he floundered through the Virginian woods, hunting, as they called it, with a pack of dogs gathered from various houses, with a pack of negroes barking as loud as the dogs, and actually shooting the deer when they came in sight of him.

"Great God, sir!" said Mr. Braddock, puffing and blowing, "what would Sir Robert have said in Norfolk, to see a man hunting with a fowling-piece in his hand, and a pack of dogs actually laid on to a turkey!"

"Indeed, Colonel, you are vastly comical this afternoon!" cried Madame Esmond, with a neat little laugh, whilst her son listened to the story, looking more glum than ever.

"What Sir Robert is there at Norfolk? Is he one of the newly arrived army-gentlemen?"

"The General meant Norfolk at home, madam, not Norfolk in Virginia," said Colonel Washington. "Mr. Braddock had been talking of a visit to Sir Robert Walpole, who lived in that county, and of the great hunts the old Minister kept there, and of his grand palace, and his pictures at Houghton. I should like to see a good field and a good fox-chase at home better than any sight in the world," the honest sportsman added with a sigh.

"Nevertheless, there is good sport here, as I was saying," said young Esmond, with a sneer.

"What sport?" cried the other, looking at him.

"Why, sure you know, without looking at me so fiercely, and stamping your foot, as if you were going to charge me with the foils. Are you not the best sportsman of the countryside? Are there not all the fish of the field, and the beasts of the trees, and the fowls of the sea—no—the fish of the trees, and the beasts of the sea—and the—bah! You know what I mean. I mean shad, and salmon, and rock-fish, and roe-deer, and hogs, and buffaloes, and bisons, and elephants, for what I know. I'm no sportsman."

"No, indeed," said Mr. Washington, with a look of scarcely repressed scorn.

"Yes, I understand you. I am a milksop. I have been bred at my mamma's knee. Look at these pretty apron-strings, Colonel! Who would not like to be tied to them? See of what a charming color they are! I remember when they were black—that was for my grandfather."

"And who would not mourn for such a gentleman?" said the Colonel, as the widow, surprised, looked at her son.

"And, indeed, I wish my grandfather were here, and would resurge, as he promises to do on his tombstone; and would bring my father, the Ensign, with him."

"Ah, Harry!" cried Mrs. Esmond, bursting into tears, as at this juncture her second son entered the room—in just such another suit, gold-corded frock, braided waistcoat, silver-hilted sword, and solitaire, as that which his elder brother wore.

"Oh, Harry, Harry!" cried Madame Esmond, and flew to her younger son.

"What is it, mother?" asked Harry, taking her in his arms.

"What is the matter, Colonel?"

"Upon my life, it would puzzle me to say," answered the Colonel, biting his lips.

"A mere question, Hal, about pink ribbons, which I think vastly becoming to our mother; as, no doubt, the Colonel does."

"Sir, will you please to speak for yourself?" cried the Colonel, bustling up, and then sinking his voice again.

"He speaks too much for himself," wept the widow.

"I protest I don't any more know the source of these tears than the source of the Nile," said George. "And if the picture of my father were to begin to cry, I should almost as much wonder at the paternal tears. What have I uttered? An allusion to ribbons! Is there some poisoned pin in them, which has been struck into my mother's heart by a guilty fiend of a London mantua-maker

His Excellency, the Commander-in-Chief, departed for a visit to Madam Esmond with a splendor befitting the highest authority in His Majesty's vast dominions across North America. A detachment of dragoons marched ahead, leading the procession out of Williamsburg amidst the raucous cheers of a devoted, predominantly Black populace. The General traveled in his own carriage, attended at the threshold by Captain Talmadge, his Master of the Horse, who rode alongside the heavy, ornately decorated vehicle throughout the journey to Castlewood. Seated within the carriage were Major Danvers, the aide-de-camp, and the postmaster from Philadelphia, Mr. Benjamin Franklin.
