Once merely a printer's apprentice, Franklin had evolved into a remarkably astute individual, possessing a wealth of knowledge concerning the colony and England itself—where he had previously resided—that commanded the respect of the General and the entire Esmond family.

"'Tis truly remarkable how someone of such humble origins could acquire such diverse learning and refinement," His Excellency remarked graciously, tipping his hat to the postmaster.

Franklin bowed politely, attributing his polish to the fortunate chance of associating with gentlemen like the General, which allowed him to study their manners and adapt accordingly. Regarding formal education, he modestly noted his limitations, citing his father's financial struggles and the sparse educational resources available in his native New England. "However, I have exerted myself to the utmost," Franklin explained. "I possess nothing comparable to what exists in England."

General Braddock erupted in laughter. "Education? By George, there are officers in the army who cannot decide whether to spell 'bull' with one or two 'b's! I've heard the Duke of Marlborough was hardly a proficient penman either. Though I never had the honor of serving under that noble commander—he predated my time—I thoroughly thrashed the French despite his lack of scholarship."

"I am fully aware of both instances," Franklin replied calmly.

"Nor is my Duke a scholar," Braddock continued, noticing Franklin's knowing glance. "Aha, Mr. Postmaster, you've heard that tale too—I see the twinkle in your eye."

Instantly, Franklin withdrew his mischievous expression, replacing it with the wide-eyed innocence of a child. "He lacks scholarly pursuits, true, but he matches any French general in combat. He secured the crown for the best of kings, our Most Gracious Majesty, King George."

Franklin removed his hat, sending a cloud of powdered hair drifting from his large wig. "He is the soldier's greatest ally and the unyielding foe of those beggarly red-shanked Scottish rebels and cunning Roman Catholic Jesuits seeking to strip us of our liberties and faith. His Royal Highness, my gracious master, may not be a scholar, yet he ranks among the finest gentlemen in the world."

"I have witnessed His Royal Highness on horseback during a review of the Guards in Hyde Park," Franklin added. "The Duke is indeed a magnificent rider."

"You shall propose a toast to him today, Postmaster. He is the ideal master, friend, and son; the finest gentleman who has ever worn an epaulet."

"Epaulets are entirely outside my sphere of interest, sir," Franklin laughed. "I reside in a Quaker city."

"Naturally, they suit you less," the General agreed warmly. "Every man to his trade. You and your class belong to your studies, and we welcome you. Our role is to fight the enemy and govern the nation. Come, gentlemen! Look at these roads in this colony; how this infernal carriage bounces!"

Turning his gaze outward, he spotted two Negro boys in livery. "Who rides that fine gelding?"

"It is Mr. Washington," Major Danvers identified.

"I'd make him a corporal in the Horse Grenadiers," the General mused. "He carries himself well on horseback and clearly knows this terrain, Mr. Franklin."

"Yes, indeed," Franklin confirmed.

"And he is a remarkably genteel young man, considering his background. One might expect the polish of Europe given his opportunities."

"He strives his best," Franklin observed, casting an innocent look toward the stout Chief. The General sat swaying heavily in the carriage, his face flushed crimson matching his coat, swearing profusely while remaining ignorant of everything except the virtues of alcohol and women. Not of noble birth yet absurdly proud of his lineageless status, he was brave as a bulldog, savage yet generous, gentle in softer moments, easily amused, dull-witted, and completely uneducated. Yet, he believed his country supreme and himself equal to any gentleman in it.

"Yes, he is remarkably accomplished for a provincial," Franklin admitted. "Though he was defeated at Fort What-d'ye-call-it last year near the Thing-a-ma-jig River. What was the name of that place, Captain Talmadge?"

"The Lord knows, sir," Talmadge replied, adding dryly, "and I suspect the Postmaster is mocking us both."

"Oh, Captain!"

"A classic trap. He had only militia and Indians. Good day, Mr. Washington. Fine steed, sir. Was that your first engagement last year?"

"That at Fort Necessity? Yes, sir," Washington responded solemnly as he approached, flanked by two neatly dressed Negro grooms in velvet caps. "I began poorly, sir, having never seen action until that unfortunate day."

"You were all raw recruits, my good fellow. Imagine our militia fleeing from the Scots and cursing them. You needed proper troops."

"Your Excellency knows it is my fervent desire to serve alongside them," Washington stated earnestly.

"By George, we shall endeavor to grant that wish, sir," the General boomed, using one of his customary oaths. As the heavy carriage rolled toward Castlewood, Washington requested permission to ride ahead, announcing the General's imminent arrival to the hostess.

The Commander-in-Chief's progress was so glacial that several lesser invitees, eager to greet him, caught up to form a procession trailing the dust of his wheels. Leading this rear guard was Mr. Dinwiddie, the Lieutenant-Governor, accompanied by his Negro servants and Parson Broadbent, the jovial Williamsburg chaplain. Soon joined was Mr. Dempster, the schoolmaster, sporting a grand Ramillies wig reserved for state occasions. Appearing next was Mr. Laws, the judge, riding with Madam Laws on a pillion behind him, while a Negro servant carried her cap atop a mule. The spectacle was so comical that Major Danvers and Mr. Franklin burst into laughter, though quietly enough not to disturb the sleeping General. They signaled the quirky rearguard to proceed, leaving the Commander-in-Chief and his dragoon escort to arrive at their leisure. At Castlewood, there was ample space for everyone. Meat, drink, and the finest tobacco awaited His Majesty's soldiers; joy and celebration were prepared for the Negroes; and a hearty welcome extended to their masters.

The honest General required assistance reaching nearly every dish and constantly demanded refills. He praised Nathan's *sangaree* highly, having consumed it liberally upon arrival. The table groaned with cider, ale, brandy, and abundant Bordeaux wine—some brought personally by Colonel Esmond from England, deemed worthy even of pontiffs by the diminutive Mr. Dempster, who winked knowingly at Parson Broadbent. The clergyman reciprocated the gesture, drinking without concern for the Latin phrase, for he had never troubled himself with the language. Broadbent was a notorious gambler, drinker, and cockfighter who had spent considerable time in Fleet Prison and various London taverns. Sent to Virginia to manage a church living, he had become fast friends with young George Esmond, engaging in countless duels of cocks, hunts, fishing expeditions, and the slaughter of myriad birds. Rumors circulated that Broadbent was the midnight poacher pursued by Mr. Washington's dogs at Mount Vernon, resulting in a bruised face and torn coat after narrowly escaping capture. George and Broadbent shared all games and pastimes, while Harry Esmond reportedly stayed behind with Mr. Dempster, leaving George to read his own books and keep silent about his brother's exploits.

Every bird of Virginia's sky and every seasonal fish graced Madam Esmond's table to nourish the General and the gathering of English and American gentlemen. The gumbo was hailed as perfection—a dish so renowned that young George's Black servant, discovered hiding his head in a bowl of it, was christened Gumbo upon the spot. The shad were rich and fresh; the stewed terrapins were deemed worthy of London aldermen, and indeed, any duke or emperor. The Negro women possessed a natural genius for cooking, nurtured under the strict supervision of the late and current Madam Esmond. Certain delicacies, particularly sweets and tarts, were prepared by Madam Esmond herself with exquisite neatness. Following the kindly, cumbersome customs of the era, she rolled up her lace-sleeved cuffs, revealing charming arms and slender wrists as she carved the principal meats, demonstrating a hospitality far more vigorous than contemporary standards. The traditional law of the table dictated that the mistress press her guests with eager warmth, encouraging further enjoyment, mastering culinary arts, and executing carvings of poultry, fish, game, and joints. She cheered her guests to new efforts, whispering to Mr. Braddock, "I have reserved the salmon's cheek for Your Excellency—I accept no refusal!" Turning to Mr. Franklin, she noted, "You drink only water, sir, though our cellar holds wholesome vintage that causes no headache." Addressing the Judge

Once the distinguished guests of Castlewood had been escorted to their respective quarters, the young masters retreated to their own room to discuss the monumental events of the day. Their consensus was swift and unanimous: such a marriage was unthinkable. Could the heir to the Marquis of Esmond's fortune truly wed the younger son of a colonial family, a man raised merely as a land surveyor? To hand over the stewardship of Castlewood, and the boys themselves at the tender age of nineteen, to the mercy of a stepfather barely twenty-three? It was monstrous.

Harry immediately proposed storming his mother's bedroom—where her dark-skinned maidservants were currently removing the heavy jewels and finery she had worn for the feast—to protest the abhorrent match. He declared they would return home, live off their modest estate, and abandon her forever if this unnatural union proceeded.

George, however, advocated a different strategy, outlining his plan to his awestruck brother. "Our mother cannot marry a man with whom one or both of us have fought on the field," George reasoned. "She cannot wed a man who has wounded us, killed us, or whom we have wounded or killed. We must summon him, Harry."

Harry recognized the profound logic in his brother's words and admired his superior sagacity. "No, George," he agreed. "Mother cannot marry our murderer; she wouldn't stoop so low. And if we challenge him, he is finished. *Cadit quaestio*, as Mr. Dempster used to say. Shall I dispatch my page to issue a challenge to Colonel Washington immediately?"

"My dear Harry," the elder brother replied, reflecting with some pride on his own affair of honor at Quebec, "you are not yet accustomed to matters of this gravity."

"No," admitted Harry, sighing with envy and reverence for his senior.

"We cannot insult a gentleman within our own walls," George continued, speaking with majestic authority. "The laws of honor forbid such inhospitable treatment. However, we may ride out with him. Once the park gates are shut, we shall make our intentions perfectly clear."

"That we can, by George!" Harry cried, seizing his brother's hand. "And we shall, too. Say, Georgy..." Suddenly, the lad's face turned crimson, and his brother asked what he intended to propose.

"This is my turn, brother," Harry pleaded earnestly. "If you undertake the campaign, I deserve the other affair. Indeed, indeed, I do." He prayed fervently for this promotion.

"Again, the head of the house must take the lead, my dear," George said with a superb air. "Should I fall, my Harry will avenge me. But I must fight George Washington, Hal. And it is best that I do; for, in truth, I hate him the most. Was it not he who counseled my mother to order that wretch, Ward, to seize me?"

"Ah, George," interjected the more pacific younger brother, "you ought to forget and forgive."

"Forget? Never, sir, as long as I remember. One cannot command memory to vanish from a man's mind; a wrong committed yesterday remains a wrong tomorrow. I have never harmed anyone knowingly, and I shall never allow harm to befall me if I can prevent it. I think poorly of Mr. Ward, but not so poorly as to believe he will ever forgive you for that blow with the ruler. Colonel Washington is our enemy, mine especially. He has advised one injustice against me and plots a greater. Tell you what, brother: we must punish him."

The old Bordeaux wine had set George's usually pale complexion ablaze. Harry, his brother's devoted worshipper, could not help but admire George's haughty demeanor and rapid eloquence. Prepared to follow his leader with his usual docility, the boys retired to their beds. Before retiring, George issued special instructions to his junior to remain civil to all the guests while they remained under their mother's roof the following day.

Decent manners and a reluctance to spread gossip prevented us from naming which of Madame Esmond's guests was the first to succumb to the weight of her hospitality. The respectable descendants of Messrs. Talmadge and Danvers, aides-de-camp to His Excellency, might prefer not to recall how their ancestors were intoxicated a century prior; yet, the gentlemen themselves bore no shame for the fact, and there is little doubt they or their companions were tipsy two or three times a week. Imagine them stumbling to bed, supported by sympathetic negroes; imagine their vinous General, too stout a drinker to resist six bottles of Bordeaux, led to his chamber by the young gentlemen of the house, and quickly falling asleep to the slumber bestowed by friendly Bacchus.

Madame Esmond observed the state of her guests without surprise or horror. She rose early the next morning, arranging cooling drinks for their overheated throats, which the servants delivered to their rooms. At breakfast, one of the English officers teased Mr. Franklin, who consumed no alcohol whatsoever, refusing the morning draught of toddy. The officer pointed out that the Philadelphia gentleman had lost two pleasures: the drink itself and the toddy. The young man retorted that the disease was pleasant and the remedy delicious, laughing as he proposed to repeat both.

Colonel Washington, the General's new American aide-de-camp, remained quite sober and serene. The British officers vowed they must intervene and teach him the ways of the English army, but the Virginian gentleman gravely stated he had no desire to learn that aspect of English military education.

The widow, preoccupied with the cares of a grand dinner followed by a substantial breakfast, had little leisure to scrutinize her sons' behavior closely. Yet, she noted that George was scrupulously polite to her favorite, Colonel Washington, as he was to all other guests.

Before departing, General Braddock secured a private audience with Madame Esmond. His Excellency formally offered to adopt her son into his family. When arrangements for George's departure were finalized between his mother and future commander, Madame Esmond, though aware of the perils, showed no squeamish terror regarding the dangers of the bottle—a hazard she knew would be among the severest her son faced. She understood her boy must take his part in the world and endure his share of evil and good. "Mr. Braddock is a perfect fine gentleman in the morning," she told her aide-de-camp, Mrs. Mountain, stoutly. "Though my father did not drink, it is certain that many of the finest companies in England do."

The jolly General shook hands warmly with George, who presented himself to His Excellency after the maternal interview concluded. The General welcomed him and instructed him to attend at Frederick in three days, shortly after which the expedition would set forth.

Now, the great carriage was requisitioned again. The General's escort pranced around it; the other guests mounted their horses. Madame Esmond accompanied Her Excellency to the steps of the veranda. The young gentlemen followed, standing on either side of the coach door. The guard trumpeter blew a shrill blast, and the negroes shouted, "Huzzah, and God save the King!" as Mr. Braddock graciously took leave of his hospitable hosts and rolled away toward headquarters.

As the boys ascended the steps, the Colonel was once more bidding farewell to their mother. No doubt she had been urging George into his namesake's care once more. Colonel Washington assured her, "With my life. You may depend on me," as the lads returned to their mother and the few remaining guests in the porch. The Colonel was fully booted and ready to depart. "Farewell, my dear Harry," he said. "With you, George, it is no adieu. We shall meet in three days at the camp."

Both young men were heading toward danger, perhaps even death. Colonel Washington was leaving, and she would not see him again before the campaign began. No wonder the widow was deeply moved.

George Warrington watched his mother's emotion and interpreted it with a pang of malignant scorn. "Stay yet a moment to console our mother," he said with a steady countenance. "Only the time to get ourselves booted, and my brother and I will ride with you a little way, George." George had already ordered his horses. The three young men sped off, their negro grooms trailing behind. Mrs. Mountain, knowing she had sown discord between them and trembling for the outcome, felt a vast relief that Mr. Washington departed without a quarrel with the brothers, and certainly without any open declaration of love to their mother.

No man could be more courteous in demeanor than George Warrington toward his neighbor and namesake, the Colonel. The latter was pleased and surprised by his young friend's altered behavior. The shared sense of impending danger, the necessity of future fellowship, the softening influence of the long friendship binding him to the Esmond family, and the tender farewells exchanged with the mistress of Castlewood inclined the Colonel to forget the unpleasantness of recent days. He became unusually friendly with his young companion. George was quite gay and easy; it was Harry who was now melancholy. He rode silently and wistfully beside his brother, keeping his distance from Colonel Washington, to whose side he had previously pressed eagerly. If the honest Colonel noticed his young friend's conduct, he likely attributed it to Harry's known affection for his brother and his natural anxiety to stay close to George as the day of parting approached.

They discussed the war and the probable conclusion of the campaign; none of the three doubted its successful termination. Two thousand veteran British troops under their commander must overcome any force the French could muster, provided they

Colonel Washington remained sequestered within one chamber of the tavern, lost in somber preparations for the impending confrontation. In the adjacent room, his adversary, George Warrington, deemed it prudent to attend to his final affairs. Dictating a flowery letter to his mother via his devoted brother and secretary, he offered a solemn farewell. He presumed that once the outcome of the morning's events became known—should he, as he likely would, fall—the scheme his mother had in mind would surely be abandoned.

"My dear, dear George, do not speak thus!" cried the terrified secretary, Harry.

"'As probably will be the case,'" George insisted with regal composure. "You know the skill of Colonel Washington. I, too, am quite proficient with a mark. It is highly probable that one or both of us shall drop. Therefore, I scarcely believe you intend to proceed with the plans you currently hold." His tone grew sharper, laden with a bitterness exceeding his earlier remarks. Harry wept as he transcribed the words.

"You see, I mention nothing," George continued. "Madame Esmond's name does not even appear in this quarrel. Recall our grandfather's memoirs; how he recounts Lord Castlewood fighting Lord Mohun over a pretext of a card dispute, yet never once hinting at the lady whose presence was the true catalyst? I took my cue from that, Harry. My mother is not implicated in this—"

"Why, child, what are you scribbling, and who taught you to spell?"

Harry had penned the final phrase, "in view," as *vew*, while a great blot of salty tears from his honest, boyish eyes had obscured several other errors.

"I cannot concern myself with spelling now, Georgy," whimpered the clerk, burying his face in his hands. "I am too miserable. I begin to suspect it is all nonsense. Perhaps Colonel Washington never..."

"Never intended to seize Castlewood? Never assumed airs among us? Never advised my mother to have me flogged? Never planned to wed her? Never insulted me before the King's officers? Nor wrote to his brother claiming we would benefit from his parental authority?" The young man cried out, slapping his breast pocket. "The letter is there! If anything happens to me, Harry Warrington, you will find it upon my corpse!"

"Write it yourself, Georgy; I cannot," Harry replied, digging his fists into his eyes until ink and tears smeared the entire composition.

On this, George took a fresh sheet, seated himself at his brother's desk, and composed a document filled with the longest vocabulary, the loftiest Latin quotations, and the deepest satire the youthful scribe could muster. He instructed that his enslaved boy, Sady, be freed; that his favorite book, *Horace*, along with a suitable stipend, be provided for his beloved tutor, Mr. Dempster; that his silver fruit-knife, music scores, and harpsichord be gifted to little Fanny Mountain; and that his brother bear a lock of his hair as a token of his eternal devotion. He sealed the missive with the ancestral coat-of-arms inherited from his grandfather.

"The watch, naturally, belongs to you," George said, retrieving his grandfather's gold timepiece. "Two and a half hours have already elapsed. It is time for Sady to return with the pistols. Take the watch, Harry dear."

"It is useless!" cried Harry, throwing his arms around his brother. "If he fights you, I will fight him too. If he kills my Georgy... oh, let him draw breath once more, and he shall have a shot at me!" The poor lad uttered phrases so poignant they were said to weigh heavily upon the recording angels tasked with documenting human sins in the celestial chancery.

Meanwhile, General Braddock's new aide-de-camp had drafted five letters in his bold, resolute hand, sealing them with his personal signet. One went to his mother at Mount Vernon; one to his brother; one simply to "M.C."; and one to His Excellency, Major-General Braddock.

"And one, young gentleman, is for your mother, Madam Esmond," noted the boys' informant.

Once again, the recording angel was compelled to depart with a violent expression torn from the lips of George Warrington. That celestial bureaucracy was often crowded with such cases, its messengers perpetually airborne. Yet, fearing for young George and his oath, there seemed to be no valid excuse. It was an execration born of a heart swollen with hatred, rage, and jealousy.

It was the tavern landlord who relayed these details to the youths. The Captain had donned his old militia uniform to honor the occasion, informing the boys that the Colonel was pacing the garden, awaiting them, and that the Regulars were nearly sober. A patch of earth near the Captain's log cabin had been fenced with shingles and cleared for a vegetable garden; there walked Colonel Washington, hands clasped behind his back, head bowed, a grave sorrow etched upon his handsome countenance. Negro servants crowded the fence line, peering over. Officers beneath the porch had roused themselves as well, as the host observed.

Captain Waring paced steadily under the balcony formed by the sloping porch and roof of the wooden structure, while Captain Grace lounged over the railing, staring with wide, unfocused eyes. Benson's was a notorious rendezvous for cockfights, horse matches, boxing, and wrestling bouts that drew Virginian country folk from afar. Many brawls had erupted there; men arrived sound and sober only to leave with broken ribs and gouged eyes. Squires, farmers, and negroes alike partook in the spectacle.

There, then, stalked the tall young Colonel, immersed in dismal contemplation. There was no escape from his predicution save the customary cruel path mandated by the laws of honor and local custom. Goaded into fury by the insolence of a boy, he had spoken insulting words. The young man had demanded reparation. George Warrington was shocked to realize that his jealousy and desire for revenge had festered in the boy's heart so deeply, yet the wrong lay squarely with the Colonel, who was bound to pay the forfeit.

Suddenly, a great hollering and shouting rose in the distance—a sound characteristic of negroes who loved noise, especially the raucous screams accompanying a gallop on horseback. All heads, whether woolly or powdered, turned toward the commotion. It emanated from the road the travelers had traversed three hours prior. Soon, the clatter of hooves echoed, and Mr. Sady appeared atop his foaming steed, firing a pistol into the midst of a tremendous uproar from his woolly companions. He discharged a second shot, to which Sady's horse, accustomed to hunting with Harry, reacted calmly. Now in the courtyard, surrounded by a dozen bellowing comrades, he descended amidst fluttering poultry, turkeys, kicking horses, and shrieking pigs. Brother-negroes swarmed around him, and he immediately began chatting with them.

"Sady, sir, come here!" roared Master Harry.
"Sady, come here! Confound you!" shouted Master George.
(Once more, the recording angel was summoned to file the incident in the register office.)

"Come directly, mas'r," Sady replied, resuming his conversation with his fellows. He grinned, drew the pistols from his holster, snapped the locks, pointed them at a grunting pig that charged through the yard, then aimed down the road he had just ridden, watching the woolly heads turn again. "Coming, mas'r. Everyone is coming."

Then, the gallop of additional horses was heard. Who approached? Little Mr. Dempster spurred his pony forward. And that figure in riding habit upon Madam Esmond's small gray mare—could it be Madam Esmond herself? No, she was too stout. By my life, it was Mrs. Mountain astride Madam's gray!

"Oh Lor! O Golly! Hoop! Here dey come! Hurray!" a chorus of negroes erupted.
"They're here!"

Dr. Dempster and Mrs. Mountain clattered into the yard, dismounted, pushed through the crowd, rushed inside, darted across the porch where British officers sat in bewildered stupor, sprinted down the stairs to the garden where George and Harry stood, their tall enemy looming opposite them. Before George Warrington could sternly inquire, "What do you do here, madam?", Mrs. Mountain threw her arms around his neck and sobbed, "Oh, George, my darling! It is a mistake! A terrible mistake, and entirely my fault!"

"What is a mistake?" George asked, majestically pulling away.
"What is it, Mounty?" cried Harry, trembling.
"That paper I retrieved from his portfolio, that scrap I picked up, children; wherein the Colonel declares his intention to marry a widow with two children. Who else could it be but you, children, and who else but your mother?"
"Well?"
"Well, it is—not your mother. It is that little widow Custis whom the Colonel intends to wed. He always sought a wealthy match; I knew he would. It is not Mrs. Rachel Warrington. He confided this to Madam today just before departing, stating the wedding would occur after the campaign. And—and your mother is furious, boys. When Sady fetched the pistols and announced to the whole house that you were dueling, I told him to fire them off; I galloped after him, and I have nearly shattered my poor old bones rushing to you."

"I have

Let us imagine, for a moment, that the separation between the brothers is complete. Let us picture George having taken his rightful place within Mr. Braddock's command, while Harry has returned to Castlewood to fulfill his duties. Yet, Harry's heart remains tethered to the army; the quiet pursuits available to him at home afford the boy no joy. He refuses to acknowledge the depth of his despair at being forced to remain beneath the homely, somber roof of Castlewood—a home rendered even more melancholy by George's absence.

Harry avoids passing through his brother's empty chambers, turning his face away; he claims George's seat at the head of the table, yet sighs heavily as he lifts his silver tankard. Madam Warrington raises the toast of "The King" with vigorous spirit every single day. On Sundays, when Harry recites the service and prays for travelers by land and sea, she adds a peculiar solemnity to her plea: *"We beseech Thee to hear us."* She insists on speaking constantly of George, always with a cheerful demeanor, as if his return is merely a matter of time. She strides confidently into his vacant room, her head held high, masking any outward sign of grief. She observes that his books, linens, and papers are arranged with meticulous care. She speaks of him with profound respect, appealing to the elderly servants during meals about tasks to be completed "when Mr. George returns."

Mrs. Mountain, conversely, is perpetually on the verge of weeping whenever George's name is uttered, causing Harry's face to wear an expression of ghastly alarm. In stark contrast, his mother maintains a grave and sedate composure. She makes far more errors at piquet and backgammon than one would expect from a lady of her station; the servants note that she rises before dawn, fully dressed and ready. She has petitioned Mr. Dempster to resume his residence at Castlewood. Though she is no longer severe or haughty—as she certainly once was—her speech with the household is quiet, her assertions gentle, and her replies mild. She talks endlessly of her father and his campaigns, noting how he emerged from them with only minor injuries, expressing hope that her eldest son will fare similarly.

George writes frequently to his brother, and now that the army is on the march, he compiles a rough journal, forwarding it as opportunity allows. This document is devoured with delight and eagerness by the recipient. More than once, it is read aloud during family councils on long summer evenings. Madam Esmond sits upright at her tea-table, refusing to lean on the back of her chair; little Fanny Mountain busies herself with sewing; Mr. Dempster and Mrs. Mountain play cards; and the silent, aged servants of the house move softly in the twilight, listening intently to the words of their young master.

Listen to Harry Warrington reading his brother's letter. As we gaze at the slender characters on the yellowed page, carefully preserved and set aside, we can almost conjure the image of the writer and the reader—and yet, alas! they seem as though they never existed. Are their portraits but faint echoes in frames of tarnished gold? Were they truly real, or mere phantasms? Did they live and die once? Did they love each other as true brothers and loyal gentlemen? Can we still hear their voices from the past?

Surely I know Harry's voice. There he sits in the warmth of a summer evening, reading his young brother's simple account:

*"It must be admitted that the provinces act scurvily toward His Majesty King George II., and his representative here burns with fury. Virginia is bad enough, and poor Maryland is scarcely better, but Pennsylvania is the worst of all. We implore them to send us troops from home to fight the French, promising to maintain them upon arrival. Yet, we not only fail to keep this promise, making scant provision for our defenders, but our populace insists on charging exorbitant prices for cattle and stores, actually cheating the soldiers who have come to fight their battles. No wonder the General swears, and the troops are sulky. The delays have been endless. Due to the failure of the various provinces to supply promised stores and means of locomotion, weeks and months have passed. During this time, undoubtedly, the French have strengthened their positions on our frontier and reclaimed the forts we had previously captured.*

*Though there will never be any love lost between me and Colonel Washington, I must admit that your favorite—I am not jealous, Hal—is a brave man and a capable officer. The family respects him deeply, and the General constantly seeks his opinion. Indeed, he is nearly the only man who has seen the Indians in their war paint, and I confess I believe he was right in firing upon Monsieur Jumonville last year.*

*There is to be no further suit regarding the quarrel at Benson's Tavern, nor the proposed duel between Colonel Washington and a certain young gentleman whose name I shall withhold. Captain Waring wished to pursue the matter upon entering camp, delivering a message from Captain Grace. Your friend, who is as bold as Hector, was inclined to accept the challenge, employing a brother aide-de-camp, Colonel Wingfield, on his side. However, when Wingfield learned the circumstances—that the dispute arose from Grace being drunk and fomented by Waring's intoxication, and that the two officers of the 44th chose to insult a militia officer—he swore that Colonel Washington would not meet the 44th men. He resolved to take the matter directly to His Excellency, who would convene a court-martial for the captains regarding brawling with the militia, drunkenness, and indecent behavior. Thus, the captains were forced to sheath their toasting irons and swallow their wrath. They were good-natured enough when sober, eating their humble pie with excellent appetite at a reconciliation dinner hosted by Colonel Washington, where he behaved as perfectly stupid and correct as Prince Prettyman needs to be. Hang him! He has no faults, and that is precisely why I dislike him. When he marries that widow—ah, me! what a dreary life she will lead!"*

"I wonder at the taste of some men, and the effrontery of some women," remarked Madam Esmond, setting down her teacup. "I wonder at any woman who, having been married once, forgets herself enough to marry again! Do you agree, Mountain?"

"Monstrous!" exclaimed Mountain, with a peculiar look.

Dempster kept his eyes steadily fixed on his glass of punch. Harry appeared as though he were choking with laughter or some other concealed emotion, but his mother interjected, "Go on, Harry! Continue with your brother's journal. He writes well; but, ah, will he ever be able to write like my papa?"

Harry resumed: *"We maintain the strictest order here in camp. The orders against drunkenness and misconduct among the men are severe. The roll of each company is called at morning, noon, and night, and a return of the absent and disorderly is submitted to the commanding officer of the regiment, who ensures proper punishment. The men are punished, and the drummers are constantly at work. Oh, Harry, it made one sick to witness the first blood drawn from a great, strong white back, and to hear the piteous yell of the poor fellow."*

"Oh, horrid!" cried Madam Esmond. "I think I would have murdered Ward if he had flogged me. Thank Heaven he escaped with only a crack of the ruler! The men, I say, are looked after carefully enough. I wish the officers were treated with similar care. The Indians have just broken up their camp and retired in a rage, because the young officers were forever drinking with the squaws—and—and—hum—ha."

Here, Mr. Harry paused, unwilling to proceed with the narrative in the presence of little Fanny, who sat primly in her chair beside her mother, working her sampler.

"Pass over that about the odious tipsy creatures," commanded Madam.

And Harry began, in a loud tone, a much more satisfactory account: *"Each regiment holds Divine Service at the head of its colors every Sunday. The General does everything in the power of mortal man to prevent plundering and encourages the surrounding populace to bring in provisions. He has declared that soldiers shall be shot if they dare to interrupt or molest market-goers. He has ordered the price of provisions to be raised by a penny a pound and has lent money from his own pocket to support the camp. Altogether, he is a strange compound, this General. He flogs his men without mercy, yet gives without stint. He swears terrible oaths in conversation and tells stories that would shock Mountain after dinner—but he never misses service. He adores his Great Duke, keeping his name constantly on his lips. Our two regiments both served in Scotland, where I dare say Mr. Dempster knows the color of their facings."*

"We saw the tails of their coats, as well as their facings," grumbled the little Jacobite tutor.

"Colonel Washington has had the fever severely and has hardly been well enough to keep pace with the march. Had he not better return home to be nursed by his widow? When either of us falls ill, we are almost as good friends as ever. But I feel somehow as if I cannot forgive him for having wronged me. Good Powers! How I have hated him for these past months! Oh, Harry! I was in a fury at the tavern the other day because Mountain arrived so soon and ended our disagreement. We ought to have burned a little gunpowder between us and cleared the air. But though

With every mile Harry Warrington traveled toward Pennsylvania, the tales of the British defeat swelled in magnitude and grim detail. Two legendary regiments, veterans of Scotland and the Continent, had reportedly fled from an enemy that seemed invisible. Their famed discipline and valor had crumbled before a mere band of "savages" and a handful of French infantry. Yet, amidst the chaos, the commander of the expedition had displayed supreme courage. His horse had been shot beneath him four times; twice he had been wounded, and finally, mortally injured, he had succumbed three days later.

Harry's informants painted a harrowing picture of the engagement: the crossing of the river, the long, weary advance through the wilderness, the futile frontal assault, and the desperate attempt to use artillery to clear a path. Then came the ambush—fire erupting from behind every bush and tree, followed by a murderous fusillade that claimed at least half of the expeditionary force. But Harry learned that not everyone perished. One of the General's aides-de-camp, a Virginian gentleman, lay suffering from fever and exhaustion at Dunbar's camp. Which one? Harry did not know, but he rushed to the camp immediately.

There, stretched within a tent, he found George Washington—not his missing brother. A sharp pang of disappointment pierced Harry, compounded by the feverish heat of the season. Washington offered no news of George. He dared not speak the full truth. For three days following the battle, his duty had bound him to the General's side. On the fatal ninth of July, he had witnessed George march forward with orders from the chief, never to return. After Braddock himself drew his last breath, the aide-de-camp had managed to retrace his steps to the battlefield. What he found was a scene of horror: the remaining corpses stripped and gruesomely mutilated. He had buried one body, convinced it was George Warrington's. His own illness, perhaps exacerbated by the agony of his search, deepened with every hour spent hunting for the lost youth.

"Ah, George!" Harry cried out, his voice breaking. "If you had truly loved him, you would have found him either dead or alive!"

Nothing satisfied the young man but to walk the ground himself. Armed with money, he hired a couple of guides. They forded the river at the exact point where the army had crossed, traversing the dreadful field from end to end. The Indian raiders were gone, but the air was thick with the carrion birds feasting on mangled, festering carcasses. Except for the serene face of his grandfather, Harry had never encountered Death so visibly. The grotesque spectacle of mutilation forced him to turn away in shuddering loathing. Could the empty woods or those rotting bodies under the trees reveal any sign of his brother?

Desperate, Harry considered approaching the French fort alone, bearing a white flag, knowing the enemy had retreated there after their victory. But his guides refused to accompany him. "Keep your hair for your lady mother, my young gentleman," one advised gently. "'Tis enough that she loses one son in this campaign."

When Harry returned to the English encampment at Dunbar's, it was his turn to fall victim to the fever. Delirium seized him; he lay for days in tents and beds, alternating between unconsciousness and waking confusion. Poor Dempster, who had nursed him through previous illnesses, feared the widow would lose both her children. Eventually, the fever broke. Harry rallied enough to mount his horse, escorted home by Mr. Washington and Dempster.

They approached the gates of Castlewood with heavy hearts. A servant had been sent ahead to announce their arrival. First came Mrs. Mountain and her little daughter, Fanny, who welcomed Harry with tearful embraces. Yet, she barely nodded to Mr. Washington. Little Fanny, however, stopped the young officer cold, her small hands clasped behind her back. "Why have you not brought George back too?" she asked.

Harry did not hear her. The sobs and caresses of his nurse kept him from listening to the child's piercing question. Dempster was greeted warmly by the ladies. "Whatever could be done, we know you would do, Mr. Dempster," Mrs. Mountain said, offering her hand. She instructed Fanny to curtsy to Dempster and express gratitude to their benefactors. Turning to the Colonel, she asked if he would accept refreshment before riding on.

Mr. Washington declined politely. "The time to feed my horse, and a glass of water for myself, and I will trouble Castlewood hospitality no further."

"Hush! Your mother won't see him, child," Mrs. Mountain whispered urgently to Fanny.

"Not see George? Why, he is like a son of the house!" Harry protested.

"She had best not see him," Mrs. Mountain replied firmly. "I don't meddle in family matters anymore. But when the Colonel's servant rode in saying you were coming, Madam Esmond left this room... she said she felt she could not see Mr. Washington."

Harry hesitated, then took his friend's arm. Excusing himself briefly to the Colonel, he left the parlor and ascended to the upper rooms. He moved quickly across the corridor, averting his gaze from the door of his brother's room—a door he dreaded facing. Just as he reached it, Madam Esmond emerged. Without a word, she threw her arms around him, pulling him into her embrace, and led him inside.

The room was frozen in time. A settee sat by the bed, and a book of Psalms lay open on the covers. Everything else remained exactly as George had left it.

"My poor child! How thin you have grown—how haggard!" she exclaimed, smoothing his hair. "Never mind. A mother's care will make you well again. It was nobly done to brave sickness and danger searching for your brother. Had others been as faithful, he might be here now. Never mind, my Harry; our hero will come back to us. I know he is not dead. One so good, so brave, so gentle, and so clever as he was, cannot be lost to us entirely."

(Perhaps Harry thought silently that his mother had not always spoken of her eldest son with such reverence.)

"Dry up thy tears, my dear! He will come back to us, I know he will." When Harry pressed her for the reason behind her certainty, she revealed that she had seen her father in dreams two nights running. In those visions, he had told her her boy was merely a prisoner among the Indians.

Madam Esmond's grief had not crushed her as Harry's had initially; instead, it had sharpened her. Her eyes burned with eagerness, her countenance twisted with anger and a desire for revenge. Harry marveled at her altered state. When he begged her to descend and offer a proper welcome to George Washington, who had accompanied him, the lady's agitation spiked dangerously.

"I should shudder at touching his hand," she declared. "He took my son from me! I cannot sleep under the same roof with him."

"He gave me his bed when I was ill, Mother," Harry argued desperately. "And if our George is alive, how can George Washington be responsible for his death? Ah! Please God, let it be only as you say."

"If your brother returns—and he will—it will not be through Mr. Washington's help," Madam Esmond insisted. "He neither defended George on the field nor brought him out of it."

"But he tended me most kindly during my fever," Harry interjected. "He was still recovering himself when he gave up his bed to me, thinking only of his friend when any other man would have thought only of himself."

"A friend! A pretty friend!" the lady sneered. "Of all His Excellency's aides-de-camp, my gentleman is the only one who returns unwounded. The brave and noble fall, but he remains untouched. I entrusted my boy to him—the pride of my life, whom he swore to defend with his own life—and he leaves my George in the forest, bringing me back himself! Oh, what a welcome I must give him!"

"No gentleman," Harry cried warmly, "was ever refused shelter under my grandfather's roof."

"Oh no—no gentleman!" the little widow retorted sharply. "Let us go down, if you like, son, and pay our respects to this one. Will you please give me your arm?"

Taking an arm that could hardly support her weight, she descended the broad stairs into the apartment where the Colonel sat. She performed a stiff, ceremonious curtsey and placed one of her small, trembling hands in his.

"I wish that our meeting had been happier, Colonel Washington," she said softly.

"You do not grieve more than I do that it is otherwise, madam," the Colonel replied calmly. "I might have wished the meeting spared, that I might not have kept you from friends you are naturally anxious to see—that my boy's indisposition had not detained you. Home, his good nurse Mountain, his mother, and our good Doctor Dempster will soon restore him. 'Twas scarce necessary, Colonel, that you, with so many military and domestic affairs on your hands, should turn doctor as well."

"Harry was ill and weak, and I thought it my duty to ride by him," the Colonel faltered.

"You yourself, sir, have endured the fatigues and dangers of the campaign in a most wonderful manner," the widow said, curtseying again, her dark eyes unreadable. "I wish to Heaven someone else had come back in my place!"

"Nay,

When the legendary wanderer from Troy recounted his trials and triumphs to Queen Dido, history records that the monarch listened with rapt attention, captivated by the eloquent storyteller who spun his perils with such vivid charm. A chronicle emerged, far more poignant than any prior event in the life of Pius Aeneas, leaving the unfortunate princess to regret the moment she yielded to the persuasive tongue of that dangerous orator.

Harry Warrington possessed none of Aeneas's rhetorical mastery, nor was his elderly aunt, we may assume, quite as susceptible to sentiment as the tragic Dido. Yet, the lad's account proved deeply moving, delivered with an artless sincerity and a warm, cordial voice. More than once during his narration, Madam Bernstein found herself swept into a tenderness she rarely permitted herself. In that barren expanse of her existence, there had been few oases—few moments of genuine refreshment. It had been a prolonged solitude until this friendly voice entered her ear, stirring her heart with unfamiliar pangs of affection and empathy. She adored this boy, intoxicated by this newfound sense of compassion and regard. Except for a fleeting, faint sensation in her earliest youth, she had known no tender emotion for any human soul.

Such a woman would naturally scrutinize her own feelings with keen vigilance, surely smiling inwardly as she observed the flush rising to her cheeks whenever the boy approached. Her eyes welcomed him eagerly, tracking his movements with fond delight. "Ah, if she could have borne a son like that, how she would have cherished him!" whispered her inner monologue. Then, the dark, scoffing spirit of Conscience intervened: "Wait, Beatrix Esmond. Wait. You know you will tire of this infatuation, just as you have of everything else. You know that when this passing fancy fades, the boy may perish without a single tear from you; or worse, you may grow bored with his stories. Your destiny is loneliness—absolute, enduring loneliness."

"Then what?" countered the practical side of her mind. "If life is a desert, there are still halting places, shadows, and springs of water. Let us enjoy them for today. We know we must march toward tomorrow and trudge on toward our fate regardless."

Smiling softly as she followed the lad's tale, she recognized familiar traits in his simple stories about his mother—a striking resemblance to herself. Madam Esmond was indeed jealous? Yes, Harry admitted. Did he love Colonel Washington? He liked him, but merely as a friend, declaring a hundred times that his mother held no deeper feeling for him. Though he felt shame owning that he himself had once been absurdly jealous of the Colonel, the truth remained.

"Well," remarked Madam Beatrix, "you will see that my half-sister will never forgive him. And do not be surprised, sir, that women take a fancy to men younger than themselves. Do I not dote upon you? And do not all the people of Castlewood fret with jealousy?"

Despite the intense envy harbored by the family regarding Madame de Bernstein's new favorite, the House of Castlewood displayed no outward ill will toward their young guest and kinsman. Within two days of arriving at the ancestral home, Mr. Harry Warrington had transformed into "Cousin Harry" in the eyes of both the young and the middle-aged. Particularly in the presence of the Baroness, the Countess of Castlewood treated her kinsman with exceptional grace. Seizing numerous private moments, she lavished praise upon the young Huron, marveling at the elegance of his manners and appearance, and wondering how a child raised in a distant province could possibly acquire such refinement.

These notes of admiration and inquiry were met by the Baroness with equal composure. Speaking parenthetically, the chronicler cannot resist offering a respectful observation on the peculiar conduct of ladies toward one another—their smiles and curtsies that serve as daggers, their compliments laced with hatred, their determined patience in avoiding offense, and their innocent dexterity in poisoning the communal cup of conversation while maintaining a serene smile. One imagines my Baroness and Countess doing exactly this a century ago, exchanging greetings of "My dear," "My dear creature," and "My dear sister"—even when they were most ready to draw swords.

"You wonder, my dear Maria, that the boy should be so polite?" cried Madame de Bernstein. "His mother was nurtured by two impeccable gentlemen. Colonel Esmond possessed a certain grave courtesy and a majestic bearing, qualities I fail to find among modern gentlemen."

"Eh, my dear, we all praise our own era!" retorted the Countess. "My grandmother used to insist there was nothing comparable to Whitehall under Charles II."

"My mother witnessed King James II's court briefly," the Baroness replied. "Though not court-trained herself, as her father was a country clergyman, she was exquisitely well-bred. Her second husband, the Colonel, was a man of vast travel, experience, and learning, frequenting the finest circles of Europe. They could not abandon their good manners upon retiring to their estate, and our boy inherited them naturally."

"Nay, excuse me, my dear, for doubting your perception of your mother," the Countess countered gently. "She could not possess the perfection your filial devotion imagines. She ceased loving her daughter—you have acknowledged that—and I cannot conceive of a complete woman with a cold heart. No, no, my dear sister-in-law! Manners are certainly essential, and for a daughter of a country parson, your mother was adequate—I have seen many of the clergy who are quite respectable. Mr. Sampson, our chaplain, is very well. Dr. Young is very well. Mr. Dodd is very well. But they lack the true *air*. As how should they? I protest, I beg pardon! I forgot your lord bishop, your ladyship's first choice. But as I stated, to be a complete woman, one requires what you possess, what I thank Heaven for possessing myself—a good heart. Without affection, the entire world is vanity, my love! I swear I live, exist, eat, drink, and rest solely for my sweet, sweet children—for my wilful Willy, for my self-willed Fanny, my dear naughty loves!"

As she spoke, she rapturously kissed bracelets on each arm containing miniatures of the two young girls. "Yes, Mimi! Yes, Fanchon! You know I adore you, you dear, dear little things! And if either of you were to die, your poor mistress would follow!"

Mimi and Fanchon, two trembling Italian greyhounds, leaped into their lady's arms to kiss her hands, though respectfully avoiding her rouge-covered cheeks.

"No, my dear! For nothing do I bless Heaven more (though it subjects me to excruciating torment frequently) than for endowing me with sensibility and a feeling heart!"

"You are full of feeling, dear Anna," said the Baroness. "You are renowned for your sensibility. You must share a little of it with our American nephew—or cousin—I hardly know the precise relation."

"Nay, I am here merely as a guest in Castlewood. The house belongs to my Lord Castlewood, not to me, nor to him unless he chooses to reclaim it. What can I do for the young Virginian that hasn't already been done? He is charming. Are we even jealous of him for being so, my dear? And though we see the fancy the Baroness de Bernstein has taken to him, do your ladyship's nephews and nieces—your actual blood relatives—complain? My poor children might feel mortified, for indeed, in mere hours, this charming young man has achieved more progress than my poor darlings have managed in their entire lives: but are they angry? Willy has taken him riding. This morning, was not Maria playing the harpsichord while my Fanny taught him the minuet? 'Twas a delightful trio, I assure you, bringing tears to my eyes to behold such youthful harmony. Poor lad! We are as fond of him as you are, dear Baroness!"

It was later revealed that Madame de Bernstein had inadvertently overheard the true nature of events unfolding during this seemingly harmless scene. Lady Castlewood had entered the room where the young people amused themselves, accompanied by her son, William, who arrived fresh from the kennels in his riding boots.

"Bravi, bravi! Oh, charming!" exclaimed the Countess, clapping her hands, nodding with one of her brightest smiles at Harry Warrington, and casting a sharp glance at his partner that Lady Fanny instantly comprehended. Perhaps Lady Maria at the harpsichord understood it too, for she played with renewed vigor, shaking her waving curls over the keys.

"Infernal young Choctaw! Is he teaching Fanny the war-dance? And is Fan planning to try her tricks upon him now?" demanded Mr. William, whose temper was notoriously volatile. That was precisely the message conveyed by his mother's look to Fanny.

"Are you going to try your tricks upon him now?"

Fanny executed a very low curtsey, Harry blushed crimson, and they both halted their dancing, visibly disconcerted. Lady Maria rose from the instrument and withdrew.

"Nay, go on dancing, young people! Do not let me spoil the sport. Let me play for you," suggested the Countess, sitting down to the keyboard.

"I—I don't know how to dance," Harry murmured, hanging his head, his blush surpassing the finest carmine the Countess could apply.

"And Fanny was teaching you? Go on

The second day following Harry's arrival at Castlewood fell upon a Sunday. The chapel attached to the castle served double duty as the parish church for the village. A private door connecting the main house opened directly into a grand state pew reserved for the family. Upon the appointed hour, they all filed in with proper decorum, while a respectable congregation from the village filled the pews beneath them. High above, a few ancient, dust-laden banners hung from the rafters; Harry indulged in a pleasant fantasy that these had once been carried by retainers of his family during the Commonwealth Wars, in which his ancestors had fought with such loyalty and distinction.

Within the altar rails stood the effigy of the Esmond who lived in the time of King James the First—the common progenitor of everyone currently seated in the family pew. Madame de Bernstein, acting in her capacity as the Bishop's widow, never missed mass. She conducted her devotions with a gravity nearly as impressive as that of the ancestor depicted nearby, a figure with a square beard and red gown, forever kneeling on his stone hassock before his marble desk and open book, sheltered beneath his emblazoned coat of arms.

The clergyman was a tall, ruddy-faced, handsome young man who read the service in a lively, agreeable tone, imparting a dramatic flair to the scriptural passages. The music was excellent, performed by one of the young ladies of the family at the organ, though it might have been superior had it not been interrupted by a sudden burst of laughter emanating from the servants' pew. The cause was Mr. Warrington's lacquy, Gumbo. Knowing the tune assigned for the psalm, Gumbo launched into it with a voice so excessively loud and sweet that the entire congregation turned toward the "African warbler." Even the parson covered his mouth with his handkerchief, and the liveried gentlemen from London were thrown into a state of complete impropriety. Delighted by the attention he commanded, Mr. Gumbo continued his performance until it resembled a solo concert, silencing the clerk's voice entirely. The truth was, despite holding onto the hymnal alongside Pretty Molly, the porter's daughter—who had been the first to welcome the strangers to Castlewood—Gumbo sang and recited entirely by ear. He could not read a single syllable of the verses printed before him.

Once this impromptu chorus concluded, a brief sermon duly followed, though Harry found it far too short. In a discursive, familiar, and striking address, the clergyman recounted a scene witnessed the previous week: the execution of a horse-thief following the Assizes. He painted a portrait of the man, detailing his formerly good character, his loving family, and his agonizing separation from them. He depicted the execution itself with startling, terrible, and picturesque vividness. Unlike the stern, Calvinistic preachers Harry's mother preferred, this divine spoke not in archaic scripture phrases, but as one man of the world addressing other sinful souls who might profit from sound advice.

The unfortunate man, once a farmer with promising prospects, had succumbed to the vices of the age: drinking, card-playing, horse-racing, and cock-fighting. Against these sins, the young clergyman expressed generous indignation. The man had progressed to poaching and horse-stealing, crimes for which he now suffered. The minister rapidly sketched terrifying images of these rural offenses. He startled his listeners by describing how the "Eye of the Law" watched the poacher at midnight and set traps to ensnare criminals. He galloped the stolen horse over highways and commons, from county to county, yet showed Retribution always riding hard behind, seizing the malefactor at a country fair, dragging him before the justice, and refusing to unlock his manacles until they were dropped at the foot of the gallows. *Heaven be pitiful to the sinner!*

The clergyman acted out the scene. He whispered in the criminal's ear as he sat in the cart. He dramatically dropped his handkerchief onto the clerk's head. Harry recoiled as the cloth fell. The clergyman had been speaking for over twenty minutes; Harry felt he could have listened for an hour more and still believed he had been in the pulpit less than five minutes. The gentlemen in the great pew were thoroughly enlivened by the discourse. Occasionally, Harry noticed the servants' pew, observing their intense attention—especially Gumbo, his own man, sitting in an attitude of profound consternation. Yet, the smock-frocks seemed utterly unconcerned, striding out of the church as if nothing had occurred. Gaffer Brown and Gammer Jones accepted matters exactly as they came, while the rosy-cheeked, red-cloaked village lassos sat beneath their broad hats, entirely unmoved.

"My lord," the gentleman in the pew observed, nodding slightly as the divine's head and wig rose from the cushion, "Sampson has been strong today. He has assaulted the Philistines in great force."

"Beautiful, beautiful!" exclaimed Harry.

"Bet five to four it was his Assize sermon. He has been over to Winton to preach and to see those dogs," cried William.

The organist led the small congregation out into the sunshine. Only Sir Francis Esmond, dating from the time of James I, remained kneeling on his marble hassock before his stone prayer-book. Mr. Sampson emerged from his vestment in his cassock and nodded to the gentlemen still lingering in the great pew.

"Come up, and tell us about those dogs," said Mr. William, and the divine nodded with a laughing assent.

The gentlemen moved from the church into the gallery of their house, which connected them to the sacred building. Mr. Sampson made his way through the courtyard and soon joined them. Introduced by My Lord to the Virginian cousin of the family, Mr. Warrington, the chaplain bowed deeply and hoped Mr. Warrington would benefit from the virtuous example of his European kinsmen. Was he related to Sir Miles Warrington of Norfolk? Sir Miles was Mr. Warrington's father's elder brother. "What a pity he had a son!" the chaplain lamented. "'Twas a pretty estate, and Mr. Warrington looks as if he would become a baronetcy, and possess a fine estate in Norfolk."

"Tell me about my uncle," cried Virginian Harry eagerly.

"Tell us about those dogs!" added English Will, breathless.

"Two more jolly dogs, two more drunken dogs, saving your presence, Mr. Warrington, than Sir Miles and his son, I never saw. Sir Miles was a staunch friend and neighbor of Sir Robert's. He can drink down any man in the county, except his son and a few others. As for the other dogs Mr. William is anxious about—for Heaven hath made him a prey to dogs and all kinds of birds, like the Greeks in the *Iliad*—"

"I know that line in the *Iliad*," said Harry, blushing. "I only know five more, but I know that one." His head fell. He was thinking, *Ah, my dear brother George knew all the *Iliad* and all the *Odyssey*, and almost every book that was ever written besides!*

"What on earth" (mentioning only a place underground) "are you talking about now?" asked Will of his reverend companion.

The chaplain returned to the topic of the dogs and their exploits. He opined that Mr. William's dogs were more than a match for theirs. From dogs, the conversation drifted to horses. Mr. William was particularly eager regarding the Six Year Old Plate at Huntingdon.

"Have you brought any news of it, Parson?"

"The odds are five to four on Brilliant against the field," said the parson gravely, "but mind you, Jason is a good horse."

"Whose horse?" asked My Lord.

"Duke of Ancaster's. By Cartouche out of Miss Langley," replied the divine.

"Do you have horse races in Virginia, Mr. Warrington?"

"Haven't we!" cried Harry. "But oh! I long to see a good English race!"

"Do you... do you... bet a little?" continued his reverence cautiously.

"I have done such a thing," replied Harry with a smile.

"I'll take Brilliant even against the field, for ponies with you, cousin!" shouted Mr. William.

"I'll give or take three to one against Jason!" declared the clergyman.

"I don't bet on horses I don't know," said Harry, surprised to hear the chaplain speak thus, recalling his sermon from half an hour prior.

"Hadn't you better write home and ask your mother?" suggested Mr. William with a sneer.

"Will, Will!" called out My Lord. "Our cousin Warrington is free to bet, or not, as he pleases. Take care how you venture on either of them, Harry Warrington. Will is an old file, in spite of his smooth face. And as for Parson Sampson, I defy our ghostly enemy to get the better of him."

"Him and all his works, my lord!" said Mr. Sampson, bowing.

Harry was highly indignant at this allusion to his mother. "I'll tell you what, cousin Will," he said, "I am in the habit of managing my own affairs in my own way, without asking any lady to arrange them for me. I am used to making my own bets upon my own judgment and do not need relations to select them

Having won substantial sums from his cousin and the chaplain, our young Virginian felt compelled to allow them a chance to recover their losses—a prospect that surely would not meet with approval from his mother or other upright moralists. Indeed, his penchant for card-playing was excessive. Beyond the daily rounds of whist or quadrille with the ladies, which commenced shortly after dinner at three o'clock and concluded only at supper, there existed high-stakes games involving fortunes, in which every gentleman, including my lord, participated. Following their Sunday conversations, his lordship had become notably more open and confiding toward his kinsman, betting with him affably and engaging him in backgammon and piquet. Both Mr. William and the pious chaplain relished a bit of hazard; however, this diversion was pursued secretly, unbeknownst to the ladies of the house. Having extracted repeated vows from Cousin Will that he would neither lead the Virginian into mischief nor participate himself, the ladies remained unaware. Thus, Will pledged as much as his aunt or mother demanded—giving his solemn word that he would never play—and whenever the family retired, Mr. William would stroll over with a dice-box and a bottle of rum to Cousin Harry's quarters. There, alongside Hal and the reverend gentleman, they would gamble until dawn.

When Harry offered Lord Castlewood those glowing descriptions of his maternal estate in America, he harbored no intent to deceive, boast, or lie; the lad possessed a fundamentally honest nature. Yet, in his home life, it must be admitted, the young fellow had acquainted himself with all manner of peculiar company—horse-jockeys, tavern loafers, gamblers, and sportsmen, of whom the colonies teemed. A landed aristocracy, supported by a population of enslaved Africans to till their fields and cultivate tobacco and corn, found little amusement other than the hunt or the card table and punch bowl. Hospitality in the province was boundless: every man's house was effectively his neighbor's. Idle gentlemen rode from mansion to mansion, finding in each similar sport, welcome, and rough plenty. The Virginian squire might occasionally possess a barefooted valet and a patched saddle, but within the crumbling fences and behind the broken windows of the hall, there was abundant corn for the horses, plentiful drink and venison for the master. Harry had slept on countless straw mattresses and engaged in endless jovial nocturnal bouts over claret and punch in cracked bowls until morning broke, signaling the hour to follow the hounds. His poor brother, conversely, was of a far more sober disposition, as the lad confessed with contrition. Thus does Nature vary its creations; some cherish books and tea, while others prefer Burgundy and a gallop across the countryside.

Harry's tastes quickly became evident to his friends in England. None favored the rigid Puritan discipline, nor did they dislike Harry for lacking the demeanor of a mere "milksop." Manners, one observes, were considerably looser a century prior; tongues were far freer, and names were spoken and deeds performed that would now scandalize us into silence. Yes, madam, we are not as our ancestors were. Should we not thank the Fates for improving our morals so prodigiously, rendering us so eminently virtuous?

Keeping a shrewd eye on those around him, and correctly surmising that his cousins sought to probe his affairs, Harry Warrington wisely kept his own counsel. In games of chance or sport, he proved quite a match for the three gentlemen into whose company he had fallen. Even in the noble game of billiards, he held his own after a few days of play with his cousins and their revered pastor. His grandfather, fond of the game, had imported from Europe one of the very few tables existing in His Majesty's province of Virginia. Though Mr. William initially bested him, he could not secure undue odds against the young gambler. After their first wager, Harry guarded himself against Mr. William, and Cousin William acknowledged, with genuine respect, that the American was his equal in most things and superior in many. Yet, despite Harry's prowess—beating the parson and soon matching Will, who could easily defeat the girls—it remained a mystery why, in contests with these, particularly one of them, Mr. Warrington frequently emerged second. He was profoundly courteous to every soul wearing a petticoat; nor has that traditional politeness vanished from his country. Every woman in the Castlewood establishment adored the young gentleman. The stern housekeeper was mollified by him; the portly cook greeted him with flushed smiles; the ladies' maids, whether French or English, smirked and giggled in his favor; and the pretty porter's daughter at the lodge always replied kindly to his advances. Madame de Bernstein noted all these things, saying nothing aloud but watching the boy's disposition and behavior with care.

Who can determine the precise age of Lady Maria Esmond? Books of the Peerage were scarce in those days compared to our blessed modern era, and I cannot pinpoint her age to within a few years, or even a decade or two. When Will claimed she was thirty-five, he was merely being abusive and prone to exaggeration. Maria was Will's half-sister, daughter of the late Lord Castlewood's first wife, a German lady whom my lord married during the reign of Queen Anne. Baron Bernstein, who wed Maria's Aunt Beatrix (widow of Bishop Tusher), was also a German, a Hanoverian nobleman and relative of the first Lady Castlewood. If Lady Maria was born under George I., and if His Majesty George II. had already reigned for thirty years, how could she be twenty-seven, as she told Harry Warrington? "I am old, child," she would say. She addressed Harry as "child" when they were alone. "I am a hundred years old. I am twenty-seven. I might almost be your mother." To which Harry would retort, "Your ladyship might be the mother of all the Cupids, I am sure. You don't look twenty, on my word you do not!"

Lady Maria appeared any age one desired. She was a fair beauty with a dazzling white and red complexion, an abundance of golden hair flowing over her shoulders, and beautiful round arms that displayed to uncommon advantage when playing billiards with Cousin Harry. When she stretched across the table to strike, the youth caught glimpses of a delicate ankle, a finely clocked stocking, and a tiny black satin slipper with a small red heel, filling him with unutterable rapture and causing him to swear that no such foot, ankle, stocking, or slipper existed in the world. And yet, oh, foolish Harry! Your mother's foot was far more slender and half an inch shorter than Lady Maria's. But somehow, boys do not gaze upon their mothers' slippers and ankles with such ecstasy.

Undoubtedly, Lady Maria was very kind to Harry when they were alone. Before her sister, aunt, stepmother, she treated him lightly, calling him a simpleton, a chit, and various trivial names. Behind his back, and even in his presence, she mimicked his accent, which retained a distinct provincial flavor. Harry blushed and corrected the faulty intonation under the guidance of his English tutors. His aunt declared they would soon make him a fine gentleman.

As stated earlier, Lord Castlewood grew daily more familiar and friendly with his guest and relative. Until the harvest was gathered, there was little sport save for occasional cock-fighting at Winchester and bull-baiting at Hexton Fair. Harry and Will rode to many jolly fairs and races throughout the region. The young Virginian was introduced to several county families—the Henleys of the Grange, the Crawleys of Queen's Crawley, the Redmaynes of Lionsden, and so on. Neighbors arrived in their heavy coaches, spending two or three days in country fashion. Fewer would have visited had not the entire Castlewood family feared offending Madame de Bernstein. She disliked country company; the rustic society and conversation annoyed her. "We shall be merrier when my aunt leaves us," the young folks admitted. "We have cause, as you may imagine, to be very civil to her. You know what a favorite she was with our father? With reason. She secured his earldom, being exceptionally well-connected at Court with the King and Queen then. She commands here naturally, perhaps a trifle too much. We are all afraid of her: even my elder brother stands in awe of her, and my stepmother is far more obedient to her than she ever was to my father, whom she ruled with a rod of iron. But Castlewood is merrier when our aunt is absent. At least, we have much more company. You will come to us in our gay days, Harry, won't you? Of course you will: this is your home, sir. I was so pleased—oh, so pleased—when my brother said he considered it your home!"

A soft hand was extended following this charming speech, accompanied by a pair of remarkably well-preserved blue eyes radiating friendship. Harry grasped his cousin's hand with ardor. I know not what privileges of kinship he would not wish to claim, only that he was so timid. They call the English selfish and cold. He initially thought his relatives were thus; how mistaken he was! How kind and affectionate they truly were, especially the Earl—and dear, dear Maria! How he wished he could recall the letter he had sent to Mrs. Mountain and his mother, hinting that his welcome had been chilly! The Earl, his cousin, embodied kindness, promising to introduce him to London society, present him at Court, and at White's

Young Harry Warrington's act of rebellion struck Madame de Bernstein with such sudden force that her only recourse was the immediate eruption of anger with which we left her in the previous chapter. As she stormed from the room, she cast two piercing glares at Lady Fanny and her mother. Meanwhile, Lady Maria, hidden behind her tambour frame, managed to escape detection entirely. She barely lifted her head from her embroidery to watch her aunt retreat or to observe the exchange of looks between her "mamma-in-law" and her sister.

"So, despite all our efforts, you have won?" the maternal gaze seemed to inquire.

"Win what?" Lady Fanny's eyes asked in return. Yet, innocence offered no defense here. She appeared merely puzzled, lacking the calculated composure of a guilty party. Had she truly been culpable, she would have mastered the art of feigning ignorance with greater dexterity, composing a face ready to meet any accusation. Whatever expression graced Fanny's features, her mother stared at her with such intensity that it felt as though she might tear the eyes from her sockets. However, Lady Castlewood lacked the theatrical cruelty of the monsters described in *King Lear*. When her ladyship intended to harm her daughter, she would publicly embrace her with a smile, only to administer the punishment privately.

"So, you do not wish to accompany the old lady to Tunbridge Wells?" was all she finally said to Cousin Warrington, masking her displeasure with a perfectly bred, albeit thin-lipped, smile.

"And small blame to our cousin!" interjected my lord. (The figure behind the tambour frame glanced up for a fleeting second.) "A young man ought not to spend his entire existence idling and taking holidays. He should blend utility with pleasure; perhaps visit the fountains and pump-rooms at Tunbridge or Bath later. Mr. Warrington is destined to manage a vast estate in America; let him learn how estates are conducted here in England. Will has already shown him the kennels and stables, and the fashionable games, which I believe, cousin, you play as well as your tutors. After the harvest, we shall introduce him to English fowling and shooting; in winter, we will take him hunting. Although there has been a cooling of relations between us and our kinswoman in Virginia, we share the same blood. Before sending our cousin back to his mother, let us demonstrate to him what the life of an English gentleman entails at home. I should delight in reading with him as well as sporting alongside him, which is precisely why I have pressed him recently to remain and keep me company."

My lord spoke with such absolute candor that his mother-in-law, half-brother, and sister could not help but wonder at his intent. These three frequently engaged in quiet conspiracies, plotting or grumbling against the head of the household. When he adopted this open tone, his meaning remained unfathomable, often remaining so for months. He did not state facts; rather, he dictated the narrative his family was expected to accept. It was a known convention that Lord Castlewood possessed a noble desire to cultivate domestic affection and to educate, amuse, and improve his young relative, having developed a genuine fondness for the lad and wishing for Harry to stay near him for some time.

"What is Castlewood playing at now?" William whispered to his mother and sister as they slipped away down the corridor.

"Wait! By George, I have it!"

"What, William?"

"He intends to get him involved in gambling, hoping to win the Virginia estate back from him. That is his game!"

"But the lad holds no claim to the Virginia estate if he loses," remarked his mother.

"If my brother has no scheme in mind, may I be..."

"Hush! Of course he has a scheme. But what is it?"

"Could he mean Maria? Maria is as old as Harry's mother," Mr. William mused.

"Pooh! With her aged face, sandy hair, and freckled complexion! Impossible!" cried Lady Fanny, letting out a slight sigh.

"Of course, your ladyship fancied the Iroquois, too!" exclaimed his mother.

"I trust I know my station and duty better, madam! Even if I had liked him, that is no reason to marry him. Your ladyship has taught me that much."

"My Lady Fanny!"

"I am certain you married our father without loving him. You have told me so a thousand times!"

"And if you did not love our father before the wedding, you certainly did not fall in love with him afterward," interrupted Mr. William with a laugh. "Fan and I remember how our honored parents used to quarrel. Did we not, Fan? And our brother Esmond kept the peace."

"Do not recall those dreadful, low scenes, William!" cried his mother. "When your father drank too much, he became a madman; his behavior should serve as a warning to you, sir, who are fond of the same detestable habit."

"I am sure, madam, you were not much happier marrying the man you did not love, and your ladyship's title has brought very little fortune with it," whined Lady Fanny.

"What is the use of a coronet with the jointure of a tradesman's wife? How many of them are richer than we are? Recently, a grocer's widow from London Bridge moved into our square in Kensington. Her daughters possess three gowns where I have one, and although they are attended by only a man and two maids, I know they eat and drink a thousand times better than we do, subsisting on scraps of cold meat while our great, flaunting, impudent, lazy lackeys loiter!"

"He! He! Glad I dine at the palace and not at home!" said Mr. Will. (Through his aunt's influence with Count Puffendorff, Groom of the Royal Powder-Closet, Mr. Will held one of the minor court positions of Deputy Powder.)
