"Why should I not be happy without any title save my own?" continued Lady Frances. "Many people are. I dare say they are even happy in America."

"Yes—provided they have a mother-in-law who is a perfect Turk and Tartar, as I hear, with Indian war-whoops echoing around them, and a constant danger of losing your scalp or being eaten by a wild beast every time you attend church."

"I would not go to church," said Lady Fanny.

"You would go with anyone who asked you, Fan!" roared Mr. Will. "And so would old Maria, and so would any woman, that is the fact." He laughed at his own wit.

"Pray, good folks, what is all this merriment about?" Madame Bernstein asked, peeking in from the tapestried door leading to the gallery where the conversation was held.

Will explained that his mother and sister had been fighting—a non-event for Madame Bernstein, who knew them well—because Fanny wished to marry their cousin, the wild Indian, and my lady Countess forbade it. Fanny immediately protested this fabrication. Since the day her mother ordered her not to speak to the young gentleman, she had scarcely exchanged two words with him. She knew her place. She did not wish to be scalped by wild Indians or devoured by bears.

Madame de Bernstein looked perplexed. "If he is not staying for you, for whom is he staying?" she asked. "In the households to which he has been sent, you have ensured he sees no woman who is not either a fright or confined to the nursery; and I believe the boy is too proud to fall in love with a dairymaid, Will."

"Hmph! That is a matter of taste, ma'am," said Mr. William, shrugging. "It is Mr. William Esmond's taste, as you say; but not that of the boy yonder. The Esmonds raised by his grandfather would not go courting in the kitchen."

"Well, ma'am, every man to his taste, I say again. A fellow might fare worse than my brother's servants' hall, and besides Fan, there is only the maids or old Maria to choose from."

"Maria! Impossible!" Yet, as she spoke, a sudden thought crossed Madame Bernstein's mind: this elderly Calypso might have captivated her young Telemachus. She recalled numerous instances in her own experience of young men infatuated by older women. She remembered Harry Warrington's recent frequent absences, which she had attributed to his love for field sports. She also noted how often, when Harry was away, Maria Esmond was likewise absent. Walks in cool avenues, whispers in garden temples, behind clipped hedges, casual hand-squeezes in twilight corridors, and sweet glances exchanged on the stairs—all these possibilities, suggesting a lively fancy and intimate worldly knowledge, occurred to Madame de Bernstein just as she dismissed them as impossible.

"Impossible, ma'am! I don't know," Will continued. "My mother warned Fan off him."

"Oh, your mother did warn Fanny off?"

"Certainly, my dear Baroness!"

"Didn't she? Didn't she pinch Fanny's arm black and blue? Didn't they fight about it?"

"Nonsense, William! For shame, William!" cried both implicated ladies in unison.

"And now, since we have heard how rich he is, perhaps it is sour grapes, that is all. And now, since he is barred from the young bird, perhaps he is hunting the old one, that's all. Why impossible? Do you know old Lady Suffolk, ma'am?"

"William, how

As Lady Castlewood, her son, and her daughter filed out through one door of the salon, having concluded their sitting, my Lord Castlewood exited by another. This departure allowed the demure eyes of the sisters to rise from the tambour-frame, where they had been diligently stitching innocent violets and jonquils. Their gazes lifted toward Harry Warrington, who stood beneath a grand ancestral portrait near the great fireplace. He appeared somewhat disheveled, having gathered a formidable heap of blushing flowers—those blooms that rarely appear after the gentler seasons—and using them to decorate his honest countenance, his cheeks, his forehead, and even his youthful ears.

"Why did you refuse to accompany our aunt, cousin?" asked the lady holding the tambour-frame.

"Because your ladyship commanded me to stay," replied the lad.

"I bid you stay! La, child! What one says in jest, you take as gospel! Are all you Virginian gentlemen so excessively obedient that you mistake every idle remark of a lady for a direct order? Virginia must be a delightful land for our sex if this is true!"

"You said—" Harry stammered, his voice trembling with sudden emotion, "—when we walked along the terrace two nights ago—Oh, heaven!"

"Oh, that sweet night, cousin!" cried the Tambour-frame lady.

"When you gave me this rose from your own neck," roared out Harry, suddenly producing a crumpled, decaying vegetable from his waistcoat pocket—"which I shall never part with! By heavens, not while this heart beats!"

"You said, 'Harry, if your aunt asks you to leave, you will go, and if you go, you will forget me.' Didn't you say that?"

"All men forget!" sighed the Virginian.

"In this cold, selfish country, perhaps," Harry continued, still elevated in spirit, "cousins may forget, but not in ours. I would sooner lose an arm than disobey the old lady. It went straight to my heart to deny her, and she has been so kind to me, and who introduced me to—Oh, heaven!" (Here, he kicked an intervening spaniel, which fled yelping from before the fire, followed immediately by a rapid advance toward the tambour-frame.)

"Look here, cousin! If you were to bid me leap from that window, I would do it; or commit murder, I would do it."

"La! But you need not squeeze my hand so tightly, you silly child!" remarked Maria.

"I can't help it—we are southerners. Where my heart is, I cannot help speaking my mind, cousin—and you know exactly where that heart lies! Since that evening—that—Oh, heaven! I tell you I have scarcely slept since. I feel compelled to do something—to distinguish myself—to become someone great. I wish there were giants, Maria, as I've read in books, that I might fight them. I wish you were in distress so I could help you somehow. I wish you needed my blood so I could spend every drop for you. And when you told me not to go with Madame Bernstein..."

"I tell thee, child, never."

"I thought you told me. You said you knew I preferred my aunt to my cousin. Then I said what I say now: 'Incomparable Maria! I prefer thee to all the women in the world and all the angels in Paradise—I would go anywhere, even to dungeons, if you ordered me!' And do you think I wouldn't stay anywhere, simply because you desire my presence?" he added after a brief pause.

"Men always talk in that manner—that is, that is what I have heard," corrected the spinster, adjusting her tone; "for what should a country-bred woman know about you creatures? When you are near us, they say you are all rapture and flame and promise and I-know-not-what; but when you depart, you forget us entirely."

"But I think I shall never wish to leave as long as I live," groaned the young man.

"I have grown weary of many things; not books, for I never cared much for study, but games and sports which I enjoyed as a boy. Before I saw you, I most desired to be a soldier; I tore my hair with rage when my poor dear brother went away instead of me on that expedition in which we lost him. But now, I care for only one thing in the entire world, and you know what that is."

"You silly child! Don't you know I am almost old enough to be..."

"I know—I know! But what is that to me? Haven't your brothers—or well, never mind who—told me stories against you? Didn't they show me the Family Bible, listing all your names and birthdates?"

"The cowards! Who did that?" cried Lady Maria.

"Dear Harry, tell me who did it? Was it my mother-in-law, the grasping, odious, abandoned, brazen harpy? Do you know everything about her? How she married my father while he was drunk—the horrid hussey!—and..."

"It certainly wasn't Lady Castlewood," interjected the bewildered Harry.

"Then it was my aunt," continued the infuriated lady. "A pretty moralist, indeed! A bishop's widow, forsooth, and I should like to know whose widow before and after. Why, Harry, she intrigues with the Pretender, and with the Court of Hanover, and I dare say she would with the Court of Rome and the Sultan of Turkey if she possessed the means. Do you know who her second husband was? A creature who..."

"But our aunt never spoke a word against you," broke in Harry, increasingly amazed by the nymph's vehemence.

She checked her anger. Reading a certain alarm in the inquisitive countenance opposite hers, she realized she was losing control.

"Well, well! I am a fool," she said softly. "I want you to think well of me, Harry!"

A hand reached out, was seized, and undoubtedly kissed by the rapturous youth.

"Angel!" he cried, gazing into her face with eager, honest eyes.

Two fish pools irradiated by a pair of stars would not glow with greater warmth than those elderly orbs into which Harry poured his gaze. Yet, he plunged into their blue depths and fancied he saw heaven in their calm brightness.

So that silly dog (of whom Aesop or the Spelling-book used to tell us in our youth) beheld a beef-bone in the pond, snapped at it, and lost the very bone he was carrying. Oh, absurd cur! He saw the beef-bone in his own mouth reflected in the treacherous pool, which dimpled, I dare say, with countless smiles, coolly sucked up the meat, and returned to its usual placidity. Ah! What a heap of wreckage lies beneath some of those quiet surfaces! What treasures we have dropped into them! What chased golden dishes, what precious jewels of love, what bone after bone, and sweetest heart's flesh! Do not some very faithful and unlucky dogs jump in bodily, getting swallowed up head and tail entirely? When some women come to be dragged, it is a marvel what will be found in the depths of them. *Cavete, canes!* Have a care how you lap that water. What do they want with us, the mischievous siren sluts? A green-eyed Naiad never rests until she has inveigled a fellow underwater; she sings after him, she dances after him; she winds around him, glittering tortuously; she warbles and whispers dainty secrets to his cheek, she kisses his feet, she leers at him from out of her rushes: all her beds sigh out, "Come, sweet youth! Hither, hither, rosy Hylas!" Pop goes Hylas. (Is the fable renewed forever and ever?) Does his captivator derive any pleasure? Does she take any account of him? No more than a fisherman landing at Brighton does of one out of a hundred thousand herrings....

The last time. Ulysses rowed past the Sirens' bank; he and his men did not care though a whole shoal of them were singing and combing their longest locks. Young Telemachus was for jumping overboard: but the tough old crew held the silly, bawling lad. They were deaf, and could not hear his bawling nor the sea-nymphs' singing. They were dim of sight, and did not see how lovely the witches were. The stale, old, leering witches! Away with ye! I dare say you have painted your cheeks by this time; your wretched old songs are as out of fashion as Mozart, and it is all false hair you are combing! In the last sentence, you see *Lector Benevolus* and *Scriptor Doctissimus* figure as tough old Ulysses and his tough old Boatswain, who do not care a quid of tobacco for any Siren at Sirens' Point; but Harry Warrington is green Telemachus, who, be sure, was very unlike the soft youth in the good Bishop of Cambray's twaddling story. He does not see that the siren paints the lashes from under which she ogles him; she puts by into a box when she has finished the ringlets into which she would inveigle him; and if she eats him, as she proposes to do, she will crunch his bones with a new set of grinders just from the dentist's, warranted for mastication. The song is not stale

When the family gathered for the next meal, there was not a trace of displeasure upon Madame de Bernstein's countenance. Her behavior toward everyone, including Harry, was perfectly kind and cordial. She praised the cook, declared the fricassee excellent, and insisted that no eels in the world could compare to those swimming in the Castlewood moats. She refused to admit the wine was corked or entertain the idea of opening a fresh bottle for a "useless old woman" like herself. When the new vintage was served, she raised a toast to Madam Esmond Warrington of Virginia and expressed hope that Harry had secured his mother's permission to bring an English bride back with him.

Harry did not recall his grandmother; he focused instead on Madame de Bernstein's dear mother. The Baroness delighted the company with numerous anecdotes of her mother—her beauty, her goodness, and her profound happiness with her second husband, despite the fact that the wife was significantly older than Colonel Esmond. "To see them together is simply delightful," she remarked, noting that their attachment was celebrated throughout the countryside. She concluded that talking of disparities in marriage was futile after such an example. While My Lady Castlewood and her two children remained silent, allowing the Baroness to prattle on, Harry was completely enraptured, while Maria appeared surprisingly moved.

Lord Castlewood was puzzled by this sudden transformation in his aunt's demeanor, wondering what scheme had caused such prodigious amiability. Yet, he maintained a mask of perfect satisfaction, showing no sign of solicitude or doubt. The Baroness's good humor seemed to infect the entire household; not a single guest escaped a gracious word from her.

In response to a compliment directed at Mr. Will, the artless youth expressing surprise at his aunt's change of heart, she spoke frankly. "Complimentary, my dear! Of course I am. I wish to make amends for being exceedingly rude to everyone this morning. When I was a child, living here with my parents, I adopted exactly the same behavior. If I had been naughty in the morning, I would try to coax my parents at night. I remember doing this right here, at this very table—oh, hundreds of years ago!—coaxing my father, my mother, and your grandfather, Harry Warrington. There were eels for supper, just as we have tonight, and it was that specific dish of collared eels that brought the memory rushing back. I was just as wayward at seven years old as I am today at seventy. So, I confess my sins and ask forgiveness, like a good girl."

"I absolve your ladyship!" cried the chaplain, who was present.

"But your reverence does not know how cross and ill-tempered I truly was," she continued. "I scolded my sister, Castlewood; I scolded her children; I boxed Harry Warrington's ears—all because he refused to accompany me to Tunbridge Wells."

"But I will go, madam! I will ride with you with all the pleasure in life," promised Mr. Warrington.

"You see, Mr. Chaplain, what good, dutiful children they all are. It was I alone who was cross and peevish. Oh, it was cruel of me to treat them so! Maria, I ask your pardon, my dear."

"Sure, madam, you have done me no wrong," Maria replied humbly.

"Indeed I have, a very great wrong, child!" the Baroness exclaimed. "Because I was weary of myself, I told you that your company would be wearisome to me. You offered to come with me to Tunbridge, and I rudely refused you."

"Nay, ma'am, if you were sick and my presence annoyed you..."

"But it will not annoy me! You were most kind to say you would come. I do, of all things, beg, pray, entreat, and implore—and command—that you will come."

My lord filled his glass and sipped it, looking utterly unconscious of the drama unfolding. This, then, was the meaning of the earlier comedy.

"Anything which can give my aunt pleasure, I am sure, will delight me," Maria said, striving to look as happy as possible.

"You must come and stay with me, my dear, and I promise to be good and good-humoured. My dear lord, you will spare your sister to me?"

"Lady Maria Esmond is quite of age to judge for herself regarding such matters," his lordship replied with a bow. "If any of us can be of use to you, madam, you surely ought to command us."

Which sentence, when interpreted correctly, undoubtedly meant: *Plague take the old woman! She is taking Maria away to separate her from this young Virginian.*

"Oh, Tunbridge will be delightful!" sighed Lady Maria.

"Mr. Sampson will go and see Goody Jones for you," my lord continued.

Harry drew pictures with his finger on the tablecloth. What delights had he not already speculated upon? What walks, rides, interminable conversations, delicious shrubberies, and sweet, secluded summer houses had he not imagined? What poring over music books, moonlit nights, and billing and cooing! Yes, the day was coming. Everyone else was departing—My Lady Castlewood to her friends, Madame Bernstein to the waters—and he was to be left alone with his divine charmer. Alone with her and unutterable rapture! The thought of the pleasure was maddening. These people were leaving, leaving him to enjoy that heaven—to sit at the feet of that angel and kiss the hem of her white robe.

*O Gods!* he thought. *'Twas too great a bliss to be real!*

"I knew it couldn't be," thought poor Harry. "I knew something would happen to take her from me."

"But you will ride with us to Tunbridge, nephew Warrington, and keep us from the highwaymen?" asked Madame de Bernstein.

Harry Warrington hoped the company did not notice how red he had grown. He forced his voice to remain calm and steady. Yes, he would ride with their ladyships, and he was certain they needed fear no danger. Danger! Harry felt he would rather embrace danger than avoid it. He would slay ten thousand highwaymen if they approached his mistress's coach. At the very least, he would ride alongside that coach, catching glimpses of her eyes at the window occasionally. He might not speak to her, but he would be near her. He would press her blessed hand at the inn at night and feel it resting on his as he led her to the carriage in the morning.

They would spend two full days traveling to Tunbridge, perhaps staying there a day or two longer. Was not the poor wretch condemned to die at Newgate thankful for even two or three days of respite? One sees that we have merely indicated, rather than described in detail, the condition of Mr. Harry Warrington, or that utter depth of imbecility into which the poor young man had now fallen. Some boys suffer from the complaint of love favorably and gently. Others, when struck by the fever, become sick unto death; or, recovering, carry the scars of the malady to their graves or to remotest old age. I say, it is unfair to record a young fellow's words when he is raging in such a delirium. Suppose he loves a woman twice his own age; have we not all read of the young gentleman who committed suicide due to his fatal passion for Mademoiselle Ninon de l'Enclos, who turned out to be his grandmother? Suppose you are making an ass of yourself, young Harry Warrington of Virginia! Are there not people in England who heehaw too? Kick and abuse him, you who have never brayed; but bear with him, all honest fellow-cardophagi, long-eared messmates; recognize a brother-donkey!

"You will stay with us for a day or two at the Wells," Madame Bernstein continued. "You will see us settled into our lodgings. Then you can return to Castlewood for the partridge shooting and all the fine pursuits you and my lord are to study together."

Harry bowed in acquiescence. A whole week of heaven! Life was not entirely a blank anymore.

"And since there is sure to be plenty of company at the Wells, I shall be able to present you," the lady added graciously.

"Company! Ah! I shan't need company," sighed Harry.

"I mean that I shall be quite contented in the company of you two ladies," he added eagerly, and no doubt Mr. Will wondered at his cousin's peculiar taste.

Since this was to be the last night of cousin Harry's visit to Castlewood, cousin Will suggested that he, the chaplain, and Warrington meet at Harry's quarters to settle accounts. To this proposal, Harry, being a considerable winner in his recent gambling transactions with the two gentlemen, had no objection.

Accordingly, when the ladies retired for the night and my lord withdrew, as was his custom, to his own apartments, the three gentlemen found themselves assembled in Mr. Harry's small room before the punch-bowl, which was Will's usual midnight companion. However, Will's method of settling accounts involved producing two fresh decks of cards and offering to submit Harry's debt to the process of being doubled or acquitted. The poor chaplain possessed no more ready cash than Lord Castlewood's younger brother. Harry Warrington desired to win the money of neither. Would he cause pain to the brother of his adored Maria, or allow any of her kin

While the virtuous Bishop of Cambray, in his recent romance concerning Calypso, lamented the sorrow of the sorceress at Ulysses' departure, I recall no mention of the grief suffered by Calypso's lady's-maid upon bidding farewell to Odysseus's own gentleman. Yet, surely, the menials must have wept together within the kitchen precincts while their masters embraced wildly in the drawing-room; they must have clustered around each other in the fore-cabin as their principals shattered their hearts in the grand salon. When the final bell rang and Ulysses's mate cried out, "Now! Any one for shore!" both Calypso and her attendant must have stepped onto the same plank, hearts pounding and eyes streaming. Standing on the quay, they would have waved pocket-handkerchiefs—of vastly different textures and values—to their friends aboard the departing vessel, while crowds on land and in the ship's bows shouted *hip, hip, hurrah* (or whatever the ancient Greek equivalent might be). The essential truth remains: if Calypso could not console herself, neither could her maid. They traversed the same plank of grief, feeling the identical pang of separation. Upon returning home, they might not employ handkerchiefs of equal quality, but the tears shed in her marble halls were undoubtedly as salty and abundant as those poured forth in the servants' quarters.

Not only did Harry Warrington depart Castlewood a captive to love, but Gumbo also left the premises prey to the same delightful passion. His wit, accomplishments, good humor, and skills in dancing, cooking, and music had endeared him to the entire female domestic circle. While some men might have harbored jealousy, the ladies were entirely his. In those days in England, there existed no such prejudice against Black men as exists today among white-skinned peoples. Their condition was certainly not one of equality, yet they commanded a certain tolerant sympathy from all, and from women, a kindness far more generous. When Ledyard and Parke in Blackmansland were persecuted by the men, did they not find Black women pitiful and kind toward them? Women are invariably kind toward our sex. What mental infirmities do they not cherish? What moral deformities, what lepers, what idiots, what dull drivellers, what misshapen monsters (speaking figuratively) do they not fondle and cuddle? Gumbo was treated by the women with the same warmth afforded to many individuals far superior to himself; it was merely the men in the servants' hall who rejoiced at the Virginian lad's departure. I would dearly like to witness his farewell. I imagine Molly, the housemaid, slipping to the terrace gardens in the grey dawn to gather a wistful bouquet. I picture Betty, the kitchenmaid, snipping a thick lock of her chestnut ringlets to exchange for a woolly token from young Gumbo's head. Naturally, he claimed to be of *regum progenies*, a descendant of Ashantee kings. Given the vast numbers of hereditary bondsmen currently inhabiting Caffraria, Connaught, and elsewhere, there must once have been countless such potent sovereigns in those lands to account for the descendants now extant.

On the morning appointed for Madame de Bernstein's departure, the numerous domestics of Castlewood crowded the doors and passages. Some sought a final glimpse of her ladyship's men and the fascinating Gumbo; others intended to say goodbye to her ladyship's maid; all aimed to intercept the Baroness and her nephew for parting fees, a custom of the era involving the liberal distribution of gratuities among household staff. Both the Baroness and her nephew showered gold upon the livery-clad society, the gentlemen in black and ruffles, and the swarm of female attendants. Castlewood was the home of the Baroness's youth. As for her honest Harry, who had not only lived at free expense but had won horses and money—or promises thereof—from his cousin and the unfortunate chaplain—he was naturally generous and felt compelled not to stint his benevolence at this critical moment. "My mother, I know," he reasoned, "will wish me to be liberal to all the retainers of the Esmond family." Thus, he scattered his gold pieces right and left, acting as if he were as wealthy as Gumbo had proclaimed. No one approached him without receiving a share of his bounty. From the major-domo to the shoe-black, Mr. Harry offered a peace-offering to them all. He gave tokens to the stern housekeeper in her still-room and the feeble old porter in his lodge. When a man loves one woman in a family, it is astonishing how fond he becomes of everyone connected with it. He ingratiates himself with the maids; he is bland with the butler; he interests himself in the footman; he runs errands for the daughters; he offers advice and loans to the young son at college; he pets little dogs he would otherwise kick; he smiles at old stories that would induce yawns from anyone else but Papa; he drinks sweet port wine he would otherwise curse the steward for serving; he endures the cantankerous old maiden aunt; he keeps time as darling little Fanny plays the piano; and he smiles when wicked, lively little Bobby spills coffee over his shirt.

For a brief period, according to the customs of that age, Harry Warrington had bestowed these artless testimonies of affection upon the Castlewood family regarding one of them. Cousin Will should have reclaimed his money and welcome, or secured as much of Harry's own funds as the lad could spare. Nevertheless, though a lover, the lad was shrewd, keen, fond of sport and fair play, and a discerning judge of a good horse. Having gambled for and won all the money Will possessed, alongside a collection of Mr. Esmond's valuable autographs, Harry was delighted to win Will's brown horse—the very quadruped that had nearly thrown him into the water on his first evening at Castlewood. Having watched the horse perform often, amidst all his passion and romance, he was not sorry to possess such a sound, swift, well-bred hunter and roadster. After gazing at the stars shining over his mistress's window and extinguishing her candle, he retired to his dormitory. There, no doubt, he reflected on his Maria and his horse with youthful satisfaction, imagining the sweetness of riding one pillioned on the other, touring the entire island with a pair of white arms encircling his waist. He fell asleep ruminating on these thoughts, meditating a million blessings on his Maria, in whose company he was to luxuriate for at least another week.

Early in the morning, poor Chaplain Sampson sent his little black mare via his groom, footman, and gardener, who wept and bestowed numerous kisses upon the beast's white nose as they handed it over to Gumbo. Both Gumbo and his master were moved by the fellow's sensitivity; the Negro servant expressed his sympathy through tears, while Harry produced two guineas, astonishing and swiftly comforting the chaplain's boy. Then Gumbo and the former groom led the beast to the stable, instructed to bring him round with Mr. William's horse after breakfast, at the hour the Baroness's carriages were scheduled.

So courteous was he to his aunt, or so grateful for her departure, that the master of the house appeared at the morning meal to bid his guests farewell. The ladies and the chaplain were present—the only family member absent being Will, who left a note for his cousin stating, in exceedingly poor spelling, that he was obliged to travel to Salisbury Races that morning but had left the horse his cousin had won the previous night. Tom, Mr. Will's groom, would deliver it to Mr. Warrington's servant. Will's absence did not hinder the remainder of the party from sharing tea amicably. Soon, the carriages rolled into the courtyard, servants packed them with the Baroness's multiplied luggage, and the moment of departure arrived.

A large open landau carried the stout Baroness and her niece, flanked by two men-servants mounting the box with pistols and blunderbusses ready for potential highwaymen. Another carriage held their ladyships' maids, and a third guarded the trunks, vast and numerous as they were, yet insignificant compared to the enormous baggage train accompanying a lady of the present time. Mr. Warrington's modest valises were placed in this second carriage under the maid's guardianship, and Mr. Gumbo proposed to ride by the window for the majority of the journey.

My Lord, accompanied by his stepmother and Lady Fanny, escorted their kinswoman to the carriage steps, bidding her farewell with many dutiful embraces. Her Lady Maria followed in a riding-dress, which Harry Warrington deemed the most becoming costume in the world. A host of servants stood around, begging Heaven bless her ladyship. The Baroness's departure was known throughout the village, and scores of locals waited under the trees outside the gates, cheering and waving their hats as the ponderous vehicles departed.

Gumbo had gone ahead to fetch Mr. Warrington's horses while My Lord, with his arm under his young guest's, paced up and down the court. "I hear you are carrying away some of our horses out of Castlewood?" My Lord asked.

Harry blushed. "A gentleman cannot refuse a fair game at cards," he replied. "I never wanted to play, nor would have wagered money had not my cousin William forced me. As for the chaplain, it went to my heart to win from him, but he was as eager as my cousin."

"I know—I know! There is no

Lest any tender-hearted reader should be in alarm for Mr. Harry Warrington's safety, and fancy that his broken-kneed horse had carried him altogether out of this life and history, let us set her mind easy at the beginning of this chapter by assuring her that nothing very serious has happened. How can we afford to kill off our heroes, when they are scarcely out of their teens, and we have not reached the age of manhood of the story? We are in mourning already for one of our Virginians, who has come to grief in America; surely we cannot kill off the other in England? No, no. Heroes are not despatched with such hurry and violence unless there is a cogent reason for making away with them. Were a gentleman to perish every time a horse came down with him, not only the hero, but the author of this chronicle would have gone under ground, whereas the former is but sprawling outside it, and will be brought to life again as soon as he has been carried into the house where Madame de Bernstein's servants have rung the bell.

And to convince you that at least this youngest of the Virginians is still alive, here is an authentic copy of a letter from the lady into whose house he was taken after his fall from Mr. Will's brute of a broken-kneed horse, and in whom he appears to have found a kind friend:

"At her House at Richmond, in Virginia

"If Mrs. Esmond Warrington of Virginia can call to mind twenty-three years ago, when Miss Rachel Esmond was at Kensington Boarding School, she may perhaps remember Miss Molly Benson, her class-mate, who has forgotten all the little quarrels which they used to have together (in which Miss Molly was very often in the wrong), and only remembers the generous, high-spirited, sprightly, Miss Esmond, the Princess Pocahontas, to whom so many of our school-fellows paid court.

"Dear madam! I cannot forget that you were dear Rachel once upon a time, as I was your dearest Molly. Though we parted not very good friends when you went home to Virginia, yet you know how fond we once were. I still, Rachel, have the gold etui your papa gave me when he came to our speech-day at Kensington, and we two performed the quarrel of Brutus and Cassius out of Shakspeare; and 'twas only yesterday morning I was dreaming that we were both called up to say our lesson before the awful Miss Hardwood, and that I did not know it, and that as usual Miss Rachel Esmond went above me. How well remembered those old days are! How young we grow as we think of them! I remember our walks and our exercises, our good King and Queen as they walked in Kensington Gardens, and their court following them, whilst we of Miss Hardwood's school curtseyed in a row. I can tell still what we had for dinner on each day of the week, and point to the place where your garden was, which was always so much better kept than mine. So was Miss Esmond's chest of drawers a model of neatness, whilst mine were in a sad condition. Do you remember how we used to tell stories in the dormitory, and Madame Hibou, the French governess, would come out of bed and interrupt us with her hooting? Have you forgot the poor dancing-master, who told us he had been waylaid by assassins, but who was beaten, it appears, by my lord your brother's footmen?

"My dear, your cousin, the Lady Maria Esmond (her papa was, I think, but Viscount Castlewood in those times), has just been on a visit to this house, where you may be sure I did not recall those sad times to her remembrance, about which I am now chattering to Mrs. Esmond. Her ladyship has been staying here, and another relative of yours, the Baroness of Bernstein, and the two ladies are both gone on to Tunbridge Wells; but another and dearer relative still remains in my house, and is sound asleep, I trust, in the very next room, and the name of this gentleman is Mr. Henry Esmond Warrington.

"Now, do you understand how you come to hear from an old friend? Do not be alarmed, dear madam! I know you are thinking at this moment, 'My boy is ill. That is why Miss Molly Benson writes to me.' No, my dear; Mr. Warrington was ill yesterday, but to-day he is very comfortable; and our doctor, who is no less a person than my dear husband, Colonel Lambert, has blooded him, has set his shoulder, which was dislocated, and pronounces that in two days more Mr. Warrington will be quite ready to take the road.

"I fear I and my girls are sorry that he is so soon to be well. Yesterday evening, as we were at tea, there came a great ringing at our gate, which disturbed us all, as the bell very seldom sounds in this quiet place, unless a passing beggar pulls it for charity; and the servants, running out, returned with the news, that a young gentleman, who had a fall from his horse, was lying lifeless on the road, surrounded by the friends in whose company he was travelling. At this, my Colonel (who is sure the most Samaritan of men!) hastens away, to see how he can serve the fallen traveller, and presently, with the aid of the servants, and followed by two ladies, brings into the house such a pale, lifeless, beautiful, young man! Ah, my dear, how I rejoice to think that your child has found shelter and succour under my roof! that my husband has saved him from pain and fever, and has been the means of restoring him to you and health! We shall be friends again now, shall we not? I was very ill last year, and 'twas even thought I should die. Do you know, that I often thought of you then, and how you had parted from me in anger so many years ago? I began then a foolish note to you, which I was too sick to finish, to tell you that if I went the way appointed for us all, I should wish to leave the world in charity with every single being I had known in it.

"Your cousin, the Right Honourable Lady Maria Esmond, showed a great deal of maternal tenderness and concern for her young kinsman after his accident. I am sure she hath a kind heart. The Baroness de Bernstein, who is of an advanced age, could not be expected to feel so keenly as we young people; but was, nevertheless, very much moved and interested until Mr. Warrington was restored to consciousness, when she said she was anxious to get on towards Tunbridge, whither she was bound, and was afraid of all things to lie in a place where there was no doctor at hand. My Aesculapius laughingly said, he would not offer to attend upon a lady of quality, though he would answer for his young patient. Indeed, the Colonel, during his campaigns, has had plenty of practice in accidents of this nature, and I am certain, were we to call in all the faculty for twenty miles round, Mr. Warrington could get no better treatment. So, leaving the young gentleman to the care of me and my daughters, the Baroness and her ladyship took their leave of us, the latter very loth to go. When he is well enough, my Colonel will ride with him as far as Westerham, but on his own horses, where an old army-comrade of Mr. Lambert's resides.

"And, as this letter will not take the post for Falmouth until, by God's blessing, your son is well and perfectly restored, you need be under no sort of alarm for him whilst under the roof of, madam, your affectionate, humble servant, MARY LAMBERT.

"P.S. Thursday. "I am glad to hear (Mr. Warrington's coloured gentleman hath informed our people of the gratifying circumstance) that Providence hath blessed Mrs. Esmond with such vast wealth, and with an heir so likely to do credit to it. Our present means are amply sufficient, but will be small when divided amongst our survivors. Ah, dear madam! I have heard of your calamity of last year. Though the Colonel and I have reared many children (five), we have lost two, and a mother's heart can feel for yours! I own to you, mine yearned to your boy to-day, when (in a manner inexpressibly affecting to me and Mr. Lambert) he mentioned his dear brother. 'Tis impossible to see your son, and not to love and regard him. I am thankful that it has been our lot to succour him in his trouble, and that in receiving the stranger within our gates we should be giving hospitality to the son of an old friend."

Nature has written a letter of credit upon some men's faces, which is honoured almost wherever presented. Harry Warrington's countenance was so stamped in his youth. His eyes were so bright, his cheek so red and healthy, his look so frank and open, that almost all who beheld him, nay, even those who cheated him, trusted him. Nevertheless, as we have hinted, the lad was by no means the artless stripling he seemed to be. He was knowing enough with all his blushing cheeks; perhaps more wily and wary than he grew to be in after-age. Sure, a shrewd and generous man (who has led an honest life and has no

Sinking into a sweet slumber, lulled by harmonious sounds, our young patient passed a night of pleasant unconsciousness. He awoke in the morning to find a summer sun streaming through the window, illuminating his bed where his kind host and hostess smiled warmly at him. Ravenously hungry, he was immediately permitted by his doctor to partake of a hearty mess of chicken, which the doctor's wife proudly announced had been prepared by one of her own daughters.

A fleeting image of a young person—or rather, two young persons—with flushed cheeks and dark, waving locks, smiling around his couch before suddenly vanishing, arose in the young man's mind. Immediately following this, the memory of a different woman returned: lovely, yet undeniably elderly, possessing... well, let us simply say she was considerably older. Oh, horror and remorse! He writhed with anguish as a specific recollection crossed his mind. An immense gulf of time yawned between him and the past. How long had it been since he realized those pearls were artificial and those golden locks merely brass? A long, long time ago, when he was an innocent boy. Now he was a man—quite an old man. He had been bled copiously, suffered a slight fever, gone without food for many hours, and finally succumbed to a sleeping draught followed by a deep slumber.

"What is it, my dear child?" cried kind Mrs. Lambert as he startled awake.

"Nothing, madam; just a twinge in my shoulder," replied the lad. "Am I speaking to my host and hostess? You have been very kind to me."

"We are old friends, Mr. Warrington," said Mrs. Lambert. "My husband, Colonel Lambert, knew your father, and I and your mother were schoolgirls together at Kensington. You were no stranger to us when your aunt and cousin informed us of your identity."

"Are they here?" asked Harry, looking somewhat blankly.

"They must have stayed at Tunbridge Wells last night. They sent a horseman from Reigate yesterday seeking news of you."

"Oh!" said Harry, glancing at his bandaged arm. "I remember."

"I have effected a good cure of you, Mr. Warrington. From now on, Mrs. Lambert and the cook will attend to you."

"Nay; Theo prepared the chicken and rice, Mr. Lambert," interjected the lady. "Shall Mr. Warrington rise after breakfast? We will send your valet to assist you."

"If howling proves fidelity, your man must be a most devoted creature," remarked Mr. Lambert.

"He allowed your baggage to depart in your aunt's carriage after all," noted Mrs. Lambert. "You must wear my husband's linen, which, I assure you, is not inferior to your own."

"Pish, my dear! My shirts are fit for any Christian," cried the Colonel.

"They are Theo's and Hester's work," said the mother, prompting her husband to arch his eyebrows and glance at her. "Moreover, Theo has ripped and resewn your sleeve to ensure comfort for your injured shoulder," she added.

"How beautiful those roses are!" exclaimed Harry, admiring a fine Chinese vase filled with them on the dressing table beneath a Japanese-framed glass cover.

"My daughter Theo cut them this morning. Well, Mr. Lambert? Did she indeed cut them?"

Presumably, the Colonel was reflecting that his wife had introduced Theo too prominently into the conversation, stepping on Mrs. Lambert's slipper, pulling her robe, or otherwise nudging her toward propriety.

"And I fancied I heard someone singing the Evening Hymn very sweetly last night—or was it merely a dream?" asked the young patient.

"Theo again, Mr. Warrington!" laughed the Colonel. "Our servants claimed your negro servant began humming it in the kitchen as if he were a church organ."

"Our people sing it at home, sir. My grandfather loved it dearly. His wife's father was a close friend of the late Bishop Ken, who composed it; and—and my dear brother also cherished it," said the boy, his voice softening.

It was then, presumably, that Mrs. Lambert felt inclined to kiss the boy. His recent accident, illness, and recovery, combined with the kindness of those around him, had softened Harry Warrington's heart, opening it to nobler influences than those imposed upon him during the previous six weeks. He breathed a purer air than the tainted atmosphere of selfishness, worldliness, and corruption into which he had been thrust upon arriving in England. Often, a young man's fate, choices, or weaknesses lead him into the company of the frivolous and vain; fortunate is he whose lot introduces him to wiser circles, where lamps are trimmed and pure hearts maintain a modest vigil.

The pleased matron left her young patient devouring Miss Theo's portion of rice and chicken, while the Colonel sat by the lad's bedside. Gratitude toward his hospitable hosts and satisfaction from a comfortable meal induced a very pleasant state of mind and body in Mr. Warrington. He was now ready to speak more freely than usual; for, unless stirred by strong interest or emotion, the young man was typically taciturn and cautious in conversation, lacking an imaginative turn of mind. Regarding books, his youth had been a negligent student, and his comments on even simple works lacked profundity or value. However, concerning dogs, horses, and the ordinary business of life, he was a far superior critic, conversing freely with anyone interested in such matters.

Harry's host, possessing considerable shrewdness and experience with books, livestock, and men, quickly gauged his young guest through their ensuing discussion. It was now, for the first time, that the Virginian learned Mrs. Lambert had been an early friend of his mother, and that the Colonel's own father had served alongside Harry's grandfather, Colonel Esmond, in the renowned wars of Queen Anne. He found himself in a friend's country, soon at ease with his honest host, whose manners were simple and cordial, appearing perfectly gentlemanly despite wearing a plain fustian coat and a lace-less waistcoat.

"My sons are both away," said Harry's host. "Otherwise, they would have shown you the countryside upon your rising, Mr. Warrington. Currently, you have only the company of my wife and her daughters. Mrs. Lambert has already spoken of one of them, Theo, our eldest, who made your broth, cut your roses, and mended your coat. She is not such a marvel as her mother imagines; however, little Theo is a smart little housekeeper and a very good, cheerful lass, though her father admits it."

"It is very kind of Miss Lambert to take such care of me," said the young patient.

"She is no kinder to you than to any other mortal; she merely performs her duty." The Colonel smiled. "I laugh at their mother for praising our children," he said, "and I confess I am equally foolish about them. The truth is, God has gifted us with excellent and dutiful children, and I see no reason to disguise my gratitude for such a blessing. Do you have no sisters, I trust?"

"No, sir, I am alone now," Mr. Warrington replied.

"Ay, truly; I beg your pardon for my thoughtlessness. Your man has told our household what happened last year. I served with Braddock in Scotland and hope he recovered before passing. He was a wild fellow, sir, yet possessed a fund of truth beneath his rough, swaggering manner. Your black fellow speaks very freely about his master and his affairs. I suppose you allow him these liberties since he rescued you—"

"Rescued me?" cried Mr. Warrington.

"From numerous Indians on that very expedition. My Molly and I did not anticipate entertaining such a prodigiously wealthy gentleman. He claims half of Virginia belongs to you; but if the entirety of North America were yours, we could offer only our best."

"Those negro boys, sir, lie like the father of all lies. They deem it honorable to represent us as ten times richer than we are. My mother possesses an estate in England that would be considered vast, and she is very well-off at home. We are as prosperous as most of our neighbors, sir, but no better; all our splendor exists only in Mr. Gumbo's foolish imagination. He never rescued me from an Indian in his life and would flee at the sight of one, just as my poor brother's boy did on that fatal day when he fell."

"The bravest man acts thus at unfortunate times," said the Colonel. "I witnessed the finest troops in the world running at Preston before a ragged mob of Highland savages."

"That was because the Highlanders fought for a righteous cause, sir."

"Do you think," asked Harry's host, "that the French Indians held the righteous cause in the battle of last year?"

"The scoundrels! I would have the scalp of every murderous redskin among them!" cried Harry, clutching his fist. "They were robbing and invading British territories as well. Yet the Highlanders fought for their king."

"We, on our side, fought for our king; and we ultimately won the battle," said the Colonel, laughing.

"Ah!" cried Harry. "If His Royal Highness the Prince had not retreated at Derby, your king and mine would now be His Majesty King James the Third!"

"Who made such a Tory of you, Mr. Warrington?" asked Lambert.

"Nay, sir, the Esmonds have always been loyal!" answered the youth. "Had we remained at home and lived twenty years earlier,

Mrs. Lambert's little daydream had finally evaporated. Both Miss Theo and her mother were forced to admit, deep in their hearts, that their hero was merely an ordinary mortal. Though they exchanged few words on the subject, each knew the other's thoughts with the intuitive clarity of those who love deeply; and Mama, through an extra measure of tenderness and a specially caressing manner toward her daughter, sought to soothe her disappointment.

"Never mind, my dear," the maternal kiss whispered against the filial cheek. "Our hero has turned out to be but an ordinary mortal, and none such is good enough for my Theo. Thou shalt have a real husband ere long, if there be one in England. Why, I was scarce fifteen when your father saw me at the Bury Assembly, and while I was yet at school, I used to vow that I never would have any other man. If Heaven gave me such a husband—the best man in the whole kingdom—sure it will bless my child equally, who deserves a king if she fancies him!"

Indeed, I am not sure that Mrs. Lambert—who, of course, knew the age of the Prince of Wales and was fully aware of how handsome and virtuous the young prince was—did not secretly expect that he too would ride past her gate, tumble down from his steed, be ushered inside, be cured, and thereby cause his royal grandfather to grant Martin Lambert a regiment, all while falling hopelessly in love with Theo.

For his part, the Colonel, along with his second daughter, Miss Hetty, took the laughing, scornful, and unbelieving side. Mama was always prone to matchmaking; indeed, Mrs. Lambert was much addicted to novels and cried her eyes out over them with great assiduity. No coach ever passed the gate without her expecting a husband for one of her girls to alight from it and ring the bell.

As for Miss Hetty, she allowed her tongue to wag in a more than usually saucy manner, peppering the conversation with a hundred sly allusions to their guest. She dragged Prussia and Persia into their discourse with abominable pertness and frequency. She asked whether the current King of Prussia was called the Shaw or the Sophy, and queried how far it was from Ispahan to Saxony—which his Majesty was currently invading—and about which war Papa was so busy studying with his maps and newspapers. She retrieved *The Persian Tales* from her mother's closet and laid them slyly on the parlor table where the family sat.

"I would not marry a Persian prince for my part," she declared. "I prefer a gentleman who might not have more than one wife at a time." She referred to their young Virginian guest as "Theo's prince" and asked her mother if she wished Hetty to take the other visitor, the "black prince," for herself. Indeed, she rallied her sister and her mother unceasingly on their sentimentalities, refusing to stop until she had driven them to anger, at which point she would burst into tears, kiss them violently one after another, and coax them back into good humor.

Simple Harry Warrington, meanwhile, knew nothing of the jokes, the tears, the quarrels, the reconciliations, the hymeneal plans, and all the rest of the domestic drama of which he was the innocent occasion. A hundred allusions to Prussians and Persians were shot at him, yet those Parthian arrows failed to penetrate his hide at all. Was he a Shaw? A Sophy? Very likely he thought a Sophy was a lady and would have deemed it the height of absurdity that a man with a great black beard should bear such a name.

We often find ourselves dropping like a stone into a quiet pool, perfectly compact and cool, little knowing the flutter and excitement we create beneath the surface, disturbing the fish, frightening the ducks, and agitating the entire expanse of the water. How should Harry know the effect his sudden appearance produced in this little, quiet, sentimental family? He thought quite well enough of himself on many points, but was still diffident regarding women, being of that age when young gentlemen require encouragement and to be brought forward. Having been raised at home in very modest and primitive relations toward the opposite sex, he remained oblivious to the subtext.

So Miss Hetty's jokes played around the lad, whom she considered stupid, and he minded them no more than so many summer gnats. It was not that he lacked intelligence; rather, he was simple, too occupied with himself and his own honest affairs to consider others. Why, what tragedies, comedies, interludes, intrigues, farces, and dramas go on under our noses in friends' drawing-rooms where we visit daily, leaving us utterly ignorant, self-satisfied, and blind!

As these sisters sat combing their flowing ringlets of night or conversed in the great bed—a custom of the day where they lay together—how should Harry know that he held such a large share in their thoughts, jokes, and conversation?

Three days after his arrival, his new and hospitable friends walked with him through the fine park belonging to my Lord Wrotham, where they were free to wander. Upon reaching a stretch of water, they encountered some swans, which the young ladies were accustomed to feeding with bread. As the birds approached the women, Hetty cast a queer look at her mother and sister, followed by a glance at her father, who stood nearby, honest and happy in a red waistcoat.

"Mamma's swans are something like these, Papa," Hetty said.

"What swans, my dear?" Mama asked.

"Something like, but not quite. They have shorter necks than these, and there are scores of them on our common," continued Miss Hetty. "I saw Betty plucking one in the kitchen this morning. We shall have it for dinner, with apple-sauce..."

"Don't be a little goose!" interrupted Miss Theo. "...and sage and onions. Do you love swan, Mr. Warrington?"

"I shot three last winter on our river," replied the Virginian gentleman. "Ours are not such white birds as these—they eat very well, though."

The simple youth had not the slightest idea that he himself was an allegory at that very moment, and that Miss Hetty was narrating a fable concerning him. In some exceedingly obscure Latin work, I have read that long before Virginia was discovered, other folk were equally dull of comprehension. Thus, it was a premature sentiment on the part of Miss Theo—that little tender flutter of the bosom we have acknowledged she felt upon first beholding the Virginian, so handsome, pale, and bleeding. This was not the great passion she knew her heart was capable of feeling. Like the birds, it had awakened and begun to sing at a false dawn.

"Hop back to thy perch, and cover thy head with thy wing, thou tremulous little fluttering creature! It is not yet light, and roosting is as yet better than singing. Anon will come morning, and the whole sky will redden, and you shall soar up into it and salute the sun with your music."

One little phrase, some thirty-three lines back, perhaps the fair and suspicious reader has noticed: "Three days after his arrival, Harry was walking with," etc., etc. If he could walk—which it appeared he did perfectly well—what business did he have to be walking with anyone but Lady Maria Esmond on the Pantiles at Tunbridge Wells? His shoulder was set; his health was entirely restored; he had not even changed his coat, owing to the Colonel's generosity. Surely a young man in such a condition had no right to linger at Oakhurst and was bound by every tie of duty, convenience, love, relationship, and a gentle heart waiting for him, alongside the washerwoman, to depart for Tunbridge.

Why did he stay behind, unless he was in love with either of the young ladies (and we maintain he was not)? Had the gracious reader grasped the meaning of the mystic 'S' with which the previous chapter commenced, wherein the designer feebly endeavored to depict the notorious Sinbad the Sailor surmounted by that odious old man of the sea? What if Harry Warrington were that sailor, and his fate that choking, deadening, inevitable old man? What if, for the past two days, he had felt those knees throttling him round the neck? If his fell aunt's purpose was answered, and if his late love was killed as dead by her poisonous communications as fair Rosamond was by her royal and legitimate rival...

Is Hero then lighting the lamp and preparing supper, while Leander sits comfortably with some other party, never thinking of taking to the water? Ever since that coward's blow was struck in Lady Maria's back by her own relative, surely kind hearts must pity her ladyship. I know she has faults—yes, and she wears false hair—but a woman in distress, shall we not pity her? A lady of a certain age—are we to laugh at her because of her years? Between her old aunt and her unhappy delusion, be assured, Lady Maria Esmond is having no very pleasant time at Tunbridge Wells. There is no one to protect her. Madam Beatrix has her all to herself. Lady Maria is poor and hopes for money from her aunt; Lady Maria possesses secrets which the old woman knows and brandishes over her.

I, for one, am quite melted and grow soft-hearted as I think of her. Imagine her alone, a victim to that old woman! Paint to yourself that antique Andromeda (if you please, we will allow that rich, flowing head of hair to fall over her shoulders), chained

Mrs. Lambert stood holding her lily-white handkerchief, a silent token of farewell to the travelers departing from Oakhurst. Alongside her daughters, she watched them pace leisurely down the first few hundred yards of the road, keeping her gaze fixed until a cluster of lime trees obscured them from view. Behind that verdant screen, the good matron had often watched those she loved most vanish—her husband heading toward battle and peril, her sons marching off to school, one after another. Each had disappeared behind those green trees, returning only when Heaven's will decreed, bringing with them renewed joy and affection to their happy little household.

Beyond mere instinct, which naturally aided such sentiments, the leisure and contemplation inherent in domestic life fostered the tenderness and fidelity of women. With the men gone, the entire day belonged to thoughts of them—the anticipation of tomorrow's letter, the endless "tomorrows" that promised correspondence. There was the empty room where the boy had slept the previous night, the faint impression of his carpet bag still lingering on the bedspread. In the hall hung his riding crop; in the corner lay his fishing rod and basket, mute memorials of fleeting pleasures. At dinner, the cherry tart appeared, half of which the darling had devoured at two o'clock despite his melancholy, sandwiched between his choking younger sisters. Evening prayers were recited without the young scholar's voice joining the responses. Then came midnight and silence, and the good mother lay awake, pondering how one of her cherished brood had flown from the nest.

As dawn broke, signaling the end of holidays and the beginning of toil, the rustling limes seemed to form a protective barrier between the outside world and the ladies of Oakhurst. Whenever Mrs. Lambert and her girls ventured near these trees in the absence of the men, the kind-hearted matron would fall silent and thoughtful. She confessed she longed to carve their names onto the grey, silvered trunks amidst the traditional "true-lover's knots," a custom popular at the time. Miss Theo, possessing an exceptionally elegant talent for verse, composed poems about the trees, which her delighted parent subsequently submitted to a contemporary periodical.

"Now we are out of sight of the ladies," Colonel Lambert remarked, tipping his hat in a final salute as the two gentlemen trotted past the lime grove. "I know my wife stands at her window watching until we round this corner. I hope we shall see the trees and the house again, Mr. Warrington. Perhaps, with the boys back home, there will be better sport for you."

"I never wish to be happier than I have been, sir," replied Mr. Warrington warmly. "And I hope you will allow me to say that I feel as though I am leaving behind quite old friends."

"The host at whose house we dine tonight has a son who is also an old friend of our family," the Colonel continued. "My wife, an inveterate matchmaker, would have arranged a union between him and one of my daughters, but alas, the Colonel has chosen to fall in love with someone else."

"Oh!" sighed Mr. Warrington. "Others have done the same. There were brave fellows before Agamemnon."

"I beg your pardon, sir. Did you say the gentleman's name was Aga...?" the young traveler inquired meekly, having missed the earlier syllable.

"No, his name is James Wolfe," the Colonel corrected, smiling. "He is still considered young, or what we call so, being scarcely thirty. He is the youngest lieutenant-colonel in the army, unless we count a score of nobles who hold rank above us commoners."

"Of course, of course!" exclaimed the Colonel's young companion, displaying genuine colonial notions of aristocratic precedence. "I have seen him commanding captains who were thirty years his senior, yet possessed neither his merit nor his fortune. Lucky as he is, no one envies his superiority; indeed, most acknowledge him as our superior. He is beloved by every man in our old regiment, knows them all intimately, and is a scholar as well as a consummate soldier, mastering many languages."

"Ah, sir!" breathed Harry Warrington, sighing with profound humility. "I feel I have neglected my youth terribly. I have returned to England but an ignoramus. If my dear brother were alive, he would represent our name and our colony far better than I can. George was a scholar, a musician, capable of conversing with the most learned minds in our country. I am certain he would have held his own here. Tell me, sir, am I glad to have come home, and to you especially, simply to realize how ignorant I am?"

"If you recognize your ignorance so clearly, 'tis a great gain already," the Colonel replied with a smile. "At home, especially recently since losing my brother, I used to consider myself a mighty fine fellow, undoubtedly flattering myself as the locals around here did. Now I am wiser—or I hope so, though perhaps I am merely bragging again. You see, sir, the gentry in our colony know very little, save for dogs, horses, betting, and games. I wish I knew more about books and less about them."

"Nay. Dogs and horses are excellent books in their own right; we can read much truth from them. Not everyone is made to be a scholar, yet some may be worthy citizens and gentlemen despite their lack of learning. Why should we all strive to be specially learned or wise, or claim a first place in the world? His Royal Highness commands, Martin Lambert is colonel, and Jack Hunt, riding behind yonder, was once a private soldier and is now a very honest, worthy groom. As long as we do our best in our respective stations, does it matter whether that station is high or low? How do we know what is truly high or low? Perhaps Jack's currycomb, my epaulets, or His Royal Highness's baton prove equally significant. When I began life—and served without shame—I dreamed of success and honor. Now, I think of duty, and of those folks we parted with just hours ago. Let us trot on, or we shall miss Westerham before nightfall."

At Westerham, the two friends were welcomed by their hosts: a stately matron, an elderly soldier whose memories and services dated back fifty-four years, and his son, the Lieutenant-Colonel of Kingsley's Regiment, currently stationed at Maidstone. The Colonel had visited briefly to see his parents before departing. Harry observed the young officer with curiosity; despite his youth, he had seen extensive service and earned a formidable reputation. Little beauty graced his face; he was lean and pale, with red hair and high cheekbones. Yet, he possessed a fine courtesy toward elders, a cordial greeting for friends, and an animated conversational style that made listeners forget, and even admire, his plain features.

"Is Mr. Warrington bound for Tunbridge?" the lady of the house asked. "Our James will escort him." She whispered something to Colonel Lambert during supper, eliciting smiles and knowing winks from the officer. He raised a toast to "Miss Lowther."

"With all my heart!" cried the enthusiastic Colonel James, draining his glass to the dregs.

Mamma whispered to her friend that James and the lady intended to make a match, noting that she hailed from the illustrious Lowther family of the North. "If she were the daughter of Charlemagne," Lambert shouted jovially, "she is not too good for James Wolfe, or for his mother's son."

"Mr. Lambert would not say so if he knew her," the young Colonel declared.

"Oh, of course! She is the priceless pearl, and you are nothing," Mamma retorted.

"No. I share Colonel Lambert's opinion. Even if she brought all of Cumberland as a jointure, I'd say it was my James's due. That is the way with them, Mr. Warrington. We nurse our children through fevers, measles, whooping cough, and smallpox; we send them to the army and sleepless nights worry us; we break our hearts parting, welcoming them home only for weeks or perhaps years at a stretch. And after all that care, along comes a girl with bright eyes, and away goes our boy, caring not a fig for us afterward."

"And pray, my dear, how did you come to marry James's father?" asked the elder Colonel Wolfe. "Why didn't you stay with your parents?"

"Because James's father was gouty and needed someone to care for him, I suppose—not because I liked him a bit," the lady answered easily. Thus, the evening passed in warm talk and kindness.

The next morning, laden with expressions of gratitude and friendship, Colonel Lambert handed the young Virginian over to Mr. Wolfe's charge and turned his horse homeward. Meanwhile, the two gentlemen spurred toward Tunbridge Wells. Wolfe was eager to arrive; Harry Warrington, however, was perhaps less so. Indeed, as Lambert rode back toward Oakhurst, Harry's thoughts trailed after him with deep longing for the parlor where he had spent three days in happy tranquility.

Wolfe echoed Harry's enthusiastic praise of the Lamberts—their name, their life, their wife, their daughters, and their entire excellent family. "To possess such a good name and lead such a life as Colonel Lambert's," Wolfe said, "seems to me now the pinnacle of human ambition."

"And glory and honor?" Warrington asked. "Are those nothing? Would you give up winning them?"

"They were my dreams once," the Colonel answered, his perspective now markedly different. "Now my

Cousin Maria made her entrance, flanked by two gardeners' boys carrying baskets brimming with fresh blooms, intended to adorn Madame de Bernstein's drawing-room for the anticipated arrival of her guests. Three footmen, resplendent in liveries gorgeously laced with worsted, arranged double the number of card tables. Following them was a major-domo dressed in black and white, sporting fine lace ruffles and carrying a bag; he moved with the air of a man who ought to bear a sword at his hip. He trailed behind the lackeys carrying bundles of wax candles, placing a pair upon each card table and setting others in the silver sconces mounted on the wainscoting. Sunlight streamed through the windows, gilding the walls and casting slanting rays across the scene, mirroring the view outside: the green common dotted with rocks, tree clusters, and distant houses twinkling in the warmth.

Groups of figures in hoops, powdered wigs, and brocade sauntered across the lawn, dappling the grass with their shadows. Beyond the Baroness's windows lay the Pantiles, where a perpetual fair was in full swing, filled with the clamor and buzz of society. A band played music specifically for the benefit of the visitors to the spa town. While Madame Bernstein's main sitting-room might seem ill-suited for a recluse or a scholar, it was perfect for those who preferred bustle, gaiety, bright cross-lighting, and a panoramic view of the cheerful, busy place. Once the windows were lit, passersby below would know her ladyship was hosting a card assembly, accessible to anyone with an introduction.

It bears mentioning that the nightlife of society a century prior was considerably darker than today. There was scarcely one wax candle for every ten we now see in a modern drawing-room, let alone gas or electric illumination. Horrible, guttering tallow smoke and odor permeated the corridors. The candle-snuffer was a notorious figure in theaters. Recall Hogarth's paintings: how dim they are, how begrimed with tallow drippings! In "Marriage à la Mode," within Lord Viscount Squanderfield's grand saloons, where he and his wife yawn listlessly before a horrified steward after their party, there are merely eight candles—one per card table and half a dozen in a brass chandelier. If Jack Briefless had invited friends to oysters and beer in his chambers at Pump Court, he would have used twice as many. Let us find comfort in knowing that even Louis XIV, in all his glory, held his revels in the dark, and thank Mr. Price and other luminous benefactors for banishing the abominable gloom of our ancestors.

Maria, the fairest flower herself, diligently arranged her roses, sweet-williams, and other blossoms into vases scattered throughout the apartment, decorating the space to the best of her ability. She lingered fondly over one bowl or another dragon-shaped jar, casting only sly, timid glances toward young cousin Harry. His own blush would have suited any young woman, and one might have suspected she intended to stay longer than her aunt allowed. However, the Baroness, seated comfortably in her armchair with a crooked tortoiseshell stick in hand, imperiously directed the servants to their duties. She scolded Tom for a darn in his stocking and John for greasing his locks excessively from the candle box, maintaining a stern dominion over the household.

Another observation regarding the humble conditions of a hundred years ago: James slept two to a bed, four to a room—often a cellar—and washed in a trough rarely seen in London outside the barracks of Her Majesty's Foot Guards. If Maria hoped for a private moment, her fond heart was soon disappointed.

"Where are you going to dine, Harry?" asked Madame de Bernstein.

"My niece Maria and I shall have a chicken in the little parlour," she replied. "I think you should go to the best ordinary. There is one at the White Horse in a few moments; you'll hear his bell shortly. Understand, sir, you ought not to spare expense, but behave like the son of Princess Pocahontas. Your trunks have been transferred to the lodging I secured for you. It is not becoming for a lad to constantly hover around the aprons of two elderly women, is it, Maria?"

"No," said her ladyship, lowering her meek eyes, while the other lady glared in triumph.

"I believe Andromeda has been significantly exposed to the Dragon during the last five or six days," the narrator muses. "If Perseus had severed the latter's cruel head, he would have committed no unjustifiable murder. Yet, he drew neither sword nor shield; he merely looked mechanically at the lackeys in tawny and blue as they creaked about the room."

"There are excellent mercers and tailors from London always here to serve the company at the Wells," the lady continued. "You had better visit them, my dear, for your suit lacks the very latest fashion—a bit of lace, perhaps—"

"I cannot leave mourning, ma'am," the young man replied, looking down at his sable garments.

"Ho, sir!" cried the lady, rising abruptly on her cane. "Wear black for your brother until you are as old as Methuselah, if you wish. I am certain I do not wish to prevent you. I simply want you to dress properly, act like others, and make a figure worthy of your name."

"Madam," said Mr. Warrington with great formality, "I have done nothing to disgrace it that I know."

Why did the old woman pause and start as if struck? Let bygones be bygones. They had shared numerous brief exchanges where swords were metaphorically crossed and thrusts rapidly parried. She liked Harry no less for his courage in facing her.

"Surely a finer linen shirt than the one you wear would not be a disgrace to you, sir," she said with a forced laugh.

Harry bowed and blushed. This was one of the homely gifts of his Oakhurst friends. He felt strangely pleased to wear it, thinking of his new acquaintances—who were so good, pure, simple, and kindly—with immense tenderness. Clad in this garment, he felt as though evil could not touch him. He stated he would return to his lodging and ensure he appeared in the best linen available.

"Come back here, sir," commanded Madame Bernstein. "If our company has not yet arrived, Maria and I will find some ruffles for you!"

Guided by a footman, the young man departed for his new quarters. Harry discovered not only handsome, spacious apartments but also a groom standing ready to be hired, along with a second valet should he choose to employ one to attend to Mr. Gumbo. Before many minutes had passed, emissaries from a London tailor and bootmaker arrived with cards and compliments from their employers, Messrs. Regnier and Tull. Gumbo laid out the finest articles from Harry's modest wardrobe alongside the exquisite linens provided by his thrifty Virginian mother.

Visions of his snow-covered homeland arose: the crackling logs, the trim, quiet ladies working by the fire. For the first time, a fleeting thought crossed his mind that his homely clothes were not quite smart enough, that the home-worked linen lacked fineness. Could he feel shame for anything belonging to him or to Castlewood? It seemed strange. The simple folk there were entirely content with everything done, said, or produced at Castlewood; and Madam Esmond, when sending her son forth on his travels, believed no young nobleman needed superior provisions. The clothes might have fit better or been of a later fashion, certainly—but still, the young fellow presented a comely figure once his toilette was complete. Gumbo hailed a chair, marching beside it until they reached the ordinary where Harry was to dine.

Here, he expected to find the beau he had met earlier at his aunt's lodging, who had indicated the White Horse as the most fashionable dining spot. He mentioned his friend's name to the host, but the landlord and waiters, greeting him with many smiles and bows, assured his honor that no further introduction was needed. They helped him hang his coat and sword, offered choices between Burgundy, Pontac, or champagne, and guided him to a table. Although the most fashionable ordinary in the village, the White Horse was not crowded that day. Monsieur Barbeau, the landlord, informed Harry that a great entertainment at Summer Hill had drawn away most of the company. Indeed, upon entering, only four other gentlemen remained. Two were finishing wine after their meals; the other two were mid-course, to whom the landlord must have whispered the newcomer's name, for they looked at him with apparent interest and offered a slight bow as the smiling host bustled away to serve Harry.

Mr. Warrington returned the salutes of the two gentlemen, who welcomed him to Tunbridge and expressed hope he would enjoy the place upon closer acquaintance. Then, they exchanged waggish glances with each other, meanings unclear to Harry, while casting knowing looks at the two other guests over their wine. One wore a somewhat tarnished velvet coat with a massive queue, bag, voluminous ruffles, and embroidery. The other was a little, beetle-browed, hook-nosed, high-shouldered gentleman whom his companion addressed as "milord" or "my lord" in a very high voice.

"And so you know the nephew of the old woman—the Croesus who is arriving?" asked the little Baron.

"You're thrown out there, Jack!" said one young gentleman to the other.

Just within the precincts of the White Horse Tavern, leading up to the windows of the dining room, lay a bowling green dotted with a couple of tables where guests might sit to sip punch or tea. Having finished their dinner roughly simultaneously, Mr. Morris suggested they move to the green for a cool bottle. "Jack Morris would use the excuse of moving to the Dust Hole to get another drink," observed my Lord. To this, Jack retorted that surely every gentleman had his own preferred method of heading toward the deuce, admitting frankly that his own weakness was a bottle.

"My Lord Chesterfield's deuce is deuce-ace," noted my Lord March. "His lordship cannot keep away from cards or dice."

"My Lord March possesses not one devil, but several," countered Jack. "He loves gambling, horse-racing, betting, drinking, eating, money, and women. You have certainly fallen into bad company, Mr. Warrington, by associating with his lordship. He will play you for every acre you hold in Virginia."

"With the greatest pleasure in life, Mr. Warrington!" interjected my Lord.

"And for all your tobacco, spices, slaves, oxen, and asses, and everything else you possess."

"Shall we begin now?" asked my Lord. "Jack, you are never without a dice box or a bottle screw. I propose setting Mr. Warrington against whatever he desires."

"Unfortunately, my lord," replied Harry, "the tobacco, slaves, asses, and oxen are not yet mine. I am just of age, and my mother, who is scarcely twenty years older than myself, has as good a chance of longevity as I do."

"I will bet you that you survive her," my Lord offered. "I will pay you a sum now against four times that amount payable at her death. I will set a fair stake over this table against the reversion of your estate in Virginia upon the old lady's departure. What is your place called?"

"Castlewood."

"A principality, I hear it is," my Lord mused. "I wager its value has been exaggerated tenfold among the gossip-mongers here. How did you acquire the name Castlewood? Are you related to my lord? Ah, wait—I know. Your mother descends from the true head of the house. He took the losing side in '15. I have heard this story a dozen times from my old Duchess. She knew your grandfather; he was a friend of Addison, Steele, Pope, and Milton, I dare say, and the bigwigs. It is a pity he did not stay home and transplant the other branch of the family to the plantations."

"I have just been staying at Castlewood with my cousin there," remarked Mr. Warrington.

"Hm! Did you play with him? He is fond of pasteboard and bones."

"Never, save for sixpences and a pool of commerce with the ladies."

"So much the better for both of you. But did you play with Will Esmond if he was at home? I will lay ten to one you did."

Harry blushed and admitted that, in the evenings, he and his cousin had played a few games of cards.

"And Tom Sampson, the chaplain?" cried Jack Morris. "Was he of the party? I wager Tom made a third, and may the Lord deliver you from Tom and Will Esmond combined!"

"Nay; the truth is, I won of both of them," said Mr. Warrington.

"And they paid you? Well, miracles will never cease!"

"I did not speak of miracles," remarked Harry, smiling over his wine.

"And you do not tell tales out of school—the *volto sciolto*—hey, Mr. Warrington?" asked my Lord.

"I beg your pardon," said straightforward Harry. "French is the only language besides my own of which I know a little."

"My Lord March has learned Italian at the Opera, and a pretty penny his lessons have cost him," noted Jack Morris. "We must show him the Opera—mustn't we, March?"

"Must we, Morris?" my Lord asked, as if he barely tolerated the other's familiarity.

Both gentlemen were dressed identically in small, unpowdered wigs, blue frocks with plate buttons, buckskin breeches, riding boots, and small hats adorned with a narrow lace cord, showing no outward marks of high fashion.
