"I care little for the Opera, my lord," said Harry, warming to his Burgundy. "But I should like to go to Newmarket and long to see a good English hunting field."

"We will show you Newmarket and the hunt, sir. Can you ride well?"

"I think I can," Harry replied. "And I can shoot fairly well, and jump some."

"What is your weight? I bet we weigh even, or I weigh more. I bet Jack Morris beats you at birds or a mark at fifty paces. I bet I jump farther than you on flat ground right here on this green."

"I do not know Mr. Morris's shooting—I have never seen either gentleman before—but I accept your bets, my lord, at whatever you please," cried Harry, now thoroughly warmed by the wine.

"Ponies on each!" shouted my Lord.

"Done and done!" cried my Lord and Harry together. The young man felt it was for the honor of his country not to be ashamed of any bet made to him.

"We can try the last bet now, if your feet are steady," said my Lord, springing up, stretching his arms and limbs, and examining the crisp, dry grass. He removed his boots, then his coat and waistcoat, buckled his belt around his waist, and threw his clothes to the ground.

Harry treated his garments with far more respect. It was his finest suit. He removed the velvet coat and waistcoat, folded them carefully, and, seeing the nearby tables slopped with drink, placed them on a table in the dining room, where the windows stood open.

At that moment, a new guest entered: none other than Mr. Wolfe, soberly eating a chicken and salad with a modest pint of wine. Harry, in high spirits, told the Colonel he had a bet with my Lord March and asked if the Colonel would stand him halves. The Colonel claimed he was too poor to bet, but would he come out to witness fair play? With all his heart, he agreed. Colonel Wolfe set down his glass and stalked through the open window after his young friend.

"Who is that tallow-faced Put with the carroty hair?" asked Jack Morris, whose Burgundy had taken full effect.

Mr. Warrington explained that this was Lieutenant-Colonel Wolfe of the 20th Regiment.

"Your humble servant, gentlemen!" the Colonel said, offering a rigid military bow.

"Never saw such a figure in my life!" cried Jack Morris. "Did you—March?"

"I beg your pardon, I think you said March?" the Colonel asked, looking very surprised.

"I am the Earl of March, sir, at Colonel Wolfe's service," the nobleman bowed. "My friend, Mr. Morris, is so intimate with me that, after dinner, we are quite like brothers."

"Why is not all of Tunbridge Wells here to hear this?" thought Morris. Delighted, he shouted, "Two to one on my lord!"

"Done!" called Mr. Warrington; and the enthusiastic Jack was obliged to cry "Done!" as well.

"Take him, Colonel," Harry whispered to his friend.

But the Colonel said he could not afford to lose, and therefore could not hope to win.

"I see you have won one of our bets already, Mr. Warrington," my Lord March remarked. "I am taller than you by an inch or two, but you are broader round the shoulders."

"Pooh, my dear Will! I bet you you weigh twice as much as he does!" cried Jack Morris.

"Done, Jack!" said my Lord, laughing. "The bets are all ponies. Will you take him, Mr. Warrington?"

"No, my dear fellow—one's enough," said Jack.

"Very good, my dear fellow," said my Lord. "Now we will settle the other wager."

Having already arrayed himself in his best silk stockings, black satin-net breeches, and neatest pumps, Harry did not wish to remove his shoes, unlike his antagonist, whose heavy riding-boots and spurs were ill-suited for leaping. Before them lay a fine, even stretch of green turf about thirty yards long, sufficient for a run and a jump. A gravel walk bordered this green, beyond which stood a wall and gate sign featuring a field azure with the Hanoverian White Horse rampant between two skittles, bearing the motto of the landlord and the animal depicted.

My Lord's friend laid a handkerchief on the ground as the mark from which the leapers were to start, while Mr. Wolfe stood at the far end of the grass plat to note where each landed. "My lord went first," writes Mr. Warrington in a letter to Mrs. Mountain at Castlewood, Virginia, which still exists. "He urged I take the lead; but, remembering the story of the Battle of Fontenoy my dearest George used to tell, I said, 'Monseigneur le Comte, tirez le premier, s'il vous plaît.' So he took his run in his stocking feet, and for the honor of Old Virginia, I had the gratification of beating his lordship by more than

"Tell me about those children, child—the ones I saw darting around the house where you were taken in after your terrible fall?" Maria asked, pacing alongside him on the common. "Oh, that fall, Harry! I nearly died of fright when I saw it happen. Please, stop squeezing my arm so tightly. Do you not care for me?"

"The people are simply the finest, kindest souls I have ever encountered in this world," Mr. Warrington exclaimed warmly. "Mrs. Lambert was a close friend of my mother during her time in Europe. Colonel Lambert is a distinguished gentleman who has served in every theater of war—from Scotland with His Royal Highness, to Flanders, and Minorca. No natural parents could have shown me greater devotion. How can I possibly repay such kindness? I must give them a gift. I simply must," Harry declared, reaching into his pocket where the crisp winnings from Morris and March waited.

"We could visit the toy shop, my dear, and purchase a pair of dolls for the children," suggested Lady Maria. "Offering payment for their charity would surely insult them."

"Dolls for Hester and Theo! Do you really believe a woman ceases to be a woman until she turns forty, Maria?" Harry teased, though the arm beneath his hand gave a barely perceptible wince. "Miss Hester hardly considers herself a child, and Miss Theo is older than her sister. They speak several languages fluently. They have consumed mountains of books, played the harpsichord admirably, and sung together beautifully. Theo even composes original songs."

"Indeed!" Maria replied, feigning surprise. "I scarcely noticed them. I assumed they were merely children. I had no notion they possessed such talents or were such marvels."

"That is precisely how women are, Maria! Back home, if George or I praised a woman, Mrs. Esmond and Mountain would immediately find fault with her!" Harry protested.

"I am certain I would find fault with no one who treats you so kindly, Mr. Warrington," Maria sighed softly. "Though, you are not angry with me for envying them? For they cared for you when you were wounded and ill, while I... I had to leave you?"

"You dear, good Maria!"

"No, Harry! I am neither dear nor good. There, sir, you need not press your attentions so heavily. Look! There is your black servant walking among a dozen others in livery. Those wretched creatures are heading to the tea garden to get drunk just like their masters. That dreadful Mr. Morris was completely intoxicated when I found you, frightening you half to death."

"I had just secured massive bets from both of them. What shall I buy for you, my dear cousin?" Harry recounted his recent triumphs, laughing and boasting slightly. "For the honor of Virginia, I was determined to demonstrate what true jumping entails. With a bit of practice, I believe I could leap two feet further."

Maria felt pride swelling in her chest for her young champion. "But you must be wary of gambling, child," she warned gently. "It has ruined our entire family. My brother Castlewood, Will, our poor father, and even Lady Castlewood herself fell victim to it. As for my Lord March, he is the most reckless gambler and yet the most successful nobleman in the land."

"I have no intention of fearing him, nor his friend Mr. Jack Morris," Harry retorted, tracing the gold coins in his palm. "What games do you play at Aunt Bernstein's? Cribbage, all-fours, brag, whist, commerce, piquet, quadrille? I am ready for any of them. What time is that clock striking—seven already?"

"And you wish to begin now?" Maria asked, her voice tinged with sadness. "You seem to have forgotten your poor cousin. You used to enjoy walking with her."

"Hah! Youth is youth, cousin!" Harry cried, throwing his head back. "A young man must have his fun!" He strided ahead beside his partner, confident, joyful, and eager for pleasure. Just the day before, he had preferred the company of Theo and Hester and Mrs. Lambert; but now, the allure of life, gaiety, the thrill of shining and conquering proved too strong. He seized the cup of chance like any other lad, refusing to calculate the headache awaiting him tomorrow.

As they spoke, the fiddles from the open orchestra on the Parade began their usual evening tuning—a cacophony of squeaks and adjustments. Maria knew her aunt was already awake and waiting; she would soon return to her duties of servitude. Harry never inquired about this slavery, though he must have known of it if he had bothered to think. He offered no pity to his cousin. In truth, he was not thinking of her at all. Yet, when disaster struck him, she had been wounded far more cruelly. She had carried his burdens in her thoughts, burying them beneath a mask of smiling hypocrisy, as was expected of women of her station.

*My dear Mrs. Grundy,* the narrator mused internally, *you might think she was an old fool? Ah, do you suppose fools' caps do not cover gray hair as effectively as jet or auburn? Treat our elderly *fredaines* gently, O you Minerva of a woman! Perhaps you are so wise you do not read novels at all. But I know this: there are late crops of wild oats as well as early harvests. From observing myself and my neighbors, I suspect the *avena fatua* grows right up to the very last day of the year.*

Like worldly parents eager to shed a troublesome child to attend their own evening party, Madame Bernstein and her staff had prematurely set the sun to bed while daylight still lingered. They drew the curtains shut, turning their attention to cards, candles, tea, negus, and other refreshments. One carriage after another deposited ladies at the Baroness's door—some painted, some patched, all brocaded. Gentlemen in gala attire followed.

Mr. Poellnitz arrived with the largest star pinned to his lapel and the most embroidered coat of the evening. My Lord March and Ruglen appeared transformed from the rough individual Harry had met at the White Horse. His tight brown wig was replaced by a neatly curled feather-top with a bag and powdered hair; his jockey's dress and leather breeches swapped for a rich, elegant French suit. Mr. Jack Morris wore a similar wig and a suit of fabric mimicking his lordship's style. Mr. Wolfe attended his beautiful mistress, Miss Lowther, and her aunt, who loved cards as much as anyone else.

When Lady Maria Esmond entered, her appearance contradicted Madame Bernstein's earlier descriptions. Her figure was exquisite, her dress revealing much of it. Her complexion was naturally pale, enhanced by a dark frilled ribbon clasped with a jewel around her neck, which made her skin look snow-white. Her cheeks held a healthy bloom, openly purchased from perfumeries, though a touch of artful rouge likely added to her charm. Her hoops were modest compared to modern iron contraptions; indeed, the costume, while absurd in parts, was pleasing overall. One might wonder if ladies today adopted bangles and nose rings; we would likely laugh at the ornaments rather than dislike them, and lovers would gladly lift a ring to kiss the rosy lips beneath.

As for the Baroness de Bernstein, despite her elaborate toilette, she appeared handsome yet magnificent, melancholy, and even terrifying. One reads the past in such old faces. Some lapse into mere meekness; the fire goes out of their eyes, replaced by crow's-feet that crinkle around them. They no longer flash with scorn, anger, or love. They gaze, unmelted by sapphire glances; they look, dazzling no one.

*My fair young reader,* the narrative continued, addressing the audience directly, *if you are not as perfect as the peerless Lindamira, Queen of the Ball; if, by bedtime, you admit to having only two or three partners while Lindamira danced with a crowd all night—console yourself. Think that at fifty, you will look as kind and pleasant as she appears now at eighteen. You will not have to trade your coach-and-six of beauty for another step, walking alone through the rest of life. You will not lose your accustomed homage, nor witness the depreciation of your smiles. Fashion will not forsake your quarter. You will avoid seeing your splendid saloons turn to dust, gloom, and cobwebs, with placards in your windows declaring you gaunt, lonely, and to let! You may not have known grandeur, but you will escape bankruptcy. You may not have enjoyed millions, but you will survive.*

"Our hostess," whispered Lord Chesterfield to his friend, unaware of the volume of his tone, "reminds me of Covent Garden in my youth. Then it was the heart of high fashion. Now, a nobleman's house is merely a gaming den, or you enter with a friend to order a bottle."

"Hah! A bottle and a tavern are good things in their way," Lord March shrugged. "I was born before the Georges ascended, though I intend to live to a hundred. I knew the Bernstein only as an old woman; if she ever possessed beauty, hang me if I know how she squandered it."

"No, hang me, how did she spend it?" Jack Morris laughed. "Here is a table! Shall we sit and play? Do not let the Frenchman join; he will not pay. Mr. Warrington, will you take a card?"

Lord Warrington and Lord

After spending a few days at Tunbridge Wells, our young Virginian discovered himself to be the most significant figure in that cheerful, small resort. No nobleman commanded such intense curiosity. Even the Bishop of Salisbury received less deference than Harry. As he strolled through the town, heads turned to follow him; country folk paused in the marketplace just to stare. Inside the assembly rooms, matrons eagerly ushered him toward their daughters, finding ways to leave them alone together, where smiles were plentiful. Everyone seemed to know the exact acreage of his Virginia estate down to the penny, and the precise sum of his annual income. At every tea table, his gambling winnings were discussed and tallied with mathematical precision. One wonders at the depth of neighbors' knowledge regarding one another's private affairs!

So profound was the public interest in Harry that even his servant, Gumbo, was treated with favor. People smiled upon the lad, inviting him aside for ale and cold meat solely to extract news of his master. Under this regimen, Gumbo grew fat, becoming a prominent member of the local valet society, and began to fabricate tales of even greater magnitude than usual. No social gathering was considered complete without Mr. Warrington's presence. The young man was neither amused nor astonished by this sudden elevation; indeed, he bore his new honors with surprising composure. Having been raised at home with a healthy sense of self-worth, his current good fortune merely reinforced his natural satisfaction. Yet, he remained unboasted. He did not trumpet his victories or affect airs of superiority. Guided by a peculiar code of honor, he felt compelled to answer every summons—whether to a racecourse, a drinking bout, or a card match—for the sake of Old Virginia. He refused to shrink from any challenge.

His new acquaintances were eager to test him. Possessed of a steady mind, a skilled hand, a secure seat, and unflinching nerves, the representative of Old Virginia acquitted himself splendidly against the representatives of the Mother Country. Madame de Bernstein, who had exhausted her nightly supply of cards (and presumably rectified the misfortunes hinted at previously), was delighted by her nephew's rising fame. He had bested Jack Morris at shooting, defeated Mr. Scamper in a riding match, and held his own remarkably well against Captain Batts at tennis, despite having never attempted the sport before. He also faced the renowned gamblers, the Earls of Chesterfield and March, earning praise from both for his coolness, gallantry, and breeding. While he lacked brilliance at the books, his handwriting was competent enough to satisfy most fashionable circles, amusing the old lady with its naive simplicity.

Madame de Bernstein, a woman who preferred the world to the library, relished humor and possessed a taste for Molière and Fielding, yet she valued a lively game of cards above any novel. With fond pleasure, she supervised Harry's attire, scouring for fine laces for his ruffles and discovering a lovely diamond brooch for his cravat. Thus, he became the favorite of all her nephews and kin. Lady Maria, it seems, was only too happy to see her brother surpassed by the lad, though her brothers likely trembled with fear and envy upon hearing of Mr. Warrington's prodigious success and his rapid ascent in their wealthy aunt's affections.

Two weeks into his stay, Harry had become quite a personage. He knew all the good company in the place, and inevitably, he became acquainted with the bad as well. Was it his fault for accepting the world as he found it, or for drinking from that sweet, sparkling cup of pleasure that had been filled to the brim for him? His aunt enjoyed his triumphs and simply urged him to continue his enjoyment. She was neither a rigid moralist nor a particularly wholesome guide for youth. Should the Cattarina have sent him love letters, I suspect Aunt Bernstein would have encouraged him to accept them. However, Harry arrived from his colonial home carrying a reserve of modesty that he retained alongside his honest homespun linen. Libertinism was unknown in the sparsely populated regions from which he hailed; the vices of great cities were rarely practiced in the rough towns of America. Consequently, Harry blushed like a maiden at the bold talk of his new European associates. Even Aunt Bernstein's worldly jests astounded him, prompting the old woman to address him as "Joseph" or "simpleton." Yet, regardless of his innocence, the world credited him with the same depravity as everyone else. How could he possibly know that associating with the saucy Cattarina was improper? He had seen my Lord March driving her in his carriage. Harry perceived no harm in offering her his arm or parading openly with her on the public walks.

When she spotted a trinket in a toy shop, Harry, whose pockets were heavy with coin, delighted to buy her the coveted locket. The next day it was lace; the next, something else. There seemed no limit to Madame Cattarina's whims, until the young gentleman gently declined further requests with a joke and a laugh. He was shrewd enough and not reckless, though generous; he had no intention of buying diamond drops for the petulant lady's ears. But who was to credit him with this modesty? Old Bernstein insisted her nephew was playing the role of Don Juan, supplanting my Lord March. Her conviction grew stronger whenever poor Maria was present, delighting in stabbing the spinster's tender heart and reveling in her niece's painful silence and confusion.

"Why, my dear," the Baroness would say, "boys will be boys, and I do not wish Harry to be the first milksop in his family!" Sometimes the bread Maria ate at her aunt's expense tasted hard and indigestible. Oh, how skillfully some women know how to make food seem bitter!

Meanwhile, Mr. Wolfe continued his courtship of the lady of his heart at Westerham. Knowing the Colonel was fully occupied, Mr. Warrington did not expect to see much of him, despite liking the officer's conversation. This was distinct from the ribald chatter surrounding Harry. Mr. Wolfe never spoke of cards, horse pedigrees, hunting exploits, conquests, or the endless scandals of the age. It was indeed a difficult time. That old world was more dissolute than ours. An old king kept mistresses openly in his train, honored by the great. The nobility was often mad and reckless in the pursuit of pleasure, marked by a looseness of speech and action that history must record faithfully, without needing to shock honest readers with particulars.

Our young gentleman had stumbled upon some of the wildest elements of this crowd and found an old relative living right in the center of the chaos. Harry failed to notice how Colonel Wolfe deliberately avoided him, or how the Colonel's demeanor grew cold and distant when they occasionally met. He knew nothing of the stories circulating about him. Who knows the stories told of others? Who creates them? Who fathers these wonderful lies? Poor Harry was unaware of the reputation he was cultivating. While he rode, played, and frolicked, he was passing among respectable people as the most abandoned, profligate, and godless young man alive.

Alas, to think that the gentle, quiet lad we admired—the one so simple and easily pleased—should be viewed as a hardened profligate, a spendthrift, a confirmed gambler, and a frequent visitor to abandoned women! These rumors reached honest Colonel Lambert at Oakhurst. First came one bad tale, then another, until they swelled into a flood, filling the good man's heart with grief and anxiety. His family noticed the disturbance. Initially, Lambert refused to speak of it, dismissing his wife's affectionate inquiries. Mrs. Lambert feared a great misfortune: ruin, a dangerous mission, illness, disgrace, or death. Who can withstand an anxious woman or escape the interrogation of the marital pillow? Eventually, Lambert was forced to reveal part of what he knew.

The wife was as grieved and amazed as her husband. From the privacy of their bedroom, where the sorrow was briefly stifled beneath the pillows, the grief descended to the lower floors. Theo and Hester inherited the complaint, suffering terribly. Oh, kind, little, wounded hearts! At first, Hester turned red, flew into a rage, clenched her fists, and vowed she would believe not a word of the wicked stories. But eventually, she succumbed. Scandal almost always masters people, especially the good and innocent. Oh, the serpent they had nurtured by their fireside! Oh, the wretched, wretched boy! To imagine him walking with that horrid painted Frenchwoman, dangling diamond necklaces around her neck, and parading his shame before all society at the Wells!

After the three ladies wept over the tale and the father was deeply moved, they confided in the parish priest. In vain he preached his favorite sermon on scandal the following Sunday, denouncing humanity's tendency to assume the worst. We repent, we promise to stop—but when the next malicious story arises concerning our neighbor, we believe it anyway. So did those kind, wretched people of Oakhurst believe everything they heard about poor Harry Warrington.

Harry, meanwhile, was far too pleased with himself to realize how poorly his friends regarded him. He pursued an idle, pleasant, albeit unprofitable life, oblivious to the hubbub he created and the terrible reputation he held in the eyes of many good men.

Exiting a tennis match with Mr. Batts, satisfied with his performance and the general atmosphere, Harry encountered Colonel Wolfe, who had just visited the lady of his heart. Harry extended his hand, which the Colonel accepted, but the greeting was so icy that the young man could not help noticing

**Chapter XXIX: In Which Harry Continues to Enjoy *Otium Sine Dignitate***

While there were plenty of card players eager to meet her at her lodgings and the assembly rooms, Madame de Bernstein chose to remain comfortably at the Wells, busy scolding her niece and engaging in rounds of rubber bridge. At Harry's age, nearly every location offers delight if it provides lively company, fresh air, and ample diversion. Indeed, all pleasures seem exquisite at twenty. One rushes forth to encounter them with alacrity, speculates eagerly on their arrival, and counts down the days until the rendezvous. Yet, how gently and coolly we view these joys toward the twilight of life's long season! Do you not recall your first ball, Madam? Does your memory not occasionally drift to that golden past while you sit adorning the walls as your daughters dance? As for myself, I can still remember thinking it delightful to walk three miles and return through the countryside merely to dine with old Captain Jones. Can you imagine enjoying such a trek today just to share a meal with Jones and sip his half-pay port? Undoubtedly, it was purchased from a local village merchant for a modest price; however, it was served with a warm welcome, and youth imbued it with a flavor that neither aged wine nor matured men can replicate now. *Viximus nuper*—we have recently existed.

I am not inclined to judge young Harry's conduct and idleness as harshly as his friend, the stern Colonel of the Twentieth Regiment. Oh, blessed idleness! Divine lazy nymph! Fetch me a novel while I lounge in my dressing gown at three in the afternoon; mix a sherry cobbler for me, and bring a cigar! You, dear slatternly, smiling enchantress! They may assail you with vile names, swear away your reputation, and label you the Mother of Evil; yet, despite this, you remain the finest company in the world!

Lord March departed for the North, and Lord Chesterfield, discovering that the Tunbridge waters failed to cure his deafness, returned to his solitude at Blackheath. However, other gentlemen remained to sport and indulge, and Mr. Warrington found sufficient companionship at his regular gathering at the White Horse. Soon, he mastered the art of ordering a French dinner as well as any fashionable gentleman from St. James's. He conversed with Monsieur Barbeau in his native tongue far more fluently than most others, cultivated an elegant and discerning palate for wines, and could distinguish between Clos Vougeot and Romande with remarkable expertise. He became the young king of the Wells, where the frequenters were easygoing men of the world who were hardly shocked by Harry's growing reputation for gallantry and extravagance—a reputation that had terrified Mr. Wolfe.

Though our Virginian lived amidst the revellers, swimming and sporting in the same waters as the "loose fish," the boy possessed a natural shrewdness and honesty that kept him clear of the snares and baits typically set for the unwary. He made very few foolish bets with the jolly idle fellows around him, and the oldest hands found it difficult to deceive him. He engaged in games indoors and out due to his innate skill and aptitude, proving capable of holding his own against almost any fair competitor. He was scrupulous, playing only with gentlemen he knew and always settling his debts immediately. While he might have fared poorly in a college examination, he possessed prudence, fidelity, keen perception, great generosity, and dauntless personal courage. There were indeed occasions to demonstrate the quality of his character.

For instance, when the unfortunate little Cattarina, who had caused him so much trouble, pushed her demands beyond the limit Harry believed his generosity should reach, he withdrew from the advances of the Opera-House siren with perfect coolness and skill, leaving her to attempt her blandishments on a more susceptible victim. In vain did the mermaid's hysterical mother wait upon Harry, vowing that a cruel bailiff had seized all her daughter's goods for debt and that her venerable father was currently languishing in a London gaol. Harry declared that between himself and the bailiff there could be no dealings. Since he had the good fortune to know Mademoiselle Cattarina and had gratified her caprices by presenting her with various trinkets and knick-knacks she fancied, he was not bound to pay her family's past debts. Furthermore, he refused to act as bail for her father in London or settle her outstanding accounts at Tunbridge.

First, the Cattarina's mother called him a monster and an ingrate; then, with a veteran smirk, she asked why he did not accept payment for the services he had rendered to the young person. Initially, Mr. Warrington could not comprehend the nature of such payment. But once the old woman clarified the matter, the honest lad rose in horror at the thought of a woman trafficking in her child's dishonor. He told her he hailed from a country where even the savages would recoil from such a bargain. Having bowed the old lady ceremoniously to the door, he ordered Gumbo to note her well and never admit her to his lodgings again. No doubt she retired breathing vengeance against the Iroquois; she declared that no Turk or Persian would treat a lady thus. She and her daughter retreated to London as soon as their anxious landlord permitted them.

Then came Harry's perils of gaming, alongside his perils of gallantry. A man who plays at bowls, as the phrase goes, must expect to encounter rubbers. After dinner at the ordinary, having declined to play piquet further with Captain Batts, and being roughly questioned for his refusal, Harry frankly told the Captain that he only played with gentlemen who paid, like himself. Yet, he expressed such readiness to settle Mr. Batts' outstanding account that the Captain declared himself satisfied *d'avance*, straightway leaving the Wells without paying Harry or any other creditor.

He also had an occasion to display his spirit by beating a chairman who had been rude to old Miss Whiffler one evening as she headed to the assembly. Moreover, finding that the calumny regarding himself and that unlucky opera dancer was repeated by Mr. Hector Buckler—one of the fiercest frequenters of the Wells—Mr. Warrington approached Buckler in the pump room. There, the latter was regaling a group of water drinkers with the very slander. Mr. Warrington publicly informed Buckler that the story was false and warned him that he would hold anyone accountable who uttered it again. Thus, though our friend, being at Rome, certainly did as Rome did, he proved himself a valorous and worthy Roman. By hurling with the wolves, he was acknowledged by Mr. Wolfe himself to be as brave as the best of them.

If that officer had revealed the stories that had caused Colonel Lambert so much pain, we may be certain that when Mr. Wolfe discovered his young friend's innocence, he took the first opportunity to withdraw the odious charges. Joy rippled through the Lamberts upon the lad's acquittal—something akin to the pleasure higher natures feel at the recovery of sinners. Never had the little family been happier—not even when they received news of Brother Tom winning his scholarship—as when Colonel Wolfe rode over with the account of his conversation with Harry Warrington.

"Hadst thou brought me a regiment, James, I think I should not have been better pleased," said Mr. Lambert. Mrs. Lambert called to her daughters in the garden, kissed them both upon their entry, and shouted the good news to them. Hetty leaped for joy, and Theo performed some unusually brilliant operations on the harpsichord that night. When Dr. Boyle arrived for his game of backgammon, he could not initially account for the illumination on all their faces until the three ladies, in a happy chorus, told him how right he had been in his sermon and how dreadfully they had wronged that poor, dear, good young Mr. Warrington.

"What shall we do, my dear?" asked the Colonel of his wife. "The hay is in, the corn won't be cut for a fortnight—the horses have nothing to do. Suppose we..." Here, he leaned over the table and whispered in her ear.

"My dearest Martin! The very thing!" cried Mrs. Lambert, taking her husband's hand and pressing it.

"What's the very thing, mother?" cried young Charley, who was home for his Bartholomew tide holidays.

"The very thing is to go to supper. Come, Doctor! We will have a bottle of wine tonight and drink repentance to all who think evil."

"Amen," said the Doctor, "with all my heart!" And with this, the worthy family proceeded to their supper.

One afternoon, Mr. Warrington repaired to his usual dinner at the White Horse ordinary. To his delight, he spotted the jovial, handsome face of Parson Sampson seated among the diners. The clergyman was currently regaling the company with tales and witty remarks that drew roaring laughter from the crowd. Although Harry had not been in London for several months, the parson possessed the latest gossip—what passed for news among the townsfolk. He spoke of the intrigues at the King's house in Kensington and the Duke's residence in Pall Mall; of Mr. Byng's behavior in prison and his visitors; of the odds at Newmarket and the current favorite toast in Covent Garden.

Mr. Sampson, a jolly chaplain, offered information on every conceivable topic. While the accuracy of his reports might be questionable, the content was perfect for the country gentlemen listening. If Lord Viscount Squanderfield was ruining himself for Mrs. Polly, Sampson might call her Mrs. Lucy; if Lady Jane loved the actor rather than Lady Mary, or if Harry Hilton of the Horse Grenadiers fought Chevalier Solingen instead of Tommy Ruffler of the Foot Guards, it mattered little. Names and dates were secondary to the liveliness and wickedness of the stories. Mr. Sampson laughed and chatted endlessly, charming the assembly with his spirit and conversation, draining bottle after bottle while his delighted audience insisted on paying for them.

A century prior, the "Abbe Parson"—the clergyman frequenting theaters, taverns, racecourses, and high society—was a familiar figure in English life. His voice could be heard loudest in the hunting field; he could sing the catchiest songs at the Rose or the Bedford Head after a performance at Covent Garden, and call a bet as well as anyone at a gaming table. Whether modesty or claret caused his reverend face to flush a deeper crimson, when he saw Mr. Warrington enter, he leaned toward the laughing country squire beside him—a man in a drab coat and gold-laced red waistcoat—and whispered, *"Maxima debetur."* Rising abruptly, the chaplain nearly stumbled forward in his haste to greet the Virginian.

"My dear sir, my very dear sir, my conqueror of spades, clubs, and hearts!" cried the chaplain. "I am delighted to see your honor looking so fresh and well."

Harry returned the greeting with genuine pleasure, equally happy to see Mr. Sampson and to compliment the clergyman on his cheerful countenance and rosy cheeks. The squire in the drab coat recognized Mr. Warrington immediately, making space beside himself and urging the parson to resume his seat and continue his tale of Lord Ogle and the grocer's wife. Before he could finish the location, however, a shout and an oath erupted, directed at the parson for stepping on a gouty toe.

Apologizing hastily, the chaplain turned to Mr. Warrington and announced, to the amusement of the entire company, that Lord Castlewood sent his affectionate regards to his cousin and had specifically ordered Mr. Sampson to travel to Tunbridge Wells to oversee the young gentleman's morals. He reported that Lady Viscountess and Lady Fanny had departed for Harrogate, that Mr. Will had won his money at Newmarket and was visiting the Duke, and that Molly, the housemaid, was weeping over Gumbo, Mr. Warrington's valet. In short, he delivered all the news from Castlewood and its vicinity.

As Mr. Sampson boasted that all of Hampshire had heard of Harry's successes at Tunbridge, the guests began to drift away, leaving the young Virginian alone with his wine and the chaplain. "Though I have had plenty," declared the jolly priest, "that is no reason why I should not have plenty more." He proceeded to drink toast after toast and bumper after bumper, much to Harry's amusement, who always enjoyed his company. By the time Sampson had satisfied his appetite, Harry had become notably generous, warm-hearted, and friendly.

"Why should Mr. Sampson incur the expense of an inn when there is a room at Harry's quarters?" Harry asked. The chaplain's trunk was dispatched to his lodgings, and Gumbo was instructed to ensure his comfort. Nothing would satisfy Mr. Warrington except that Sampson visit the stables to see his horses. Harry now owned several fine mounts. When Sampson arrived, he recognized his own horse—the one Harry had recently won from him. The animal whinnied with delight, rubbing its nose against his old master's coat. Harry exclaimed energetically, vowing by Jupiter that Sampson should have his horse back. He gave it to the chaplain as a gift, an offer the priest accepted by seizing Harry's hand, blessing him, throwing his arms around the horse's neck, and weeping tears of Bordeaux wine and gratitude.

Arm in arm, the friends walked to Madame Bernstein's, carrying the scents of the stables into her ladyship's apartment. Their flushed cheeks and brightened eyes testified to the day's amusement. Many gentlemen's cheeks naturally flushed in those days for similar reasons. Madame Bernstein received her nephew's chaplain kindly. She relished Sampson's broad jokes and rapid chatter, much as she enjoyed a highly spiced dish or a novel entree from her cook, provided it appeared only twice or thrice. The only activity she never grew tired of, she confessed, was cards.

"The cards don't cheat," she often remarked. "A bad hand tells you the truth to your face; and there is nothing so flattering in the world as a good suit of trumps." When in a good humor, sitting down to her favorite pastime, she would laughingly bid her nephew's chaplain say grace before the meal.

At first, honest Sampson hesitated to join the game at Tunbridge Wells. Her ladyship's stakes were too high, he admitted with a comical, pitiful slap to his pocket, noting that his funds had already been transferred to the fortunate youth at Castlewood. Like most of her generation, Madame Bernstein was not prodigal with money. Presumably, it was from Harry Warrington, whose heart overflowed with generosity as much as his purse with guineas, that the chaplain secured a small sum of ready coin, enabling him soon to sit at the card table.

Our young gentleman welcomed Mr. Sampson to his coins as readily as he shared all the other good things he had gathered. It was remarkable how quickly the young Virginian adapted to the customs of the people among whom he lived. His suits remained black, yet of the finest cut and quality. "With a star and ribbon, stockings pulled down, and hair flowing over his shoulders, he would make a pretty Hamlet," observed the gay old Duchess Queensberry. "And I make no doubt he has been the death of a dozen Ophelias already, here and amongst the Indians," she added, thinking no less of Harry for his alleged success with the fair sex.

Harry's lace and linen were as exquisite as his aunt could desire. He purchased fine shaving plates from toy-shop women and a pair of magnificent brocade bedgowns in which he lounged at ease, sipping his morning chocolate. He owned swords, walking canes, French watches with painted backs and diamond settings, and snuffboxes enamelled by artists of that cunning nation. A levee of grooms, jockeys, and tradesmen waited daily in his anteroom, admitted one by one to him and Parson Sampson over their chocolate by Gumbo, the groom of the chambers. We have no record of exactly how many men Mr. Gumbo now served, but it is certain that no single Negro could have managed all the fine possessions Mr. Warrington now held, let alone the horses and the postchaise his honor had purchased.

Furthermore, Harry instructed himself in the arts proper to a gentleman of the era. A French fencing-master and a dancing-master of the same nationality resided at Tunbridge during that season. The young Virginian attended their classes assiduously, acquiring considerable skill and grace in both peaceful and martial accomplishments. Within weeks, he could handle foils against his master or any frequent visitor of the fencing school. With a sigh, Lady Maria—who danced very elegantly herself—confessed that no gentleman at court could perform a minuet more gracefully than Mr. Warrington.

Regarding riding, although Mr. Warrington took a few lessons on a large horse from a master who visited Tunbridge, he declared that their native Virginian style was sufficient. He claimed to see no one among the fine folk and jockeys who could ride better than his friend, Colonel George Washington of Mount Vernon.

The obsequious Sampson found himself in better quarters than he had known for a long time. He knew much of the world and told even more, and Harry was delighted with his stories, whether true or fabricated. The man of twenty looked up to the man of thirty, admiring the latter's old jokes, stale puns, and tarnished anecdotes, seasoned with the wine of a hundred dinner tables. Sampson's urban and collegiate witticisms were all new and charming to the young Virginian. A hundred years ago—no doubt such people do not exist today—there were grown men in London who loved to associate with fashionable youths entering life, tickling their young fancies with merry stories, acting as mentors at Covent Garden, accompanying lads to the gaming table, drinking lemonade to replace Master Hopeful's Burgundy, and staggering through the streets with perfectly cool heads while young lords reeled out to beat the watch.

Of this extinct race, Mr. Samp

Those who know the truth of Harry Warrington's situation understand the reality of his connection with the unfortunate Cattarina. However, the older ladies of Tunbridge Wells held a different view; they suspected the Virginian of being as dissolute as any high-born English nobleman. Madame de Bernstein, in particular, remained deeply skeptical of her nephew's innocence. To her, it was self-evident that Harry was leading not merely a merry life, but a wicked one. Her wish shaped her belief that the lad was no better than his neighbors. Being an old Roman at heart, she preferred her nephew to emulate the ways of Rome. Consequently, she eagerly accepted every scrap of scandal regarding Mr. Warrington's "Lovelace" adventures.

While we have witnessed instances where he hosted tea and music for the company at the Wells, and displayed gallantry toward ladies of superior figure and character compared to Cattarina, Madame Bernstein eventually ceased to worry about the foolish romance that began at Castlewood. She relaxed her vigilance over Lady Maria. Many elderly people are simply too self-absorbed to maintain long-term interest in their neighbors' affairs. The Baroness had her own concerns: her social engagements, her dinners, and her rheumatic pains. Her previously lively suspicions regarding Maria and Harry drifted into slumber, replaced by a careless, unobservant watch. Perhaps she believed the danger had passed, or perhaps she no longer cared if it existed. Alternatively, the crafty Maria may have successfully cajoled, soothed, and misled the old dragon entrusted with her care. By Maria's age—or even younger—maidenhood teaches cunning, while at Madame Bernstein's stage of life, dragons lose their ferocity. Their teeth drop out, their eyes grow heavy, and their capacity for harm diminishes. Having observed several such female dragons throughout my travels, I admire the softening influence of time upon these former destroyers. Their scales soften until a knight with moderate skill can pierce them; their claws, once capable of tearing out a thousand eyes, now tap feebly against the skin; their tongues, emerging from toothless gums, spit venom that is unpleasant but hardly lethal. Watch them now, dragging their languid tails back to their caverns at dusk! How weak their powers of injury have become! How diminished their malice! How changed they are from the brisk days when their eyes flashed wicked fire, their tongues spat poison, their breath blasted reputations, and they devoured a victim daily!

Given that the virtuous folk of Oakhurst could not withstand the testimony concerning Harry's misdeeds, why should Madame Bernstein, who possessed more experience of evil in her long days than the entire Oakhurst family combined, be any less credulous? Naturally, every elderly woman in her ladyship's circle believed every tale spun about Mr. Harry Warrington's dissipation, ready to accept even greater accusations. When the little dancer returned to London, it was because heartless Harry had abandoned her. He deserted her for someone else—a name whispered confidently by society at Tunbridge Wells, a name comprising half a dozen variations discussed by women of fashion, reputation, demi-reputation, virtue, and none. All mingled in the same rooms, danced to the same fiddles, drank from the same glasses at the Wells, united in their pursuit of health, society, or pleasure. A century prior, our ancestors, whether free or strait-laced, gathered at scores of such merry venues as this current scene unfolds, dancing, frolicking, gaming, and drinking at Epsom, Bath, Tunbridge, and Harrogate, just as they do today at Homburg and Baden.

Thus, Harry's tarnished reputation comforted his old aunt immensely, easing her mind regarding the boy's passion for Lady Maria. So relieved was she that when the chaplain arrived to escort her ladyship home, Madame Bernstein showed no desire to part with her niece. Instead, she preferred keeping her under close observation, discussing her wicked young cousin's wild extravagances, whispering that "boys will be boys," and confiding her intention to secure a proper wife for Harry—someone of suitable age and fortune. Poor Maria bore these pleasantries with as much fortitude as she could muster.

During the previous century, a certain French Duke and Marquis distinguished himself across Europe and America, obliging posterity with a choice volume of memoirs. The gentle reader is specifically warned against consulting this work. Having played the role of Don Juan in his homeland, ours, and elsewhere, he meticulously recorded the names of countless court beauties who fell victim to his fascinations. While undoubtedly pleasant reading for the grandsons and descendants of the fashionable figures among whom our brilliant nobleman moved—to find their ancestresses adorning M. le Duc's sprightly pages and their frailties chronicled by such a candid writer—the content remains cautionary.

In his travels through North America, this nobleman followed his customary pattern in Europe and immediately sought romantic entanglements. It is curious to contrast the elegant refinements of European society, where, according to Monseigneur, one need only lay siege to a woman to vanquish her, with the simple lives and habits of the colonial populace. Among these Americans, this European enslaver of hearts apparently failed to make a single conquest. Had he succeeded, he surely would have narrated his victories in Pennsylvania and New England with the same pride he described his triumphs in France and his own country. Travelers in America have loudly criticized the rudeness and barbarism of transatlantic manners; yet, let the present writer offer humble testimony that the conversation of American gentlemen is generally modest, and, to the best of his belief, the lives of American women are pure.

We have noted that Mr. Harry Warrington brought this colonial modesty to the Old Country. Though he could not avoid overhearing the free talk of the pleasure-seeking individuals around him, he remained largely silent amidst their chatter. He never indulged in double entendres in conversation with women, boasted of no victories over the sex, and appeared shy and awkward when hearing such tales recounted by others.

Mr. Sampson had observed this youthful modesty during his time at Castlewood, where Mr. Warrington often seemed uneasy while cousin Will told his choice stories. My Lord had curtly rebuked his brother, instructing him to reserve his jokes for the usher's table at Kensington and avoid offending their kinsman. Hence, the chaplain's exclamation of *"Reverentia pueris"* (Reverence for children) addressed to his neighbor at the ordinary on Harry's first appearance. Mr. Sampson, lacking the strength to do right himself, at least possessed the grace not to offend innocent young gentlemen with his cynicism.

The chaplain was deeply touched by Harry's gift of the horse and felt a genuine friendship toward the lad. "You see, sir," he remarked, "I am of the world and must act as the rest do. I have led a rough life, Mr. Warrington, and cannot afford to be more particular than my neighbors. *Video meliora, deteriora sequor* ('I see better things, but follow worse'), as we said at college. I have a little sister at boarding school nearby, and since I keep a decent tongue in my head when speaking with her, Patty, and expect others to do the same, I feel I may try to do as much for you."

The chaplain lavished praise upon Harry before his aunt, the old Baroness, who delighted in hearing him commended. She loved him dearly, pleased by his good looks, manly bearing, ready blushing, bright eyes, and deep youthful voice. His combination of shrewdness and simplicity constantly amused her; she would have grown weary of him long ago had he been clever, learned, or witty. "We must find a good wife for him, Chaplain," she told Mr. Sampson. "I have one or two in mind who seem suitable. We must establish him here; he will never bear returning to his savages or living with his little Methodist mother."

On this point, Mr. Sampson was equally anxious and had a wife in mind for Harry. Presumably, he had conversed with My Lord at Castlewood regarding the Earl's intention to provide a living or provision for the chaplain should he facilitate a marriage for Lady Maria. If his good offices could help that anxious lady find a husband, Sampson was eager to employ them, waiting to determine the most effective way to wield his influence.

Sampson's company was delightful, and within a few hours, he and his young friend became intimate. The parson rejoiced in high spirits, a hearty appetite, and good humor. He pretended to no squeamishness and avoided sanctimonious hypocrisy, yet he carefully refrained from shocking his young friend with unnecessary levity or immoral talk. Initiating his pupil, perhaps out of policy or compunction, he revealed only the minor mysteries of life, withholding the darker secrets with which the unlucky adept himself was all too familiar. With Harry, Sampson was merely a brisk, lively, jolly companion, ready for drinking bouts, sports, cockfights, shooting matches, card games, or gallops across the common. Yet his conversation remained decent, aiming more to amuse than to lead astray. The chaplain was remarkably successful; possessing immense animal spirits, natural wit, aptitude, and experience in the trade of "toad-eating"—his calling since entering college as a servitor—he navigated the path to fortune with ease.

To clarify a previous remark: there are still many men of Sampson's profession today, and even little boys at public schools are apprenticed to this craft early on. However, the flattery is no longer so overt

Our pleasant task now is to reveal the secret Mr. Lambert whispered into his wife's ear at the close of the previous chapter—a revelation that brought such delight to the entire Oakhurst household. With the hay harvested and the corn still green, the farm horses were free. "Why," asked Colonel Lambert, "should we not harness them to the coach? Let us all pay a visit to Tunbridge Wells, stopping at Westerham to join our friend Wolfe on the way?"

Mamma embraced the proposal with evident enthusiasm, and one dares say the honest gentleman who devised it felt equally pleased. The children leaped for joy. Instantly, the girls scattered to gather their finest attire: calamancoes, paduasoys, falbalas, furbelows, capes, cardinals, sacks, negligees, solitaires, caps, ribbons, mantuas, clocked stockings, and high-heeled shoes, along with countless other articles of toilet. Mamma's best robes were summoned from the presses, garments reserved solely for rare, solemn occasions, retreating afterward to lavender and silence. The brave Colonel produced his laced hat, waistcoat, and silver-hilted hanger. Charley rejoiced in a *rasee* holiday suit belonging to his father—the very coat in which the Colonel had been married—which Mrs. Lambert cut up, not without a pang of regret. Even the dogs were dressed; Ball and Dumpling had their tails and manes tied with ribbon. Chump, the old white cart-horse, was appointed unicorn leader to assist the carriage-horses up the first five hilly miles from Oakhurst to Westerham.

The carriage itself was an ancient vehicle, rumored to have served in the procession bringing George I. from Greenwich to London upon his first assumption of sovereignty. It had belonged to Mr. Lambert's father, and the family had regarded it, ever since memory served, as one of the most splendid coaches in the three kingdoms. Brian, coachman—and, must it be confessed?—ploughman of the Oakhurst family, took his place on the box beside Mr. Charley. Their precious clothes were packed in imperials atop the roof. The Colonel's pistols were secured in the carriage pockets, while the blunderbuss hung behind the box within easy reach of Brian, an old soldier.

Fortunately, no highwayman molested the convoy, nor did any innkeeper levy contributions on Colonel Lambert, whose slender purse and large family rendered him immune to such depredators on the King's Highway. A reasonably cheap, modest lodging had been arranged by young Colonel Wolfe at his usual haunt, whither he rode ahead to meet them. These lodgings lay directly opposite Madame Bernstein's establishment. As the Oakhurst family arrived on a Saturday evening, they witnessed chair after chair discharging powdered beaux and patched, brocaded beauties at the Baroness's door, where she hosted one of her numerous card parties. The sun had not yet set—for our ancestors began their dissipation at early hours, indulging in meat, drink, or cards anytime after three in the afternoon until late at night or early morning. From their window, the young country ladies and their mother observed the various personages entering the Bernstein rout.

Colonel Wolfe identified the guests for the ladies. It was almost as delightful as attending the party themselves, Hetty and Theo decided, for they could not only see the arrivals but also gaze through the Baroness's open casements to watch them inside. Among the few figures they recognized, the passing of the Duchess of Queensberry prompted Martin Lambert to recite a score of lines from his favorite Mat Prior regarding "Kitty, beautiful and young."

"Think that old lady was once like you, girls!" cried the Colonel.

"Like us, Papa? Well, certainly, we never set ourselves up for being beauties!" replied Miss Hetty, tossing her little head.

"Yes, like you, you little baggage! Like you at this very moment, wishing to go to that drum yonder:—'Inflamed with rage at sad restraint / Which wise mamma ordained, / And sorely vexed to play the saint / Whilst wit and beauty reigned.'"

"We were never invited, Papa," said the satirical daughter. "And I am sure if there is no beauty more worth seeing than that, the wit cannot be much worth the hearing."

"Oh, but he is a rare poet, Mat Prior!" continued the Colonel. "Though, mind you, girls, you will skip over all the poems I have marked with a cross. A rare poet! To think you should see one of his heroines! 'Fondness prevailed, mamma gave way' (she always will, Mrs. Lambert!)—'Fondness prevailed, mamma gave way, Kitty at heart's desire / Obtained the chariot for a day, / And set the world on fire!'"

"I am sure it must have been very inflammable," remarked Mamma.

"So it was, my dear, twenty years ago; much more inflammable than it is now," noted the Colonel.

"Nonsense, Mr. Lambert," answered Mamma.

"Look, look!" cried Hetty, running forward to point to the little square and covered gallery where the door to Madame Bernstein's apartments stood, surrounded by a crowd of street urchins, idlers, and yokels watching the company.

"It's Harry Warrington!" exclaimed Theo, waving a handkerchief toward the young Virginian. However, Warrington did not see Miss Lambert. He walked arm-in-arm with a portly clergyman in a crisp, rustling silk gown, and the two entered Madame de Bernstein's door.

"I heard him preach a most admirable sermon here last Sunday," said Mr. Wolfe. "A little theatrical, but most striking and eloquent."

"You seem to be here most Sundays, James," said Mrs. Lambert.

"And Monday, and soon till Saturday," added the Colonel.

"See, Harry has beautified himself already; his hair is in a buckle, and I have no doubt he is going to the drum too."

"I had rather sit quietly generally on a Saturday evening," said the sober Mr. Wolfe, "at any rate, away from card-playing and scandal; but I own, dear Mrs. Lambert, I am under orders. Shall I go across and send Mr. Warrington to you?"

"No, let him have his sport. We shall see him tomorrow. He won't care to be disturbed amidst his fine folks by us country-people," said meek Mrs. Lambert.

"I am glad he is with a clergyman who preaches so well," said Theo softly, her eyes suggesting, *You see, good people, he is not so bad as you thought him, and as I, for my part, never believed him to be.*

"The clergyman has a very kind, handsome face."

"Here comes a greater clergyman," cried Mr. Wolfe. "It is my Lord of Salisbury, with his blue ribbon, and a chaplain behind him."

"And whom, a mercy's name, have we here?" broke in Mrs. Lambert as a sedan-chair, covered in gilding and topped with no fewer than five earl's coronets, approached the door. Carried by bearers in richly laced clothes and preceded by three footmen in matching splendid liveries, it halted before the Baroness's entrance. The Bishop, who had been about to enter, stopped and ran back with the most respectful bows and curtsies to the sedan-chair, offering his hand to the lady who stepped forth.

"Who on earth is this?" asked Mrs. Lambert.

"Sprechen Sie Deutsch? Ja, mein Herr. Nichts verstanden," said the waggish Colonel.

"Pooh, Martin."

"Well, if you cannot understand High Dutch, my love, how can I help it? Your education was neglected at school. Can you understand heraldry?—I know you can."

"I make," cried Charley, reciting the shield, "three merions on a field or, with an earl's coronet."

"A countess's coronet, my son. The Countess of Yarmouth, my son."

"And pray, who is she?"

"It hath ever been the custom of our sovereigns to advance persons of distinction to honor," continued the Colonel, gravely. "And this eminent lady hath been so promoted by our gracious monarch to the rank of Countess of this kingdom."

"But why, Papa?" asked the daughters together.

"Never mind, girls!" said Mamma.

But that incorrigible Colonel would go on. "Y, my children, is one of the last and most awkward letters of the whole alphabet. When I tell you stories, you are always saying 'Why.' Why should my Lord Bishop be cringing to that lady? Look at him rubbing his fat hands together and smiling into her face! It is not a handsome face anymore. It is all painted red and white like Scaramouch's in the pantomime. See, there comes another blue-ribbon, as I live. My Lord Bamborough. The descendant of the Hotspurs. The proudest man in England. He stops, he bows, he smiles; he is hat-in-hand, too. See, she taps him with her fan. Get away, you crowd of little blackguard boys, and don't tread on the robe of the lady whom the King delights to honor."

"But why does the King honor her?" asked the girls once more.

"There goes that odious last letter but one! Did you ever hear of Her Grace the Duchess of Kendal? No. Of the Duchess of Portsmouth? Non plus. Of the Duchess of La Vall

Colonel Lambert's initial reaction to discovering the secret hidden within his youngest daughter's heart was anger directed squarely at the young man responsible. "A plague upon all scapegraces, whether English or Indian!" he bellowed to his wife. "I wish this fellow had broken his nose against every doorpost in the house except our own!"

"Perhaps we are destined to cure him of being a scapegrace, my dear," Mrs. Lambert interposed gently. "And that fall at our doorstep holds something providential. You laughed at me, Mr. Lambert, when I suggested this before; yet if Heaven did not send this young gentleman to us, who did? It may well be for the blessing and happiness of us all that he arrived."

"It's hard, Molly!" the Colonel groaned. "We cherish and fondle them, rear them through sickness and health, toil and scheme, and hoard money in stockings while patching our own old coats. If they have a headache, we cannot sleep; if they have a whim, we work day and night to grant it. They are darling daddy and dearest pappy, and whose father is like ours? Then, on Tuesday morning, I am king of my household. But on Tuesday evening, Prince Whippersnapper appears, and my reign ends. A whole life is forgotten and forsaken for a pair of blue eyes, a pair of lean legs, and a head of yellow hair."

"'Tis written that we women must leave everything to follow our husbands," the matron said, placing her hand on her husband's arm. "I think our courtship was not very long, dear Martin!"

"'Tis human nature, and what can you expect of the jade?" the Colonel sighed.

"And I believe I fulfilled my duty to my husband, though I admit I left my father for him," Mrs. Lambert added softly.

"Excellent wench! Perdition catch my soul, but I do love thee, Molly!" the good Colonel declared. "But then, mind you, your father never loved me; and if I ever have sons-in-law—"

"Ever, indeed! Of course my girls are to have husbands, Mr. Lambert!" cried the mother.

"Well, when they come, I'll hate them, madam, just as your father hated me; and quite rightly, for taking his treasure away from him."

"Don't be irreligious and unnatural, Martin Lambert! I say you are unnatural, sir!" the matron continued.

"Nay, my dear, I have an old tooth in my left jaw here," the Colonel replied, tapping his chin. "'Tis natural for the tooth to come out. But when the tooth-drawer pulls it, 'tis natural that I should feel pain. Do you suppose, madam, that I do not love Hetty better than any tooth in my head?"

Yet no woman is ever truly averse to the idea of her daughter finding a husband, even if fathers revolt against the intrusion of the son-in-law. Mothers and grandmothers, in fact, are remarried anew in the marriages of their young ones; their souls don fresh laces and muslins reminiscent of forty years past, the postilion's white ribbons bloom once more, and they flutter into the post-chaise, driving away. What woman, regardless of age, has not stored away bridal favors and raiment, packed in lavender in the deepest corners of her heart?

"It will be a sad thing, parting with her," Mrs. Lambert continued, sighing.

"You have settled that point already, Molly," the Colonel laughed. "Had I not best go out and order raisins and currants for the wedding cake?"

"And then I shall have to leave the house in their charge when I go to her in Virginia. How many miles is it to Virginia, Martin? I should think it must be thousands."

"A hundred and seventy-three thousand three hundred and ninety-one and three-quarters, my dear, by the near way—that through Prester John's country. By the other route, through Persia—"

"Oh, give me the one with the least amount of sea, and your horrid ships, which I simply cannot bear!" cried the Colonel's spouse.

"I hope Rachel Esmond and I shall become better friends. She had a very high spirit when we were girls at school."

"Shall we not attend to the baby linen, Mrs. Martin Lambert?" her husband interjected, sensing a shift in mood.

Mrs. Lambert likely assumed there was no cause for wonderment, having recently admired some lovely lace caps and bibs in Mrs. Bobbinit's toy shop. That Sunday afternoon, when the discovery was made, while little Hetty lay upon her pillow with feverish cheeks, closed eyes, and a pitiful expression, her mother regarded the child with remarkable equanimity, seeming almost pleased by Hetty's distress.

The girl was not merely unhappy; she was enraged with herself for having revealed her secret. Perhaps she had not fully understood her own state until the sudden emotion clarified it. Now, the little maid behaved as if she had committed a grave wrongdoing and been caught red-handed. Indignant at her own weakness, she erupted in transports of self-loathing, vowing never to forgive herself for such humiliation. Like the wounded young stag chafing in rage against the hunter's arrow, she bit and snarled at her sister-cubs and the leopardess, her spotted mother. Little Hetty tore and gnawed and growled so ferociously that I would not wish to be her sibling, her mother, or her father.

"What business has any young woman," she cried aloud, "to indulge in such nonsense? Mamma, I ought to be whipped and sent to bed. I know perfectly well that Mr. Warrington does not care a fig about me. I daresay he prefers French actresses and the commonest little milliner-girl in the toy shop to me. And so he ought, and so they are better than me. Why, what a fool I am to burst into tears like a ninny about nothing, simply because Mr. Wolfe said Harry plays cards on Sundays! I know he is not clever like Papa. I believe he is stupid—I am certain he is stupid—but he is not so stupid as I am. Why, of course, I cannot marry him. How am I to go to America and leave you and Theo? Of course, he likes someone else, perhaps in America, or Tunbridge, or Jericho, or somewhere. He is a prince in his own country and cannot possibly think of marrying a poor half-pay officer's daughter with only twopence to her fortune. Did you not tell me, when I was a baby, that I cried wanting the moon? I am a baby now, a most absurd, silly little baby—do not talk to me, Mrs. Lambert, I am one. Only, there is this to be said: he knows nothing about it, and I would rather cut out my tongue than tell him."

Dire were the threats Hetty leveled against Theo, should her sister betray her. As for the infant Charley, whose mind was entirely fixed on cheese-cakes, he remained oblivious to Miss Hester's turmoil. Naturally, the parents and the kind sister promised to keep the little maid's secret safe.

"I begin to think it had been best for us to stay at home," sighed Mrs. Lambert to her husband.

"Nay, my dear," the other replied. "Human nature will always be human nature. Surely Hetty's mother told me herself that she had a slight liking for a certain young curate before falling head-over-heels in love with a certain young officer of Kingsley's. And as for me, my heart was wounded in a dozen places before Miss Molly Benson took entire possession of it. Our sons and daughters must follow in the footsteps of their parents, I suppose. Indeed, yesterday you were scolding me for grumbling at Miss Hetty's precocious fancies. To do the child justice, she disguises her feelings entirely, and I defy Mr. Warrington to guess from her behavior how she feels toward him."

"A daughter of mine and yours, Martin," the mother cried with great dignity, "is not going to fling herself at a gentleman's head!"

"Neither herself nor the teacup, my dear," answered the Colonel.

"Little Miss Het treats Mr. Warrington like a vixen. He never visits us without her boxing his ears in one fashion or another. I protest she is barely civil to him; but, knowing what is passing in the young hypocrite's mind, I am not going to be angry at her rudeness."

"She needs no reason to be rude, Martin; and our girl is good enough for any gentleman in England or America. Why, if their ages suit, shouldn't they marry after all, sir?"

"Why, if he wants her, shouldn't he ask her, my dear? I am sorry we came. I intend to put the horses into the carriage and turn their heads toward home immediately."

"But mamma fondly insisted, 'Depend on it, my dear, that these matters are wisely ordained for us. Depend upon it, Martin, it was not for nothing that Harry Warrington was brought to our gate in that manner; and that he and our children are thus brought together again. If that marriage has been decreed in Heaven, a marriage it will be.'"

"At what age, Molly, I wonder, do women begin and cease matchmaking? If our little chit falls in love and then falls out again, she will not be the first of her sex, Mrs. Lambert. I wish we were on our way home again, and

Generous with his easily acquired wealth, hospitable, and cordial to all, our young Virginian felt bound by the customs of a man of fashion to treat his country friends to an entertainment at the Assembly Rooms. According to the tradition of the day, he invited nearly the entire remaining company gathered at the Wells.

Card tables were arranged in one apartment for those who, as was common across European society, could not endure an evening without pastime. Another chamber was prepared for supper, featuring champagne in profusion and bowls of negus. The large assembly room was reserved for the dance, enjoyed by Harry Warrington's guests in the homely fashion of our ancestors. I cannot imagine the amusement was particularly lively. Minuets were called first, two or three of which were performed by various couples. The spinsters of the highest rank withdrew for the minuet, and since Lady Maria Esmond, being an earl's daughter, held the position of highest rank present—save for the lame Lady Augusta Crutchley—Mr. Warrington danced the first minuet with his cousin. He acquitted himself to the satisfaction of the entire room, moving far more elegantly than Mr. Wolfe, who stood up with Miss Lowther.

Having finished with Lady Maria, Mr. Warrington requested the honor of Miss Theo for the next minuet. Accordingly, Miss Theo, blushing and looking radiant, performed her exercise to the great delight of her parents and the sheer fury of Miss Humpleby, Sir John Humpleby's daughter of Liphook, who had expected, at the very least, to follow Lady Maria.

Following the minuets came the country dances, accompanied by a harp, fiddle, and flageolet perched in a small balcony. They thrummed through the evening with rather feeble and melancholy tunes. If one takes up an old book of music today and plays these airs, one wonders how people ever found them anything but sad. Yet, they loved, frisked, laughed, and courted to this somber accompaniment. Scarce one of the airs lacks an *amari aliquid*, a tang of sadness. Perhaps it is because they are old and defunct; their plaintive echoes call out to us from the limbo of the past, to which they have been consigned for this century. Perhaps they were gay while alive, and our descendants, hearing the works of certain modern masters, will exclaim, "Bon Dieu, is this the music which amused our forefathers?"

Mr. Warrington had the distinction of a duchess's company at his tea—the Duchess of Queensberry, the heroine of Colonel Lambert's and Mr. Prior's affections. Though the Duchess carefully turned her back upon a countess present, laughed loudly, glanced over her shoulder, and pointed at her with her fan, almost the entire company pushed, bowed, cringed, smiled, and backed away from this countess, paying scant attention to Her Grace of Queensberry, her jokes, her fan, and her airs. This countess was none other than the Countess of Yarmouth-Walmoden, the lady whom His Majesty George II, King of Great Britain, France, and Ireland, Defender of the Faith, delighted to honor.

She had met Harry Warrington in the walks that morning and had been exceedingly gracious to the young Virginian. She had promised a game of cards that night. Purblind old Colonel Blinkinsop, mistaking the invitation for himself, had made the profoundest bow.

"Pooh! Pooh!" declared the Countess of England and Hanover. "I do not mean you. I mean the young Firshinian!"

Everybody congratulated the youth on his good fortune. At night, to demonstrate their loyalty, the world thronged around Lady Yarmouth. My Lord Bamborough was eager to make her a partner at quadrille. Lady Blanche Pendragon, that model of virtue; Sir Lancelot Quintain, that pattern of knighthood; Mr. Dean of Ealing, that exemplary divine; along with numerous gentlemen, noblemen, generals, colonels, matrons, and spinsters of the highest rank, watched for a smile from her or jumped up to join her card table. Lady Maria waited upon her with meek respect, and Madame de Bernstein treated the Hanoverian lady with profound gravity and courtesy.

Harry's bow had been no lower than hospitality required, but Miss Hester chose to be indignant. She scarce spoke a word to her partner during their dance, and when he led her to the supper room, she was little more communicative. To enter, they had to pass Madame Walmoden's card table. Good-naturedly, she called out to her host as he passed, asking if her "breddy liddle bardner liked tanzing?"

"I thank your ladyship, I do not like tanzing, nor do I like cards," replied Miss Hester, tossing up her head. Dropping a curtsey like a cheese, she strutted away from the Countess's table.

Mr. Warrington was deeply offended. Sarcasm from the young toward the old pained him; flippancy toward himself hurt him. Courteous in his simple way to everyone he met, he expected similar politeness in return. Hetty perfectly understood the offense she caused; she could see the displeasure reddening on her partner's honest face with a sidelong glance, yet she wore her most ingenuous smile. As she approached the sideboard where refreshments were set, she artlessly said, "What a horrid, vulgar old woman that is; don't you think so?"

"What woman?" asked the young man.

"That German woman—my Lady Yarmouth—to whom all the men are bowing and cringing."

"Her ladyship has been very kind to me," Harry said grimly. "Won't you have some of this custard?"

"And you have been bowing to her, too! You look as if your negus was not nice," Miss Hetty continued harmlessly.

"It is not very good negus," Harry admitted with a gulp.

"And the custard is bad too! I declare 'tis made with bad eggs!" cried Miss Lambert.

"I wish, Hester, that the entertainment and the company had been better to your liking," said poor Harry.

"'Tis very unfortunate; but I dare say you could not help it," cried the young woman, tossing her little curly head.

Mr. Warrington groaned in spirit, perhaps in body, clenching his fists and teeth. The little torturer artlessly continued, "You seem disturbed: shall we go to my mamma?"

"Yes, let us go to your mamma," cried Mr. Warrington, with glaring eyes and shouting, "Curse you, why are you always standing in the way?" at an unlucky waiter.

"La! Is that the way you speak in Virginia?" asked Miss Pertness.

"We are rough there sometimes, madam, and can't help being disturbed," he said slowly, with a quiver in his whole frame, looking down upon her with fire flashing from his eyes.

Hetty saw nothing distinctly afterward until she reached her mother. Never had she seen Harry look so handsome or so noble.

"You look pale, child!" cried her mother, anxious, like all *pavidae matres*. "'Tis the cold—or no, I mean the heat. Thank you, Mr. Warrington." She made him a faint curtsey as Harry bowed tremendously and walked elsewhere among his guests.

He hardly knew what was happening at first, so angry was he. He was aroused by another altercation between his aunt and the Duchess of Queensberry. When the royal favorite passed the Duchess, Her Grace gave her Ladyship an awful stare out of eyes no longer bright as in the young days when they "set the world on fire." Turning round with an affected laugh to her neighbor, she shot a ceaseless fire of giggles and sneers at the jolly Hanoverian lady.

The Countess pursued her game at cards, either unaware or unwilling to know how her enemy was gibing at her. A feud of many years' date existed between their Graces of Queensberry and the family on the throne.

"How you all bow down to the idol! Don't tell me! You are as bad as the rest, my good Madame Bernstein!" the Duchess said.

"Ah, what a true Christian country this is! And how your dear first husband, the Bishop, would have liked to see such a sight!"

"Forgive me, if I fail quite to understand your Grace."

"We are both of us growing old, my good Bernstein, or perhaps we won't understand when we don't choose to. That is the way with us women, my good young Iroquois."

"Your Grace remarked that it was a Christian country," said Madame de Bernstein. "I failed to perceive the point."

"Indeed, my good creature, there is very little point in it! I meant we were such good Christians because we were so forgiving. Don't you remember reading, when you were young, or your husband the Bishop reading in the pulpit, how when a woman amongst the Jews was caught doing wrong, the Pharisees were for stoning her out of hand? Far from stoning such a woman now, look how fond we are of her! Any man in this room would go round it on his knees if yonder woman bade him. Yes, Madame Walmoden, you may look up from your cards with your great painted face and frown with your great

Our worthy Colonel, undoubtedly in consultation with his equally virtuous spouse, had resolved to remove their little Hetty from the vicinity of the "charmer" with all possible haste. While complaints of a similar nature often prove curable in men through the simple remedies of absence and distance, I maintain that women are not so readily healed by the alibi treatment. Some will travel ever so far and stay away forever, yet the stubborn malady remains attached to them, defying both geography and climate. One may whip, abuse, torture, or insult them, and still these deluded creatures will persist in their fidelity. Indeed, based on my profound and extensive observations, there are few more effective methods of securing the faithfulness and admiration of our fair partners than a judicious application of mild ill-treatment, a brisk dose of occasional violence as an alterative, and, for the general diet, a cooling but persistent neglect. Administer small quantities of love and kindness at sparing intervals; but avoid daily doses, for this medicine, when taken too frequently, loses its potency. Those who are most indifferent to their husbands are precisely those who have become cloyed by an excess of sugar-plums and lollipops. I have witnessed a young woman, with every desire gratified, yawn in her adoring husband's face while preferring the trivial conversation of a mere booby; conversely, I have seen Chloe—whom Strephon has thrown his bootjack at in the morning or cursed before the servants at dinner—creeping fondly to his knee at tea-time, playing his favorite tunes, and looking around proudly when the butler enters, as if to say, "Now, John, observe how good my dearest Henry is!"

Make your choice, gentlemen! There is the path of coaxing and fondling when one is henpecked and bored out of existence; and there is the manly, selfish, and effectual system where she responds to the whistle, comes in at "Down Charge," knows her master, frisks about him, nuzzles his knees, and "licks the hand that is raised"—that hand raised to do her good, as Mr. Pope so finely observed. What did the late lamented O'Connell remark, over whom a grateful nation has erected such a magnificent monument? "Hereditary bondsmen, know ye not, who would be free, themselves must strike the blow?" Naturally, this applies to both political and domestic spheres. So raise your cudgels, my enslaved and injured boys! Women will appreciate these remarks due to their taste for humor and understanding of irony.
