I should not be surprised if young Grubstreet, who writes for three-penny papers and chronicles the conversations of gentlemen at his clubs, declares, "I told you so! He advocates thrashing women! He lacks nobility of soul! He has no heart!" Nor do I, my eminent young Grubstreet! any more than you have ears. Ladies, please accept my assurance that I am merely joking; I do not advocate beating your sex at all. Since you cannot comprehend the simplest jest, allow me to state plainly that I consider your sex a hundred times more loving and faithful than ours.

So, what purpose is served by Hetty's parents taking her home if the little maid intends to be just as fond of Harry in his absence as in his presence? Why not let her see him before Ball and Dobbin depart, saying, "Good-bye, Harry! I was wilful and fractious last night, and you were kind; but good-bye, Harry!" She would show no special emotion, too ashamed of her secret to betray it. Harry, too preoccupied to notice, would not detect the grief lurking behind Hetty's glances or hidden beneath the artifice of her innocent smiles. He, perhaps, harbors cares of his own. He would part from her calmly, assuming she is happy to return to her music, poultry, and flower garden. He did not even ride partway home beside his friend's carriage. Having other engagements that afternoon, when he returned, the good Lamberts were gone from Tunbridge Wells. Their windows stood open, and a card indicated the apartments were once more to let. A fleeting sorrow at the blank appearance of rooms recently enlivened by such frank and friendly countenances might cross the young gentleman's mind; but he dined at the White Horse at four, ate his meal, and demanded his bottle fiercely.

Poor little Hester would choke over her tea at roughly the same hour when the Lamberts arrived to sleep at their friends' house in Westerham. By morning, the young roses would be wan in her cheeks, and black circles would surround her eyes. It was the thunder; the night was hot; she could not sleep. She would recover by the next day. And so they came home. There was the gate where he had fallen; the bed he had lain in; the chair in which he used to sit. What ages seemed to have passed! What a gulf separated today from yesterday! Who was that little child calling chickens or watering roses yonder? Was she and that girl the same Hester Lambert? Why, she appeared so much older than Theo now—Theo, who had always been so composed, clever, and prematurely aged. Yet in a night or two, Hester had lived—oh, long, long years! So had many others; and neither poppy nor mandrake could medicate them to the sweet sleep they had tasted the previous evening.

Maria Esmond watched the Lambert cavalcade drive away and felt a grim relief. She looked with hot eyes at Harry when he entered his aunt's card-room, flushed with Barbeau's fine wine. Laughing and rattling in response to his aunt's query about which girl was his sweetheart, he gaily declared he loved them both like sisters. He had never seen a better gentleman or finer people than the Lamberts. "Why isn't Lambert a general?" he remarked. "He has been a most distinguished officer; His Royal Highness the Duke is very fond of him." Madame Bernstein noted that Harry must make interest with Lady Yarmouth for his protégé. *"Elle ravvole de fous, cher bedid anche!"* Madame Bernstein exclaimed, mimicking the Countess's German accent. The Baroness was delighted with her son's success. "You carry off the hearts of all the old women, don't you, Maria?" she asked, sneering at her niece, who quivered under the sting. "You were quite right, my dear, not to perceive that she cheated at cards, and you play like a grand seigneur," continued Madame de Bernstein. "Did she cheat?" cried Harry, astonished. "I am sure, ma'am, I saw no unfair play." "No more did I, my dear, but I am sure she cheated. Bah! Every woman cheats, I and Maria included, when we can get a chance. But when you play with the Walmodens, you do no wrong to lose in moderation; and many men cheat in that way. Cultivate her. She has taken a fancy to your *beaux yeux*. Why should your Excellency not be Governor of Virginia, sir? You must go and pay your respects to the Duke and His Majesty at Kensington. The Countess of Yarmouth will be your best friend at court."

"Why should you not introduce me, aunt?" asked Harry. The old lady's rouged cheek grew redder. "I am not in favor at Kensington," she replied. "I may have been once; and there are no faces so unwelcome to kings as those they wish to forget. All of us want to forget something or somebody. I dare say our ingenu here would like to wipe a sum or two off the slate. Wouldst thou not, Harry?" Harry turned red, as did Maria, and his aunt laughed one of those wicked laughs not altogether pleasant to hear. What did those guilty signals on the cheeks of her nephew and niece signify? What account was scored upon the memory of either that they desired to erase? I fear Madame Bernstein was correct, and that most people possess some ugly reckonings written on their consciences, which they are glad to be rid of. Had Maria known one of the causes of Harry's disquiet, the middle-aged spinster would have been far more uneasy.

For several days, Harry had missed a pocket-book. He remembered possessing it the day he drank so much claret at the White Horse, when Gumbo carried him to bed. He searched for it in the morning, but none of his servants had seen it. He inquired at the White Horse, but found no trace. He dared not advertise the loss and could only make very cautious inquiries. He must not have it known that the book was missing. Imagine the condition of mind Lady Maria Esmond would be in if she knew that the outpourings of her heart were in the hands of the public! The letters contained all sorts of disclosures: a hundred family secrets narrated by an artless correspondent; ample satire and abuse of persons connected with her and Mr. Warrington; expostulations regarding his attentions to other ladies; scorn, scandal, jokes, appeals, and protests of eternal fidelity—the usual farrago, dear madam, which you may remember you wrote to your Edward when engaged, before becoming Mrs. Jones. Would you like those letters read by anyone else? Do you recall what you said about the Miss Browns in two or three letters, and the unfavorable opinion you expressed of Mrs. Thompson's character? Do you remember the words used regarding Jones himself, whom you subsequently married after your engagement to Edward was broken off due to settlement disputes? Would you like Mr. J. to see those remarks? You know you wouldn't. Therefore, please withdraw the imputation you have already

Though the Baroness Bernstein had clearly suffered the worst of the skirmish detailed in the previous chapter, she displayed no trace of rancour or anger upon her next encounter with her niece.

"Of course, my Lady Maria," she began smoothly, "you cannot expect me, as Harry Warrington's close relative, to be delighted by the prospect of him marrying a woman who is nearly his mother's age and possesses no fortune whatsoever. Yet, if he chooses to pursue such a folly, the matter is entirely outside my control. Indeed, I suspect I would hardly have taken the offer of five thousand pounds seriously had I not been speaking in the heat of the moment."

"So it was already settled at Castlewood?" the Baroness pressed gently. "Had I known how far things had progressed, my dear, I might have spared some unnecessary opposition. When a pitcher is broken, what railing can possibly mend it?"

"Madam!" interrupted Maria sharply. "Please pardon me—I mean nothing against your ladyship's honor or character, which, I am sure, remain perfectly intact. Harry confirms it, and you confirm it—what more could anyone desire?"

"You have spoken with Mr. Warrington, madam?"

"And he admitted making you a promise at Castlewood; furthermore, you possess it in his own handwriting."

"Certainly I do, madam!" declared Lady Maria.

"Ah!" The elder lady did not flinch at this admission. "I must confess, however, that at first I misinterpreted the tone of your letters to him. They seemed to implicate other members of the family..."

"Who have spoken most wickedly of me and endeavored to prejudice me against my dear Mr. Warrington in every possible way. Yes, madam, I admit I wrote against them to defend my reputation."

"But surely, you are pained to think that any wretch should seize upon stories damaging to our family name and turn them into public scandal. Hence your recent unease."

"Exactly so," replied Lady Maria. "From Mr. Warrington, I could conceal nothing henceforth, and I spoke freely to him. However, that is vastly different from wishing the entire world to know the domestic disputes of a noble household."

"Upon my word, Maria, I admire you, and I have done you a grave injustice. For these—say, twenty years."

"I am exceedingly glad, madam, that you conclude by granting me justice at all," said the niece.

"When I saw you last night, opening the ball with my nephew, can you guess what occupied my thoughts, my dear?"

"I truly have no idea what the Baroness de Bernstein was thinking," Lady Maria retorted haughtily.

"I merely recalled that you once performed to that very tune with the dancing-master at Kensington, my dear!"

"Madam, that was an infamous slander."

"Which resulted in the poor dancing-master receiving a severe beating for nothing!"

"It is cruel and unkind, madam, to revive that calumny—and I shall respectfully request to decline residing any longer with anyone who repeats it," Maria continued with remarkable spirit.

"You wish to return home? I imagine you will find Tunbridge disagreeable. It will be terribly hot for you if those letters are discovered."

"There was not a single word against you in them, madam; rest assured on that point."

"So Harry said, and you have done your ladyship justice. Well, my dear, we are both weary of one another and shall be better apart for a while."

"That is precisely my own opinion," said Lady Maria, curtsying briefly. "Mr. Sampson can escort you to Castlewood. You and your maid may take a postchaise."

"We can take a postchaise, and Mr. Sampson can escort me," echoed the younger lady. "You see, madam, I act like a dutiful niece."

"Do you know, my dear, I have a notion that Sampson has obtained the letters?" the Baroness said frankly.

"I confess that such a thought has crossed my own mind."

"And you intend to go home in the chaise and coax the letters from him! Delilah! Well, they can be of no use to me, and I trust you may succeed in retrieving them. When will you depart? The sooner the better, you say? We are women of the world, Maria. We only exchange names when we are in a passion. We do not require each other's company anymore, and we part on good terms. Shall we proceed to Lady Yarmouth's? It is her night. Nothing dispels unpleasant thoughts like a change of scene after one of those nervous attacks you have suffered, and card-playing drives away gloom better than any physician."

Lady Maria agreed to attend Lady Yarmouth's gathering. She was dressed and waiting in the drawing-room before her aunt arrived. As Madame Bernstein descended the stairs, she noted that Maria's door stood ajar.

"She has the letters upon her," the old lady thought.

The pair departed for their respective entertainments in separate carriages, displaying toward one another that charming cordiality and mutual respect which women can exhibit even after, and sometimes during, the bitterest quarrels.

That night, upon returning from the Countess's assembly, Mrs. Brett, the Baroness's maid, presented herself to answer Lady Maria's summons as the lady prepared to retire. When Lady Maria rang her handbell, Mrs. Brett hesitated. Betty, her companion, was not in a fit state to appear before her mistress. Betty had been carousing with Mr. Warrington's black attendant, Lord Bamborough's valet, and several other gentlemen and ladies of similar station. The liquor, Mrs. Brett confessed with shock, had proven too much for Mrs. Betty.

"Should I undress my lady?" Mrs. Brett asked timidly.

"My lady will undress without assistance," Lady Maria replied, giving Mrs. Brett leave to withdraw.

"She has the letters hidden in her stays," Madame Bernstein surmised.

Having bid each other an amicable goodnight on the stairs, the servants retreated. The following morning, Betty received a stern scolding from the closet adjoining Lady Maria's apartment, where she lay recovering. With contrition, she acknowledged her weakness for rum-punch—a delicacy brewed by Mr. Gumbo, whose skill in mixing it was renowned. She accepted her reprimand meekly, performed her usual duties regarding her mistress's person, and retired.

Betty was one of the Castlewood maids who had been utterly captivated by Gumbo. She was a striking, blue-eyed lass upon whom Mr. Case, the Baroness's confidential man, had also cast affectionate eyes. Consequently, jealousies and even quarrels had arisen between Messrs. Gumbo and Case. This tension had caused Gumbo, naturally a peaceful soul, to become shy around the Baroness's gentlemen, the majority of whom vowed to break his bones or end his life if he persisted in his attentions to Mrs. Betty.

However, on the night of the rum-punch, although Mr. Case spotted Gumbo and Mrs. Betty whispering in the doorway amidst the cool breeze, and Gumbo would have turned pale with terror had he been capable, no one was more gracious than Mr. Case. It was he who proposed the bowl of punch, which was brewed and consumed in Mrs. Betty's room, concocted by Gumbo with exquisite skill. He complimented Gumbo on his musical talents. Though usually sober, he insisted on drinking more and more until poor Mrs. Betty was reduced to the state that occasioned her ladyship's just censure.

As for Mr. Case himself, who remained out of the house, he was so violently ill from the punch that he confined himself to his bed the entire next day. He lacked the strength to appear and wait upon his ladies until supper-time, when his mistress, in a display of good nature, rebuked him lightly, noting that it was rare for him to sin in such a manner.

"Why, Case, I could have sworn it was you I saw on horseback this morning galloping along the London road," remarked Mr. Warrington, who was dining with his relatives.

"Me! Law bless you, sir! I was in bed, and I thought my head would roll off with the ache. I ate a bite at six and drank a fair amount of small beer, but I am almost my own man again now. But that Gumbo, saving your honor's presence, I shall not taste another drop of his punch."

Thus, the honest major-domo resumed his duties among the bottles and glasses.

After the meal, Madame Bernstein was surprisingly friendly. She prescribed strong fortifying drinks for Maria to prevent the recurrence of her fainting fits, which occurred not infrequently. She urged her to consult her London physician and instructed her to send an account of her case via Harry.

"By Harry?" the lady queried.

"Yes. Harry is departing for two days on an errand for his aunt to London."

"I do not care to inform you, my dear, that it is business which will prove beneficial to him. I wish Mr. Draper to include him in my will. Furthermore, as I am traveling on a round of visits when we part, I believe, for security's sake, I shall request that Mr. Warrington carry my trinket-box in his postchaise to London, for there have been robberies lately, and I have no inclination to be halted by highwaymen."

Maria looked blank at the notion of the young gentleman's departure but hoped she might secure his escort back to Castlewood,

Mr. Warrington broke his fast while reading the *London Advertiser*, which had been delivered alongside his meal. An advertisement caught his eye, inviting all lovers of manly British sport to witness a trial of skill between two renowned champions, Sutton and Figg. Determined not to miss such an event, he resolved to attend. He mounted the pair of horses he had purchased the previous day and set off for the Wooden House in Marybone Fields.

His young driver, unfamiliar with the route, navigated with excessive caution, weaving and turning far more often than necessary. He became momentarily lost in the green lanes trailing behind Mr. Whitfield's Round Tabernacle near Tottenham Road and the grounds surrounding Middlesex Hospital. Eventually, however, he arrived at his destination.

A considerable crowd had already gathered to witness the valorous feats of the two champions. Around the doors of this "temple of British valour" stood a motley assembly of London ruffians, mixed with the carriages and equipages of fashionable patrons like Mr. Warrington. Beggars and cripples pressed against the young gentleman, whining for alms; shoe-black boys scrambled over one another to polish his boots; women selling nosegays and fruit hawked their wares; and a chaotic mix of pie-men, peddlers, tramps, and street performers surrounded the arena. A flag fluttered atop the building, and a manager, flanked by a drummer and a horn-player, stepped onto the stage to announce that the noble English sports were about to commence.

Mr. Warrington paid his admission and secured a seat in a gallery offering a clear view of the platform. Below, in the amphitheatre, sat Mr. Gumbo. When weary, Gumbo would retreat to the outer world to drink a pot of beer or gamble with his fellow servants and the coachesmen waiting outside. The scene was dominated by the remnants of old society—lacquies, liveried footmen, and valets. It was noted that grand estates and chapels were once encumbered by armies of these men, who swarmed hallways, guzzled ale, and bullied visitors for tips. Though the race was largely extinct, having passed away like the Red Indians of America, a few thousand still lingered, seen occasionally on levee days with their powdered hair, buckles, and plush coats. Yet, the fatal decree had been issued; figures like Uncas with his tomahawk and Jeames with his cocked hat were fading from the world.

Before the main event, several minor contests took place. A boxing match occurred, yet neither fighter displayed much courage, receiving little punishment, which offered scant entertainment to the spectators. This was followed by cudgel-play, but the injuries inflicted were trivial and unimpressive, prompting the crowd to hiss and grow restless.

"The masters!" the people shouted.

Finally, the famous champions appeared. First ascended the steps the intrepid Sutton, sword in hand. He saluted the company, making a special bow toward a private box occupied by a stout gentleman of apparent importance. Soon after, the celebrated Figg entered, receiving a wave of approval from the same spectator. Both men were shirtless, their heads shaved clean but marked with the scars of past battles. Each wore an "armiger"—a ribbon denoting his color—around his burly sword arm.

The gladiators shook hands, and as a contemporary poet noted, "The word it was bilboe."

At the start of the combat, Figg struck with such tremendous force that, had the blow landed true, it would have severed Sutton's head cleanly. However, Sutton deftly intercepted the strike with his own sword. The impact was so violent that Figg's weapon snapped in his grip, breaking under the strain. New swords were distributed, and the fight resumed. The first blood spilled from Figg's side, drawing a cheer from Sutton's supporters. Yet, the veteran appealed to the audience—and specifically to the stout gentleman in the private box—revealing that the injury resulted from the earlier breakage of his sword.

During this brief pause, Mr. Warrington observed a gentleman in a riding frock and plain wig entering the box. Recognizing his friend from Tunbridge Wells, Lord March and Ruglen, Harry felt a surge of pleasure. Lord March, usually sparing with compliments, showed singular deference to the stout gentleman, accepting bank-notes with a profound bow. As the Earl concluded his business, he spotted the Virginian in the gallery. Once his conversation ended, Lord March approached Harry warmly.

They watched the duel unfold with varying degrees of success, though both men displayed immense skill and bravery. After exhausting themselves with swords, the warriors switched to quarter-staves. The prolonged and delightful battle ultimately favored the ancient champion, Figg.

As the combat raged, a thunderstorm broke over the building. Seizing the opportunity, Mr. Warrington gladly transferred to Lord March's carriage, leaving his own phaeton to be driven home by his groom. Harry was thoroughly delighted by the spectacle, declaring it the finest sport he had witnessed since arriving in England.

"I wish..." he sighed, pausing mid-thought.

"No, I don't," he corrected himself immediately.

"What do you wish, and what don't you wish?" Lord March asked.

"I was thinking, my lord, of my elder brother," Harry replied. "We promised to enjoy such sports together at home. Many times we discussed it. But he wouldn't have liked this rough kind of fighting, nor did he care for it, though he was the bravest lad alive."

"Oh! He was the bravest lad alive, was he?" Lord March queried, lounging on his cushion and studying his young friend with curiosity.

"You should have seen him in a quarrel with a very gallant officer," Harry continued. "It was an absurd affair, but it was hard to keep George from engaging. I never saw a fellow so cool, nor more savage and determined. For the honor of the country, I wish he had come here instead of me, to show you a true Virginian gentleman."

"Nay, sir, you'll do very well," the amused nobleman responded. "What is this I hear of Lady Yarmouth taking you into favor?"

"I shall do as well as anyone else," Harry insisted. "I can ride, and I believe I can shoot better than George. But then, my brother possessed the mind, sir—the head!" He tapped his own skull. "He had read nearly every book ever written; he could play the fiddle and harpsichord; he composed elegant poetry and sermons. What can I offer? Only riding, card-playing, and drinking Burgundy."

Penitently, he lowered his head.

"But those things I can do as well as most fellows," he added, recovering his spirits. "In fact, my lord, I'll back myself."

Lord March relished the young man's naive confidence, much as a jaded voluptuary enjoys a fresh, wholesome chop of meat.

"By Gad, Mr. Warrington," the Earl declared, "you ought to be taken to Exeter Change and put in a show."

"And for what reason?"

"A gentleman from Virginia who has lost his elder brother and mourns him deeply. Such a breed is unknown here. Upon my honor and conscience, I believe you would prefer to have him back."

"Believe!" cried the Virginian, flushing red. "That is, you believe you believe you would like him back. But depend on it, you wouldn't. 'Tis not in human nature, sir—not as I understand it."

As the carriage rolled along, pointing out landmarks like Sir Richard Littleton's house and the converted Montagu House, now a museum filled with stuffed birds and rhinoceroses, the conversation shifted. Lord March lamented the chaos of the new roads and the changing city.

"Shall we go down Tyburn Road and the Park, or through Swallow Street to the habitable quarter?" the Earl suggested. "We can dine at Pall Mall, or perhaps with you. Spend the evening as you like—with the Queen of Spades, or..."

"With the Queen of Spades, if your lordship pleases," Mr. Warrington blushed, agreeing.

The carriage proceeded to his hotel in Covent Garden. The landlord, recognizing Lord March, bowed low, pressing his wig against the Earl's shoes in humble greeting.

A rich young English peer in the reign of George II versus a wealthy patrician in the age of Augustus: which would you choose?

After a sumptuous dinner, wine flowed freely, of quality sufficient even for an epicurean earl. Conversation turned to fireworks at Vauxhall or a game of cards. Harry, having only seen a modest display of squibs in Williamsburg during Guy Fawkes Night, yearned for Vauxhall but yielded to his guest's preference for Piquet.

Soon, they were engrossed in the game. Harry, as usual, started strong, winning steadily. However, within half an hour, fortune turned in favor of Lord March. Just as the Earl grew surly at the shift in luck, Mr. Draper, Mr. Warrington's business agent, entered the room to accept an invitation to join them for a drink.

Mr. Warrington invariably invited everyone to share his hospitality. If he had bread, he shared it; if a haunch of meat, he divided it; if a jug of water, he drank it amicably; if a bottle of Burgundy, he consumed it joyfully with friends. One should not mistake this generosity for mere etiquette. While

My early fortune led me to form a friendship with a gentleman of standing, born on an island often hailed by impartial critics as the "first gem of the ocean." The tales this man recounted concerning his kin—those inhabitants of the aforementioned jewel—were sufficient to curdle the blood of youth. He described a lineage where every conceivable crime was committed; the Ten Commandments shattered in a single sweep, accompanied by such audacious frauds and villainy that no fiction writer could conceive, and murders and robberies surpassing even the exploits of Thurtell or Turpin. Yet, despite this catalogue of depravity, the family somehow managed to survive without ending up in the hulks.

My brother Tim had brought his father's gray hairs to the grave with sorrow; my brother Mick had repeatedly robbed the parish church; and my sister Annamaroia had jilted a captain, eloped with an ensign, forged her grandmother's will, and stolen the silver spoons—an offense for which Larry the knife-boy eventually hung. Compared to the infamous House of Atreus, this clan of O'What-d'ye-call-'em was merely a shadow, yet I dare not name their homeland. What struck me as naive astonishment was witnessing these murderers, parricides, and habitual forgers correspond with one another as "dearest brother" and "dearest sister," maintaining amicable relations for months on end. Tim, whose hands reeked of patricide, would mix a screeching tumbler for Maria; Mick, whose lips were blackened by perjury regarding his grandmother's will and the murder of his brother Thady's orphans, would kiss Julia's cheek. They would share a jolly night, weeping and reminiscing about the old castle, the quarreling Onetyoneth, the Major's proposal for Cyaroloine, and the tomb of their scent-filled mother who had disinherited them.

They embraced with such fervor that one forgot their bloody histories and the threats to hang each other. What is finer than forgiveness? After hurling every insult under the sun, apologizing for hasty words, withdrawing the decanter once flung at an enemy's head, and becoming friends anew—is there anything more rational? Some possess this angelic gift. It was beautiful to observe our two ladies at Tunbridge Wells forgiving one another, smiling and fondling despite yesterday's harsh words, forgetting bygones while perfectly remembering them. Can you and I achieve such heights? We must strive for this pacable, Christian spirit. While I believe one can learn to forgive bad language through practice, embracing after a quarrel renders insults meaningless.

Thus, Aunt Bernstein and her niece played cards amiably, toasted each other's health, shared wings of chicken, and even scratched neighbors' eyes out rather than each other's. This mirrors the behavior of Peninsular warriors who fraternized over tobacco and wine during truces, only to resume combat instantly afterward. Similarly, our veterans laid down their weapons to hobnob gaily. Of course, while drinking with a Frenchman, one keeps a pistol handy, yet amidst the *A votre santé*, everything remains pleasant.

Regarding Aunt Bernstein's threatened gout, the twinges had vanished. Maria's fainting fits? None returned, save for a slight recurrence the prior night, prompting the Baroness's concern and offers of bottled waters. Yet, private whispers suggest the old lady dismissed these fits as epilepsy inherited from a "scrofulous German mother," while Lady Maria might have ordered her maid to test every medicinal glass for poison. Such precautions are like the loaded firelocks of old soldiers: ready, but currently resting quietly on the grass.

Possessing Harry's bond in her pocket, the veteran Maria refrained from pressing for immediate payment. Knowing the world too well, she granted him ample grace, understanding his youth and need for diversion. "Take your ease, cousin," she advised. Even the cat, Grey Grimalkin, purred contentedly in the corner, watching with green eyes.

Lady Maria and her aunt discussed Harry's anticipated arrival in London with frankness typical of the era. In his absence, the wily Calypso sought diversions, finding joy in the company of Mr. Jack Morris, a gentleman who delighted in associating with titled figures and losing money to an earl's daughter. Since Lady Maria was eager to win, she secured permission to invite Morris to the Countess of Yarmouth's assembly, ensuring everyone's satisfaction.

The first eighty-four hours following Mr. Warrington's departure passed cheerfully at Tunbridge Wells until Friday arrived, marking the day of Mr. Sampson's sermon. The company was eager to attend; if there were no breakfasts, conjurers, or dancing bears, the Wells folk would endure a sermon. Sampson knew Lady Yarmouth was present, wielding immense influence over livings and bishoprics, and thus prepared his finest discourse.

When the Great Man visits the local church, bringing dukes and cabinet ministers, the curate becomes acutely aware of the audience. He watches the Great Pew nervously, fearing judgment on his future prospects. His wife and daughters join the gaze, hoping for a favorable reaction. Once the initial nervousness fades, the noble voice warms, kindling into passion until he reaches the exquisite passage that moves the congregation to tears. Then comes the humming noise—the Right Honourable Lord Naseby snoring loudly in the front pew. Poor Trotter's vision of a mitre vanishes with the snores.

Sampson, the domestic chaplain to Madame Bernstein's nephew and favored by the Esmond ladies, appeared that morning rosy and handsome in a fresh wig and a new cassock bought on credit. Accompanied by lacquemen carrying gilt prayer books, he marched from breakfast to church. The Baroness looked dazzling, laughing warmly with her niece, her tortoiseshell cane in hand. Outside, a dazzling confluence of ranks gathered, including Lady Yarmouth in flame-colored taffeta. Among the crowd were shabbier figures, notably two men in ragged coats entering as the organ ceased. One, with carrot hair peeking from a scratch-wig, mechanically crossed himself, muttering, "Bedad, Tim, I forgawt!" Clearly, he hailed from the island mentioned earlier. Whisky followed them inside.

Even Catholics and ancient Hebrews paid homage. Two men with hooked noses and sallow complexions pushed past the beadle's objections, bribing him with coin and promising conversion. The doors closed, the organ began, and the sermon commenced.

Suddenly, as Sampson rose from his pulpit cushion, he turned pale as death. Glancing toward the west door, he saw the "horrible Hebrew caryatides." Looking toward the vestry near the rector's pew, he noticed two perfumed Irish gentlemen seated nearby, eyes cast meekly downward. How could he deliver his sermon when his mind was elsewhere?

Despite his distraction, the sermon was deemed excellent. Upon its conclusion, the ladies praised it effusively. Madame Walmoden declared it bewitching; Madame Bernstein called it excellent. Lady Maria waved a handkerchief, attempting to catch the reverend's eye, but he remained transfixed, staring toward the font where the two Hebrews still sat. As the congregation filed out, the two men remained by the door.

Inside the rector's pew, the Baroness conversed deeply with a gentleman while Lady Maria fretted. "Who are those horrible men at the door? And what a smell of spirits!" cried Lady Maria to her maid, Mrs. Brett. Meanwhile, the Baroness bid farewell to a doctor, asking about his son's clerical ambitions, before insisting that Lady Maria exit the pew first.

As the younger lady stepped forward, the two men advanced. One produced a long strip of paper. Lady Maria started, turning pale, and fled toward the vestry, hoping to escape. However, the men intercepted her. "At the suit of Mistress Pincott, mercer of Kensington, I arrest your ladyship," declared one, introducing himself as Michael Costigan, a poor Irishman forced into a disagreeable profession. "Will you walk, or shall my man fetch a cheer?"

In reply, Lady Maria shrieked three times and swooned. "Keep the door, Mick!" shouted Costigan, politely informing the Baroness that the lady had fainted and required rest. "Unlace her, Brett!" commanded the old lady, her eyes twinkling strangely. Seizing a small bag hanging from Lady Maria's necklace, the Baroness cut the chain. "Dash cold water on her face!" she instructed. Turning to Costigan, she asked, "How much is your suit?"

"The debt stands at one hundred and thirty-two pounds," Costigan replied.

Where was Reverend Sampson? Like the opossum who admits defeat when spotted by a hunter, he surrendered immediately. "At whose suit, Simons?" he asked sadly. "Buckleby Cordwainer," came the reply. "Forty-eight pounds and charges," Sampson sighed. Lacking funds, he was ushered into two chairs by Mr. Lyons, who offered his house as accommodation. Thus, Lady Maria and Sampson were escorted to Mr. Lyons's residence.

Shortly thereafter, the Baroness sent her servant, Mr. Case, with a note expressing ardent affection but explaining that heavy card losses made repayment impossible. She wrote to Mrs. Pincott requesting time, promising to settle once an answer was received. Meanwhile, Mrs. Betty attempted to raise funds for a carriage to London. Finding only eighteen shillings between her mistress and herself, she decided to sell a gold chain. The shopkeeper, suspecting theft, nearly involved a constable

"My dear Lord March" (wrote Mr. Warrington from Tunbridge Wells, on Saturday morning, the 25th of August, 1756): "This is to inform you (with satisfaction) that I have won all our three bets. I was at Bromley two minutes within the hour; my new horses kept a-going at a capital rate. I drove them myself, having the postilion by me to show me the way, and my black man inside with Mrs. Betty. Hope they found the drive very pleasant. We were not stopped on Blackheath, though two fellows on horseback rode up to us, but not liking the looks of our countenances, rode off again; and we got into Tunbridge Wells (where I transacted my business) at forty-five minutes after eleven. This makes me quits with your lordship after yesterday's piquet, which I shall be very happy to give your revenge, and am—Your most obliged, faithful servant, H. ESMOND WARRINGTON."

And now, perhaps, the reader will understand by what means Lady Maria Esmond was enabled to surprise her dear aunt in her bed on Saturday morning, and walk out of the house of captivity. Having despatched Mrs. Betty to London, she scarcely expected that her emissary would return on the day of her departure; and she and the chaplain were playing their cards at midnight, after a small refecton which the bailiff's wife had provided for them, when the rapid whirling of wheels was heard approaching their house, and caused the lady to lay her trumps down, and her heart to beat with more than ordinary emotion. *Whirr* came the wheels—the carriage stopped at the very door: there was a parley at the gate: then appeared Mrs. Betty, with a face radiant with joy, though her eyes were full of tears; and next, who is that tall young gentleman who enters? Can any of my readers guess? Will they be very angry if I say that the chaplain slapped down his cards with a huzzah, whilst Lady Maria, turning as white as a sheet, rose up from her chair, tottered forward a step or two, and, with an hysterical shriek, flung herself in her cousin's arms? How many kisses did he give her? If they were *mille*, deinde centum, dein mille altera, dein secunda centum, and so on, I am not going to cry out. He had come to rescue her. She knew he would; he was her champion, her preserver from bondage and ignominy. She wept a genuine flood of tears upon his shoulder, and as she reclined there, giving way to a hearty emotion, I protest I think she looks handsomer than she has looked during the whole course of this history. She did not faint this time; she went home, leaning lovingly on her cousin's arm, and may have had one or two hysterical outbreaks in the night; but Madame Bernstein slept soundly, and did not hear her.

"You are both free to go home," were the first words Harry said. "Get my lady's hat and cardinal, Betty, and, Chaplain, we'll smoke a pipe together at our lodgings, it will refresh me after my ride." The chaplain, who, too, had a great deal of available sensibility, was very much overcome; he burst into tears as he seized Harry's hand, and kissed it, and prayed God to bless his dear, generous, young patron. Mr. Warrington felt a glow of pleasure thrill through his frame. It is good to be able to help the suffering and the poor; it is good to be able to turn sorrow into joy. Not a little proud and elated was our young champion, as, with his hat cocked, he marched by the side of his rescued princess. His feelings came out to meet him, as it were, and beautiful happinesses with kind eyes and smiles danced before him, and clad him in a robe of honour, and scattered flowers on his path, and blew trumpets and shawms of sweet gratulation, calling, "Here comes the conqueror! Make way for the champion!" And so they led him up to the king's house, and seated him in the hall of complacency, upon the cushions of comfort. And yet it was not much he had done. Only a kindness. He had but to put his hand in his pocket, and with an easy talisman, drive off the dragon which kept the gate, and cause the tyrant to lay down his axe, who had got Lady Maria in execution. Never mind if his vanity is puffed up; he is very good-natured; he has rescued two unfortunate people, and pumped tears of goodwill and happiness out of their eyes:—and if he brags a little to-night, and swaggers somewhat to the chaplain, and talks about London, and Lord March, and White's, and Almack's, with the air of a macaroni, I don't think we need like him much the less.

Sampson continued to be prodigiously affected. This man had a nature most easily worked upon, and extraordinarily quick to receive pain and pleasure, to tears, gratitude, laughter, hatred, liking. In his preaching profession he had educated and trained his sensibilities so that they were of great use to him; he was for the moment what he acted. He wept quite genuine tears, finding that he could produce them freely. He loved you whilst he was with you; he had a real pang of grief as he mingled his sorrow with the widow or orphan; and, meeting Jack as he came out of the door, went to the tavern opposite, and laughed and roared over the bottle. He gave money very readily, but never repaid when he borrowed. He was on this night in a rapture of gratitude and flattery towards Harry Warrington. In all London, perhaps, the unlucky Fortunate Youth could not have found a more dangerous companion. To-night he was in his grateful mood, and full of enthusiasm for the benefactor who had released him from durance. With each bumper his admiration grew stronger. He exalted Harry as the best and noblest of men, and the complacent young simpleton, as we have said, was disposed to take these praises as very well deserved.

"The younger branch of our family," said Mr. Harry, with a superb air, "have treated you scurvily; but, by Jove, Sampson my boy, I'll stand by you!" At a certain period of Burgundian excitement Mr. Warrington was always very eloquent respecting the splendour of his family. "I am very glad I was enabled to help you in your strait. Count on me whenever you want me, Sampson. Did you not say you had a sister at boarding-school? You will want money for her, sir. Here is a little bill which may help to pay her schooling." And the liberal young fellow passed a bank-note across to the chaplain. Again the man was affected to tears. Harry's generosity smote him.

"Mr. Warrington," he said, putting the bank-note a short distance from him, "I—I don't deserve your kindness—by George, I don't!" and he swore an oath to corroborate his passionate assertion.

"Psha!" says Harry. "I have plenty more of 'em. There was no money in that confounded pocket-book which I lost last week."

"No, sir. There was no money!" says Mr. Sampson, dropping his head.

"Hallo! How do you know, Mr. Chaplain?" asks the young gentleman.

"I know because I am a villain, sir. I am not worthy of your kindness. I told you so. I found the book, sir, that night, when you had too much wine at Barbeau's."

"And read the letters?" asked Mr. Warrington, starting up and turning very red.

"They told me nothing I did not know, sir," said the chaplain. "You have had spies about you whom you little suspect—from whom you are much too young and simple to be able to keep your secret."

"Are those stories about Lady Fanny, and my cousin Will and his doings, true then?" inquired Harry.

"Yes, they are true," sighed the chaplain. "The house of Castlewood has not been fortunate, sir, since your honour's branch, the elder branch, left it."

"Sir, you don't dare for to breathe a word against my Lady Maria?" Harry cried out.

"Oh, not for worlds!" says Mr. Sampson, with a queer look at his young friend. "I may think she is too old for your honour, and that 'tis a pity you should not have a wife better suited to your age, though I admit she looks very young for hers, and hath every virtue and accomplishment."

"She is too old, Sampson, I know she is," says Mr. Warrington, with much majesty; "but she has my word, and you see, sir, how fond she is of me. Go bring me the letters, sir, which you found, and let me try and forgive you for having seized upon them."

"My benefactor, let me try and forgive myself!" cries Mr. Sampson, and departed towards his chamber, leaving his young patron alone over his wine.

Sampson returned presently, looking very pale.

"What has happened, sir?" says Harry, with an imperious air.

The chaplain held out a pocket-book.

"With your name in it, sir," he said.

"My brother's name in it,"

Our friends in Tunbridge were growing weary of the spa waters and eager to depart. With the arrival of autumn, Bath became Madame de Bernstein's destination—a city brimming with cards, company, and vibrant life. She planned to reach it after paying a few calls on her country acquaintances. Harry, with a distinctly reluctant air, promised to ride with Lady Maria and the chaplain to Castlewood.

They passed Oakhurst village once more, gliding past the hospitable house where Harry had previously been so warmly entertained. Lady Maria, however, could not resist making sharp remarks about the young ladies of Oakhurst, noting how they seemed to be "setting their caps" at Harry, and observing the mother's obvious desire to secure him for one of them. Irritated, Mr. Warrington declared in a petulant mood that he would simply ride past his friends' door, claiming her ladyship disliked and abused them. That evening, at the inn where they halted a few miles further, Harry was notably haughty and sulky.

At supper, Lady Maria's smiles failed to elicit any warmth from Harry's face. Her manufactured tears elicited no sympathy; instead, he grumbled surly replies to her querulous complaints. Eventually, my lady was forced to retire to her chamber without securing even a brief tête-à-tête with her cousin—the obstinate chaplain, seemingly by design, refusing to leave the room. Did Harry give specific orders to Sampson to remain? She departed with a heavy sigh. He bowed her to the door with stiff, obstinate politeness, consigning her to the care of the landlady and her maid.

Ten minutes after Lady Maria had retired, what horse galloped out of the inn yard? An hour later, when Mrs. Betty entered to retrieve her mistress's bottle of smelling salts, she found Parson Sampson smoking a pipe alone. Mr. Warrington had gone to bed—or perhaps taken a moonlit walk; how could the maid possibly know where Harry was? Sampson answered the maid's questions calmly. Mr. Warrington intended to set out the following morning and took his seat beside Lady Maria's carriage. Yet his brow was black; the dark spirit still haunted him. He barely spoke to her throughout the journey. Inside her own mind, Lady Maria fretted: *Great heavens! She must have told him that she stole it!*

In truth, as they ascended a steep hill roughly three miles from Oakhurst on the Westerham road, Lady Maria Esmond, leaning heavily on her beloved youth's arm and deeply infatuated with him, had whispered sentimental vows, protests, and declarations of affection into his ear. As she grew fonder, he grew colder. As she gazed up at his face, the sunlight illuminated hers—fresh and well-preserved, yet betraying the lines and wrinkles of twenty years—and poor Harry, feeling the weight of her arm, found the walk up the hill utterly intolerable. To think that such a burden would weigh upon him for the rest of his life! It was a dreary prospect, causing him to curse the moonlit stroll, the stifling heat of the evening, and the hot wine that had compelled him to make that foolish pledge, binding him fatally.

Maria's praises and raptures annoyed Harry beyond endurance. The poor girl poured out fragments of the few plays she knew that related to her situation, striving with every ounce of energy to charm her young companion. She repeatedly called him her champion, her Henrico, her preserver, vowing that his Molinda would remain eternally faithful. She clung to him desperately. "Ah, child! Have I not thy precious image, thy precious hair, thy precious writing here?" she exclaimed, gazing into his face. "Shall it not accompany me to the grave? It surely would, sir, were I to meet with unkindness from my Henrico!" she sighed.

What a strange tale! Madame Bernstein had given him the small silken case; she had burned the hair and the note it contained, yet Maria still held them close to her heart! It was then, as Harry started forward and suddenly moved as if to drop her arm—that moment of sudden motion—that Lady Maria felt her first pang of remorse. She realized she had spun a fib, or worse, that she had been exposed in telling one. If everyone were required to perform penance for lying, would they ever emerge from sackcloth and ashes?

Upon arriving at Castlewood, Harry's good humor had not improved. My Lord was absent, the ladies were away, and the only family member present was Mr. Will, who returned from partridge shooting just as the carriage and cavalcade reached the gate. He turned pale upon seeing his cousin and received a sulky scowl of recognition from the young Virginian.

Nonetheless, Harry attempted to maintain a pleasant demeanor. They met at supper, where their conversation was initially civil but lacking liveliness.
"Have you been to the races?" asked Mr. Warrington.
"To several," replied Will.
"Were you successful in your bets?"
"I hope so," said Mr. Warrington.
"Pretty well."
"And you have brought back my horse sound?" asked Mr. Warrington.

"My horse? What horse?" asked Will, confused.
"What horse? My horse!" Mr. Harry said curtly.
"Protest, I don't understand you," said Will.
"The brown horse for which I wagered with you, and which I won from you the night before you rode away upon it," Mr. Warrington stated sternly. "You remember the horse, Mr. Esmond."

"Mr. Warrington, I perfectly recall playing you for a horse, which my servant handed over to you on the day of your departure."
"The chaplain was present at our game. Mr. Sampson, will you act as umpire between us?" Mr. Warrington requested gently.
"I am bound to decide that Mr. Warrington played for the brown horse," said Mr. Sampson.
"Well, he got the other one," said sulky Mr. Will, grinning.
"And sold it for thirty shillings!" added Mr. Warrington, maintaining his calm tone.

Will chuckled waggishly. "Thirty shillings? And a devilish good price, too, for the broken-kneed old rip. Ha, ha!"
"Not a word more. 'Tis merely a question regarding a bet, my dear Lady Maria. Shall I serve you some more chicken?" Nothing could be more studiously courteous and gay than Mr. Warrington, so long as the lady remained in the room. When she rose to leave, Harry followed her to the door, closing it upon her with the most courtly bow of farewell. He lingered at the closed door for a moment, then commanded the servants to retire. Once the menials were gone, Mr. Warrington locked the heavy door, pocketing the key.

As the lock clicked shut, Mr. Will, who had been sitting over his punch and occasionally glancing sideways at his cousin, asked—with one of the oaths common to his speech—what in the world Mr. Warrington meant by that?
"I suspect a quarrel is brewing," said Mr. Warrington blandly. "There is no point in having these fellows look on at a row between their betters."
"Who is going to quarrel here, I should like to know?" asked Will, looking very pale and grasping a knife.
"Mr. Sampson, you were present when I played Mr. Will fifty guineas against his brown horse?"
"Against his horse!" roared Mr. Will.
"I am not such a fool as you take me for," said Mr. Warrington, "though I do hail from Virginia!" He repeated his question firmly. "Mr. Sampson, you were here when I played the Honourable William Esmond, Esquire, fifty guineas against his brown horse?"
"I must admit it, sir," said the chaplain, casting a deprecatory glance toward the Earl's brother.
"I don't own no such a thing," said Mr. Will, forcing a laugh.
"No, sir: because it costs you no more pains to lie than to cheat," said Mr. Warrington, stepping closer to his cousin. "Stand aside, Mr. Chaplain, and let us have fair play! For you are no better than a—"

Before he could finish the insult, Mr. Will threw a wine bottle at Mr. Warrington's head. The Virginian ducked just in time; the missile flew across the room, shattered against the wainscot opposite, smashing the face of a pictured ancestor of the Esmond family, and then rebounded against the wall, splashing a pint of fine port wine over the chaplain's face and powdered wig.
"Great heavens, gentlemen, I pray you to be quiet!" cried the parson, dripping with liquid.

However, gentlemen are sometimes disinclined to heed the Church's commands. With the bottle having failed, Mr. Esmond seized a large silver-handled knife and lunged at his cousin. But Harry caught up the attacker's right hand with his left—as he had observed boxers do at Marybone—and delivered a rapid blow to Mr. Esmond's nose. This sent the Earl's brother reeling backward against the oak panels, presumably blinding him momentarily. He dropped his knife as he retreated against the wall, which his swift opponent promptly kicked under the table.

Now, Will had also attended matches at Marybone and Hockley-in-the-Hole.

In the era of Harry Warrington, the intellectual world was deeply engrossed in the debate concerning the relative merits of ancient versus modern literature—a controversy to which our young hero paid little attention. The scholars and the general public alike tended to side with the ancients. Yet, looking backward from our vantage point today, we realize that "the moderns" of that age are simply "the ancients" of ours. Just as we judge the writers of the mid-18th century, so too will our descendants, a century hence, pass judgment upon us.

Regarding book learning, one might argue that the respectable ancestors of that time—despite possessing the formidable works of Gibbon—are ultimately defeated. One need only look to the professors of Cambridge and Glasgow today to find individuals who command a knowledge of Greek surpassing what was available across all European universities, including the famed academy of Athens itself. In matters of science, they were scarcely more advanced than the very heathens to whom they admitted inferiority in literature. But what of public and private morality? Is the year 1858 truly superior to the century preceding it?

Gentlemen of Mr. Disraeli's House of Commons, has every one of you a price, as was common in the times of Walpole or Newcastle—or, more delicately put, have you all succumbed to corruption? Ladies, I do not claim you are a society of Vestals; however, the chronicles of a hundred years ago bristle with scandal, making you fortunate not to have lived in such perilous times. On my conscience, I believe men and women are both better now. Not only are the Susannas more numerous, but the Elders are far less wicked. Have you ever read *Clarissa*, *Tom Jones*, or *Roderick Random*? Have you seen the paintings of contemporary artists depicting the men, women, and society of that day? Imagine describing the exploits of a Mr. Lovelace or Lady Bellaston, or that remarkable "Lady of Quality" who lent her memoirs to the author of *Peregrine Pickle*. How the pure, outraged Nineteenth Century would blush, scream, flee the room, summon the young ladies, and order Mr. Mudie to cease sending such odious volumes!

Perhaps you, madam, at fifty-eight, are too squeamish, crying out before you are harmed, when no offense was intended. Perhaps the novelist's art suffers from the restraints placed upon him, much like an honest statue at St. Peter's or the Vatican, marred by the tin draperies in which pious old women have swaddled the fair limbs of marble. Yet, in your prudery lies reason, just as there is in state censorship of the press. If a page contains matter dangerous to good morals, out with the scissors, censor, and clip the prurient paragraph! We have no choice but to submit. Society, the despot, has issued his imperial decree. We may lament that the statue looked better without the drapery, or plead that the moral lesson would be clearer if the whole fable were told. But away with him—not a word!

I have never seen pianofortes in the United States wearing frilled muslin trousers on their legs; but depend on it, that muslin covered some of the notes as well as the mahogany, muffling the music and stopping the player. To what does this prelude lead? It introduces us to Harry Warrington, Esquire, in his Bond Street lodgings in London, and the life he and many other fashionable young bucks led at that time. I cannot take my fair young reader into such scenes any more than Lady Squeams could take her daughter to Cremorne Gardens on an ordinary evening.

My dear Miss Diana (pshaw! I know you are eighty-three, despite your shyness and your desire to convince us you have just left schoolroom dinners and a pinafore): consider your grandfather when he was a young man about town, a member of a club at White's, dining at Pontac's off the feasts provided by Braund and Lebeck, riding to Newmarket with March and Rockingham, and toasting the best in England alongside Gilly Williams and George Selwyn—and failing to understand George's jokes, the flavor of which has largely evaporated since the bottling process. The old gentleman led a life your noble aunt, author of *Legends of the Squeams*; or, *Fair Fruits of a Family Tree*, has not hinted at. This was before your grandmother adopted those serious views that characterized her later residence at Bath, and after Colonel Tibbalt married Miss Lye, the wealthy heiress of a soap boiler. During those days, her ladyship's wild oats were sown.

When young, she was as giddy as the rest of the genteel world. At her Hill Street mansion, she hosted ten card tables on Wednesday and Sunday evenings, save for the brief period when Ranelagh opened on Sundays. Every night of her life she gambled for eight, nine, or ten hours. Everyone else in society did the same. She lost; she won; she cheated; she pawned her jewels—who knows what else she was unwilling to pledge to fund her fury for play? What of that infamous after-supper duel at the Shakespeare's Head in Covent Garden, between your grandfather and Colonel Tibbalt? They drew swords and engaged solely in the presence of Sir John Screwby, who sat drunk beneath the table. Their fight was interrupted by Mr. John Fielding's constables, and your grandfather was carried home to Hill Street wounded in a sedan chair.

Let me tell you, those gentlemen in powder and ruffles, who turned the toes of their buckled pumps with such delicate precision, were terrible fellows. Swords were perpetually drawn; bottle after bottle was consumed; oaths roared incessantly in conversation; tavern drawers and watchmen were stabbed and maimed; chairmen were beaten; and citizens were insulted by reeling pleasure-seekers. Have you visited Cremorne with proper vouchers? Do you recall our great theaters thirty years ago? You were deemed too refined for the stage. Yet, you have no idea what the playhouses were, or what the green boxes represented, when Garrick and Mrs. Pritchard performed before them! And I, for my children's sake, thank that good actor in his retirement who was the first to banish that shame from the theater.

No, madam, you are mistaken. I do not boast of superior virtue. I do not claim you are naturally better than your ancestress in her wild, rouged, gambling, tear-stained days, nor even than poor Polly Fogle, recently arrested for shoplifting, who would have been hanged a century ago. Simply, I am heartily grateful that my temptations are fewer, having quite enough to contend with those of the present century. Thus, if Harry Warrington rides down to Newmarket for the October meeting and loses or wins his fortune there; if he joins a party at the Shakespeare or Bedford Head; if he dines at White's Ordinary and sits down to macco and lansquenet afterward; if he boxes the watch and appears at the Roundhouse; if he briefly becomes a wild, dissipated, harum-scarum young Harry Warrington—I, knowing the weakness of human nature, shall not be surprised. Quite aware of my own shortcomings, I do not intend to be overly savage at my neighbor's expense.

Mr. Sampson was different. In his chapel in Long Acre, he whipped Vice tremendously; he gave Sin no quarter; he cursed Blasphemy with superior anathemas; he knocked Drunkenness down and trampled the prostrate brute wallowing in the gutter; he dragged out conjugal infidelity and pounded her with endless rhetorical stones. Yet, after service, he would come to dinner at the Star and Garter, make a bowl of punch for Harry and his friends at the Bedford Head, join a hand at whist at Mr. Warrington's lodgings or Lord March's, or attend anywhere there was a supper and good company.

I often reflect, regarding Mr. Warrington's conduct during this period of his arrival in London, that I may have held my usual degrading and uncharitable view of him—for, seeing as I have uttered not a single word of virtuous indignation against his behavior, and if it was not reprehensible, I have certainly judged him most cruelly. Oh, Truthfulness, Oh Beauty, Oh Modesty, Oh Benevolence, Oh Pudor, Oh Morals, Oh Blushing Shame, Oh Namby-Pamby—each with your respective capital letters honoring your names! Oh Niminy, Oh Piminy! How shall I dare to say that a young man ever was a young man?

No doubt, dear young lady, I am calumniating Mr. Warrington according to my heartless custom. As proof, here is a letter from the Warrington collection, written by Harry to his mother, containing not a single word suggesting he was leading a wild life. Such a letter from an only son to a fond and exemplary parent must be true:

"BOND STREET, LONDON, October 25, 1756.

"I take up my pen to acknowledge your honored favor of July 10 via the *Lively Virginia* packet, which has duly arrived, forwarded by our Bristol agent, and I rejoice to hear that the prospects of the crops are so promising. 'Tis Tully who says that agriculture is the noblest pursuit; how delightful when that pursuit is also profitable!

"Since my last, dated from Tunbridge

Harry Warrington remained steadfast in keeping the grim promise his cousin had wrung from him. We trust no benevolent reader will judge him harshly for believing the engagement was anything but a burden he would gladly shed. Perhaps the beating inflicted upon poor Will served this very purpose. Harry likely reasoned thus: *"A fistfight between us is bound to escalate into a family quarrel. In such a fray, Maria will undoubtedly side with her brother. I, of course, will offer no retraction or apology. Will, in that scenario, may challenge me to settle the score physically, and I shall ensure I emerge as the victor. Amidst the chaos of the feud, the agreement will dissolve, and I shall be a free man once more."*

So, honest Harry laid his trap and pulled the fuse. Yet, when the explosion faded, no lasting damage was wrought—save for William Esmond's swollen nose and bruised eye, which plagued him for a week. Will did not issue a formal challenge to his cousin, Harry Warrington; consequently, neither man killed the other. Will was knocked down, but he rose again. How many sensible men would act similarly if they could settle their grievances privately, unseen by the public eye?

Maria did not align herself with her family in the dispute; instead, she championed her cousin, a stance supported by Lord Castlewood upon learning of the disturbance. "Will struck the first blow," the Lord declared, citing the chaplain's provocation. "It was not the first nor the tenth time he has been found quarreling while drunk. Mr. Warrington merely displayed proper spirit in defending his honor; it was for Will, not Harry, to seek forgiveness." Harry countered that he would accept no apology until his horse was returned and his wager settled. While the chronicler cannot ascertain from existing records how the bet was ultimately resolved, it is certain that the cousins soon met in the homes of mutual friends, avoiding further physical altercation.

Initially, Maria's elder brother had been content with his sister marrying any available suitor, especially a gentleman from Virginia. He viewed her as a woman past her prime, her gown trailing through life's path gathering briars, mud, rents, and stains. He imagined her retiring to his wigwam in the forest to be quietly disposed of, expecting Harry to outlive her elderly bride and console himself—or not—as he saw fit. However, following an audience with Aunt Bernstein upon arriving in London, the Lord reversed his opinion. He attempted to dissuade Maria from the match, feigning pity for the young man destined for misery due to a foolish promise made at twenty-one.

"Misery, indeed!" Maria retorted, confused by his sentiment. "Pity, forsooth?" Lord Castlewood believed there was none to be found. Maria, however, understood the concept of pity. Her brother had visited Aunt Bernstein, who had offered funds to break the engagement. She grasped the Lord's implication: Mr. Warrington was a man of honor, and she trusted him implicitly.

Enraged, the Lord departed for White's Club or similar haunts. It is probable Maria had correctly deduced the nature of his conversations with Madame Bernstein. *"And so,"* he mused, *"my virtue ends with the Mohock falling prey to others. There is no use in sparing him. 'Quem deus vult'—what was that schoolmaster's adage? If I do not claim him, someone else will. My brother has taken a bite; my dear sister intends to devour him whole. Here I have been at home, preaching respect for his youth and innocence, refusing to gamble beyond a sixpence, acting as his guardian and mentor. Why am I a fool fattening a goose only for others to feast upon? Few good deeds have I performed in this life, and this one benefits whom? Others. Talk of remorse! By all the fires and furies, the remorse I feel is for things I did not do and might have done! Why did I spare Lucretia? She hated me thereafter, and her husband met his predestined fate. Why have I let this lad off? Let March and the rest, who desire nothing of him, pluck him bare! I carry a bad reputation; I am the wicked lord women warn their sons against! Pardi, I am not a penny worse, only vastly unluckier than my neighbors, and my cursed weakness has been my greatest enemy!"*

The chronicler admits that attributing such internal monologues to a gentleman stretches credulity, yet history is filled with speeches invented by Livy and Thucydides that their heroes never uttered. Knowing Lord Castlewood's character intimately, we may reasonably infer these thoughts. A pack of wolves hunts this lamb; one hungry hunter stands aside while others feast. Has anyone not admired Lord Clive's boast upon returning from India, claiming surprise at how little he exploited his vast wealth? *"Upon my life,"* the hero of Plassey remarked, *"when I reflect on my opportunities, I am astonished I took so little!"*

To recount disagreeable tales of a gentleman forces a sensitive mind to pause. Thus, despite knowing early on the true nature of Lord Castlewood and his standing among contemporaries, the narrator withheld much unpleasantness, revealing only enough to show he was a nobleman unworthy of affection. True, Lord March and others would have gambled Mr. Warrington's last shilling and taken the winnings without scruple, exploiting every advantage of skill and insider knowledge—a common practice among gentlemen. Yet, when they played, they played fair, and when they lost, they paid.

Madame Bernstein hesitated to reveal everything she knew about her nephew's family disgrace. Touched by the Lord's initial forbearance toward Harry upon his European arrival and pleased by his compliance with her wishes, she listened to his cynical rantings. He called himself a fool for sparing a boy doomed to ruin, highlighted Harry's undeniable extravagance and spendthrift circle, his nights at Faro and Hazard, and his rides to Newmarket, asking why he alone should restrain the young man. When Madame Bernstein pleaded Harry's poverty, the Lord scoffed. *"Bah! He is heir to a principality that ought to belong to him, Castlewood's, and could restore our ruined family."* (In truth, Madame Bernstein believed Mr. Warrington's Virginia holdings were far greater than they actually were). Money-lenders abounded in the city, eager to lend on post-obits. The Lord knew this to his cost; during his father's lifetime, such creditors had devoured two-thirds of his miserable income.

Speaking with such desperate candor and ill humor, Madame Bernstein grew alarmed for her favorite and decided to caution him immediately. That evening, she began drafting a letter to Mr. Warrington. Yet, throughout her life, she was slow with her pen and disliked its use. *"I never knew any good come of writing more than 'bonjour' or business,"* she often said. *"Why write poorly when clever people exist to do it well? Even then, silence is best."* Instead, she dispatched a servant to invite Harry to tea the next day, intending to warn him.

However, the next morning she fell ill and could not receive him. She confined herself to her chambers for two days, and on the third, a major engagement occupied her attention. By the fourth day, Mr. Harry was off on his own expedition. In the whirlwind of London life, who sees their neighbor, their sibling, or their old friend? Days passed before the confidential conversation occurred.

She began by gently chiding him for his extravagance and wild frolics (though, in truth, she was charmed by both). He replied that young men must be young men, noting that his dutiful attendance upon his aunt had led to the acquaintanceship he currently enjoyed. Then, with some preamble, she warned him regarding his cousin, Lord Castlewood. At this, Harry burst into a bitter laugh. *"The good-natured world has told me plenty about Lord Castlewood already,"* he said. *"To advise a man of his rank, or any gentleman, 'Do not play with him,' is more than I care to do."*

"Oh, go on, aunt!" Harry interjected, with something resembling an oath on his lips.

"And have you played with your cousin already?" the worldly old monitoress asked.

*"Lost and won, madam!"* Harry answered gallantly. *"It does not become me to specify. If we have a bout with a neighbor in Virginia—over a bottle, a deck of cards, or a quarrel—we do not return home to tell our mothers. I mean no offense, aunt!"* Blushing, the handsome young fellow approached and kissed the old lady. He appeared brave and brilliant, adorned in rich lace, with a fair face and hair, wearing a fine new suit of velvet and gold. Upon departing, he bestowed his customary lavish tip upon her servants, who swarmed around him.

It was a rainy winter day, and to protect his fine silk stockings, Harry insisted on traveling in a chair. *"To White's!"* he commanded the driver, heading to the establishment where he spent much of his time. His friends might have wished for less frequent visits to this house of entertainment, but much credit is due to Mr. Warrington: having engaged in play, he fought his battles like a hero. Unflustered by good fortune and perfectly calm when luck turned against him, he embodied the ideal gambler. If Fortune is proverbially fickle to players, how many players are fickle to Fortune, fleeing her advances in fear?

*"By George, Mr. Warrington,"* exclaimed Mr

Mr. Harry Warrington of Virginia kept his lodgings in Bond Street, London, living upon the fat of the land and drinking bumpers of the finest wines available. To the fashionable world, he was known as "Fortunate Youth." Young, wealthy, handsome, and seemingly blessed by fortune, he was welcomed with open arms. Do not, my dear readers, cry out too loudly against the selfishness of the world for treating the young, the handsome, and the lucky with such favor, while frowning upon us—old, ugly, and miserable creatures. If I have the right to choose my acquaintances—for instance, preferring the company of a lively, handsome, well-dressed gentleman who amuses me over that of a slouching, ill-washed misanthrope, a ceaselessly prating coxcomb, or whatever else—does not society, the aggregate of you and me, possess the same right to its choices?

Harry was liked because he was likeable. He was rich, handsome, jovial, well-born, and well-bred; brave, and fond of a jolly song and a bottle alongside his topers. He loved any game on foot or horseback with the gentlemen sportsmen. With ladies, he possessed a modest, blushing timidity that made him intriguing. To those humbler than himself in station, he was magnificently liberal and eager to spare them annoyance. Our Virginian was indeed very grand, high, and mighty; yet, in those times when distinctions of rank still held sway, maintaining a distance from inferiors brought no unpopularity to a gentleman. Recall that in those days, the Secretary of State knelt when presenting despatches to the King, and the Under-Secretary dared not sit in his chief's presence. If I were Secretary of State, I should dislike kneeling to my audience; if Under-Secretary, standing while the Right Honorable Benjamins or Edwards looked over papers. Yet, *modus in rebus* exists; certain lines must be drawn. I am only half pleased when Bob Bowstreet, whose connection to literature runs through Policemen X and Y, and Tom Garbage, an esteemed contributor to the *Kennel Miscellany*, propose fellowship as brothers-in-arms, slap me on the back, and address me as "Old Boy" or by my Christian name.

During the winter season of 1756–57, the town offered Mr. Warrington everything he could desire for the asking. There were operas, though he took but moderate delight in them (despite the prodigious satire leveled against Italian operas as foolish, Popish, unmanly, and meaningless). There were the theaters featuring Mr. Garrick and Mrs. Pritchard at one house, and Mrs. Clive at another. There were masquerades and ridottos frequented by fine society, along with the private drums and assemblies of lords and ladies, which invariably began and ended with cards—a pastime Mr. Warrington preferred less than White's Club, finding the play there neither so high nor so fair.

One day, his kinsman, Lord Castlewood, took him to court to present him to His Majesty, who had recently arrived in town from Kensington. However, the gracious sovereign either disliked Harry's introducer or harbored other reasons for being sulky. The King merely remarked, "Oh, heard of you from Lady Yarmouth. The Earl of Castlewood"—then turning to his lordship and speaking in German—"shall tell him that he plays too much!" With that, the Defender of the Faith turned his royal back. Lord Castlewood shrank back, frightened by this cold reception.

"What does he say?" asked Harry.
"His Majesty thinks they play too high at White's, and is displeased," whispered the nobleman.
"If he does not want us, we had better not come again, that is all," said Harry, simply. "I never, somehow, considered that German fellow a real King of England."
"Hush! For Heaven's sake, hold your confounded colonial tongue!" cried my lord. "Do you not see the walls here have ears!"
"And what then?" asked Mr. Warrington.
"Why, look at the people! Hang me, if it is not quite a curiosity! They were all shaking hands with me, bowing, and flattering me just now; and now they avoid me as if I were the plague!"

At that moment, a broad-faced, broad-shouldered gentleman in a scarlet-laced waistcoat and a great old-fashioned wig approached.
"Shake hands, nephew," said the gentleman. "I heard what you said. I have ears like the wall, look you. And now, if other people show you the cold shoulder, I'll give you my hand;" and he grasped Harry's with a great brown paw. "Something of my brother about your eyes and face. Though I suppose in your island you grow more wiry and thin. I am thine uncle, child. My name is Sir Miles Warrington. My lord knows me well enough."

Lord Castlewood looked very frightened and yellow. "Yes, my dear Harry. This is your paternal uncle, Sir Miles Warrington."
"Might as well have come to see us in Norfolk, as dangle about playing the fool at Tunbridge Wells, Mr. Warrington, or Mr. Esmond—which do you call yourself?" said the Baronet sharply. "The old lady calls herself Madam Esmond, doesn't she?"
"My mother is not ashamed of her father's name, nor am I, uncle," said Mr. Harry, rather proudly.
"Well said, lad! Come home and eat a bit of mutton with Lady Warrington at three in Hill Street—that is, if you can do without your White's kickshaws. You need not look frightened, my Lord Castlewood! I shall tell no tales out of school."
"I—I am sure Sir Miles Warrington will act as a gentleman!" said my lord, in much perturbation.
"Belike, he will," growled the Baronet, turning on his heel. "And thou wilt come, young man, at three; and mind, good roast mutton waits for nobody. Thou hast a great look of thy father. Lord bless us, how we used to beat each other! He was smaller than me, and in course younger; but many a time he had the best of it. Take it he was henpecked when he married, and Madam Esmond took the spirit out of him when she got him in her island. Virginia is an island. Ain't it an island?"
Harry laughed and said, "No!"
And the jolly Baronet, going off, declared, "Well, island or not, thou must come and tell all about it to my lady. She'll know whether 'tis an island or not."

"My dear Mr. Warrington," said my lord, with an appealing look, "in this great city, every man has enemies, and there is a great deal of detraction and scandal. I never spoke to you about Sir Miles Warrington precisely because I knew him, and because we have had differences. Should he permit himself remarks to my disparagement, you will receive them *cum grano salis*, and remember that it is from an enemy they come."

Thus, the pair walked out of the King's apartments into Saint James's Street. By the time Harry reached White's, the news of his cold reception had already preceded him. The King had turned his back upon him; the King was jealous of Harry's favor with the favorite; Harry was *au mieux* with Lady Yarmouth. A score of gentlemen wished him compliments upon his conquest. Before night, it was a settled matter that this was among the other victories of the Fortunate Youth.

Sir Miles told his wife and Harry as much when the young man appeared at the appointed hour at the Baronet's dinner table, rallying him in his simple rustic fashion. The lady, initially a grand and stately personage, admitted that the reputation the world had made for Harry was so bad that at first she had given him but a frigid welcome. With the young ladies, Sir Miles's daughters, the greetings were "How d'ye do, cousin?" and "No, thank you, cousin," accompanied by numerous prim curtseys.

The little boy, the heir of the house, dined at table under the care of his governor. Having his glass of port by Papa after dinner, he gave loose rein to his innocent tongue and asked many questions of his cousin. Finally, the innocent youth said, after staring hard at Harry's face, "Are you wicked, cousin Harry? You don't look very wicked!"
"My dear Master Miles!" expostulated the tutor, turning very red.
"But you know you said he was wicked!" cried the child.
"We are all miserable sinners, Miley," explained Papa. "Haven't you heard the clergyman say so every Sunday?"
"Yes, but not so very wicked as cousin Harry. Is it true that you gamble, cousin, and drink all night with wicked men, and frequent the company of wicked women? You know you said so, Mr. Walker—and Mamma said so, too, that Lady Yarmouth was a wicked woman."
"And you are a little pitcher," cried Papa: "and my wife, nephew Harry, is a staunch Jacobist—you won't like her the worse for that. Take Miles to his sisters, Mr. Walker, and Topsham shall give thee a ride in the park, child, on thy little horse."

The idea of the little horse consoled Master Miles;

Upon the rejection of his peace offerings, our warlike young American chief resolved to harbor a profound wrath—not merely against Colonel Lambert, but against the entire family of that gentleman. "He has humiliated me before the girls!" the young man fumed internally. "He and Mr. Wolfe, who were constantly preaching morality to me while posturing with airs of superior virtue and protection, have once again held me up to the family as a mere scapegrace and prodigal. They are so sanctimonious they refuse to even shake my hand; and when I attempt to demonstrate a shred of common gratitude, they simply fling my gifts back in my face!"

"Why, sir, the things must be worth a small fortune!" exclaimed Parson Sampson, casting a greedy eye upon the two morocco boxes resting on white satin cushions, filled with Mr. Sparks's golden trinkets.

"They cost some money, Sampson," replied the young man. "Though I would not grudge tenfold the sum to anyone who has shown me kindness."

"No, faith, sir, not if I know your honor!" interjected Sampson, seizing every opportunity to praise his young patron directly to his face.

"The repeater, they told me, was a magnificent bargain, worth a hundred pounds in Paris," Harry continued. "Little Miss Hetty mentioned she longed for a repeating watch."

"Oh, what a lovely piece!" cried the chaplain. "With a small circle of pearls set on the back and a diamond knob for the handle! Why, 'twould win any woman's heart, sir!"
