"There passes an apple-woman with a basket," shouted Mr. Warrington fiercely. "I have a mind to fling the thing out to her!"

When Harry ventured out on business, taking him to the City and the Temple, his parasite did not follow him far into the Strand; turning away instead, citing a terror of Chancery Lane, its inhabitants, and its precincts. Mr. Warrington proceeded to his broker, and they walked to the Bank, conducting some minor transactions. After signing a few trivial documents, Harry departed with a handful of crisp banknotes in his pocket. His broker escorted him to one of the great dining-houses for which the City was renowned, and subsequently guided him along the Virginian Walk on 'Change. Through this thoroughfare, Harry passed with considerable shame. *What would a certain lady in Virginia say,* he wondered, *if she knew that he was carrying off in that bottomless gambler's pocket a significant portion of his father's patrimony?* *Those are all Virginia merchants,* he thought, observing the bustling crowd, *all talking among themselves about me, declaring, 'That is young Esmond of Castlewood, on the Potomac, Madam Esmond's son; and he has been squandering his money at play, selling out so much, so much, and so much.'*

His spirits did not lift until he had passed beneath the Traitors' Heads of Temple Bar and emerged fully from the City. From the Strand, Mr. Harry walked home, glancing briefly into St. James's Street en route, though the houses were empty; the company did not arrive at the Chocolate-House until a later hour.

Once arrived home, Mr. Harry produced his bundle of banknotes. He placed three of them inside a sheet of paper, sealing it carefully after writing within: *"Much good may they do you. H. E. W."* Directing this packet to the Reverend Mr. Sampson, he left it on the chimney-glass, instructing his servants to deliver it to the clergyman upon his arrival.

Now, his honor's phaeton was brought to the door, and he stepped in, intending to drive around the park. However, driven by the onset of rain, an east wind, or some other unforeseen circumstance, he turned his horses' heads down St. James's Street and returned to White's Club by approximately three o'clock. Scarcely anyone had arrived yet; it was the hour when patrons were still dining. Nevertheless, there sat his cousin, Lord Castlewood, lounging over the *Public Advertiser*, having just returned from his duties at Court nearby.

Lord Castlewood yawned over the newspaper. "What shall we do?" he asked. "Shall we play a little piquet?" Harry had no objection to a brief game. "Just for an hour," said Lord Castlewood. "I dine at Arlington Street at four." "Just for an hour," agreed Mr. Warrington, and they summoned the cards.

"Or shall we move upstairs?" suggested my lord. "Away from the noise?"

"Certainly, out of the noise," replied Harry.

By five o'clock, a half-dozen gentlemen had entered after dinner, engaging in cards, coffee, or conversation. The regulars from the ordinary had not yet cleared their tables. Thus, the gentlemen of White's often remained seated until well past midnight.

From behind the coffee-house blinds, a toothpick pointed into the street. "Whose phaeton is that?" asked Toothpick Number 1 of Number 2.

"The Fortunate Youth's," replied Number 2.

"Not so fortunate the last three nights. Luck confoundedly against him. He lost thirteen hundred to the table last night. Has Mr. Warrington been here today, John?"

"Mr. Warrington is in the house now, sir. In the little tea-room with Lord Castlewood since three o'clock. They are playing piquet," answered John.

"What fun for Castlewood!" remarked Number 1, shrugging.

The second gentleman growled an execration. "Curse the fellow!" he spat. "He has no right to be in this club at all. He doesn't pay if he loses. Gentlemen ought not to play with him. Sir Miles Warrington told me at court the other day that Castlewood has owed him money on a bet for three years."

"Castlewood," said Number 1, "doesn't lose if he plays alone. A large company flurries him; that is why he avoids the table." The facetious gentleman grinned, displaying teeth polished to perfection.

"Let's go up and stop them," growled Number 2.

"Why?" asked the other. "It is much better to look out the window. See the lamplighter climbing the ladder—famous sport. Look at that old quiz in the chair: have you ever seen such a specimen?"

"Who is that just leaving the house? By my life, it's Fortunatus! He seems to have forgotten his phaeton has been waiting here all this time. I bet you two to one he has been losing to Castlewood."

"Jack, do you take me for a fool?" demanded one gentleman of the other. "Pretty pair of horses the youth has got. How he is flogging them!" They watched Mr. Warrington gallop up the street, causing terrified coachmen and chairmen to clear the path. Presently, Lord Castlewood was seen entering a hackney carriage and departing.

Harry drove up to his own door. It was but a few yards, and those poor horses had beaten the pavement all this while in the rain. Mr. Gumbo was engaged at the door in conversation with a country-looking lass, who curtsied and hurried away. Mr. Gumbo was perpetually occupied with some pretty maid or other.

"Gumbo, has Mr. Sampson been here?" asked his master from the driving seat.

"No, sar. Mr. Sampson have not been here!" answered Mr. Warrington's servant. Harry instructed him to go upstairs and retrieve a letter addressed to Mr. Sampson.

"Addressed to Mr. Sampson? Oh yes, sir," said Mr. Gumbo, unable to read.

"A sealed letter, stupid! On the mantelpiece, in the glass!" snapped Harry. Gumbo leisurely retired to fetch the document. As soon as Harry secured it, he turned his horses toward St. James's Street. Meanwhile, the two gentlemen still yawning out the window at White's beheld the Fortunate Youth return in an instant.

As they passed out of the little tea-room where he and Lord Castlewood had played piquet, Mr. Warrington observed that several gentlemen had entered the playroom and established a bank. Some were already steadily working, wearing their gaming jackets—a garment the club kept for members wishing to engage in serious nightly play.

Mr. Warrington approached the clerk's desk, settled his account for the previous night, and sat down at the table, requesting fresh counters. This had been decidedly an unlucky week for the Fortunate Youth, and tonight proved no more fortunate than the preceding nights. He called for more counters, and then more. He appeared slightly pale and silent, though remaining easy and polite when spoken to. Yet, he could not win.

Finally, he rose. "Hang it! Stay and mend your luck!" urged Lord March, sitting beside him with a heap of green and white counters. "Take a hundred of mine and go on!"

"I have had enough for tonight, my lord," said Harry, rising and departing. He ate a broiled bone in the coffee-room and walked back to his lodgings sometime around midnight. A man following a great catastrophe commonly sleeps soundly. It is the waking in the morning that proves sometimes queer and unpleasant. Last night you proposed to Miss Brown; you quarreled over your cups with Captain Jones and valorously pulled his nose; you played cards with Colonel Robinson and issued numerous IOUs. These thoughts, accompanied by a severe headache, assailed him

We have all, no doubt, encountered the world with wide-open eyes and witnessed a vast parade of human types. Yet, there exists a particular breed of man—often a favorite subject for satire in novels and plays—that I confess I have rarely, if ever, met in my dealings with this sinful race. I speak of the mere religious hypocrite: the man who preaches endlessly while believing not a single word of his own sermons; the infidel disguised in a broad-brimmed hat and sable cloak, expounding scripture, offering exhortations, pronouncing curses, and bestowing blessings, all without a shred of faith in his own heaven or fear of his own hell.

Consider the candid throngs of "hobnail" shoes clumping toward church on a Sunday evening; consider the rustling maid-servants in their ribbons trailed by young apprentices; the little regiments of schoolboys; the trim young maidens and staid matrons marching with their glistening prayer-books as the chapel bell rings out (likely passing Ebenezer, where the congregation of umbrellas, great bonnets, and pattens is already gathered beneath the flaring gas-lamps). Look at them! How many are true hypocrites, you ask? Perhaps the maid-servant is thinking of her sweetheart; perhaps the grocer is calculating how to buy that parcel of sugar and worrying if the County Bank will accept his notes; perhaps the head-schoolboy is cramming Latin verses for Monday's exercise; perhaps the young rogue is remembering that after service comes his father's exposition at home, followed by pie for supper; perhaps the clerk calling out the psalm has a daughter in trouble and drones through his responses without understanding a syllable; perhaps the very moment the parson hides his face on his cushion, he is thinking of a bill due on Monday.

These people are not heavenly-minded; they are of the world, deeply worldly, and their feet remain firmly planted upon it. Yet, they are not hypocrites, mark you. They possess their religion in some convenient mental locker—a valuable medicine reserved for times of illness. A man administers his nostrum to his neighbor, recommending his private cure for another's ailment. "My dear madam, you suffer from spasms? These drops are infallible!" "You have been drinking too much wine, my good sir? This pill will defy any evil consequence, allowing you to enjoy your port daily." Who are more eager dispensers of spiritual and bodily physic than women? Indeed, a century ago, every lady in the countryside maintained a still-room and a medicine chest filled with pills, powders, and potions for the entire village. My Lady Warrington took charge of the consciences and digestions of her husband's tenants and family alike. She held the faith and health of the servants' hall in her keeping. Whether she knew how to doctor them rightly is for Heaven to judge; but whether it was pill or doctrine, she administered either with equal conviction in her own authority, and her disciples swallowed both obediently. She believed herself to be one of the most virtuous, self-denying, wise, and learned women in the world. By constantly dunning this opinion into the ears of everyone around her, she succeeded in convincing many to join her persuasion.

At Sir Miles's dinner, with its magnificent sideboard of plate and numerous men in livery, it required a considerable amount of mental fortitude to realize that the beer served in the splendid tankard was of the poorest quality, and that there was merely one joint of mutton on the grand silver dish. When Sir Miles toasted the King's health and smacked his jolly lips over his wine, he regarded the liquor and the company as if it were ambrosia. He asked Harry Warrington if they drank port like that in Virginia. He claimed it was nothing compared to the wine Harry should taste in Norfolk. He praised the vintage so highly that Harry almost believed it was good, winking into his own glass in an attempt to discern the merits his uncle perceived in the ruby nectar.

Just as we observe in many well-regulated families of the present day, the Warringtons possessed their two paragons. Among the two grown daughters, one was the greatest beauty, and the other the greatest genius and angel of any young lady alive, according to Lady Warrington's description to Harry. The eldest, the Beauty, was engaged to dear Tom Claypool, a fact the fond mother shared with her cousin Harry in confidence. However, the second daughter—the Genius and the Angel—was forever setting herself upon our young friend to improve his wits and morals. She sang to him at the harpsichord (though rather out of tune for an angel, Harry noted); she offered advice, instruction, and conversation—with almost too much instruction and advice, Harry felt, preferring instead the society of the little cousin who reminded him of Fanny Mountain at home. Yet, after dinner, the last-mentioned young maiden usually retired to her nursery. Beauty departed on her own avocations; the mother attended to her correspondence or wrote voluminous letters; the father dozed in his armchair; and the Genius remained to keep her young cousin company.

The calm of the house somehow pleased the young man, and he liked to take refuge there, away from the riot and dissipation in which he ordinarily lived. Certainly, no welcome could be kinder than that which he received. Doors were opened to him at all hours. If Flora was not at home, Dora was ready to receive him. Within a few days of acquaintance, he and his little cousin Miles had gone for a galloping match in the Park. Harry, kind and generous to every man who crossed his path, had even considered purchasing a small horse for his cousin, far superior to the one the boy currently rode, until circumstances arose that brought all our poor Harry's coaches and horses to a sudden halt.

Though Sir Miles Warrington had imagined Virginia to be an island, the ladies were much better instructed in geography and were eager to hear from Harry about his home and native country. On his part, he was not averse to talking about it. He described the length and breadth of his estate, the rivers that bordered it, and the produce it yielded. Having practiced surveying with a friend in his boyhood, he drew a map of his county, marking fine towns here and there—which, in truth, were but log-huts (though, for the honor of his country, he wished them to appear as handsome as possible). There was the Potomac; there was the James River; there were the wharves from which his mother's ships and tobacco were brought to the sea. In reality, the estate was as large as a county. He did not boast excessively about the place. Seeing the handsome young fellow, dressed in a fine suit of velvet and silver lace, making his draughts, pointing out hills, forests, and towns, one might have imagined him a traveling prince describing the realms of his mother, the Queen. He almost fancied himself to be so at times. He had miles where gentlemen in England had mere acres.

Not only did Dora listen, but the beauteous Flora also bowed her fair head and heard him with attention. Why, what was young Tom Claypool, the brother baronet's son in Norfolk, with his great boots, his booming voice, and his inheritance of a mere five thousand acres, compared to this young American prince and charming stranger? As an angel, Dora began to lose her temper and started twitting Flora for flirting. Claypool, in his red waistcoat, sat dumbfounded before the splendid Harry in his ruffles and laces, listening to discussions of March, Chesterfield, Selwyn, Bolingbroke, and the entire company of macaronis.

Mamma began to love Harry more and more as a son. She grew anxious about the spiritual welfare of the poor Indians and the unfortunate negroes in Virginia. What could she do to help dear Madam Esmond (a precious woman, she knew) in this good work? She had a serious butler and housekeeper; they were delighted with the spiritual behavior and sweet musical gifts of Gumbo. "Ah! Harry, Harry! You have been a sad wild boy! Why did you not come sooner to us, sir, and not waste your time among spendthrifts and the vain world? But 'tis not yet too late. We must reclaim thee, dear Harry! Mustn't we, Sir Miles? Mustn't we, Dora? Mustn't we, Flora?"

The three ladies all looked up to the ceiling. They would reclaim the dear prodigal. It remained to be seen which would succeed most. Dora sat by watching Flora. As for Mamma, when the girls were away, she spoke to him more and more seriously, more and more tenderly. She would be a mother to him in the absence of his own admirable parent. She gave him a hymn-book. She kissed him on the forehead. She was actuated by the purest love, tenderness, and religious regard toward her dear, wayward, wild, yet amiable nephew.

While these sentimentalities unfolded, it is presumed that Mr. Warrington kept his own counsel regarding his affairs outdoors, which we have seen were in the very worst condition. He, who had been favored by fortune for so many weeks, was suddenly deserted by her, and a few days sufficed to knock down his heap of winnings. Did we say that my Lord Castlewood, his own kinsman, had dealt unfairly with the young Virginian, robbing him during a couple of afternoons of private play? We would insinuate nothing so disrespectful to his lordship's character; but he had won from Harry every sh

A profound sense of remorse for his past follies surged within Harry Warrington as he realized he was a prisoner in a dismal lock-up. Anger and indignation flared at the humiliation of arrest, yet he quickly convinced himself that this misery would be fleeting. He possessed twenty friends capable of securing his release; the only dilemma was deciding which one to approach. There was Mr. Draper, the pragmatic businessman who had previously bowed low to him; his benevolent uncle, the Baronet, who had offered his estate as Harry's home and loved him as a son; his cousin Castlewood, from whom he had gambled away vast sums; his aristocratic cronies at the Chocolate House; and his good Aunt Bernstein. Any of these would undoubtedly intervene, though some relatives might also deliver a stern lecture regarding his imprudence.

The paramount concern, however, was discretion. Mr. Warrington feared that the public knowledge of a gentleman of his stature undergoing such a vulgar procedure would cause a scandal. Yet, he reasoned cynically that secrecy was an illusion. "As if the world does not eventually learn everything it cares to know!" he mused. "As if the dinner I enjoy today or the hole in my stocking right now remains hidden from some prying enemy, despite my locked door and polished boots?" He acknowledged that while society could pry into details, there was a solace in the realization that the world ultimately did not care. Scandal was merely vanity. One should tumble down, brush off the mud, smile, and proceed as if nothing had happened. Society would continue to eat, joke, and yawn regardless of whether one graced itself or disgraced itself. To think otherwise was sheer conceit.

Yet, Mr. Harry Warrington chafed and fumed as though the entire world was trembling with anticipation of his downfall.

*"What a sensation my arrest must have caused at the club!"* Harry thought bitterly. *"Mr. Selwyn will surely be cracking jokes about my misfortune. Everyone at the table will know. March will tremble over the bet I owe him, and I shall struggle to pay it. They will cheer at the Savage Tavern, calling me 'the Prisoner.' How can I ever face the world again? Whom shall I ask for help?"*

With a mixture of sharp intellect and naive simplicity, Harry resolved not to contact his relations or his noble friends at White's immediately. Instead, he decided to seek the counsel of Sampson, the chaplain, who had faced similar predicaments before. As dawn broke—delayed in Harry's perception as if the sun had forgotten Cursitor Street—he dispatched a messenger to Long Acre, begging the reverend for advice and comfort.

Unbeknownst to Harry, sending for Sampson was akin to declaring, "I have fallen among lions; come down into the pit with me." Harry likely assumed Sampson's troubles were resolved or simply remained too absorbed in his own chaos to consider his neighbor's plight. Once the message was sent, the captive felt slightly relieved and ordered breakfast. When the attendant asked if he wished to dine privately or join other gentlemen at Mrs. Bailiff's table, Harry firmly declined. He intended to leave before dinner time. The chamberlain, skeptical of such declarations from young gentlemen, closed the door, remarking that there was good beef and carrots available at two o'clock if Harry chose to stay.

While Harry awaited Sampson, his messenger returned with a note stating the chaplain would arrive shortly. However, ten o'clock passed, then eleven, then noon, and Sampson did not appear. Just as despair set in, Gumbo, Harry's enslaved valet, burst in carrying a portmanteau of his master's clothes. Roaring with feigned grief, Gumbo threw himself at Harry's feet, vowing to sell himself into slavery anew rather than allow his beloved master to suffer.

Touched by the display, Harry commanded Gumbo to rise and assist him with dressing, strictly ordering him to keep silent about the arrest. "Mind you, not a word, sir, to anyone!" Harry insisted severely.

"Oh no, sir! Never to anybody!" Gumbo declared solemnly, proceeding to dress his master with meticulous care. He powdered Harry's hair and arranged his attire so perfectly that Harry looked as though he were stepping directly into a carriage bound for St. James's Palace.

Gumbo performed every task love and servility allowed, yet he lacked the power to alter the past or maintain his silence. The nature of slavery, both black and white, relied heavily on deception; servants often hid behind subterfuge like hunted animals seeking cover. These were strange relics of feudalism, where domestics became hypocrites, unable to speak the whole truth to their masters. Despite swearing an oath of silence, Gumbo's loquacious nature betrayed him. With a heart full of genuine sorrow but a tongue incapable of restraint, he had already broadcast the news to countless acquaintances, particularly those wearing shoulder knots and worsted lace. He had spread the tale to Colonel Lambert's and Lord Wrotham's households, boasted at the footman's club frequented by high nobility, recounted the story while drinking ale in Sir Miles Warrington's butler's room, and finally visited Madame Bernstein's circle and Lord Castlewood's household. Thus, when Gumbo later swore to his master that he would tell "nobody," the credibility of his claim was nonexistent.

By mid-afternoon, still no Sampson had arrived. Finally, a letter bearing a damp seal and fresh ink reached Harry. It read:

*"Oh, sir, dear sir, I have done all that a man can at the command and in the behalf of his patron! You did not know, sir, to what you were subjecting me, did you? Else, if I was to go to prison, why did I not share yours, and why am I in a lock-up house three doors off?*

*Such is the fact. As I was hastening to you, knowing full well the danger to which I was subject... I was seized by two villains who had a writ against me, and who have lodged me at Naboth's, hard by, and so close to your honour, that we could almost hear each other across the garden walls of the respective houses where we are confined.*

*I had much and of importance to say, which I do not care to write down on paper regarding your affairs. May they mend! May my cursed fortunes, too, better themselves, is the prayer of—*

Since Sampson refused to speak personally, the reader must be informed of the matters the chaplain deemed unworthy of paper.

Sampson remained unaware of the full extent of Harry's calamity until receiving the letter and messenger from Chancery Lane. Still ardent in his gratitude for the service Harry had recently rendered him, the chaplain eagerly sought a way to aid his distressed patron. Knowing the large sum Lord Castlewood had won from his cousin, Sampson rushed to the nobleman's residence, hoping to stir pity. He delivered an eloquent and moving plea regarding his kinsman's misfortune, speaking with genuine kindness and sympathy. However, his efforts failed to move Lord Castlewood.

"My lord peevishly and curtly put a stop to the chaplain's passionate pleading," wrote the narrative. "Did I not tell you, two days since, when you came for money, that I was as poor as a beggar, Sampson?' said his lordship. 'Has anybody left me a fortune since? The little sum I won from my cousin was swallowed up by others. I not only can't help Mr. Warrington, but, as I pledge you my word, not being in the least aware of his calamity, I had positively written to him this morning to ask him to help me.'" Indeed, a letter to this effect had reached Harry's lodgings via the penny post.

"I must get him money, my lord," cried the chaplain desperately. "Were I to pawn my cassock and bands, he must have money."

"Amen. Go and pawn your bands, your cassock, anything you please. Your enthusiasm does you credit," replied the lord, resuming his reading while Sampson departed in deep despondency.

Meanwhile, Lady Maria had heard rumors of the chaplain's meeting with her brother and guessed the subject. Seizing him as he exited the futile interview, she drew him into the dining room, her face marked by profound grief. "Tell me, what is this has happened to Mr. Warrington?" she asked.

"Your ladyship, then, knows?" asked the chaplain.

"Have I not been in mortal anxiety ever since his servant brought the dreadful news last night?" Lady Maria countered. "We had it as we came from the opera—from my Lady Yarmouth's box—my lord, my Lady Castlewood, and I."

"His lordship, then, did know?" continued Sampson.

"Benson told the news when we came from the playhouse to our tea," confirmed Lady Maria.

Patience snapped for the chaplain. "This is too bad," he exclaimed, swearing, and recounted his frustrating conversation with Lord Castlewood, detailing the nobleman's refusal to help his cousin despite winning huge sums from him, all while ignoring Harry's past generosity toward the chaplain himself.

Lady Maria then erupted into a furious tirade against her own family. Though rarely truthful, when familial disputes arose, she revealed shocking facts. With tears, imprecations, and language far stronger than polite society tolerated, she lashed out at the history of her noble lineage, questioning why God had created such monsters

Mr. Gumbo's generous and compassionate conduct succeeded in soothing the angry heart of his master. Consequently, Harry's second night in the spunging-house was passed with considerably less bitterness than the first. There was, at last, someone there to assist and sympathize with him. Yet, despite this softening in one specific corner of his soul, Harry's heart remained hard and proud toward nearly everyone else in the world. He viewed them all as selfish and ungenerous. His pious Aunt Warrington, his lordly friend March, and his cynical cousin Castlewood—all had been tested and found wanting. He would not stoop to ask another favor of any of them merely to avoid twenty years of prison. What a fool he had been to believe their promises and confide in their friendship! There was no friendship in this cursed, cold, selfish country. He resolved to leave it. He would trust no Englishman, great or small.

His options were clear: he might go to Germany to make a campaign with the King, or return home to Virginia. There, he could bury himself in the woods, spend his days hunting, become his mother's factor and land-steward, and marry either Polly Broadbent or Fanny Mountain. He could turn into a regular tobacco-grower and farmer. He would do anything rather than remain among these English "fine gentlemen."

So, he rose with an outwardly cheerful countenance but an angry spirit. Early in the morning, the faithful Gumbo attended to his master's chamber, having traveled from Bond Street to retrieve Mr. Harry's correspondence.

"I wanted to bring some more clothes," honest Gumbo said, "but Mr. Ruff, the landlord, refused to let me take any more."

Harry had no desire to examine the letters immediately. He opened one, then two, then three; they were all bills. When he opened a fourth, it was from the landlord, stating that no further items belonging to Mr. Warrington could leave the house. Unless his bill was paid, the landlord would sell Mr. W.'s goods to settle the debt and evict his black servant, forcing him to sleep elsewhere. Harry hesitated to let Gumbo remove his own clothes and portmanteau. However, the black man reported finding refuge elsewhere—among friends at Lord Wrotham's house.

"With Colonel Lambert's people," Gumbo observed, looking intently at his master. "And Miss Hetty fell into a faint when she heard you were taken up. Mr. Lambert is a very good man; he told me this morning, 'Gumbo, tell your master if he wants me, send to me, and I shall come.'"

Harry was touched to learn that Hetty had been distressed by his misfortune. Though he did not fully believe Gumbo's account of her fainting—he was accustomed to translating his black man's speech while allowing for exaggeration—the mention of the Colonel darkened the young Virginian's spirit anew.

*"I am to send to Lambert,"* Harry thought, grinding his teeth. *"The man who insulted me and threw my presents back in my face! Even if I were starving, I would not ask him for a crust!"*

Shortly thereafter, dressed and ready, Mr. Warrington requested breakfast and dispatched Gumbo with a brief note to Mr. Draper in the Temple, demanding the attorney's immediate attendance.

"The note was as haughty as if he were writing to one of his negroes, not to a freeborn English gentleman," Draper remarked later. Indeed, Harry had always treated the lawyer with insufferable condescension. "It's all very well for a fine gentleman to give himself airs, but for a fellow in a spunging-house! Hang him!" Draper muttered, adding, "I've a great mind not to go!"

Nevertheless, Mr. Draper did go. He found Mr. Warrington in his misfortune even more arrogant than during the days of his utmost prosperity. Mr. W. sat on his bed like a lord, wearing a splendid gown with his hair meticulously dressed. He motioned for Gumbo to fetch a chair for the lawyer.

"Excuse me, madam, but such haughtiness and airs I am not accustomed to!" exclaimed the outraged attorney.

"Take a chair and proceed with your story, my good Mr. Draper!" replied Madame de Bernstein, smiling as she listened to the proceedings. She was amused by the lawyer's anger and liked her nephew for being insolent in adversity.

The course Draper was to follow had been arranged the previous day between the Baroness and her man of business. Draper was generally an able man, likely to serve a client well, but he failed in this instance because he was piqued and angry—or, more likely, because he could not comprehend the gentleman with whom he had to deal.

*I presume that whoever reads these pages is the most gentle of readers.* *Gentleman, as you unquestionably are, have you not remarked in your dealings with those who are no gentlemen that you often offend them without knowing how or why?* *So the man who is no gentleman offends you in a thousand ways of which the poor creature has no idea. He does or says something that provokes your scorn. He perceives that scorn (always watching himself, uneasy about his manners) and rages. You speak to him naturally, and he fancies you are sneering. You hold indifference toward him, but he hates you, and hates you the worse because you do not care.*

"Gumbo, a chair for Mr. Draper!" commanded Mr. Warrington, folding his brocaded dressing-gown around his legs as he sat on the dingy bed. "Sit down, if you please, and let us discuss my business. Much obliged to you for coming so soon in reply to my message. Have you heard of this piece of ill-luck?"

Draper knew of the circumstances. "Bad news travels quick, Mr. Warrington," he said. "I was eager to offer my humble services the moment you required them. Your friends and family will be deeply pained that a gentleman of your rank should be in such a position."

"I have been very imprudent, Mr. Draper. I have lived beyond my means." (Mr. Draper bowed politely.) "I played with gentlemen much richer than myself, and a cursed run of bad luck has carried away all my ready money, leaving me with liabilities exceeding five hundred pounds."

"Five hundred now in the office," noted Mr. Draper. "Well, this is such a trifling sum that I thought, by sending to one or two friends yesterday, I could have paid the debt and gone home without further ado. I have been mistaken. I shall thank you to kindly put me in the way of raising the money as soon as possible."

Mr. Draper sighed gravely and pulled a long face. "Why, sir, it can be done!" said Mr. Warrington, staring at the lawyer.

Indeed, it could be done. Mr. Draper had proposed to Madame Bernstein the day prior to instantly paying the money and releasing Mr. Warrington. That lady had declared her intention to make the young gentleman her heir. Like the rest of the world, Draper believed Harry's hereditary property in Virginia to be vast both in value and extent. He had notes in his pocket and Madame Bernstein's order to pay them under certain conditions. Nevertheless, when Harry simply stated, "It can be done!" Draper pulled his long face again and said, "It can be done in time, sir; but it will require a considerable period. To touch the property in England which belongs to you upon Mr. George Warrington's death, we must prove the event and release the trustees. Who is to do either? Lady Esmond Warrington in Virginia, of course, will not allow her son to remain in prison, but we must wait six months before hearing from her. Does your Bristol agent have authority to honor your drafts?"

"He is only authorized to pay me two hundred pounds a year," replied Mr. Warrington.

"I suppose I have no resource, then, but to apply to my aunt, Madame de Bernstein. She will be my security."

"Her ladyship will do anything for you, sir; she has said so to me, often and often," the lawyer assured him. "If she gives the word at this very moment, you can walk out of this place."

"Then go to her, Mr. Draper. I did not wish to trouble my relations, but rather than continue in this horrible, needless imprisonment, I must speak to her. Tell her where I am and what has befallen me. Disguise nothing! And tell her that I confide in her affection and kindness to release me from this... this disgrace," Mr. Warrington's voice shaking slightly as he passed his hand across his eyes.

"Sir," said Mr. Draper, eyeing the young man, "I was with her ladyship yesterday when we discussed the whole of this most unpleasant—I won't say, as you do, disgraceful business."

"What do you mean, sir? Does Madame de Bernstein know of my misfortune?" asked Harry.

"Every circumstance, sir; the pawning of the watches and all," the lawyer confirmed.

Harry turned burning red. "It is an unfortunate business, the pawning of those watches and things which you had never paid for," continued the lawyer.

The young man sprang up from the bed, looking so fierce that Draper felt a momentary alarm. "It may lead to litigation and unpleasant remarks in court, sir. Those barristers respect nothing; and when they get

Driven from his morning's discourse with Harry by sheer wrath, Mr. Draper believed he heard the young prisoner muttering behind him. In truth, Harry had risen and let out a half-choked cry to recall the lawyer, but pride held him fast while the other remained offended. Reluctant to humble himself before a man he despised, and unwilling to yield merely due to a lack of funds, Harry checked his words, though Draper showed no sign of stopping.

*"An hour hence will suffice,"* Harry thought, sinking sulkingly back onto the bed. *No, he cared nothing for Maria Esmond!* Shame burned within him for the manner in which he had been ensnared into that engagement. She was a cunning, seasoned woman who had exploited his boyish ardor, taking unfair advantage of him just as her brother had at cards. They were his own flesh and blood, yet instead of sparing him, both had made prey of him, using him for their selfish ends. He recalled how they had violated the sanctity of hospitality, turning the trusting young kinsman who entered their gates into a victim. His heart felt sorely wounded; his head fell back against the pillow, and bitter tears soaked the linen.

*"Had they come to Virginia,"* he murmured, *"I would have offered them a far different welcome!"*

Gumbo's grinning face suddenly appeared at the doorway, announcing that a lady had arrived to see Master Harry. Following the lad came the lady in the capuchin, previously mentioned. Harry sat up, pale and haggard. Before the servant could fully withdraw, the lady rushed toward the young prisoner, overcome with sobs. With genuine emotion and maternal tenderness, she threw her arms around his neck, weeping over his pallid cheek and kissing it amidst a flood of tears.

"Oh, my Harry! Did I ever, ever think to see thee here?"

Harry recoiled, startled by her sudden presence, but she sank down beside the bed, seizing his feverish hand and embracing his knees. There was a profound regard and tenderness in her gaze. The wretched state in which she found him, his miserable appearance, filled her heart with sincere love and pity.

"I—I thought none of you would come!" poor Harry groaned.

More tears, more kisses upon his hot young hand, more tight embraces formed her reply for a moment or two. "Oh, my dear! My dear! I cannot bear to think of thee in misery," she sobbed. Though hardened by circumstance, her heart was not entirely marble; her dreary life was not a barren desert. Harry's mother could not have been fonder, nor her tones more tender than those of his kinswoman now kneeling at his feet.

"Some of the debts, I fear, were owing to my extravagance!" she confessed—and this was true. "You bought trinkets and jewels solely to please me. Oh, how I hate them now! I little thought I ever could! I have brought them all with me, and more trinkets—here! and here! and every coin I possess in the world!"

She poured brooches, rings, a watch, and a score of guineas into Harry's lap. The sight strangely agitated and deeply moved the young man.

"Dearest, kindest cousin!" he sobbed. Words failed him, yet his expression conveyed his gratitude, his affection, and his overwhelming emotion. Soon, however, he grew cheerful, smiling as he tucked away some of the gifts intended for Maria, recounting the dangers he had faced by selling goods purchased on credit and detailing how a lawyer had insulted him moments prior regarding these very matters. He refused Maria's money—he had enough for the present—but he valued her twenty guineas as highly as if they were twenty thousand. He vowed never to forget her love and kindness; by all that was sacred, he would not! He must tell his mother of her goodness. It had buoyed him when he stood on the brink of breaking under disgrace and misery. May Heaven bless her for it!

There is no need to prolong the cousins' conversation. After Maria's visit, the dark day seemed brighter to Harry; the imprisonment felt less burdensome. The world was not wholly selfish and cold. Here was a loving soul who truly cherished him. Even Castlewood was not as terrible as he had imagined. The Baroness had expressed the deepest grief at her inability to aid her kinsman, noting she was hopelessly in debt, having lost every shilling she had won from Harry to others the following day. Yet, anything within her power, she would do. She promised to visit Mr. Warrington soon; he was currently in waiting, imprisoned alongside Harry. Thus, the pair conversed cheerfully and affectionately until twilight descended. Then, with a sigh, Maria bid Harry farewell.

Scarcely had the door closed upon her when it swung open again to admit Draper.
"Your humble servant, sir," the attorney said.

His voice grated on Harry's ears, and his presence offended the young man.
"I had expected you some hours ago, sir," Harry replied curtly.

"A lawyer's time is not always his own, sir," Mr. Draper retorted, having just consulted with a bottle of port at the Grecian. "Never mind, I am at your orders now. Presume it is settled, Mr. Warrington? You are still in your bedgown. Let me descend to finalize arrangements while you summon your black man to freshen up. I have a coach at the door, and we shall depart to dine with the old lady."

"Are you going to dine with the Baroness de Bernstein, pray?"

"Not me—no such honor. I have already dined. It is you who are dining with your aunt, I suppose?"

"Mr. Draper, you suppose far more than you know," Mr. Warrington said fiercely, folding his brocade dressing gown tightly around himself.

"Goodness gracious, sir, what do you mean?" Draper asked.

"I mean, sir, that I have reconsidered. Having given my word to a faithful and honorable lady, it becomes me not to withdraw it."

"Confound it, sir!" shrieked the lawyer. "I tell you she has lost the paper! There is nothing binding you—absolutely nothing. Besides, she is old enough to be—"

"Enough, sir!" Mr. Warrington stamped his foot. "You seem to believe you are speaking to some other pettifogger. I take it, Mr. Draper, you are not accustomed to dealing with men of honor."

"Pettifogger, indeed!" Draper cried in a fury. "Men of honor, indeed! Let me inform you, Mr. Warrington, that I am as honorable a man as you. Perhaps I do not know so many gamblers and horse-jockeys. I have not gambled away my patrimony and lived like a nobleman on two hundred a year. I have not bought watches on credit and pawned—" Touch me if you dare, sir!" The lawyer sprang toward the door.

"That is the exit, sir. You cannot pass through the window, for it is barred," Mr. Warrington stated calmly.

"And the answer I give to my client is 'No,' then!" Draper screamed.

Harry stepped forward, fists clenched. "If you utter another word, I'll—"

The door slammed shut rapidly; the sentence was never finished. Draper departed, furious, heading to Madame de Bernstein. Though he delivered the most damning version of his story, he received even harsher language from the Baroness than he had from Mr. Warrington himself.

"What? Shall she trust me, and I desert her?" Harry paced his room, his flowing brocade rustling loudly. "Dear, faithful, generous woman! If I lie in prison for years, I shall remain true to her."

Once the lawyer left after the stormy interview, the desolate old woman was forced to sit down to the meal she had hoped to share with her nephew. The chair before her, meant for him, remained empty. Glasses gleamed beside the silverware. Dish after dish was placed before her by the silent major-domo, tasted, and pushed aside. Finally, the man pressed his mistress.

"It is eight o'clock," he said. "You have eaten nothing all day. It is good for you to eat."

She could not eat. She desired only coffee. "Let Case fetch her coffee."

The lacquies removed the dishes, leaving their mistress seated before the vacant chair. Shortly thereafter, the old servant re-entered without the coffee, his face etched with strange terror.

"MR. WARRINGTON!" he shouted.

The old woman gasped, rising from her armchair only to sink back down, trembling violently. "So you are come, sir, are you?" she whispered, her voice shaking. "Bring back the—Ah!" Here she screamed, "Gracious God, who is it?"

Her eyes widened wildly; her white face looked ghastly beneath her rouge. Clinging to the arms of her chair for support, she watched as the visitor approached. A gentleman whose face and figure mirrored Harry Warrington exactly, and whose voice possessed strangely similar tones, had followed the servant into the room. He bowed toward the Baroness.

"You expected my brother, madam?" he asked. "I have only just arrived in London. I went to his residence. I encountered his servant at your door, carrying this letter for you. I thought I would deliver it to your ladyship before proceeding to him

"Quick, coachman! Hackney-coach steeds, and bear George Warrington through the Strand and Fleet Street to his imprisoned brother's rescue!"

Any traveler who recalls Hogarth's famous depiction of a London hackney-coach and the streets of that era can easily imagine the weariness of the pace and the lengthening of the journey. There were scarce any lights save those carried by link-boys; the coaches were poorly hung, the pavements treacherous, riddled with great holes and vast quagmires of winter mud. Yet, to our young man, this frantic drive from Piccadilly to Fleet Street seemed almost as interminable as the arduous journey from Marlborough to London he had undertaken earlier that day.

George had already written to Harry, announcing his arrival in Bristol. Prior to that, he had penned a letter to his brother revealing the great news of his own existence and his miraculous return from captivity. At that time, war raged between England and France; French privateers were perpetually on the lookout for British merchant vessels, seizing them often within sight of port. The letter carrying intelligence of George's restoration must have been aboard one of the numerous American ships captured by the French. Conversely, the letter detailing George's safe arrival in England was never opened by poor Harry. It lay undisturbed in his apartments, reaching him on the third morning after his captivity, only to be ignored when the irascible Mr. Ruff refused to surrender any further items of his lodger's property.

Upon arriving in London, George immediately sought his brother. When the maid-servant answered the door, terrified by the striking resemblance between the two brothers, she screamed and fled to her mistress. The mistress, unwilling to admit the truth—that poor Harry was actually a prisoner held by her husband—deceived him, claiming Mr. Warrington had simply vacated his lodgings and offering no clue as to his whereabouts.

George knew Clarges Street was near Bond Street. He had studied the London maps countless times. Perhaps Aunt Bernstein could tell him where Harry was; she might even be holding him at that very moment. George recalled Harry's letters to Virginia, which spoke warmly of Aunt Bernstein's kindness. Indeed, even Madam Esmond had been softened by it, particularly moved by a specific letter from the Baroness—the very correspondence that had spurred George to hasten to Europe. He fervently hoped and trusted that Madam Beatrix had finally found cause to repent of her former wicked ways. It was time, certainly, given her age; heaven knew she had ample reason to regret her past. One had witnessed harmless, good souls of eighty still pummeled and stoned by irreproachable ladies of the strictest Pharisaic sect for minor slips committed decades prior. Rachel Esmond never mentioned her eldest daughter, nor did Madam Esmond Warrington mention her sister. No. Despite orders for the remission of sentences and the handwriting found on the floor of the Temple, there existed crimes that some folk never forgave, regarding which female virtue, above all else, remained inexorable.

Perhaps the Virginian agent in Bristol had fed George fearful tales of his brother's conduct. Gumbo, whom George had encountered at his aunt's door once the lad recovered from his terror at seeing his presumed-dead master, had managed to stammer out a few words concerning his young master's location and pitiable state. Hence, Mr. George's stern demeanor upon presenting himself to the old lady. To him, it seemed a matter of course that a brother in difficulty should be rescued by his relations.

"Oh, George, how little you know of London and its ways!" he chided himself internally. "Whenever you take your walks abroad, how many poor souls you meet—if a philanthropist attempted to rescue every one of them, not all the wealth of the American provinces would suffice!"

Yet, the feeling and agitation displayed by the old lady touched her nephew's heart. Jolting through the dark streets toward the house of his brother's captivity, George began to reconsider his judgment. *"She does feel my poor Harry's misfortune,"* he thought. *"I have been too hasty in judging her."* Time and again in his life, Mr. George had been forced to rebuke himself for the crime of rash judgment. How many of us have not? Alas, once mischief is done, no repentance can fully mend it.

"Quick, coachman! We are almost as slow as you are in getting from Clarges Street to the Temple."

Poor Gumbo knew the way to the bailiff's house well enough. Once more, the bell rang. The first door opened to George and his negro servant; then, warily locked upon them, they found themselves in a narrow passage guarded by a little Jewish janitor. A second door unlocked, and they entered the house. The janitor stared, his flaring tallow-torch illuminating a second Mr. Warrington before him.

"Come to see that gentleman?" the janitor asked.

"Yes. But wait a moment. This is Mr. Warrington's brother from America."

Gumbo was sent to prepare his master. "Step into this room," the porter directed. "There is a gentleman already here attending to Mr. W.'s business, and another upstairs with him now. There has been no end of people about him."

The apartment, known as Mr. Amos's office, was small. By the light of a guttering candle, a stout gentleman in a cloak and laced hat sat talking to the bailiff. The young porter preceded Mr. George, carrying a candle, ensuring sufficient illumination.

"We are not angry anymore, Harry!" the stout gentleman exclaimed in a cheery voice, rising and advancing with an outstretched hand toward the newcomer. "Thank God, my boy! Mr. Amos here says there will be no difficulty about James and me acting as your bail, and we shall settle your business by breakfast tomorrow. Why... Angels and ministers of grace! Who are you?"

He recoiled slightly as the other gripped his hand firmly.

"God bless you, sir!" the stranger said. "I know who you are. You must be Colonel Lambert, of whose kindness to him my poor Harry wrote. And I am the brother you have heard of, sir; the one left for dead in Mr. Braddock's action, who came to life again after eighteen months among the French, and who lives today to thank God and thank you for your kindness to my Harry." His voice faltered.

"James! James! Here is news!" cried Mr. Lambert to a gentleman in red who now entered the room. "Here are the dead come alive! Here is Harry Scapegrace's brother returned, and with his scalp intact on his head!" (George had removed his hat and stood by the light.)

"This is my brother-bail, Mr. Warrington! This is Lieutenant-Colonel James Wolfe, at your service. You must understand there has been a slight difference between Harry and me, Mr. George. He is pacified, is he, James?"

"He is full of gratitude," replied Mr. Wolfe, bowing respectfully to Mr. Warrington.

"Harry wrote home about Mr. Wolfe, too, sir," the young man added. "And I hope my brother's friends will extend the same kindness to me."

"I wish he had none other than us, Mr. Warrington. Poor Harry's fine folks have been too fine for him, ending by landing him here."

"Nay, your honors, I have done my best to make the young gentleman comfortable. Knowing your honor before, when you bailed Captain Watkins, and knowing your security is perfectly good—if your honor wishes, the young gentleman can leave tonight, and I shall straighten things out with the lawyer in the morning," said Harry's landlord, recognizing the rank and respectability of the two gentlemen offering bail.

"The debt is five hundred and odd pounds, I think?" asked Mr. Warrington.

"With a hundred thanks to these gentlemen, I can pay the amount at this very moment into the officers' hands, taking the usual acknowledgment and caution. But I can never forget, gentlemen, that you helped my brother in his need. For doing so, I say thank you, and God bless you, in my mother's name and mine."

Meanwhile, Gumbo had ascended to his master's apartment. Harry would likely have scolded the negro for returning that late, but the young gentleman was deeply soothed and touched by the conversation he had just shared with the friend who had departed. He sat over his pipe of Virginia tobacco in a somber mood. Somehow, even Maria's goodness and affection, as recently exhibited, had not entirely consoled him; thoughts of certain consequences bound to that very kindness and fidelity filled him with dismay. Then, Mr. Wolfe's homely features and eager, outstretched hand appeared to cheer the prisoner. He heard how Mr. Lambert was below and the urgent errand of the two officers.

In spite of himself, Lambert would be kind. In spite of Harry's ill-temper, needless suspicion, and anger, the good gentleman was determined to help him if he could—even against Mr. Wolfe's own advice.

"For you were wrong, Mr. Warrington," the Colonel stated frankly. "You wouldn't be set right. You, a young man, used hard words and unkind behavior to your senior, and what is more, one of the best gentlemen who walks God's earth. You see, sir, what his answer has been to your wayward temper. Will you bear with a friend who speaks frankly? Martin Lambert has acted thus as he always does—as the best Christian

When we were first presented to Sir Miles Warrington at the King's Drawing-Room in St. James's Palace, I confess that I, for one—casting my eye upon his jovial, round visage, his ample waistcoat, and his hearty, rustic demeanor—anticipated that I had finally discovered a most eligible and agreeable acquaintance. I was ready to become intimate with what many consider the noblest specimen of humanity: the celebrated "Good Old Country Gentleman." Indeed, to be such a figure is to occupy a station nearest the gods, atop the pinnacle of earthly bliss.

Imagine possessing a vast, unencumbered estate whose rents flow in regularly, collected by adoring farmers who bless their stars for having such a landlord. Imagine having no tenant withhold payment unless it serves merely as an occasion for the Good Old Country Gentleman to display his sublime charity and universal benevolence. Hunt three days a week, love the sport above all else, and maintain perfect health and a voracious appetite. Enjoy not only a good appetite but a magnificent dinner. Sit in church amidst a chorus of blessings from the villagers, revered as the first man in the parish and its chief benefactor, while maintaining a conscious humility—a "miserable sinner"—solely for form's sake, to provide an example for others. Such a person is healthy, wealthy, jolly, deeply respected by the vicar, honored by tenants, and beloved by his family, whose laughter echoes through generations recounting tales of grouse-shooting in the gunroom. This perfect being, declaring himself a miserable sinner! *Allons donc!* Give any man robust health, a temper, five thousand pounds a year, the adoration of his parish, and the worship of his family, and I defy anyone to make him so heartily dissatisfied with his spiritual state as to truly call himself miserable.

If you were a Royal Highness, attending church in perfect health and comfort, with the parson waiting for your arrival, would you believe yourself a "miserable sinner"? Only if racked with gout, isolated in solitude, fearing death, and forced to subsist on arrowroot instead of claret might you feel such humiliation. But in high health, sunshine, and spirits, the word "miserable" is merely a formality. You cannot genuinely pity yourself. Consider Colin Ploughman, suffering from the ague, supporting seven children, owing two pounds a year for his cottage, and scraping by on eight shillings a week. No: a healthy, rich, jolly country gentleman, if miserable, possesses a very supportable misery; if sinful, he has very few witnesses to accuse him. Perhaps he grows slightly selfish, but at least he is content with himself. Aside from the Earl at the castle, there is no one within miles so good or so great. His admirable wife ministers to him and the entire parish; his children bow before him; the local vicar reveres him; he commands respect at quarter sessions; poachers tremble at his approach; hats fly off at the market; and around his great coach, laden with his spotless daughters and sublime lady, the tradespeople cringe bareheaded, while farmers' wives drop countless curtseys. From their cushions, the ladies gaze down beneficently, smiling upon the poor. They buy yards of ribbon with affability, condescend to purchase ounces of salts or packets of seeds, and deign to haggle over geese. Their drive resembles a royal progress, with a supposedly happy populace pressing forward to bless them. Tradesmen bow, farmers' wives bob, and town boys wave ragged hats, cheering the red-faced coachman driving the fat bays, shouting, "Sir Miles for ever! Throw us a halfpenny, my lady!"

Yet, suppose the market-woman hides her fat goose when Sir Miles's coach arrives, terrified that my lady, spotting the bird, will demand it as a bargain? Suppose no copper coins ever emerge from the royal coach window? Suppose Sir Miles regales his tenants with notoriously weak beer and his poor with especially thin broth? This may indeed be the way of our fine old English gentleman. There have been many such figures—gentlemen and ladies who patronized the poor without ever truly relieving them, who shouted "Amen!" at church as loudly as the clerk, who performed every form of piety and discharged all the etiquette of old English gentlemanhood, buying virtue as a bargain and believing themselves to honor it through mere purchase.

Poor Harry, in his distress, sought help from his relations. His aunt sent him a tract and her blessing; his uncle had business out of town and could not, of course, answer the poor boy's petition. How much of this behavior occurs daily in respectable life, do you think? You can imagine Lord and Lady Macbeth conspiring a murder, meeting with some awkwardness once the deed is done. But consider Lord and Lady Skinflint consulting in their bedroom about refusing a helping hand to their luckless nephew, deciding against it, descending to family prayers, greeting their children and domestics, discoursing virtuously before them, remaining together, and talking nose-to-nose. What can they possibly think of one another? Of the poor kinsman fallen among thieves, groaning for help unheeded? How can they maintain those virtuous airs? How can they dare look each other in the face? Dare? Do you suppose they believe they have done wrong? Does Skinflint torture himself with remorse regarding the distress he ignored and the hunger he sent away empty? Not he. He is indignant with the Prodigal for being a fool, not ashamed of himself for being a curmudgeon. What? A young man with such opportunities throwing them away? A fortune squandered on gamblers and spendthrifts? Horrible, horrible! "Take warning, my child, by this unfortunate young man's behavior, and see the consequences of extravagance."

According to the great and always Established Church of the Pharisees, here lies an admirable opportunity for a moral discourse and an assertion of virtue. "And to think of his deceiving us so!" cries out Lady Warrington. "Very sad, very sad, my dear!" says Sir Miles, wagging his head. "To think of so much extravagance in one so young!" cries Lady Warrington. "Cards, bets, feasts at taverns of the most wicked profusion, carriages and riding horses, the company of the wealthy and profligate of his own sex, and, I fear, of the most iniquitous persons of ours."

"Hush, my Lady Warrington!" cries her husband, glancing toward the spotless Dora and Flora, who hold down their blushing heads at the mention of the last "naughty persons."

"No wonder my poor children hide their faces!" mamma continues. "My dears, I wish even the existence of such creatures could be kept from you!"

"They can't go to an opera, or the park, without seeing 'em, to be sure," says Sir Miles. "To think we should have introduced such a young serpent into the bosom of our family! And have left him in the company of that guileless darling!" She points to Master Miles.

"Who's a serpent, mamma?" inquires that youth.

"First you said cousin Harry was bad: then he was good: now he is bad again. Which is he, Sir Miles?"

"He has faults, like all of us, Miley, my dear. Your cousin has been wild, and you must take warning by him."

"Was not my elder brother, who died—my naughty brother—was not he wild too? He was not kind to me when I was quite a little boy. He never gave me money, nor toys, nor rode with me, nor..." Why do you cry, mamma? "Sure I remember how Hugh and you were always fighting—"

"Silence, sir!" cry out papa and the girls in a breath. "Don't you know you are never to mention that name?"

"I know I love Harry, and I didn't love Hugh," says the sturdy little rebel. "And if cousin Harry is in prison, I'll give him my half-guinea that my godpapa gave me, and anything I have—yes, anything, except... except my little horse—and my silver waistcoat—and—and Snowball and Sweetlips at home—and—and, yes, my custard after dinner." This was in reply to a hint from sister Dora.

"But I'd give him some of it," continues Miles, after a pause.

"Shut thy mouth with it, child, and then go about thy business," says papa, amused. Sir Miles Warrington possessed a considerable fund of easy humor.

"Who would have thought he should ever be so wild?" mamma goes on.

"Nay. Youth is the season for wild oats, my dear."

"That we should be so misled in him!" sighed the girls.

"That he should kiss us both!" cries papa.

"Sir Miles Warrington, I have no patience with that sort of vulgarity!" says the majestic matron.

"Which of you was the favorite yesterday, girls?" continues the father.

"Favourite, indeed! I told him over and over again of my engagement to dear Tom—I did, Dora—why do you sneer, if you please?" says the handsome sister.

"Nay, to do her justice, so did Dora too," said papa.

"Because Flora seemed to wish to forget her engagement with dear Tom sometimes," remarks the sister.

"I never, never, never wished to break with Tom! It's wicked of you to say so, D

If you, our gracious reader, will follow us across the way, you will find our young gentlemen at Lord Wrotham's residence—a house lent generously to the General—and a small gathering of the family party we previously encountered at Oakhurst and Tunbridge Wells. James Wolfe has pledged to attend dinner; however, James is currently attending exclusively to Miss Lowther, preferring a fleeting glance from her eyes to the most exquisite dishes prepared by Lord Wrotham's chef or the promised desserts awaiting your arrival. You shall occupy the sixth seat. You may take Mr. Wolfe's place, for you may be certain he will not come. As for me, I shall stand by the sideboard to chronicle the conversation.

First, observe the radiant countenance of the ladies. Recall when Harry Warrington was seized by the bailiffs; I had intended to recount how Mrs. Lambert, upon hearing of her son's plight, rushed to her husband. She implored, insisted, and beseeched that Martin assist them. "Forget his recent rebellion," she urged, "forget his anger regarding the returned gifts—anyone would be angry, especially a spirited lad like Harry! Forget our poverty and the need to save for the boys at college; we must secure his release. Remember how you extracted Charles Watkins from a similar predicament two years ago, and how he repaid every penny? Yes, and you made a whole family happy, blessed be God! Mrs. Watkins still prays for you and blesses you daily, and everything has prospered since. I have no doubt this act will make you a Major-General—there is no earthly doubt," the devoted wife declared.

Martin Lambert, requiring little persuasion to perform a kindness, readily agreed. Having resolved to locate friend James Wolfe and provide bail for Harry, he donned his hat, squeezed Theo's hand—who seemingly divined his purpose—or perhaps the sensible mother had inadvertently revealed it—and kissed little Hetty's flushed cheek before departing the room where the girls and their mother sat. Though he exited, he was immediately followed by the latter.

Once alone with him, the enthusiastic matron could no longer restrain her emotions. She threw her arms around her husband's neck, kissed him a hundred and twenty-five times in succession, invoked God's blessing, and wept profusely upon his shoulder. In this sentimental posture, she was discovered by Old Mrs. Quiggett, the lord's housekeeper, who bustled about the house and presumably stood astounded by the domestic spectacle.

"We have had a disagreement and are reconciling! Do not spread tales, Mrs. Quiggett!" the gentleman declared, strolling away.

"Well, I never!" exclaimed Mrs. Quiggett with a shrill, strident laugh reminiscent of a venerable old cockatoo—a white, hook-nosed, long-lived bird to which Mrs. Quiggett bore a striking resemblance. "Well, I never!" she laughed again, shaking her aged frame until her keys and, one might imagine, her old ribs clattered and jingled.

"Oh, Quiggett!" sobbed Mrs. Lambert, "what a man that is!"

"You've been quarrelling, have you, mum, and now making it up? That is correct."

"Quarrel with him? He never told a greater falsehood. My General is an angel, Quiggett. I wish to worship him. I wish to fall at his boots and kiss them! There has never been a man so good as my General. What have I done to deserve such a husband? How dare I possess such a good man?"

"My dear, I believe there is a match made in heaven between you two," replied the old cockatoo. "And what would you like for supper?"

When Lambert returned very late to the meal, announcing that Harry was free, that his brother had revived, and that he himself had been rescued, you can imagine the commotion among the household! If Mrs. Lambert's General was an angel in the morning, what further acts of humble devotion might she crave in the evening? Little Hetty nestled silently against her father, stealing a sip from his glass. Theo and his mother's faces beamed with happiness, like two bright moons. After supper, the four of them knelt at a specific signal, paying glad homage in joyful reverence, rejoicing with a purity akin to the angels described in scripture for the repentant sinner. Then, a loud knocking echoed at the door. Who could it be? My lord was miles away in the countryside. It was past midnight; they had talked so long and stayed up so late. Mrs. Lambert likely guessed the visitor.

"This is George," announced a young gentleman ushering in another. "We visited Aunt Bernstein. We could not retire to bed without thanking you as well. You dear, dear, good..." No further words were audible. Aunt Lambert embraced Harry; Theo snatched up Hetty, who was pale as death, and hugged her back to life. George Warrington removed his hat, and upon concluding Harry's greeting, approached to kiss Mrs. Lambert's hand; the General brushed his fingers across his eyes. I protest they were all in a state of profound tenderness and happiness. Generous hearts often feel this way when wrong is forgiven, when peace is restored, and when love returns, thought lost forever.

"We came from Aunt Bernstein's; we saw lights here, you see; we could not sleep without saying goodnight to you all," said Harry. "Could we not, George?"

"'Tis certainly a famous nightcap you have brought us, boys," remarked the General. "When are you to dine with us? Tomorrow?" No, they must visit Madame Bernstein's tomorrow.

Tomorrow, then? Yes, they would come the following day—which is precisely the day we are chronicling, and the very dinner at which, in lieu of Lieutenant-Colonel James Wolfe, absent on private business, my gracious reader has just been invited to sit.

But why sit? Not to partake of a mere phantom feast, no, no—but to listen to **MR. GEORGE ESMOND WARRINGTON'S STATEMENT**, which he is undoubtedly about to deliver. Thus, they all sat—not in my lord's grand dining room, but in the cozy study or parlor ahead. The cloth was withdrawn, the General had toasted the King, the servants had departed, and the guests sat in silence (*conticuerunt*). After a moment of hesitation and blushing, Mr. George proceeded:

"I recall, at the General's table, how the little Philadelphia agent, whose wit and shrewdness we noted at home, raised valid objections to the conduct of the campaign, the disastrous outcome proving their justice. 'Of course,' he said, 'your Excellency's troops, once before Fort Duquesne, will never be able to resist such a general, such an army, such artillery as will attack it. But do you calculate, sir, on the difficulty of reaching the place? Your Excellency's march will be through woods almost untrodden, over roads you must create yourselves, and your line will stretch some four miles long. This slender column, forced through the forest, will be subjected to endless attacks in front, rear, and flank by enemies unseen, whose constant practice is the dexterous laying of ambushes.'—'Psha, sir!' retorted the General, 'the savages may frighten your raw American militia' (Thank you, Excellency, for the compliment, Mr. Washington seems to imply, sitting at the table), 'but the Indians will never make an impression on his Majesty's regular troops.'—'I heartily hope not, sir,' sighed Mr. Franklin; and naturally, the gentlemen of the General's family sneered at the Postmaster as a pert civilian offering opinions beyond his comprehension.

"We despised the Indians on our own side, and our commander dismissed them lightly. Our officers disgraced the allied chiefs through outrageous behavior toward their women. Fewer than seven or eight remained with our force. Had we possessed a couple of hundred in our front on that fatal ninth of July, the outcome would have been vastly different. They would have repelled the French Indians, preventing the surprise and panic that ensued. It is now known that the French were prepared to surrender their fort, never dreaming of defense, and that the French Indians themselves protested the audacity of attacking such an overwhelming force.

"I was with our General commanding the main body when the firing commenced before us, and one aide-de-camp after another was dispatched forward. Initially, the enemy's assault was met briskly by our vanguard, and our men cheered with good spirits. But soon our fire slackened, while from behind every tree and bush surrounding us came single shots, felling man after man. We marched in orderly formation: skirmishers ahead, colors and two small guns in the center, and baggage well-guarded in the rear. We moved over ground open and clear for a mile or two, with a thick, tangled covert of brushwood and trees lining both sides. After brief frontal firing, the barrage opened from both sides of the encircling wood against our advancing column. Men dropped rapidly, officers falling in greater numbers than privates. At first, as mentioned, they cheered and returned fire, our guns even opening onto the wood, seemingly silencing the French ambush. Yet the hidden rifle fire resumed. Our men halted, huddled together despite the shouts and orders of the General and officers to advance, firing wildly into the brushwood—naturally achieving nothing. Those in the front ran back to the main body in terror, many wounded. They reported five thousand Frenchmen and a legion of screaming Indian demons ahead, scalping our people as they fell. We could hear their cries from the

For months, our existence at the fort was a weary cycle of conflict and reconciliation between the commandant and myself. Our days were filled with greasy card games, dismal duets featuring his wheezing flute and my cracked guitar, and the grim reality of survival. Poor Fawn endured the blows and bottles of liquor dispensed at her "lord and master"'s whim, yet she tirelessly cared for her papoose, her master suffering from gout, and our prisoner battling fever. This routine persisted until early autumn, when a hunter arrived bearing news that electrified our tiny garrison.

The Marquis de Montcalm had dispatched a substantial force to reinforce French-held forts and expand their territory into British lands. Troops had departed Quebec and Montreal, traveling up the St. Lawrence and across the lakes in bateaux, laden with artillery and vast supplies. Rumors swirled that Commandant Museau would soon be replaced by a higher-ranking officer—one who might exchange me for a prisoner or surrender me to the Indians as retaliation for atrocities committed by our forces against enemy soldiers. The men of the fort grew restless with anticipation; they dreamed of advancing into Pennsylvania and New York, seizing Albany and Philadelphia, driving the "Rosbifs" into the sea, and claiming all America from the Mississippi to Newfoundland.

Yet, despite this triumphant outlook, the prospect of French victory offered no solace to Mr. Museau.

"Eh, commandant!" I remarked. "'Tis *fort bien*, but where, meanwhile, are your farm in Normandy, your pot of cider, and your *trippes à la mode de Caen*?"

"Yes," he replied, "all very well, my boy. But consider: when poor old Museau is superseded, where will you be? Other officers lack the humanity I possess. Few men in the world can match my kindness. If a large garrison arrives, will my successors grant you the indulgences honest Museau provides? You will be kept in a sty like a pig awaiting slaughter. Just as surely as one of our officers falls into the hands of your frontier brigands and suffers, so too must you pay with your skin for his misfortune. You will be handed over to our red allies—to the brothers of La Biche yonder. Do you recall last year? What did they do to your countrymen captured at Braddock's defeat? Roasting was merely the least of their punishments, *ma foi*. Was it not, La Biche?"

Museau launched into a graphic description of tortures—eyes burned out, teeth and nails wrenching, limbs and bodies slashed. You turn pale, dear Miss Theo! Well, I shall exercise mercy and spare you the horrors Museau recounted so cheerfully as potential fates.

La Biche, however, was hardly shaken by this tale. Ladies, she had witnessed such brutality firsthand. Hailing from the Senecas, whose villages lay near the great cataract between Lake Ontario and Erie, her people fought both for and against the English. In the endless wars between our factions, it is difficult to declare whether white or red skin proves the more savage.

"They may chop me into cutlets and broil me," I said calmly. "But again, you will never have your farm in Normandy."

"Fetch the whisky bottle, La Biche," Museau commanded.

"And it is not too late, even now," I insisted. "I will reward the guide who brings me home generously. Furthermore, as a man of honor, I promise ten thousand livres to whoever brings me a token—say, my watch and seal engraved with my grandfather's arms, which I believe lies in a chest somewhere in this fort."

"Aha, scélérat!" roared the commandant, laughing hoarsely. "You have eyes, you do! Everything is fair prize in war."

"Think of a house in your village, a fine field nearby with six cows, a lush orchard heavy with fruit..."

"And Javotte at the door spinning, with a couple of rascally children, their cheeks as red as the apples! O my country! O my mother!" Museau whimpered. "Quick, La Biche, the whisky!"

That night, the commandant pondered deeply, while La Biche sat apart, silent and melancholy, nursing her child. Whenever my gaze met hers, I found her eyes fixed upon me. The infant cried, and Museau, using his customary foul language, ordered the child away to the hut where the unfortunate La Biche resided. Once she was gone, we spoke freely. I presented arguments to Monsieur that his greed could not ignore.

"How do you know," he asked, "that this hunter will serve you?"

"That is my secret," I replied. Yet, since we are not bound by honor tonight, let me explain. When hunters enter the settlements for trade, they often linger for days, seeking rest, drink, and camaraderie. Our new friend loved these things. He gambled with the men, traded furs for liquor, and reveled in the fort, singing and dancing. I suspect they enjoyed listening to my songs; lacking better entertainment, I frequently sang and scraped my guitar. We held many concerts, the men joining in chorus or dancing to my rustic tunes until interrupted by the drums and the retreat call.

Our guest, the hunter, attended one or two of these gatherings. I decided to test if he understood English. After exhausting our supply of French songs, I declared, "My lads, I shall sing an English song." To the melody of "Over the hills and far away"—a favorite air my grandfather once hummed in Marlborough's camp—I improvised some doggerel verses: *"This long, long year, a prisoner drear / Ah, me! I'm tired of lingering here: / I'll give a hundred guineas gay / To be over the hills and far away."*

"What is it?" the hunter asked. "I don't understand."

"'Tis a girl to her lover," I answered. But I noticed a twinkle in his eye; he understood.

The following day, when no men were within earshot, the trapper confirmed my suspicion. As he passed me, he hummed softly, yet clearly, in perfect English, the refrain of my previous evening's verse.

"If you are ready," he said, "I am ready. I know your people and the path to them. Speak to the Fawn; she will tell you what to do. What! You will not play with me?" He produced some cards just as two soldiers approached. "Milor est trop grand seigneur? Bonjour, my lord!"

The man bowed mockingly and sauntered off, shrugging his shoulders, offering to play and drink elsewhere.

I realized then that La Biche was to be the agent of this affair, and my offer to Museau had been accepted. The poor Fawn executed her role with remarkable faithfulness and skill. I required no further words with Museau; the understanding was complete. For a long time, she had been permitted free communication with me. She had tended to me during my wounds and illnesses, assisted with the chores of my small chamber, and cooked for me. She was free to leave the fort, venturing to the river and fields where the garrison's corn and garden produce were gathered.

Having squandered most of the money earned from selling his pelts, the trapper assembled his store of flints, powder, and blankets, then departed. Three days later, Fawn signaled that the time for my attempt at freedom had arrived.

When first wounded, I had been taken by my kind Florac and placed on his bed in the officers' room. When the fort was stripped of all officers save the old lieutenant left in command, I remained in my quarters, sometimes left relatively free, sometimes locked up and fed prisoners' rations, and occasionally invited to share the gaoler's mess while drunk.

This officers' house, or room, was constructed of logs, similar to the half-dozen others within the fort, mounting only four small-caliber guns. One cannon stood on the bastion directly behind my cabin. Looking westward over this gun, one could see a small island at the confluence of the Ohio and Monongahela rivers, where Fort Duquesne stood. On the shore opposite this island, there were some trees.

"You see those trees?" my poor La Biche asked me the day prior, speaking in her French jargon. "He waits for you behind those trees."

During the day, the door to my quarters remained open, and La Biche moved freely. On the eve of my escape, she returned from the fields carrying a pickaxe and a basket of vegetables and herbs for soup. Sitting on a bench at my door, the pick leaning against it and the basket beside her, I stood talking to her for a while. I suppose I was so foolish that I never would have considered using the pick if she had actually thrust it into my open doorway, causing it to fall inside.

"Hide it," she instructed. "You will need it soon." That afternoon, she indicated the trees to me.
