The next day, she appeared feigning anger, shouting, "My lord! My lord! Why do you not come to the commandant's dinner? He is very ill! *Entendez-vous*?" Peering into the room as she spoke, she tossed a coil of rope to me.

"I am coming, La Biche," I said, hobbling after her on my crutch. As I entered the commandant's quarters, she whispered, "*Pour ce soir*" (for tonight). Then I knew the hour

George Warrington had recounted the tale we have just heard to Madame de Bernstein the previous evening—a portion, that is, of the history. For the old lady frequently nodded off during the narration, only rousing herself when George paused to declare it "most interesting" and commanded him to continue. The young gentleman hemmed and hawed, stuttered, and flushed crimson, proceeding much against his will. He spoke far less fluently than he had before his small, friendly audience in Hill Street, where Hetty's eyes of wonder, Theo's sympathetic glances, his mother's kind countenance, and his father's humorous expressions served as applause sufficient to encourage any modest youth needing a boost for his eloquence.

As for his mother's behavior, the General remarked, "It was as good as Mr. Addison's trunk-maker; she would make the fortune of any tragedy simply by being engaged to cry in the front boxes." This was precisely why we selected my Lord Wrotham's house as the theater for George's debut performance, hoping he might speak to advantage rather than as he had done before that sleepy, cynical old lady.

"Very good and most interesting, I am sure, my dear sir," Madame Bernstein said, raising three delicate fingers encased in a lace mitten to conceal a convulsive twitch of her mouth. "And your mother must have been delighted to see you."

George gave a slight shrug and a low bow as his aunt looked up at him with her keen, aged eyes. "She has been delighted to see you," she continued dryly, "killed the fatted calf, and—well, that sort of thing. Though why I say 'calf,' I cannot understand, nephew George, for you were never the prodigal son. I may say, 'Calf to thee, my poor Harry!' Thou hast certainly been among the swine. Evil companions have stripped the money from thy pocket and the coat from thy back."

"He came to his family in England, madam," George replied with some heat. "His friends were your ladyship's."

"He could not have chosen worse advisers, nephew Warrington," the old lady retorted, tossing her head. "So I should have informed my sister sooner, had she condescended to write to me through him, as she has done through you."

Later that night, as she prepared for a rout, she murmured to her maid: "Hey! Hey! This young gentleman's mother is half sorry that he has come to life again; I can see it in his face. She is half sorry, and I am perfectly furious! Why didn't he lie still when he fell beneath the tree, and why did that young Florac carry him to the fort? I knew those Floracs when I was in Paris, during the reign of Monsieur le Régent. They were of the House of Florac of Ivry. No great house before Henri IV. His ancestor was the King's favorite. His ancestor—he! he!—his ancestress! Brett! *Entendez-vous?* Give me my card-purse. I dislike the grand airs of this Monsieur George; yet he bears a striking resemblance to his grandfather—the same look, and sometimes the same tone. Have you heard of Colonel Esmond when I was young? This boy has his eyes. I suppose I liked the Colonel's because he loved me."

Engaged as she was to a card party—an amusement she never missed, weekday or Sabbath, so long as she possessed the strength to hold trumps or sit upright—she soon dismissed her two nephews. To the elder, she offered a couple of fingers and a very stately curtsy; to Harry, however, she gave two hands and a kindly pat on the cheek.

"My poor child, now that thou art disinherited, thou shalt see how differently the world will treat thee!" she declared. "There is only, in all London, a wicked, heartless old woman who will treat thee as before. Here is a pocket-book for you, child! Do not lose it at Ranelagh tonight. That suit of yours does not become your brother half so well as it did upon you! You shall present your brother to everyone and pace the room for at least two hours, child. Were I you, I would then retire to the Chocolate-House and act as if nothing had occurred. While you are there, your brother may return to me and partake of a bit of chicken. My Lady Flint serves wretched suppers, and I wish to discuss your mother's letter with him. *Au revoir*, gentlemen!" With that, she departed toward her toilet, her chairmen and torchbearers already awaiting her at the door.

The gentlemen proceeded to Ranelagh, where only a handful of Mr. Harry's acquaintances happened to be present. They walked the circular path, encountered Mr. Tom Claypool with some country friends, listened to the music, and drank tea in a box. Harry acted as master of ceremonies, introducing his brother to the curiosities of the venue. George, meanwhile, proved even more excited than his brother had been on his initial visit to this palace of delight. George loved music far more than Harry ever did; he heard a full orchestra for the first time and witnessed a satisfactory performance of a work by Mr. Handel. A notable instance of Harry's humility and regard for his elder brother was his ability to respect George's passion for music at a time when fiddling was deemed effeminate and unmanly in England, while patriotic prints daily urged Britons to sneer at the frivolous accomplishments of the "Squallinis" and "Monsieurs."

"No one in Britain is proud of his ignorance now," the narrator observed. "There is no conceit left among us. There is no such thing as dullness. Arrogance is entirely unknown... Well, at any rate, Art has obtained her letters of naturalization and lives here on terms of almost equality. If Mrs. Thrale chooses to marry a music-master today, I doubt her friends would shudder at the mention of her name. If she possesses a good fortune and keeps a competent cook, people would even dine with her despite the misalliance, treating Mr. Piozzi with civility."

After Ranelagh, following Madame Bernstein's advice, George returned to her ladyship's house, while Harry presented himself at the club, where gentlemen assembled nightly to sup and subsequently gamble. Naturally, no one alluded to Mr. Warrington's brief disappearance. Mr. Ruff, his former landlord, visited him with the utmost gravity and civility, acting as if no rift had ever existed between them. Mr. Warrington had previously ordered his trunks and belongings removed from Bond Street in the morning; he and his brother were now established in separate apartments.

However, once supper concluded and the gentlemen prepared to ascend to the gambling tables, Harry announced he would not play further. "I have already burned my fingers," he stated, "and can afford no more extravagance."

"Why," Mr. Morris said in a somewhat flippant tone, "you must have won more than you have lost, Mr. Warrington, after all is said and done."

"And of course I do not know my own affairs as well as you do, Mr. Morris," Harry replied sternly, remembering the other's conduct upon hearing of his arrest. "But I have another reason. A few months or days ago, I was heir to a great estate and could afford to lose a little money. Now, thank God, I am heir to nothing." He glanced around, blushing slightly, at the knot of gentlemen—his gaming associates—who lounged at the tables or gathered near the fire.

"How do you mean, Mr. Warrington?" cried Lord March. "Have you lost Virginia, too? Who has won it? I always fancied playing you myself for that stake."

"And growing an improved breed of slaves in the colony," added another.

"The rightful owner has won it. You have heard me tell of my twin elder brother?"

"Who was killed in that affair of Braddock's two years ago! Yes. Good gracious, my dear sir, I hope in heaven he has not come to life again?"

"He arrived in London two days hence. He was a prisoner in a French fort for eighteen months; he escaped only a few months ago and left our house in Virginia shortly after his release."

"You haven't had time to order mourning, I suppose, Mr. Warrington?" asked Mr. Selwyn, very good-naturedly and simply. Poor Harry did not grasp the meaning of the jest until his brother explained it to him.

"Hang me, if I don't believe the fellow is absolutely glad of the reappearance of his confounded brother!" shouted Lord March as they continued discussing the matter after the young Virginian had taken his leave.

"These savages practice the simple virtues of affection—they are barely civilized in America yet," yawned Selwyn. "They love their kin and scalp their enemies," simpered Mr. Walpole. "'Tis not Christian, but natural. Wouldn't you like to attend a scalping match, George, and watch a fellow skinned alive?"

"A man's elder brother is his natural enemy," said Mr. Selwyn, placidly arranging his money and counters.

"Torture is like broiled bones and pepper. You wouldn't relish simple hanging afterwards, George!" continued Horry.

"I'm hanged if there's any man in England who would like to see his elder brother alive," said Lord March.

"No, nor his father either, my lord!" cried Jack Morris. "For the first time I've known you to have one, Jack

The maternal grandfather of our Virginians, the Colonel Esmond, who had long ago traded the bustle of England for the quiet expanses of the New World, had spent a significant portion of his American leisure composing memoirs of his early life. Within these volumes, Madame de Bernstein—then known as Miss Beatrice Esmond before her marriage—played a role of considerable importance. George had devoured his grandfather's manuscripts countless times, learning to know his kinswoman intimately long before he ever laid eyes upon her. He knew, at least, the lady of half a century prior: young, radiant, and wilful. Now, he encountered her in the twilight of her days.

One wonders, when the cheeks have faded and the eyes grown dim, whether it is a sorrow or a delight for a woman who is no longer a beauty to recall the zenith of her bloom. When the heart has withered, does it still cherish memories of its former freshness and warmth? When the spirit grows languid and weary, do we truly wish to remember how brightly it once burned, how buoyant our hopes, how ready our sympathies, and how keen our enjoyment of life? Thus do they fall—the buds of spring, the roses of youth, the golden harvests of summer—until only bare branches shiver in the winter wind.

"And that was a beauty once!" George Warrington mused as his aunt emerged from the rout, powdered and diamond-studded. "And that ruin was once a splendid palace. Crowds of lovers have sighed before those decrepit feet and been bewitched by the brilliance of those eyes." His mind drifted to a fireworks display at Williamsburg on the King's birthday, recalling the skeletal wheels and empty sockets of exploded Roman candles. The dazzling radiance of Aunt Beatrice's early career flashed before him as he reflected on his grandsire's journals.

Honest Harry had witnessed those same spectacles, yet he was no scholar of books and had not pored over the manuscript with such scrutiny. Even if he had, he likely would not have indulged in the moralizing speculations of his melancholy senior. Mr. Warrington felt no compulsion to inform his aunt of his deep familiarity with her early history, so he remained silent on the matter.

After their meal, the old lady tapped her cane toward her writing desk and instructed her attendant to retrieve a letter resting beneath the inkwell. George recognized the handwriting instantly; it was the correspondence he had carried from home.

"It appears from this letter," the old lady observed, fixing a sharp gaze upon her nephew, "that since your return, there have been certain disagreements between you and my sister."

"Indeed?" George replied smoothly. "I was unaware that Madam Esmond had alluded to them."

The Baroness adjusted a large pair of spectacles onto eyes that once had kindled passions unknown, glanced briefly at the document, and handed it to George. He began to read aloud:

*I have received, and thankfully acknowledged, your ladyship's favor via the Rose packet of October 23rd. I answer you now at a season meant for goodwill and peace among men, though Heaven has decreed that we must still bear our share of earthly sorrow and trouble. My reply shall be delivered by my eldest son, Mr. Esmond Warrington, who returned to us so miraculously from the Valley of the Shadow of Death—as our previous letters informed my poor Henry—and who desires, with my full consent, to visit Europe, despite his brief stay among us.*

*I grieve to think that my dearest Harry appeared at home—in England—under false colors, as it were. He was presented to His Majesty, to our family, and to his own kin as his father's heir, while my dear son George was still alive, though dead to us. Ah, Madam! During the eighteen months of his captivity, what anguish his mother's and his brother's hearts endured! My Harry possesses the tenderest heart of any man living. In the joy of seeing Mr. Esmond Warrington restored to life, he will surely forget the worldly misfortune that befalls him. He will return to comparative poverty without a pang. The most generous and obedient of human beings, of sons, he will gladly yield to his elder brother that inheritance which was rightfully his but for the accident of birth and the providential return of my son George.*

*Your beneficent intentions toward dearest Harry will be more than ever welcome, now that he is reduced to a younger brother's slender portion! Many years ago, an advantageous opportunity arose to provide for him within this province; by this time, he would have been master of a noble estate and numerous negroes, capable of making a figure among the elite, had my wishes been honored and his father's small portion, currently yielding little interest in British funds, been invested in this most excellent purchase. Yet, legal formalities and, I grieve to admit, my elder son's scruples prevailed, causing this admirable opportunity to slip through my fingers! Harry will find that the savings of his income have been carefully accumulated—may he live long to enjoy them! May Heaven bless you, dear sister, for whatever addition your ladyship makes to his modest store!*

*As I gather from your letter that the sum allotted to him has proven insufficient for his expenses in the refined society he has frequented—and considering he is the grandson of the Marquis of Esmond, one who stood near to inheriting his lordship's title—he surely claims equality with any other nobleman in Great Britain—I entrust a sum I had always intended for the poor child's establishment to my eldest son. To do him justice, he holds a most sincere regard for his brother, and I charge him to invest this for Harry's best advantage.*

*This remittance, I trust, will amply cover any expenses incurred by the mistake regarding his station. I wish I could trust his elder brother's prudence as confidently as I trust my Harry's! But I fear that, even during his captivity, Mr. Esmond W. has learned little of the humility becoming all Christians, a virtue I have striven to instill in my children. Should you happen to show him these lines when, by the blessing of Heaven upon those who sail the seas, the Great Ocean divides us, he will know that a fond mother's blessings and prayers follow both her sons. There is no act I have ever taken, nor desire I have ever expressed (however little he may have been inclined to obey it!), but what was dictated by the fondest wishes for my dearest boys' welfare.*

*There is a scratch with a penknife and a great blot upon the letter, as if water had fallen on it. Your mother writes well, George. I suppose you and she had a difference?*

*'Twas a question of money—a ransom I promised to the old lieutenant of the fort who aided my escape. I told you he had a mistress, a poor Indian woman who showed me kindness. Six weeks after my arrival home, the poor creature appeared in Richmond, having navigated the woods along roughly the same path I had taken, bearing the token Museau promised to send upon his connivance at my flight. A commanding officer and a substantial reinforcement had arrived at Duquesne. Charges of unspecified peculation were brought against this Museau; he was arrested, attempted escape, and, less fortunate than myself, was shot on the ramparts. On his deathbed, he sent the Indian woman to me with my grandfather's watch and a line scrawled in his prison cell, begging me to send *ce que je savais* to a notary in Havre de Grace, France, to be forwarded to his relatives in Caen, Normandy. My friend Silverheels, the hunter, assisted my poor Indian companion on her journey. I cannot fathom how she would have escaped scalping otherwise. But at home, they received her harshly. They scarcely offered her a welcome. I will not detail the suspicions they harbored regarding her and me. The poor wretch fell to drinking whenever she could procure means. I ordered her food and shelter, yet she became the sport of our negroes and the subject of scandalous gossip among the elderly fools of our little town. Fortunately, our Governor was a man of sense. I made representations to him and secured a pass to return her to her people. Her profound grief at parting from me only served to confirm the suspicions against her. A fellow preacher denounced me from the pulpit; I was forced to administer a beating to another. Furthermore, I engaged in a violent dispute with Madam Esmond—a rift not yet healed—because I insisted upon paying the heirs designated by Museau the money I had promised for my deliverance. You see, scandal flourishes at the borders of the wilderness and in the New World as readily as in the Old.*

*I have suffered from it myself, my dear!* [interjected George]

*Nay, sir, I know nothing of the sort! Why not have kept the funds to buy a commission for him, or assist toward another estate and some negroes if he fancied staying home?* [cried the old lady] *Besides, I had a fancy to settle that debt myself.*

*I hope you will allow his brother to handle that. I request permission to serve as my brother's banker in this matter, considering I have borrowed so much from my mother, to be repaid to my dear Harry.*

*Do you say so, sir? Give me a glass of wine! You are an extravagant fellow! Read on, and you will see your mother agrees. I toast your health, nephew George! 'Tis good

Of course, the young men had spent considerable time discussing their respective homes, the people within them, and recent events. They exchanged detailed accounts of their histories since their last separation. "How are Harry's dogs?" George asked. "And little Dempster? And good old Nathan, along with the rest of the household?" Conversely, Harry inquired about Mountain's health and whether Fanny had blossomed into a lovely young lady.

Harry confessed that at one point he had been deeply infatuated with Parson Broadbent's daughter. He admitted to losing significant sums at cards and consuming large quantities of strong spirits with the parson himself, solely to create opportunities for proximity to the girl. Yet, heaven forbid! Madam Esmond would never have consented to him wasting his prospects on Polly Broadbent.

Turning the conversation, Harry noted that Colonel G. Washington's wife was a beautiful woman, possessing a gentle nature, a pleasant demeanor, and a substantial fortune. He had recently escorted her to Richmond, where he paid a formal call upon Madam Esmond. George recounted the entire encounter with great humor, describing the terrifying ceremonial protocols of the meeting and the biting politeness displayed by his mother toward Mr. Washington's young bride.

"Never mind, George, my dear!" Mrs. Mountain interjected cheerfully. "The Colonel has taken another wife, but I am certain that once, two young gentlemen I know ran a very close race to become the stepsons of a towering giant six feet two in his boots." Indeed, Mrs. Mountain was perpetually engaged in matchmaking in her mind; she could not watch two people play cards or share a cup of tea without imagining their union for life. It was she—the gossipy instigator—who had spread the rumor concerning the unfortunate Indian woman.

As for Madam Esmond, she had met the suggestion with scorn when Parson Stack delivered it, declaring, "I should as soon fancy Mr. Esmond stealing the silver spoons, or marrying a negro woman out of the kitchen." However, while she disdained finding fault with the poor Biche and even thanked her for nursing her son during his illness, she treated the Indian woman with such chilling hauteur that the girl retreated to the servants' quarters, attempting to drown her disappointment in alcohol.

George painted a far from cheerful picture of his two-month stay at home. "The birthright is mine, Harry," he lamented, "yet you are the favorite. God help me! I suspect my mother almost grudgingly accepts my existence. Why did I take precedence over your worship into the world? Had you been the elder, you would possess the finest cellar, ride the best horse, and be the most popular man in the countryside. Whereas I have nothing to commend myself except a glum countenance that frightens people. I should have been the second son, setting up as a lawyer, traveling to England to earn my degrees, becoming a parson, and saying grace at your honor's table. The times are out of joint, sir. O cursed spite, that ever I was born to set them right!"

"Why, Georgy, you're reciting poetry, I protest!" Harry exclaimed. "I think, my dear, someone else spoke those verses before me," George replied with a smile. "'Tis from one of your books. You know every book ever written, I believe!" cried Harry. He then recounted seeing the two authors at Tunbridge, bowing respectfully to them. "Not that I cared much for their works, lacking the wit to appreciate them fully," Harry admitted, his voice catching slightly. "But I remembered how my dear old George used to speak of them, and that is why I loved seeing them."

He added, "Say, dear, it feels like a dream to see you again. Think of that bloody Indian with a knife at my George's throat! I'd like to offer Monsieur de Florac something for saving you—but I have little left now, save my little gold knee-buckles, which aren't worth two guineas."

"You hold half of what I have, child," George assured him. "We shall divide it as soon as I have settled the debt with the Frenchman." Upon hearing this, Harry erupted not merely into blessings but into fervent oaths, expressing his intense love and relief, swearing that there was no brother in the world comparable to George. For several days following his brother's arrival, Harry's eyes trailed George constantly. He would pause mid-meal, dropping his knife and fork, or stop reading his newspaper to burst into laughter. Walking together on the Mall or in Hyde Park, Harry would survey the crowd as if presenting a trophy: "Behold, gentlemen! This is he. This is my brother, who was dead and is now alive again! Can any man in Christendom boast of such a sibling?"

Naturally, Harry believed George should fulfill his promise to pay Museau's heirs the ransom sum. This issue had caused George considerable distress at home. With Museau deceased, Madam Esmond argued with great eagerness—and not a little rancor—that the agreement was void; her son was now free. She deemed the man a rogue from the start and refused to pay the wages of iniquity. Though Mr. Esmond possessed a small independence from his father and could squander his patrimony as he chose, being of age and master of his funds, his mother would not participate in such extravagance. Giving twelve thousand livres to a group of peasant farmers in Normandy—whom Britain was currently at war with, and who would likely funnel the money to priests and the Pope—was sheer wickedness in her eyes. She would not subscribe to such sin. If George wished to squander his father's money (though she noted he had previously shown restraint, especially regarding Harry's welfare), why not give it to his own flesh and blood, to poor Harry, who had suddenly lost his inheritance, rather than to a band of priest-ridden peasants in France?

This dispute raged between mother and son throughout George's final days in Virginia. It remained unresolved. On the morning of his departure, after a sleepless night, Madam Esmond approached his bedside and asked if he still intended to throw away his father's property. He replied, in depths of grief and confusion, that his word was given and he must act according to his honor. She answered that she would continue praying for Heaven to soften his proud heart and enable her to endure her heavy trials. The last image George held of his mother's face was hers standing beside his bed, pale and with tearless eyes, before she turned away and slowly exited the chamber.

"Where did you learn the art of winning everyone to your side, Harry?" George continued. "How do you and the entire world begin as friends? Teach me a few lessons in popularity. Nay, perhaps I won't need them; when I discover certain people hate me, I find myself rather pleased than angered."

Initially, at Richmond, Mr. Esmond Warrington—the sole survivor of Braddock's defeat, a victim of immense illness and hardship—had been a favorite among the townsfolk, receiving both private and public kindness. The parson had glorified his escape in a sermon; neighbors visited the fugitive; the family carriage was summoned, and Madam Esmond and George returned the courtesy calls. George recalled thinking some charming little caps were set at him. But his mother routed these admirers off, frightening them with her prodigious haughtiness, causing his popularity to dwindle before the incident that sealed his fate occurred.

"I wasn't jolly enough for the officers," George explained. "I didn't care for their drinking bouts, dice games, and swearing. I was too sarcastic for the ladies; their tea parties and gossip stupefied me almost as much as the men's bluster and horse talk. I cannot tell you, Harry, how lonely I felt in that place, amidst the scandal and squabbles. I regretted my prison almost, finding myself wishing repeatedly for the freedom of thought and the silent ease of Duquesne. I suppose I am very shy; I can speak unreservedly to very few. Before most, I sit utterly silent. When we were at home, it was you who dominated the table conversations, occasionally earning a smile from our mother. When she and I were together, we shared no common subjects, speaking scarcely at all until we began disputing over law and divinity."

"So the gentlemen decided I was supercilious and a dull companion (and, indeed, I think their judgment was correct)," George mused. "And the ladies thought me cold and sarcastic—they could never discern if I was serious or joking, generally voting me a disagreeable fellow before my character was completely ruined. That went hand-in-hand with the appearance of the poor Biche. Oh, what a nice character they constructed for me, my dear!" George cried out in a transport of rage. "And what a pretty life they led me after Museau's unlucky messenger appeared among us! The boys hooted the poor woman if she ventured onto the street; the ladies dropped half-curtsies and crossed to the other side of the room. That precious clergyman moved from one tea table to another, preaching on the horrors of seduction and the lax principles young men learned in papist countries and brought back with them. The poor Fawn's appearance at home just weeks after my return was declared a scheme between her and me; the best-informed agreed she had waited on the other side of the river until I signaled her to join me in Richmond. The officers bantered me at the coffee house, cracking clumsy jokes about the woman I had chosen. Oh, the world is a nice, charitable world! I was so enraged I considered fleeing to Castlewood to live alone—for our

Lord Castlewood possessed a residence in Kensington Square sufficiently grand to house his entire noble household, conveniently situated for attendance upon the Palace whenever His Majesty resided there. While Her Ladyship hosted her evening gatherings and card-parties within its walls, Kensington remained a considerable distance from London in those days—a century ago, to be precise. George Selwyn confessed that he personally dreaded the journey, fearing robbery by footpads draped in crape or by ladies powdered in rouge at the quadrille table; the specifics of such perils remain unknown to us.

About noon, the day following Harry's dramatic return to White's Club, the virtuous kinfolk gathered for breakfast, even Mr. Will, who was scheduled to begin his service later that afternoon. The ladies arrived first for their chocolate, during which Mr. Will offered his customary courtly attentions. Finally, the Master of the House entered, languid and clad in his bedgown and nightcap, having yet to don his wig for the day. This attire itself constituted news, information Mr. Will had procured the previous evening from the Star and Garter, having heard it whispered among men who had frequented White's and witnessed it at Ranelagh.

"Pray, what news? What have you seen?" queried the head of the house, picking up his *Daily Advertiser*.

"Ask Maria!" declared Lady Fanny. The Master of the House turned to his elder sister, whose countenance wore an expression of portentous sadness, her pallor matching that of a fresh tablecloth.

"'Tis merely one of Will's usual elegant and polite fabrications," dismissed Maria.

"No," swore Will, punctuating his denial with several oaths. "It was no invention of his. Tom Claypool of Norfolk saw them both at Ranelagh; and Jack Morris emerged from White's, having heard the tale directly from Harry Warrington's own lips. Curse him, I am glad of it!" roared Will, slamming his fist against the table. "What do you think of your Fortunate Youth, your Virginian, whom your lordship held in such high esteem, turning out to be a mere second son?"

"The elder brother not dead?" asked my lord.

"Not dead any more than you are. Never was. In my belief, it was a chimera born of the two."

"Mr. Warrington is incapable of such duplicity!" cried Maria.

"I never encouraged the fellow; I trust you will grant me justice on that point," assured the lady of the house. "Nor did Fanny: neither did we, indeed!"

"Not we, indeed!" echoed Lady Fanny.

"The fellow is nothing but a beggar, and I dare say he hasn't even paid for the clothes on his back," continued Will. "I am glad of it, for, hang him, I hate him!"

"You do not regard him with favorable eyes; especially since he blackened yours, Will!" grinned my lord. "So the poor fellow has found his brother, and consequently lost his estate!" Turning toward his sister Maria, who, despite appearing the picture of woe, must have struck the humorist beside her as somewhat ludicrous, my lord gazed at her for a moment before bursting into a shrill laugh. The effect was immediate: the poor lady's face flushed crimson, and soon tears streamed down her cheeks.

"It's a shame! It's a shame!" she sobbed, burying her face in her handkerchief. Maria's stepmother and sister exchanged glances. "We never quite comprehend your lordship's sense of humor," the stepmother remarked gravely.

"I perceive no reason whatsoever why you should," replied my lord, coolly. "Maria, my dear, pray excuse me if I have said—or done—anything to hurt your feelings."

"Done anything! You pillaged the poor lad in his prosperity and now mock him in his ruin!" Maria exclaimed, rising from the table and glaring around at her assembled family.

"Excuse me, my dear sister, I was not laughing at him," said my lord, gently.

"Oh, never mind at what or whom else, my lord! You have stripped him of everything he had to lose. All London points to you as the man who feasts upon his own flesh and blood. And now that you hold his entire fortune, you make merry over his misfortune!" With a rustle of silk, she departed the room, casting looks of defiant challenge at every member of the party.

"Tell us what has occurred, or what you have heard, Will, and my sister's grief shall not disturb us further." Will proceeded, at great length and with immense exultation regarding Harry's defeat, to recount the story now buzzing through London: the sudden apparition of George Warrington. Lord Castlewood professed sorrow for Harry; he was a good, brave lad, and his kinsman liked him as much as worldly folk typically like one another. Certainly, he played Harry at cards and exploited the market against him, but why not? The peach others would surely pluck, he might as well devour. *Eh!* If that were the sole reproach my conscience bore, I need not be overly uneasy, my lord reflected.

"Where does Mr. Warrington reside?"

Will expressed readiness to descend into a state of reprobation if he knew or cared.

"He shall be invited here and treated with every respect," decreed my lord.

"Including piquet, I suppose!" growled Will.

"Or shall we take him to the stables and sell him one of your bargains of horseflesh, Will?" asked Lord Castlewood. "You would have won of Harry Warrington swiftly enough if you could; but you cheat so clumsily at your game that you were paid with a cudgel. I desire, once more, that every courtesy be extended to our cousin Warrington."

"And that you are not to be disturbed when you sit down to play, of course, my lord!" cried Lady Castlewood.

"Madam, I demand fair play—for Mr. Warrington, for myself, and for every member of this amiable family," retorted Lord Castlewood, fiercely.

"Heaven help the poor gentleman if your lordship intends to be kind to him," said the stepmother, curtsying deeply; and one wonders how far this familial dispute might have escalated had not, at that very moment, a phaeton drawn up to the house, carrying the two young Virginians.

It was the carriage the prodigal had purchased in his days of prosperity. He still drove it; George sat beside him, their negro servants trailing behind. Harry had been meekly handing the whip and reins to his brother, ceding the entirety of the property to him. *"What business has a poor devil like me with horses and carriages, Georgy?"* Harry had humbly asked. *"Beyond the coat on my back and the purse my aunt gave me, I have nothing in the world. You take the driving-seat, brother; it will ease my mind if you do."* George laughed, claiming ignorance of the route, while Harry knew the way; regarding the carriage, he claimed only half, as he had already divided his brother's wardrobe. *"But a bargain is a bargain; if I share thy coats, thou must divide my breeches' pocket, Harry; that is but fair dealing!"* Again and again, Harry swore there was no such brother on earth. How he rattled his horses over the road! How pleased and proud he was to drive such a companion! They arrived at Kensington in high spirits, and Gumbo's thunderous knock upon Lord Castlewood's door was worthy of the finest footman in St. James's.

Only Lady Castlewood and her daughter, Lady Fanny, were present in the room where the young gentlemen were ushered. Will had no particular desire to face Harry; my lord was undressed; Maria had her reasons for absence, at least until her eyes had dried. As we drive up to friends' houses today in our coaches-and-six, when John carries our noble names aloft, when finally we enter the drawing-room with our best hats and Sunday smiles foremost, does it ever occur that we interrupt a family quarrel? Do we arrive simpering and smiling, stepping over the delusive ashes of a still-burning domestic fire? Do we, in the interval between the hall-door and the drawing-room, witness the Mrs., Mr., and Misses Jones group themselves into a family tableau—the girl artlessly arranging flowers, that one reclining over an illuminated devotional work, mamma on the sofa with the butcher's and grocer's bills tucked beneath a cushion, holding an elegant volume, and extending a pretty little foot advantageously; while honest Jones, far from muttering curses against Brown, extends a kindly hand, displays a pleased visage, and exclaims, *"What, Brown my boy, delighted to see you! Hope you've come to lunch!"* I ask, does it ever happen that we become victims of domestic artifices, spectators of domestic comedies staged solely for our amusement? Oh, let us be thankful, not only for faces, but for masks! Not only for honest welcomes, but for hypocrisy, which conceals unwelcome truths from us! While I speak thus easily and chattily, what right do you, my good sir, have to know what truly passes in my mind? Perhaps I am racked with gout, or that my eldest son has just sent me a thousand pounds' worth of college bills, or that I writhe under an attack of the *Stoke Pogis Sentinel*, recently delivered under cover, or that there is a dreadfully scrappy dinner, the obvious remnants of a party to which I

Madame de Bernstein was scarcely less gratified than her Virginian nephews by the outcome of Harry's final audience with Lady Maria. That evening, George dispatched a note detailing the proceedings to his aunt. Shortly thereafter, Lord Castlewood, whose visits to his aunt were typically infrequent, arrived to pay his respects. Seizing the moment, he addressed the matter with characteristic frankness; now that the contest between Maria and Harry was concluded, there was no necessity for reticence or hypocrisy. The game had been played and finished; he felt no compunction in discussing its various maneuvers, stratagems, and finesse.

"She is my own sister," declared my Lord Castlewood with affectionate gravity. "She will not have many further opportunities—fewer chances of marriage and establishment. While I might not have approved of the match in every respect, and while I pity your ladyship's favorite young Virginian, such a stroke of fortune could not be allowed to slip through one's fingers. I was bound to stand by my own flesh and blood."

"Your candour does your lordship honour," remarked Madame de Bernstein, "and your devotion to your sister is truly edifying!"

"Nay, we have lost the game, and I speak *sans rancune*," replied my lord, bowing gracefully. "It is not for you, who have emerged victorious, to harbor malice."

Madame de Bernstein protested that she had never been in better spirits. "Confess now, Eugene," she pressed, "that Maria's visit to Harry at the spunging-house—that touching surrender of all his gifts to her—was a stroke of your invention?"

"Pity for the young man, and a sense of what was owed to Maria by her friend, her affianced lover, in his misfortune; surely these were motives sufficient to prompt her action," answered Lord Castlewood, demurely.

"But you advised her, my good nephew?"

With a shrug of his shoulders, Castlewood admitted that he had counseled his sister to see Mr. Henry Warrington. "However," he continued, "we should have triumphed, despite your ladyship, had not the elder brother made his appearance. I have been attempting to console my poor Maria by reminding her that, after all, losing was fortunate."

"Suppose she had married Harry, and then Cousin George had appeared?" suggested the Baroness.

"Effectively," cried Eugene, taking a pinch of snuff. "Let us be grateful to the grave for disgorging its dead in time! I must admit, Mr. George Warrington appears to be a man of sense, and no more selfish than other elder sons or worldly gentlemen. My poor Molly fancied he might be somewhat of a greenhorn, like his younger brother. She fondly hoped he would be inclined to share everything equally with Harry junior. Indeed, so infatuated was she with the youth that I believe she would have taken him. 'Harry Warrington with half a loaf might suffice,' I argued, 'but Harry Warrington with no bread at all, my dear!' "

"How no bread?" asked the Baroness.

"Well, no bread except at his brother's side-table. The elder brother stated as much."

"What a hard-hearted wretch!" exclaimed Madame de Bernstein.

"Ah, bah! I play with you, aunt, *cartes sur table*!" retorted Eugene. "Mr. George merely did what anyone else would do; we have no right to be angry, truly we haven't. Molly herself acknowledged as much once her initial burst of grief subsided, and I brought her around to listening to reason. The silly old creature! To be so wild about a young lad at her age!"

"'Twere a genuine passion, I almost believe," said Madame de Bernstein. "You should have heard her bid him farewell. *C'était touchant*, ma parole d'honneur! I cried. Before George, I could not restrain myself. The young fellow, with muddy stockings and hair falling over his eyes, threw himself among us during dinner; he made his offer to Molly in a very frank and noble manner, and in excellent language. And she replied. By Jove, it reminded me of Mrs. Woffington in the new Scottish play written by Lord Bute's man—the *Douglas*, what is it called? She clung to the lad; she bade him adieu in heartrending tones. She stepped out of the room in stately despair—no more chocolate, thank you. Had she made a *mauvais pas*, no one could have retreated with greater dignity. It was a masterly withdrawal after a defeat. We were starved out of our position, but we retired with all the honors of war."

"Molly will not die of disappointment!" observed my lord's aunt, sipping her cup.

My lord snarled a grin, revealing his yellow teeth. "He, he!" he chuckled. "She has suffered this malady severely once or twice before and recovered perfectly. It doesn't kill, as your ladyship knows, at Molly's age."

How could her ladyship know? She did not marry Dr. Tusher until she was advanced in years, nor did she become Madame de Bernstein until still later. As the poet Old Dido noted, ignorance of misfortune breeds lack of compassion for the wretched. In the little world, people quarrel, fight, abuse one another, and remain estranged for ages. But in the great world, people are supposedly wiser. They have differences, cease seeing one another, reconcile, and reunite without asking questions. A stray prodigal, or a stray puppy-dog, is welcomed back under an amnesty, despite having been away in disreputable company.

For six months, ever since the Castlewoods and Madame de Bernstein had battled for possession of poor Harry Warrington, these two branches of the Esmond family had remained apart. Now, with the question settled, they were free to meet again as though no rift had ever existed. Madame de Bernstein drove her grand carriage to Lady Castlewood's rout, and the Esmond ladies greeted Madame de Bernstein with smiles, loving each other just as fervently as before.

"So, sir, I hear you acted like a hard-hearted monster regarding your poor brother Harry!" the Baroness declared, delighted and brandishing her stick threateningly toward George.

"I acted only upon your ladyship's hint, wishing to ascertain whether his kinsfolk sought him for himself or for his reputed wealth," replied George, turning slightly red.

"Nay, Maria could not marry a poor fellow who was utterly penniless, whose elder brother claimed he would give him nothing!"

"I did it for the best, madam," said George, still blushing.

"And so thou didst, O thou hypocrite!" cried the old lady.

"Hypocrite, madam! And why?" asked Mr. Warrington, drawing himself up with considerable state.

"I know all, my infant!" the Baroness replied in French. "Thou art very like thy grandfather. Come, let me embrace thee! Harry has told me all, and that thou hast divided thy little patrimony with him!"

"It was but natural, madam. We have shared common hearts and purses since birth. I merely feigned hard-heartedness to test those people yonder," said George, his eyes filling with emotion.

"And thou wilt divide Virginia with him too?" asked the Bernstein.

"I do not say so. It would not be just," replied Mr. Warrington. "The land must go to the eldest-born, and Harry would not have it otherwise; moreover, I may die, or my mother may outlive both of us. But half of what is mine is his. And he, it must be remembered, was only extravagant because he was mistaken about his position."

"But he is a knight of old, a Bayard, the grandfather come to life!" cried Madame de Bernstein to her attendant as she prepared to retire for the night.

That evening, when the lads departed, it was to poor Harry that she bestowed the two-fingered salute, and to George she gave the rouged cheek. George blushed, for his part, almost as deeply as the often-dyed rose, at such a mark of favor from his ancient kinswoman.

Although Harry Warrington was the least envious of men and honored his brother as his chief guide and superior in every respect, it is no wonder that a profound sense of humiliation and disappointment oppressed the young man following his deposition from the status of Fortunate Youth and heir to boundless Virginian territories. Their friends in Kensington might promise and vow to love him all the better after his fall; Harry bowed low and professed himself very thankful. Yet, he could not help noticing, as he accompanied his brother to the state entertainment hosted by my Lord Castlewood for his newly discovered kinsman, that George was all-in-all to his cousins. All the conversation, compliments, and *petits soins* were directed at George, while Harry received no notice whatsoever save from poor Maria. She followed him with wistful glances, pursued him with eyes conveying dismal reproaches, and, as it were, blamed him for accepting her offer.

*"Ah!"* her eyes seemed to whisper, *"It is mighty well of you, Harry, to have accepted the freedom I granted you; but I had no intention, sir, that you should be so pleased at being let off."*

She gave him up, yet she did not entirely forgive him for taking her at her word. She would not have him, and yet she would. Oh,

Our Virginian heroes, displaying a wisdom far more common in youth than in age, had adopted staunch Jacobite sentiments and professed a profound devotion to the exiled Stuart dynasty. The Banished Prince had recognized Madam Esmond's father as the Marquis of Esmond, and she felt no anger toward an unfortunate lineage so eager to acknowledge the merits of her own family. As for the petty scandals surrounding her sister, Madame de Bernstein, and the Old Chevalier, she dismissed them with scornful indifference, declaring with perfect truth that the first two monarchs of the House of Hanover were scarcely superior to any Stuarts regarding domestic morality. Yet, the *de facto* King was the King, just as his Majesty was the *de jure* one. Having been solemnly crowned and anointed within the church, and having utterly defeated his rival in battle for the crown, the reigning sovereign held undeniable power. In Madam's clear judgment, her sons owed it to both themselves and the sovereign to present themselves at his royal court. If His Majesty chose to confer a lucrative office or a blue or red ribbon upon either lad, she saw no reason for surprise; she was convinced the King knew the Virginian Esmonds as well as any member of his nobility.

Consequently, the lads were strictly commanded to attend court. One dares say their mother would have been furious had she known that George had taken Harry's laced coat on the very day he went to make his bow at Kensington. A century prior, the King's drawing-room was accessible nearly daily to his nobility and gentry; loyalty—especially since the war began—could satisfy itself a dozen times a month with the august sight of the sovereign. A prudent avoidance of the enemy's warships and a gracious acknowledgment of the catastrophic loss Britain would suffer if the royal personage were seized at sea caused the monarch to forego his cherished visits to his native Hanover, compelling him to remain, albeit reluctantly, among his loving subjects. A Hanoverian lady, whose virtues had endeared her to the prince, strove to console him for his enforced absence from Herrenhausen. Through the Countess of Walmoden, on whom imperial beneficence had graciously bestowed a high title of British honor, the revered Defender of the Faith could still hear the accents of his homeland.

Mr. Warrington requested his uncle, an assiduous courtier, to present him to the King. Since Mr. Lambert also needed to visit court to thank His Majesty for his promotion, the two gentlemen traveled together to Kensington, hiring a hackney-coach as Lord Wrotham's carriage was required by its rightful owner, who had returned to his townhouse. Alighting at the Kensington Palace Gate, where the sentries knew and saluted the esteemed General, they proceeded modestly on foot to the sovereign's summer residence. Walking beneath the portico, they entered the gallery leading to the great black marble staircase—a structure richly decorated and painted by Mr. Kent—and passed through several rooms hung with tapestry and adorned with pictures and busts until they reached the King's great drawing-room. Here resided the famous "Venus" by Titian and, among other masterpieces, the depiction of "St. Francis adoring the infant Saviour" by Sir Peter Paul Rubens. Alongside the other court visitors, the gentlemen waited until His Majesty emerged from his private apartments, where he was in conference with certain personages referred to in the newspapers of that era as His Majesty's Ministers.

George Warrington, having never visited a palace before, took leisure to admire the surroundings and the people around him. For the first time, he beheld fine paintings, delighting particularly in a charming work by Sir Anthony Van Dyck representing King Charles the First, his Queen, and Family, as well as the magnificent "Esther before Ahasuerus" by Tintoretto, wherein all figures wore splendid Venetian attire. So captivated was he by these artworks that he barely heard the whispers of his good friend the General, who was murmuring into his young companion's nearly heedless ear the names of the surrounding dignitaries.

"Yonder," said Mr. Lambert, "are two of my Lords of the Admiralty, Mr. Gilbert Elliot and Admiral Boscawen—the latter being your Boscawen, whose fleet fired the first gun in your waters two years ago. That stout gentleman, heavily laden with gold, is Mr. Fox, formerly Minister and now content to serve as Paymaster with a substantial salary."

"He carries the *auri famem* on his person," observed George. "Why, his waistcoat is a perfect Potosi!"

"Aliena appetens—how goes the text?" continued General Lambert. "He loves to acquire wealth and squander it."

"That is my Lord Chief Justice Willes, conversing with my Lord of Salisbury, Doctor Headley. If he serves his God as faithfully as he serves his King, he will be translated to some very high promotion in Heaven. He belongs to your grandfather's time and was loved by Dick Steele yet hated by the Dean. With them stands my Lord of London, the learned Doctor Sherlock. My lords of the lawn sleeves have lost half their honors now. I recall when I was a boy in my mother's hand, she made me kneel to the Bishop of Rochester, the man who crossed the water to become Minister to someone who shall remain nameless—Perkin's Bishop. That handsome fair man is Admiral Smith. He presided over poor Byng's court-martial and strove in vain to save him from his penalty; 'Tom of Ten Thousand,' they call him in the fleet. The French Ambassador had him broken when he was merely a lieutenant for ordering a French man-of-war to lower its topsails to him, yet the King made Tom a captain the very next day. That tall, haughty-looking man is my Lord George Sackville; now that I am a Major-General myself, he will treat me somewhat better than a footman. I wish my stout old Blakeney were here; he is the soldier's darling, as kind and brave as that poker-faced nobleman is brave and... I am your lordship's very humble servant. This is a young gentleman just arrived from America, who was involved in Braddock's sad business two years ago."

"Oh, indeed!" exclaimed the poker-faced nobleman. "I have the honor of speaking to Mr.—?"

"To Major-General Lambert, at your lordship's service, and who served in His Majesty's army some time before you entered it. That, Mr. Warrington, is the first commoner in England, Mr. Speaker Onslow. Where is your uncle? I shall have to present you personally to His Majesty if Sir Miles delays much longer."

As he spoke, the worthy General addressed himself entirely to his young friend, paying no attention whatsoever to his colleague, who stalked away with a startled expression, seemingly amazed by the other's audacity. A hundred years ago, a nobleman was a nobleman and expected to be admired precisely as such. Sir Miles's red waistcoat soon appeared in sight, and many cordial greetings exchanged between him, his nephew, and General Lambert. After all, we have described how Sir Miles was the most affectionate of men. Had the General truly departed Lord Wrotham's house? It seemed timely, given that his lordship wished to occupy it. Very well; but consider what a loss for the neighbors!

"We miss you, we positively miss you, my dear General," cried Sir Miles. "My daughters were in love with those lovely young ladies—upon my word, they were! My Lady Warrington and my girls debated endlessly how they might find an opportunity to make the acquaintance of your charming family. We feel as if we were old friends already, indeed we do, General, if you permit me the liberty of saying so; and we love you, if I may speak frankly, on account of your friendship and kindness to our dear nephews. Though we were a little jealous, I confess a little jealous of them, because they visited you so frequently. Often and often have I told my Lady Warrington, 'My dear, why don't we make acquaintance with the General? Why don't we invite him and his ladies to dine with some other plain country gentlemen?' Carry my most sincere respects to Mrs. Lambert, I pray, sir; and thank her for her goodness to these young gentlemen. My own flesh and blood, sir; my dear, dear brother's boys!" He passed his hand across his manly eyes, choking almost with generous and affectionate emotion.

While they discoursed—George Warrington meanwhile restraining his laughter with admirable gravity—the doors of the King's apartments opened, and pages entered, preceding His Majesty. He was followed by his burly son, His Royal Highness the Duke, a very corpulent Prince clad in a coat and face of blazing scarlet. Behind them came various gentlemen and officers of state, among whom George immediately recognized the famous Mr. Secretary Pitt by his tall stature, eagle eye, sharp beak, and grave, majestic presence. As I behold that solemn figure passing, even a century removed, I protest I feel a present awe and a desire to remove my hat. I am not frightened by George the Second, nor are my eyes dazzled by the portentous appearance of His Royal Highness the Duke of Culloden and Fontenoy; but the Great Commoner, the terrible Cornet of Horse! His figure seems to stride our narrow island of a century past like a Colossus. I hush as he passes in his gouty shoes, his thunderbolt hand wrapped in flannel. Perhaps, as we see him now emerging with dark looks from the

In the grand tapestry of human experience, the novelist finds that the mundane threads of daily occupation weave but a small portion of the narrative. When dealing with the profession of arms—where courage, peril, devotion, and heroic sacrifice naturally abound—the writer may confidently recount the actual affairs of life. Yet, for the merchant selling sugar and spice, the lawyer poring over dusty legal codes, or the tailor measuring breeches while seated cross-legged on a board, the raw material of their trade offers little fertile ground for storytelling. To chronicle the gritty reality of hobnailed shoes and eighteenpence-a-day wages would be tedious indeed. Even in the era we inhabit, poets romanticize shepherds in pink breeches and chintz waistcoats dancing before their flocks, ignoring the truth. Thus, with the notable exception of war, the novelist cannot expect to dwell upon the drudgery of commerce, law, theology, or drapery. Instead, authors must depict men stripped of their professions, revealing their passions, loves, laughter, and hatreds, while relegating their trades to the background, understood but unseen.

Consequently, our gaze wanders only through the theatre-lobbies, coffee-houses, pleasure-gardens, and festive rooms of life. Meanwhile, the serious world toils in its chambers and looms, oblivious to our observations. We watch Corydon cart litter and thresh barley only to pause for a moment of romance with Phillis; we glimpse Ancillula washing the nursery and serving breakfast before she can briefly meet Boopis, the policeman, through the railings. All day, the laborer beats the stale pavement, snatching only fleeting kisses or hurried pies. It is only when characters are disengaged from their labors that we can unite them with a disengaged reader.

Madame de Bernstein and the polished circles of White's and Arthur's had grown increasingly cold toward poor Harry Warrington. The Ring, the Mall, and the gaming tables no longer recognized him. Determined to conquer the world's indifference, the old lady urged her nephew to brace himself. "You pull a long face, Harry," she chided, dismissing his complaints. "Everyone must endure impertinences. If you receive a blow now that you are fallen, bear it with a smile and seek revenge later. *Moi qui vous parle!* Have I not eaten humble pie? Swallow yours, become the Clever Youth, and win back your lost place. Attend every rout, be civil to all women, yet display your spirit. With economy and your brother's generosity, you can maintain a genteel figure. Seek out the merchants in the City; they do not notice the loss of favor at Court. Visit my Lady Yarmouth; she favored you greatly!"

Yet, the woman who once embraced Harry with eager affection now met him with icy disdain. Her smiles vanished, replaced by peevishness and patronizing scorn. Before her guests, she cast gibes that flushed Harry's honest face with humiliation, striking deep wounds in his gentle heart. Her servants ignored him; Lady Yarmouth remained indisposed or occupied, refusing the warm hospitality she once offered. Devastated, Harry poured his sorrows to Mrs. Lambert. Sitting glumly in a window-seat while the ladies bustled about their domestic duties, he confessed his aunt's cruelty.

"I know you are the best people alive," he murmured to them, "the kindest, and I must be the dullest company in the world."

"You are not very lively, Harry," observed Miss Hetty, beginning to distance herself. "Is this the hero I thought you?"

"If he is unhappy, why should he be lively?" Theo asked gently. "He has a good heart, pained by his friends' desertion."

"I would rather show spirit than appear hurt," Hetty declared, clenching her fists. "I would smile even if that horrible old painted woman boxed my ears. She is dreadful, Mamma. You agree, Theo?"

Theo turned red. "I may not like her, but I will not call names before Harry's face."

"Provoking thing! You are always right!" Hetty cried, realizing her rudeness. "Harry, it was wrong of me to remark on your relations."

"It doesn't matter about the others," Harry insisted, rising with a flush of anger. "But it is hard that this one should turn upon me. I grew so fond of her. It drives me mad when those I love reject me."

"Suppose George did so?" Hetty suggested innocently.

"You are very clever, Hetty, but you do not know my brother," Harry snapped, his resolve hardening. "You would not dare suppose my brother George capable of anything unkind!" His cheeks burned crimson.

Hetty turned pale, offering no reply, only dropping a stiff curtsey as he departed. Later, whispering confidences to Theo, she lamented, "Oh! I thought it would be so happy to see him every day. Now that I do, I keep offending him. He isn't clever, Theo, but oh! Isn't he good, kind, and brave? Look how handsome he was when angry!"

Theo sighed. "Silly little thing, you try to make him look handsome."

Indeed, the dynamic among the youths shifted. General Lambert's nickname for his wife, "Bo," served as a rebuke to her sentimental matchmaking instincts. While little Hetty harbored fantasies, Harry had clearly moved on, superseded by his own maturity and lack of fortune.

"Now he is poor, we will show him the door," Mrs. Lambert remarked.

"That is what I always do, isn't it, Molly? Turn my back on friends in distress?" the General asked.

"No, my dear! I am a goose, I admit," she conceded, dabbing her eyes. "Let the poor boy come to us. Ours is almost the only house in this selfish city where he can find solace."

True to his nature, Martin Lambert opened his doors. Whenever Harry needed a meal or an evening's diversion, he found a corner at the Lambert table. George, too, was welcomed, though initially with more reserve. Cold and mistrustful compared to his twin, George nonetheless warmed to the family's inherent goodness. He saw Harry's misery and pitied his brother's depression. In his characteristic sarcasm, George would mock his own misfortune to cheer Harry, joking that Florac should have taken his scalp instead of the Indian's, allowing Harry to remain the favorite of the world and marry Maria.

"I never know if you laugh at me or yourself, George," Harry admitted.

"Precisely my case, Harry," George replied. "But I know there was never a better brother, nor better people than the Lamberts."

"And if I am unhappy, 'tis not your fault," Harry continued. "'Tis fate, 'tis having nothing to do. I must work, but how? Until I find purpose, George, I shall be miserable."

"We shall ask Mother," George said.

Later, Harry expressed alarm at the idea of returning to Virginia. "I love the girls here like sisters... but I don't want to hunt, shoot ducks, plant tobacco, and play whist forever. What else is there to do? For a clever man like you, George, you'd make your way anywhere. But for a poor fellow like me, what chance is there?"

They smoked their pipes in friendly silence, reflecting on their divergent paths. Eventually, the brothers separated somewhat; Harry avoided George's studies, sitting silently while his brother worked. They moved residences, escaping Madame de Bernstein's disapproval. George found joy in Sir Hans Sloane's new reading room in Montagu House, settling in Bloomsbury near Hampstead. The Lamberts, following Lord Wrotham's arrival in Mayfair, also relocated to Soho, remaining close neighbors. Harry frequently sought refuge at the Lambert household, joined occasionally by George, who preferred their company to the gambling circles of his aunt or the noisy coffee-houses. Together, these strangers in London explored the city's honest amusements: Vauxhall, Ranelagh, Marybone Gardens, and the Tower.

Martin Lambert ensured his children enjoyed innocent pleasures, and George, generous and open-handed, treated his friends, especially those who had shown kindness to his brother. Above all, Mr. Warrington possessed a profound passion for the theater. Having rarely enjoyed it in Virginia or Quebec, he found London's two great houses irresistible. Eager to share his delight, he invited his friends, who were equally willing. Should they choose Drury Lane for Garrick and Shakespeare, or Covent Garden for the new sensation?

A brilliant star had risen: Mr. John Home of Scotland. His tragedy was hailed as the most classic and elegant since antiquity. Unlike Shakespeare, whose works contained barbarisms that shocked polite audiences, Home refined grief and passion without descending into buffoonery. Admired by the highest ranks and celebrated by wits across Britain, including the famous Mr. Gray of Cambridge, the play was a triumph. Even in Edinburgh, the Scots shouted from the pit, "Where's Wully Shakspeare noo?"

"I should like to see the man who could beat Willy Shakspeare," the General laughed.

"Mere national prejudice," Mr. Warrington countered.

"Pooh, pooh! You've cried more over Mr. Sam Richardson than ever over Shakespeare, Molly!" the General teased. "Few women truly love Shakespeare; they claim to, but they don't."

"Oh, Papa!" cried three ladies, throwing up their hands.

Once the theatrical performances drew to a close, our companions departed in a carriage for Mr. Warrington's lodgings, where the Virginians had arranged an elegant supper. Mr. Warrington, eager to treat them with the utmost distinction, extended an invitation that the General and his wife gladly accepted. They were pleased to know they could afford their young friends such pleasure. The gathering comprised the General and Mrs. Lambert, their son recently returned from college, their two radiant daughters, and Mr. Spencer of the Temple—a new acquaintance George had met at the coffee-house. All partook cheerfully of the landlady's fare.

While I could not precisely determine the seating arrangement, it seems Miss Theo found herself beside the chickens, while Mr. George Warrington sat opposite her. Miss Hetty and a substantial ham shared the attention of Mr. Harry. Mrs. Lambert likely occupied George's right hand, leaving the positions of the General, his son, and the Templar to be deduced. Mr. Spencer had attended the same theater earlier, having even introduced George to the greenroom on a prior occasion. Conversation naturally reverted to the play, with several members of the party insisting they had been thoroughly delighted.

"As for what our gentlemen say, sir," cried Mrs. Lambert to Mr. Spencer, "do not believe a word of it. 'Tis a delightful piece, yet my husband and Mr. George behaved as poorly as possible."

"We laughed in the wrong places and wept when we should have remained dry-eyed," admitted the General. "That is the truth."

"You caused everyone in the boxes around us to glance about and shout 'Hush!' You made the pit cry, 'Silence in the boxes, yonder!' Such conduct I have never witnessed, and I quite blushed for you, Mr. Lambert!"

"Mamma considered it a tragedy, whereas we viewed it merely as amusement," the General explained. "George and I behaved perfectly, did we not, Theo?"

"Not when I happened to look your way, Papa!" Theo replied.

At this, the General exclaimed, "Was there ever such a saucy baggage seen?"

"You know, sir, I did not speak until commanded," Theo continued, adopting a modest tone. "I confess I was deeply moved by the play, particularly the beauty and acting of Mrs. Woffington. I was sorry that the poor mother should find her child and then lose him. I am sorry, too, Papa, if I ought not to have been sorry!" she added, offering a gentle smile.

"Women are not so clever as men, you know, Theo," Hetty interjected from across the table, casting a sly glance at Harry. "Next time we attend the theater, pray, Brother Jack, pinch us when we ought to cry, or give us a nudge when laughter is appropriate."

"I wish we had witnessed the fight," said General Lambert. "The clash between little Norval and the gigantic Norwegian—that would have been rare sport. You should write, Jack, and suggest it to Mr. Rich, the manager."

"I have not seen that particular scene," Harry stated gravely, wondering if his comment had been witty as the entire company laughed. "However, I saw Slack and Broughton at Marybone Gardens!" he added. "It would require no giant to knock over that little fellow in the red boots. For my part, I could throw him over my shoulder."

"Mr. Garrick is indeed a small man," noted Mr. Spencer. "Yet there are moments when he appears a giant. How magnificent he was in *Macbeth*, Mr. Warrington! That dagger scene was truly awful! You should have seen our host, ladies! I presented Mr. Warrington in the greenroom to Mr. Garrick and Mrs. Pritchard, and Lady Macbeth graciously took a pinch from his snuffbox."

"Did the wife of the Thane of Cawdor sneeze?" the General asked in a dramatic whisper.

"She thanked Mr. Warrington in tones so hollow and tragic that he recoiled, nearly upsetting his rappee, for Macbeth subsequently sneezed thrice," Mr. Spencer recounted.

"Macbeth, Macbeth, Macbeth!" cried the General. "And the great philosopher standing by Mr. Johnson declared, 'You must mind, Davy, lest thy sneeze awaken Duncan!' who, by the way, was conversing with the three witches as they sat against the wall."

"What! Have you been behind the scenes at the play? Oh, I would give worlds to go behind the scenes!" Theo exclaimed.

"And to see the ropes pulled, smell the tallow candles, examine the pasteboard gold, the tinsel jewels, and the painted old women, Theo?" George asked skeptically, demurely draining a glass of hock. "Do not look too closely."

"You were angry with your father and me," Theo protested.

"Nay, George!" the girl cried.

"Nay? I say, yes! You were angry with us because we laughed when you desired to cry." Turning to the General, George bowed. "If I may speak for you, sir, as well as myself, I believe we were not inclined to weep like the ladies because we stood behind the author's scenes, as it were. Up close to the young hero, we saw how much of him was mere rant and tinsel; and regarding the pale, tragical mother, her pallor was but white chalk, and her grief merely a pocket-handkerchief. Admit now, Theo, that you thought me very unfeeling?"

"If you discover it without my confession, what is the point of admitting it?" Theo retorted.

"Suppose I were to die?" George pressed. "And you saw Harry in grief? You would witness genuine affliction, a true tragedy, and you would grieve accordingly. But you would not be affected if you saw the undertaker in weeds and a black cloak!"

"Indeed, but I should, sir!" Mrs. Lambert assured him. "And so, I promise, would any daughter of mine."

"Perhaps we might find weeds of our own, Mr. Warrington," Theo suggested.

"Would you?" George cried, his cheeks flushing alongside Theo's; presumably, both noticed Hetty's bright, watchful eyes.

"The elder writers understood little of the pathetic," Mr. Spencer, the wit of the Temple, remarked.

"What do you think of Sophocles and *Antigone*?" called out Mr. John Lambert.

"Faith, our wits trouble themselves little about him unless an Oxford gentleman reminds us," someone replied. "I did not intend to go further back than Shakespeare, who, as you will agree, does not comprehend the elegant and pathetic as well as the moderns. Has he ever approached Belvidera, or Monimia, or Jane Shore? Can you find in his comic female characters the elegance of Congreve?" As the Templar distributed snuff, the conversation shifted.

"I think Mr. Spencer himself must have tried his hand?" someone asked.

"Many gentlemen of leisure have done so. Mr. Garrick, I admit, returned a piece of mine."

"And I confess I possess four acts of a play in one of my boxes," George announced.

"I'll bet it's as good as any of theirs," Harry whispered to his neighbor.

"Is it a tragedy or a comedy?" Mrs. Lambert inquired.

"Oh, a tragedy, containing two or three dreadful murders at least!" George replied.

"Let us perform it, and let the audience beware of their eyes! Yet my chief humor lies with a tyrant," the General declared.

"The tragedy! The tragedy! Fetch it instantly, Gumbo!" Mrs. Lambert shouted to the black servant.

Gumbo bowed low. "Tragedy? Yes, madam."

"In the great cowskin trunk, Gumbo," George said solemnly.

"Yes, sir," Gumbo responded with equal gravity.

"But my tragedy is buried beneath layers of linen, packages, books, and boots, Hetty," George noted.

"Never mind; let us retrieve it and fling the linen out the window!" Miss Hetty cried.

"And the great cowskin trunk resides with our agent in Bristol; thus, Gumbo must secure post-horses, and we shall wait until he returns the day after tomorrow," George explained.

The ladies emitted a comical "Oh!" While the father, perhaps more seriously, proposed, "Let us be grateful for this escape. Let us consider returning home. Our young gentlemen have treated us nobly. Let us all raise a parting bumper to Madam Esmond Warrington of Castlewood, in Virginia. Suppose, boys, you encounter a tall, handsome stepfather upon your return? Ladies as old as she have married before now."

"To Madam Esmond Warrington, my old schoolfellow!" Mrs. Lambert cheered. "I shall write to inform her how prettily her sons entertained us; and, Mr. George, I shall refrain from mentioning how poorly you behaved at the play!" With this final toast, the company dispersed. The General's coach and servant, accompanied by a flambeau, awaited to escort his family home.

After such an entertaining evening hosted by Mr. Warrington, was it not natural and proper for him to visit his guests later, ensuring they had arrived safely and rested well? One might imagine their coach taking the open country behind Montague House toward Oxford Road, only to be ambushed by footpads in the fields. Perhaps the ladies had caught cold or suffered restless sleep

The loyal hearts of Britain bristled with a mixture of awe and indignant sorrow upon learning of Mr. George Warrington's treatment of a gallant Prince—the beloved son of the greatest monarch, and Captain-General of the British forces. What an irrevocable favor had the youth spurned? What golden opportunity for advancement had he cast aside?

Will Esmond, whose tongue was perpetually seasoned with blasphemy, unleashed his fiercest curses upon his cousin's behavior, expressing a grim satisfaction that the "confounded young Mohock" was effectively cutting his own throat. Cousin Castlewood took a more charitable, albeit cynical, view. He suggested that a savage gentleman possessed the inherent right to scalp himself if he chose, or perhaps, he reasoned, Mr. Warrington had simply heard too much of the war-whoop during the disaster at Braddock and lacked the stomach for further combat. Will rejoiced doubly: first, that the younger brother had departed for hell, and second, that the elder was surely following close behind.

Upon their first encounter, Will declared he would ensure Mr. George understood exactly what he thought of him. "If you intend to insult George," cried Lady Maria sharply, "you had best ensure his brother Harry is out of earshot!" This remark seemed to provoke even more vitriol from Mr. Will regarding his twin kinsmen.

"My lord," Will retorted, "ta, ta, ta! No more of this squabbling! We cannot expect everyone in the family to be a warrior."

"I never heard your lordship lay claim to being one," Maria countered.

"Never, my dear; quite the opposite!" my lord replied with a simpering glance at his hands. "Will is our champion, and one fighter is sufficient for the house. With the two Mohocks present—George as the scholar and Harry as the soldier—it is a pity you did not choose George as your adversary when you sought a quarrel."

"Your lordship's hand is famous—at piquet," Will's mother interjected.

"It is a pretty one," my lord observed, examining his fingers. "My Lord Hervey's glove and mine were of identical size. Indeed, my hand, as you say, is suited for cards rather than war. Yours, my Lady Castlewood, is remarkably dexterous. How I bless the day you bestowed it upon my lamented father!"

In this game of verbal fencing, as in others requiring skill, his lordship found pleasure in engagement, possessing a cool head capable of defeating his entire family. Meanwhile, Madame de Bernstein, upon hearing of Mr. Warrington's blunder, erupted in fury, storming and scolding her household. She would have chastised George severely, but age had softened her resolve; she feared losing her courage, and furthermore, she harbored a lingering respect for her nephew.

"You will never make your fortune at court, nephew!" she groaned when he visited her shortly after his dismissal.

"It was never my wish, madam," George replied in a stately tone.

"Your wish was to assist Harry?" the Baroness pressed. "You might have been of service to your brother had you accepted the Duke's offer. Princes do not appreciate rejected favors, and I do not wonder His Royal Highness was offended."

"General Lambert said the same," George admitted, flushing slightly. "I see now I was wrong. Yet, please consider that I had never witnessed a court before, and I doubt I possess the talents to shine within one."

"I suspect not, my good nephew," the old lady remarked, taking a pinch of snuff. "And what then?"

"What then?" George echoed. "I harbor no ambition for such glory, and I can find contentment without it. When His Royal Highness spoke to me so kindly, my thought was merely that I shall make a terrible soldier, while my brother would excel. He possesses a hundred virtues for the profession wherein I am deficient; he would serve a commanding officer far better than I ever could. Imagine the Duke in battle, his horse shot beneath him as my poor chief's was at home. Would he not fare better mounted on a beast of courage and strength, capable of bearing him anywhere, rather than one unable to carry its rider's weight?"

"Au fait," the Baroness agreed. "His Royal Highness's charger must indeed be a sturdy steed, my dear!"

"Our Hannibal weighs no trifling amount," George muttered, shrugging.

"I do not quite follow your Hannibal," the Baroness noted.

"When Mr. Wolfe and Mr. Lambert remonstrated with me as you have done, madam," George laughed, "I offered the same defense. I told them I presented the Prince with the finest soldier in the family, and both gentlemen conceded that my error at least had justification. Who knows but they may rectify matters with His Royal Highness? My experience of battle has revealed how ill-suited my genius is for warfare. Last night, we viewed the Scottish play everyone discusses. When the hero, young Norval, expressed his longing to follow a warlike lord to the field, I thought to myself, 'How like my Harry is to him, save that he does not boast.' Harry pines now for a red coat; if left unchecked, he will take the shilling. He keeps the map of Germany perpetually before his eyes, following the King of Prussia wherever he goes. He fears neither men nor gods. As for me, I cherish my books and quietude above all, preferring to read of battles in Homer or Lucan."

"Then what made a soldier of you at all, my dear? And why did you not send Harry with Mr. Braddock instead of going yourself?" the Baroness asked.

"My mother loved her younger son best," George answered darkly. "Furthermore, with the enemy invading our soil, it was my duty, as the head of our family, to join the campaign. Had I been a Scot twelve years prior, I should have been..."

"Hush, sir! Or I shall be more angry than ever!" the old lady commanded, her face betraying perfect amusement.

George's explanation succeeded in pacifying Madame de Bernstein, an elderly woman whose principles were, we fear, somewhat loose. However, to the loyal hearts of Sir Miles Warrington and his wife, the young man's conduct delivered a devastating blow.

"I should have thought," her ladyship exclaimed, "that from my sister Esmond Warrington's letter, I believed her brother's widow to be a woman of sound sense and judgment, who had educated her sons fittingly. But what, Sir Miles, what, my dear Thomas Claypool, can we think of an education resulting so lamentably for both these youths?"

"The elder seems to command considerable knowledge of Latin, speaking French and German fluently as well," Mr. Claypool noted. "I heard him converse with the Hanoverian Envoy at the Baroness's rout.
