He jabbered French easily; when he struggled with High Dutch, he and the Envoy switched to Latin, chatting away until the entire room stared."

"It is not language, Thomas Claypool, but principles!" the virtuous matron cried. "What must Mr. Warrington's principles be when he rejects an offer from his Prince? Can he speak High Dutch? Precisely because he can, he *ought* to have accepted His Royal Highness's condescension and rendered himself useful in the campaign! Look at our son, look at Miles!"

"Hold up thy head, Miley, my boy!" called Papa.

"I trust, Sir Miles, that as a Member of Parliament and an English gentleman, you will attend His Royal Highness's levee tomorrow and declare that, had such an offer been made for our child, we would have accepted it, though our boy is but ten years of age."

"Faith, Miley, thou wouldst make a fine little drummer or fifer!" Papa quipped. "Shall we have thee as a little soldier, Miley?"

"Anything, sir, anything! A Warrington ought to be ready at any moment to be cut to pieces for his sovereign!" the matron shouted, pointing to the boy. Upon understanding his mother's proposal, the child protested with a loud roar, amidst which he was spirited away by Screwby.

Obedient to these conjugal commands, Sir Miles attended the Prince's levee the following day, offering a protest of love and duty which the Prince graciously accepted, remarking, "Nobody ever supposed that Sir Miles Warrington would ever refuse any place offered to him." A compliment indeed, repeated widely by Lady Warrington as proof of the implicit reliance the royal family placed on the Warringtons' loyalty.

Consequently, when Sir Miles and his wife encountered George, they greeted him with a ghastly commiseration—a look reserved for relatives or friends who have committed a fatal clumsiness, lost a lawsuit, entered a room just as the company begins to abuse them, watched their banker fail, or seen their names in the commercial columns of the *London Gazette*. It is the universal expression of pity for someone guilty of a notorious fault, blunder, or misfortune. Who does not recognize this face? Whose dear relations have not so deplored a living person, not dead, but alive? If you have never been in scrapes, never sown wild oats, never been unfortunate or spoken imprudently, never sinned and repented, then, sir, you are a wiseacre wasting your time on novels, and this tale is not for you.

Yet, it was hardly fair for Sir Miles

If Harry Warrington possessed a fervor for martial pursuits, the turmoil of Europe provided ample fuel. War stirred across the continent, and the salons of London buzzed with its rhetoric, inflaming his spirit. Yet, despite the defeat of their own gracious Hanoverian prince, the world was intoxicated by the glory of the "Protestant Hero," the King of Prussia. It was fortunate, in my humble opinion, that Harry did not participate in these campaigns. Had he fought at Rossbach on that fateful fifth of November, 1757—the celebrated Gunpowder Fête—or at the subsequent slaughter of Leuthen, my narrative would have been forced to chronicle battles already immortalized by another pen. I prefer not to compete with such a grim chariot of thundering war. Could my jaunty, yellow park-phaeton rival that steed of foam and flame? Could my meek little Pegasus withstand the shock of battle?

Dear reader, with whom I delight in conversing from the wings of history, clad in the attire and dialect of a bygone age, consider this a mercy: Harry Warrington did not follow the King of Prussia. Otherwise, I would have been compelled to describe conflicts destined for the brush of Thomas Carlyle, and I fear the comparison would prove odious.

Instead, Harry led a sulky, useless existence. He haunted military coffee-houses, caring little for literature save the daily newsprint. He held novels in contempt, unable to comprehend why ladies wept over the sentimental ravings of Mr. Richardson. He possessed a hearty laugh, yet it invariably arrived a moment too late, long after the jest had expired. Why should every gentleman possess a literary bent? Are we diminished by friends who never compose a couplet? Ruined, idle, and dependent on his brother for sustenance, Harry would drift off to sleep over any book he touched, leaving his great, strong hands with no purpose. Consider Achilles, exiled to the court of a distant king to escape harm, wasting his youth among women and distaffs. Where was Pyrrhus born? A mettlesome soul like Achilles should not be led by strings; left to dawdle, he risks far greater mischief than mere idleness.

Two elder ladies of the Lambert family, possessing hearts of uncommon tenderness, offered Harry an unfailing pity—a comfort unique to the female sex. They cheered him in grief, consoled him in trouble, soothed his wrath, and doubled his joy in prosperity. When Fortune strikes with slings and arrows, who but the gentle sea-nymphs—those sympathetic women—kiss our writhing feet and moisten our parched lips with tears? They do not abandon us after our overthrow.

Yet, within the Lambert household, sentiments varied. While Theo and her mother overflowed with compassion, Hetty's heart seemed hardened, bordering on savagery toward Harry. She chafed at his lack of glory and his continued dependency. With the world at arms, could he not wield a musket? Harvest was underway, and thousands swung their sickles; could he not cut down a sheaf of glory instead?

"Why, how savage the little thing is with him!" Papa observed after witnessing Hetty's relentless barrage against Harry, who stood as a quivering target in the drawing-room.

"Her conduct is abominable!" cried Mama. "She deserves a whipping and confinement to bed."

Theo interjected gently, suggesting Hetty's anger stemmed from affection. "Perhaps it is because she likes him better than any of us. If I loved someone, I would desire to admire them always, to deem every action right, and to feel my beloved superior to all other gentlemen."

Mrs. Lambert agreed, attributing the family's high standards to their father's excellence. "He has spoiled us. Have you ever seen anyone to compare?"

Theo admitted few could match him in temper or bravery, noting that while others were brave or learned, Mr. George possessed a loftiness of manner and a tender heart hidden beneath his satire. She recalled instances of George treating children to apples or giving coin to beggars, proving his nobility through action. Her discourse drifted into reciting verses from Molière, her voice faltering slightly as she realized her mother's odd gaze followed her every word. Blushing like a rose, she fled the room, leaving her mother to wonder at the fluttering heart and the sudden silence.

Indeed, a pattern emerged: when Harry visited, Hetty descended to greet him; when George arrived, Theo appeared. It was a curious, unspoken arrangement. Theo, with keen observation, noted the distinction between Harry's golden hair and George's dark locks, ensuring she never confused the brothers. Conversely, Hetty remained composed and polite whenever the dark-haired gentleman entered.

One afternoon, Harry sat on a couch, radiating profound despondency. Suddenly, Hetty appeared.

"And pray, sir, what makes your honor look so glum this morning?" she asked.

"Oh, Hetty!" Harry sighed. "I have nothing else to do but look glum. As boys, I begged holidays to swing on gates or make ducks and drakes, yet those idle days were the most melancholy. What fills my time now? Breakfast, walks, dinner, walks, tea, supper—and a pipe of Virginia tobacco."

"I laugh to imagine you at the head of a class, summoned by a master!" Hetty teased.

"I should not be," Harry replied humbly. "George could lead any class, but I am no scholar. Neglecting myself in youth, I failed to cultivate my mind. Perhaps a cane would have helped."

"It might do you good now!" Hetty suggested. "Does Tom's account of his schooling stir any longing in you?"

Tom's tale confirmed Harry's self-loathing. He realized he lacked genius for books and was merely suited to squandering his inheritance, lounging in coffee-houses, racing horses, or galloping hounds. "I am good for nothing," he confessed despairingly.

"What, such a great, brave, strong fellow good for nothing?" Hetty exclaimed indignantly. "I would not admit such a truth to any woman, even if I were twice as useless!"

"What am I to do? I seek leave to join the army, but Madam Esmond ignores me. It is the only path for me. I have exhausted my funds and borrowed heavily from my brother. If my mother would send me, I would leap at the chance."

"A gentleman of spirit requires no woman to buckle his sword or polish his firelock!" Hetty declared, pacing the room with a newfound military bearing. "Recall Sir John Armytage at court. Engaged to a famous beauty, he volunteered immediately when the King asked when he would depart. 'Tomorrow, Your Majesty,' he replied. That is a soldier's answer. Even my father longs to go, leaving us brats behind. Oh, why wasn't I a man? My brothers are bound for the Church, but I could have been a famous little soldier!"

Harry watched her with tender admiration. "I think I would hesitate to see a musket on that little shoulder or a wound on that pretty face, Hetty."

"Wounds! Who fears wounds?" she cried. "If I could carry a musket, I would use it. Men think we are fit only for puddings and samplers. Why wasn't I a man? George read Tasso to us yesterday. Look, here is the book marked where we left off."

She quoted passionately: *"Alas! why did not Heaven these members frail / With lively force and vigour strengthen, so / That I this silken gown..."*

"Silken gown?" Harry queried.

"Well, sir, it is actually calimanco—but the verse continues: *'...this silken gown and slender veil / Might for a breastplate and a helm forgo; / Then should not heat, nor cold, nor rain, nor hail... / Withhold me; but I would, both day and night, / In pitched field or private combat, fight.'"*

"Fight? Yes, that I would!" Hetty insisted. "Why are both my brothers becoming parsons? One child of Papa's ought to be a soldier!"

Harry laughed softly, feeling no inclination to strike such a tender warrior. "Here is a finger nearly as large as your arm," he mused. "How would you stand before a great, strong man? I would like to see a man attempt to injure you. You are a delicate, tender creature. Do you suppose any scoundrel would dare?"

Excited by this protective fantasy, Harry paced the room, chafing at the thought of any French rogue daring to insult Miss Hester Lambert. This silent courage captivated Hetty. In reality, she was no bolder than the fictional Erminia she had been reading about, unaware that Harry knew nothing of her. He was lost in his own troubles, bewildered by the literary giants and enchantresses surrounding them. Nature dictates that great, strong men often attract frail, tender women, yet Harry's potential union with Hetty promised a quiet domesticity rather than heroic conquest.

While Hetty fired her sarcastic arrows, Harry barely felt the sting, too magnanimous to grasp her subtle hints. Was she trying to shame him by offering her own casque and breastplate? The simple-minded youth either melted at the prospect of her danger or dissolved into laughter at the absurdity of her fighting.

"Pray, what is the use of a strong hand if it only holds a skein of silk for my mother?" Hetty demanded. "What good is strength in a drawing

George Warrington managed his legal studies with a distinct lack of solemnity, ensuring they never impeded his comfort, his pleasures, nor his precious health. Through letters from Madam Esmond, he had been instructed that despite wearing the student's gown and dining among nameless commoners at the hall, he bore an ancient name worthy of support and could rank alongside the foremost figures of his nation. She urged him to study as a gentleman, not as a mere professional drudge. The young man obeyed this injunction with sufficient obedience; consequently, he may be described not as belonging to the rank-and-file of the law, but as a volunteer in its service, akin to certain young gentlemen recently mentioned. Although the law a century prior was far less exacting than it is today—allowing her disciples ample leisure, pleasure, punch, coffee-house visits, and holidays, whereas modern jurisprudence scarcely permits time for amusement, recreation, instruction, sleep, or dinner—it remained a jealous mistress demanding exclusive attention. As history notes, Murray might have chosen to be an Ovid adorned with bays rather than a Lord Chief Justice draped in ermine; perhaps Mr. Warrington might have ascended to a peerage and the woolsack had he studied longer, worked harder, mastered courtiership, and gained the favor of attorneys. Had he been other than he was, in short. Yet, he treated Themis with decent respect, loving letters far more than law. The black-letter of Chaucer held infinite charm for him, vastly superior to the Gothic pages of Hale and Coke.

Indeed, letters were cherished in those quaint times, and authors served as living authorities. Gentlemen appealed to Virgil or Lucan in the Courts or the House of Commons. "What does Statius say?" "What does Juvenal declare?"—let alone Cicero or Tacitus—regarding any given point? Their reign has ended, the good old Heathens; the worship of Jupiter and Juno is no more fashionable than the cultivation of pagan poetry or ethics. The age of economists and calculators has superseded them; Tooke's *Pantheon* is now deserted and ridiculed. Occasionally, perhaps, a Stanley sacrifices a kid, a Gladstone wreaths a laurel, or a Lytton burns incense in honor of the Olympians. But what concern do they hold for Lambeth, Birmingham, or the Tower Hamlets regarding ancient rites, divinities, or worship? Who exactly are the Muses, and what utility lies in all that Greek and Latin rubbish? What is Mount Helicon, and who cares? Who was Thalia, pray, and what is the length of her 'i'? Does anyone know whether Melpomene's name comprises three or four syllables? And can you tell from whose design I stole the figure of Tragedy that adorns the initial 'G' of this chapter?

It has been noted how Mr. George, in his youth and during the long leisure afforded him at home and later in his imprisonment within the French fort on the banks of the Monongahela, spent his idle hours courting Melpomene. The result of this union was a tragedy, omitted from Bell's Theatre, though I suspect it is no inferior to many pieces printed therein. Most young men pay homage to the Tragic Muse first, much as they fall in love with women significantly older than themselves. Let the candid reader admit, if he possesses a literary inclination, that his ambition was once of the highest order. However, in his riper years, he may have lowered his pretensions, deeming the translation of an ode by Horace or the versification of a song by Waller or Prior into decent Alcaics or Sapphics the apex of his capability. Yet, tragedy and epic engaged his green, unknowing youth, and no prize but the highest seemed fit for him.

Upon arriving in London, George Warrington attended theatrical performances at both houses, frequented the theatrical coffee-houses, and listened to the opinions of the critics. He might be seen at the Bedford between plays or supping at the Cecil alongside wits and actors post-performance. Here, he gradually befriended the players and those writers and poets known to the public. One might occasionally spot the tough old Macklin, the frolicsome Foote, the vivacious Hippisley, or the sprightly Mr. Garrick himself in these houses of entertainment. Our gentleman, through his wit, modesty, and the high reputation for wealth he possessed, became exceedingly popular in the coffee-house circles. Actors drank bowls of punch with him; critics dined at his expense with great affability. To maintain intimacy with an author or actor has long been a delight for many young men; to hob-nob with Bobadil or Henry the Fifth, to accept a pinch from Aristarchus's own box, to escort Juliet into her coach, or to help Monimia into her chair are privileges that would enchant most youths of a poetic bent. No wonder George Warrington loved the theatre. Furthermore, he derived satisfaction from knowing his mother only half-approved of plays and playhouses, allowing him to feast on fruit strictly forbidden at home. He hosted several elegant entertainments for the players, and it was rumored that one or two distinguished geniuses had even condescended to borrow money from him.

As he polished and embellished his masterpiece with new beauties, he naturally sought the counsel of certain friends, who offered applause and advice. Mr. Spencer, his new acquaintance from the Temple, hosted a breakfast at his chambers in Fig Tree Court, where Mr. Warrington read portions of his play. The gentlemen present declared it possessed uncommon merit. Even the learned Mr. Johnson, invited to the gathering, graciously remarked that the piece demonstrated talent. It did, to be sure, wage war against the unities; yet these had been violated by other authors, and Mr. Warrington might sacrifice them as readily as any. There was something in Mr. W.'s tragedy that reminded Mr. Johnson of both *Coriolanus* and *Othello*. "And two very good things too, sir!" the author pleaded. "Well, well, there was no doubt on that point," replied Mr. Spencer. "'Tis certain your catastrophe is terrible, just, and being in part true, is not the less awful."

The plot of Mr. Warrington's tragedy was replete with battle and murder. A favorite book of his grandfather had been the life of Old George Frundsberg of Mindelheim, a colonel of foot-soldiers in the Imperial service at the Battle of Pavia and during the wars of the Constable Bourbon. Among Frundsberg's military companions was a certain Carpzow, or Carpezan, whom our friend selected as the hero of his tragedy. The first act, as it currently stands in Sir George Warrington's manuscript, is set before a convent on the Rhine, which the Lutherans, under Carpezan, are besieging. These are depicted as a godless gang; they have pulled the beards of Roman friars and torn the veils of hundreds of religious women. A dozen tremble within the convent walls, the garrison of which has promised to surrender unless expected reinforcements arrive before midday. Meanwhile, an armistice holds, and the sentries inside watch with hungry eyes as soldiers and camp followers gamble on the grass before the gate.

Twelve o'clock strikes: *ding, ding, dong!* upon the convent bell. No succours have arrived. "Open gates, warder! Admit the famous Protestant hero, the terror of Turks on the Danube and Papists in the Lombard plains—Colonel Carpezan!" See him approach, clad in complete steel, his battle-hammer resting over his shoulder, having battered countless infidel sconces, his flags displayed, his trumpets blaring. "No rudeness, my men," commands Carpezan. "The wine is yours, and the convent larder and cellar are excellent: the church plate shall be melted. Any of the garrison wishing to take service with Gaspar Carpezan are welcome and shall receive good pay. No insult to the religious ladies! I have promised them a safe-conduct, and he who lays a finger on them shall hang! Mind that Provost Marshal!" The Provost Marshal, a huge fellow in a red doublet, nods his head.

"We shall see more of that Provost Marshal, or executioner," Mr. Spencer explains to his guests.

"A very agreeable acquaintance, I am sure—I shall be delighted to meet the gentleman again!" says Mr. Johnson, wagging his head over his tea. "This scene of mercenaries, camp followers, and their wild sports is novel and stirring, Mr. Warrington. I offer you my compliments. The Colonel has entered the convent, I believe? Now let us hear what he intends to do there."

The Abbess and one or two of her oldest ladies appear before the conqueror. Despite his status, they grant him an audience in their sacred halls. They have heard tales of his violent behavior in conventual establishments before. That hammer, which he always carries into action, has shattered many sacred images in religious houses. Pounds of convent plate are known to have been melted by this sacrilegious plunderer! No wonder the Abbess-Princess of St. Mary's, a lady of violent prejudices, free speech, and noble birth, harbors a dislike for the low-born heretic who now lords it in her convent, and tells Carpezan a bit of her mind. This scene, wherein the lady gains the upper hand over the Colonel, was greatly appreciated by Mr. Warrington's audience at the Temple. Terrible as he might be in war

Trusty Gumbo could find no solace in his sorrow for the departure of his beloved master. To judge by his initial tears and mournful howls upon hearing the news of Mr. Harry's enlistment, one might have believed the negro's heart shattered by the separation. Naturally, he sought sympathy among the maid-servants at Mr. Lambert's lodgings. Whichever way that dusky youth turned, he found comfort in female society. Their fair and tender bosoms possessed a capacity for pity toward the poor African that rendered the darkness of Gumbo's complexion no more repulsive to them than Othello's was to Desdemona. Indeed, Europe has rarely been so squeamish regarding Africa as a certain other respectable quarter suggests. Nay, some Africans—witness the Chevalier de St. Georges, for instance—have become notorious favorites of the fair sex. Thus, in his humbler station, was Mr. Gumbo. The Lambert servants wept freely in his company; the maids kindly regarded him not merely as Mr. Harry's man, but as their brother.

Hetty could not help laughing when she discovered Gumbo roaring because his master had gone "a volunteer," as he termed it, and had not taken him along. He was ready to save Master Harry's life on any given day, declaring he would have done so and allowed himself to be cut into twenty thousand pieces rather than let harm come to his master. Yet, nature required support, and he condescended to fortify it with generous supplies of beer and cold meat in the kitchen. Though it was certain he was greedy, idle, and prone to lying, Hetty still gave him half a crown and treated him with especial kindness. Her tongue, usually so pert, grew remarkably gentle now, as if it had never known a jest. She moved about the house mute and meek. She was humble to her mother, grateful to John and Betty when they served dinner, patient with Polly when the latter pulled her hair during combing, long-suffering when Charley from school trod on her toes or disrupted her workbox, and silent in her father's presence. Oh, what a transmogrified little Hetty she had become! If her father had ordered her to roast the leg of mutton or walk to church arm-in-arm with Gumbo, she would have curtsied and replied, "Yes, if you please, dear papa!"

Leg of mutton! What sort of sustenance were some poor volunteers consuming with cannonballs flying overhead? Church! When it came to the prayers of wartime, how her knees knocked together as she knelt, hiding her head in the pew. She lowered her gaze when the parson read, "Thou shalt do no murder," from the communion rail, fearing he must be staring right at her. How she pondered all travelers by land and sea! How she sickened as she rushed to the newspaper to scan for news of the Expedition! How she watched her father when he returned from the Ordnance Office, scrutinizing his face for signs of good or ill tidings! Was he well? Had he been promoted to General? Was he wounded and imprisoned? Ah, me! Or perhaps had both his legs been severed by a single shot, like that pensioner they had seen in Chelsea Gardens the previous day? She would go on wooden legs for the rest of her life if his safety could ensure his return; at least, she vowed never to rise from her knees until he was back.

"Haven't you heard of people, Theo," she asked, "whose hair turns gray in a single night? I shouldn't wonder if mine did—I shouldn't wonder in the least." She glanced into the mirror to verify this phenomenon. "Hetty dear, you used not to be so nervous when papa was away in Minorca," remarked Theo. "Ah, Theo! One may very well see that George is not with the army, but safe at home," rejoined Hetty. At this, the elder sister blushed deeply and looked profoundly pensive. *Au fait*, if Mr. George had been in the army, that, you see, would have been another pair of boots.

Meanwhile, we shall not further harrow anyone's kind feelings, but simply state that Harry was, for the present, as safe as any officer of the Life Guards at Regent's Park Barracks. The first expedition in which our gallant volunteer participated may be deemed successful, though certainly not glorious. The British Lion, or any other lion, cannot always find a worthy adversary to combat, nor a battle-royal to deliver. Suppose he ventures forth seeking a tiger that refuses to appear, lays his paws on a goose, and gobbles it up? Lions, we know, must subsist like any other animal. But suppose, advancing into the forest in search of the aforementioned tiger and bellowing his challenge of war, he spies not one but six tigers approaching? This is manifestly not his game. He tucks his tail between his royal legs and retreats to his own snug den as swiftly as possible. Were he to attempt fighting six tigers, you might write that Lion down as an ass.

Now, Harry Warrington's first feat of war unfolded thus: He and approximately thirteen thousand other fighting men embarked in various ships and transports on the 1st of June from the Isle of Wight. At daybreak on the 5th, the fleet steamed into the Bay of Cancale in Brittany. For a while, he and the gentlemen volunteers enjoyed the pleasure of examining the French coast from their vessels, while the Commander-in-Chief and the Commodore reconnoitered the bay in a cutter. Cattle were spotted, along with some dragoons who trotted off into the distance; and a small fort armed with a couple of guns had the audacity to fire upon His Grace of Marlborough and the Commodore in the cutter. By two o'clock, the entire British fleet was anchored, and signals were made for all the grenadier companies of eleven regiments to embark on flat-bottomed boats and assemble around the Commodore's ship, the *Essex*. Meanwhile, Mr. Howe, hoisting his broad pennant aboard the *Success* frigate, approached as near to shore as possible, followed by the other frigates to protect the troop landing. Now, under the command of Lord George Sackville and General Dury, the gentlemen volunteers, the grenadier companies, and three battalions of guards pulled ashore.

On this occasion, the gentlemen volunteers could perform no heroic deeds, for the French—who should have remained to fight them—fled. Having silenced the fire of the little fort that had disturbed the Commander-in-Chief's reconnaissance, the army promptly assaulted it, capturing the entire garrison and shooting one man in the leg. Indeed, he was but an elderly gentleman who had gallantly fired his two guns and told his conquerors, "If every Frenchman had acted like me, you would not have landed at Cancale at all." The advanced detachment of invaders took possession of the village of Cancale, where they lay upon their arms all night. Our volunteer was jokingly chided by his comrades about his eagerness to tread the warpath and bring in two or three French scalps. None such, however, fell under his tomahawk; the only person slain on the whole day was a French gentleman riding with his servant, who was surprised by Volunteer Lord Downe, marching forward with a company of King's troops. My Lord Downe offered the gentleman quarter, which he foolishly refused, whereupon he, his servant, and the two horses were immediately shot.

The following day, the entire force was landed and advanced from Cancale to St. Malo. All villages through which the troops passed were emptied, and the roads were so narrow in many places that men had to march single file, vulnerable to being shot down from behind tall, leafy hedges had there been any enemy to disturb them. At nightfall, the army arrived before St. Malo and were greeted by a barrage of artillery from the town, which caused little damage in the darkness. Covering themselves under this smoke, the British set fire to ships, wooden structures, pitch, and tar magazines in the harbor, creating a prodigious conflagration that lasted the entire night. This feat was accomplished without any attempt by the French to molest the British force. However, as it was confidently asserted that a considerable French force resided within St. Malo, though they refused to emerge, His Grace the Duke of Marlborough and My Lord George Sackville decided not to disturb the garrison. Instead, they marched back to Cancale—and then boarded their ships.

If this were not a veracious history, would it not have been easier to send our Virginian on a more glorious campaign? Exactly four weeks after his departure from England, Mr. Warrington found himself back in Portsmouth. He addressed a letter to his brother George, whom the latter hastened to Dean Street upon receiving it.

"Glorious news, ladies!" cried George, finding the Lambert family all at breakfast. "Our champion has returned. He has endured all sorts of dangers but survived them all. He has seen dragons—upon my word, he says so."

"Dragons! What do you mean, Mr. Warrington?"

"But not killed any—he says so, as you shall hear. He writes: 'Dearest Brother—I think you will be glad to hear that I am returned, without any commission as yet; without any wounds or glory; but, at any rate, alive and hearty.' On board our ship, we were almost as crowded as poor Mr. Holwell and his friends in their Black Hole at Calcutta. We encountered rough weather,

"Great Powers! Will the vainglory of men, particularly the French, ever find its end?" cried Harry, his voice tinged with a mixture of indignation and weary amusement. "Can it truly be believed that following the skirmish at Saint-Cast—a mere tactical maneuver to sever a rear guard, as you know—they were so devoid of compassion as to waste countless pounds of gunpowder firing blindly at the Invalides in Paris? And then to boast and bluster over our misfortune! Is there any true magnanimity in cheering and huzzahing simply because five or six hundred brave souls were overwhelmed by ten thousand on a beach, meeting a fate often reserved for the stragglers?

"I once felt compelled to sketch a vivid portrait of the rejoicings in London celebrating our supposed glory at Saint-Malo. I imagined the polished cannons paraded by our gallant tars, the sturdy horse-grenadiers prancing proudly, while the mob waved hats, roared with cheers, and picked pockets. Meanwhile, our friends in a balcony overlooking Fleet Street watched, blessing this scene of British triumph. But now that the French Invalides have been so undignified as to mimic the Tower of London, setting up their Saint-Cast against our Saint-Malo, I scorn to revisit such a stale topic. I say *Nolo*, not *Malo*; let me be content if Harry returns from one expedition and another with his skin intact. And have I ever suggested he was even bruised? For fear of alarming my fair young readers, I declared he was as well as he had ever been in his life. The sea air merely bronzed his cheeks, and the whistling bullets spared his side-curls. The ocean dampened his gaiters and clothes without ever attempting to swallow him whole. True, he briefly exposed the lapels of his coat to the enemy, but only for the shortest possible duration, retreating from their gaze with all speed. What are lapels but reverses? Coats possess them, as do men; and our duty is to wear them with courage and good humor."

"I can tell you," Harry continued, recounting the tale to Miss Hetty, "that we were all forced to run for our lives. When our lines finally broke, survival belonged to whoever could reach the boats fastest. The French cavalry and infantry pursued us down to the shore, intermingling with us, hacking down our men and driving bayonets into the earth. Poor Armytage was shot ahead of me and fell. I scooped him up and stumbled through the churning surf until we reached a boat. It was fortunate the sailors aboard weren't terrified; shots whistled past their ears, shattered their oar-blades, and riddled their flag. Yet, the officer in command remained as cool as if he were sipping punch in Portsmouth—a drink we enjoyed upon landing, I assure you. Poor Sir John was less fortunate than I. He never made it to the ship. The service has lost a fine soldier, and Miss Howe a true gentleman to her husband. Such casualties happen; consequently, his brother receives the promotion—the baronetcy."

"It is the poor lady I am thinking of," Miss Hetty interrupted, her voice trembling slightly. "And the King. Why did His Majesty encourage Sir John Armytage to go? A gentleman cannot refuse a command from such a quarter. Now the poor gentleman lies dead! Oh, what a state of distress His Majesty must be in!"

"I have no doubt His Majesty will be in a profound state of grief," replied her father, shaking his head solemnly.

"Now you are laughing!" Hetty exclaimed, turning to him. "Do you mean, sir, that when a gentleman dies in his service, literally at his feet, the King of England feels nothing?"

"If I thought that, I vow I would support the Pretender!" the General interjected sharply. He saw the turmoil in Hetty's eyes—a mix of remorse, consternation, and overwhelming gratitude that the danger had passed—and knew her small heart was racing.

"No, my dear," the General said gently. "Were kings to weep for every soldier, what a burden you would place upon them! I hold a higher opinion of His Majesty than to suppose him so weak. Furthermore, if Miss Hester Lambert were to embrace the Pretender, I doubt she would find any greater happiness. That family was never renowned for excessive sentimentality."

"But if the King sent Harry—I mean, Sir John Armytage—directly into the war where he lost his life, ought not His Majesty to repent deeply?" the young lady pressed.

"If Harry had fallen, undoubtedly the court would have entered mourning," remarked the General. "As it stands, gentlemen and ladies wore colored clothing yesterday."

"Why shouldn't we light bonfires for a defeat and don sackcloth and ashes after a victory?" asked George.

"I protest, I do not wish to thank Heaven for aiding us in burning the ships at Cherbourg," George countered.

"Yes, you do, George! Though I have no right to speak, and you are certainly not cleverer than usual. Still, when your country wins, you are glad—I know I am. When I flee before the French, I am ashamed; I cannot help it, though I did it," admitted Harry. "'Tis not right, somehow, that Englishmen should have to do such things," he added gravely. George smiled but chose not to press his brother on what the Frenchman's perspective might be.

"'Tis a wretched business," Harry continued, his tone heavy. "But luckily, it was no worse. The story regarding the French is that their Governor, the Duke of Aiguillon, was something of a 'moistened chicken.' Our entire retreat might have been annihilated had we not moved with such haste. The French local militia behaved famously, I am happy to report, along with numerous gentleman volunteers who stood fast in the front ranks as they should. They say the Chevalier of Tour d'Auvergne engaged despite the Duke's explicit orders. Officers brought lists of prisoners and wounded to General Bligh and Lord Howe. He is now a lord, having received the title upon news of his brother's death reaching home, George. He is a brave fellow, whether titled or not."

"And his sister, who was betrothed to poor Sir John Armytage... consider her plight!" sighed Miss Hetty, recently prone to sentimentality.

"And his mother!" cried Mrs. Lambert. "Have you read her address in the papers to the electors of Nottingham? 'Lord Howe being now absent upon public service, and Lieutenant-Colonel Howe with his regiment at Louisbourg, it rests upon me to beg the favor of your votes...' Isn't this a gallant woman?"

"A laconic woman," observed George.

"How can sons fail to be brave when nurtured by a mother like that?" asked the General.

Our two young men exchanged glances. "If one of us falls defending our country, we have a mother in Sparta who would think and write thus," George noted.

"If Sparta is anywhere near Virginia, I reckon we have it," Harry remarked dryly. Then, pensively, he added, "To think that we should both meet the enemy and both be whipped by him, brother!"

Hetty looked at him, recalling the image of him the day prior: tottering through the water toward the boats, his comrade bleeding on his shoulder, enemies pursuing, bullets flying thick and fast. It was she who had driven him into that peril. Her words had stung him. Yet, now that he had returned, he offered no rebuke. Unless questioned, he spoke sparingly of his adventures. He was grave and courteous with Hetty, but especially frank and tender with the rest of the family. Those taunts, however, had wounded him.

"Little hand!" his demeanor seemed to whisper, "you should not have raised yourself against me! To scorn anyone is ill; to scorn one so devoted to you and yours is worse. I may lack quick wit, but in matters of the heart, I stand equal to the best, and the finest heart your family has known."

Harry's injustice, coupled with his magnanimous endurance of it, allowed him to reclaim Miss Hetty's esteem, erasing the loss suffered during his previous months of inglorious idleness. The respect she paid to the brave transformed her. She was no longer sharp-tongued or sarcastic regarding his conduct. In short, Miss Hetty was humbled, altered, and improved. Consequently, the entire world seemed to shift in Harry's favor, just as he shifted in theirs. No longer sulky or indolent, he ceased to despise himself or defy his neighbors. The colonel of his regiment reported his behavior as exemplary and recommended him for one of the commissions vacated by the expedition's casualties. Although the mission ended unluckily, it proved fortuitous for him.

His brother-volunteers, returning to St. James's Street, spoke highly of his conduct. These volunteers and their deeds became the talk of everyone. Had he been a general commanding, slain in the heat of victory, Sir John Armytage could hardly have garnered more sympathy than the nation showed him. The newspapers overflowed with letters in his honor, while men of wit and sensibility competed to compose epitaphs. The fate of his fiancée was widely mourned. As previously stated, she was the sister of the brave Commodore who had just returned from this unfortunate campaign, succeeding to the title of his elder brother—an officer as gallant as himself—who had recently fallen in America.

My Lord Howe was heard to speak in special praise of Mr. Warrington, securing him a

Some of my dear readers undoubtedly enjoy visiting the renowned gardens of Regent's Park, where so many of our finned, feathered, and four-legged companions are housed in exchange for their display to our instruction and amusement. While gazing at the aquatic life within the aquarium, observing how a man's business and private thoughts inevitably mingle with the surrounding nature, I found myself once again lost in contemplation of our friends, the Virginians.

One of the most magnificent creatures I have ever witnessed, slicing through his green bath in harmonious curves—alternately flashing his black, glistening back and revealing his fair, white chest—is none other than our humble acquaintance, the flounder. We have often devoured these creatures from their watery depths at Greenwich, entirely unaware of their inherent beauty. Yet, as is the case with mankind, so it is with flounders. View them in their proper element, and they appear agile, robust, and comely; remove them from it, and their beauty vanishes instantly. Their movements become grotesque as they flap their tails uselessly against the bottom, gasping out their feeble lives. Were one to lift such a specimen gently and return it to the Thames, it might recover—but alas, there is a proverb regarding "fish out of water," and surely other naturalists have noted this before me.

Consider Harry Warrington. For a long time, he had been floundering, utterly removed from his proper element. But the moment he discovered his calling, health, strength, spirits, and energy surged back into him. With the simple tap of an epaulet upon his shoulder, he transformed into a new being. He embraced his new profession with delight, mastering every detail with eager quickness. Should I possess the skills of my friend Lorrequer, I would eagerly follow Harry into camp—to witness him marching, dining at the mess, and parading on the drill ground. I would share his carouses, sleep beneath his tent, and discuss the intricacies of warfare with him. Since I cannot do so, I leave it to the reader's imagination to flesh out this picture based on the faint outlines I provide. Simply conjure the image of Mr. Harry Warrington in his scarlet coat with yellow facings, radiating joy in bearing the King's colors and eager to fulfill the duties of his trade.

As each young man admired the virtues of the other, recognizing his brother's superior qualities, George was naturally proud of Harry's success and rejoiced in his restored fortunes. He penned an affectionate letter to his mother in Virginia, detailing the high praise Harry received—a modesty that prevented Harry from repeating it himself. George recounted how Harry had secured his first commission and explained that he intended to request permission from his mother to share the cost of purchasing a higher rank for his brother. Nothing brought George greater delight than the ability to assist Harry, especially since Harry's sudden return to active life had inadvertently depleted funds that George had rightfully considered his own. Having labored under the misconception that he was the sole heir, Harry had incurred expenses he would never have dared as a younger son. Thus, George viewed his contribution not merely as aid, but as a fitting repayment for his own liberation from financial burden.

Having concluded his account of Harry's affairs, George turned his attention to matters of his own heart, addressing a topic of deep personal concern to his honored mother. She knew that the finest friends George and Harry possessed in England were the esteemed Mr. and Mrs. Lambert—the latter being Madam Esmond's schoolmate from their youth. Where their biological relatives had proven worldly and indifferent, these true friends had remained consistently generous and kind. The General was held in high respect throughout the army, and Mrs. Lambert's maternal affection for Madam Esmond's children was unparalleled. In his letter, Mr. George confessed that he had fallen deeply in love with the elder Miss Lambert, believing his entire future happiness rested upon securing her hand. Though he had made no formal proposal, he assured his mother that his sentiments were clear, and that both the young lady and her parents appeared favorable toward him. Describing her as a model of perfection, George expressed his conviction that his destiny hung in the balance of winning or losing her favor.

Why, one might ask, does the editor withhold this correspondence? Is it not cruel to silence the secrets of a young man's love? To deny the reader the chance to overhear incoherent vows, wild raptures, and the raw follies of passion? Would we not prefer to act as eavesdroppers at twilight windows, counting sighs and handshakes, bottling hot tears, and listening to the throbbing hearts of delicate maidens? I protest, for love is sacred. Whenever we encounter it—whether shooting suddenly from two pairs of eyes, glancing sadly from one, descending from a mother to her child, or passing from a father watching his daughter whirl around the room with her captain—we must salute it. It is not only wrong to "kiss and tell," but to speak of kisses at all. Let those initiated into the mystery keep quiet. Beware the gossip who prints private conversations; as sure as they sail with you, they will cast you adrift.

While Harry's love for battle drove him to seek out powder and rush toward reluctant dragons, carrying wounded comrades from the fire, George pursued a far more peaceful and delightful diversion. He penned sonnets to his mistress's brow, paced the darkness beneath her window watching her chamber lamp, and invented endless pretexts to send notes requiring no immediate reply. He gathered verses from his favorite poets and flowers from Covent Garden for her adornment. He attended services at St. James's Church, singing along from the Prayer Book while ignoring the sermon, completely absorbed by other thoughts. He was excessively affectionate toward Miss Theo's relations: her siblings at school, her brother at college, her witty companion Miss Hetty, and even her mother, whom Martin Lambert claimed was already half in love with George himself. Indeed, if fathers grow sulky at the sight of a destined son-in-law, it is a universal truth that mothers become sentimental, loving their own loves anew.

Gumbo and Sady were constantly shuttling between Southampton Row and Dean Street. During the summer months, numerous outings were planned, with countless invitations extended to Ranelagh, Hampstead, Vauxhall, and Marylebone Gardens. George desired that Miss Theo copy a famous tragedy for the stage, marveling at her exquisite Italian handwriting. Sheets passed back and forth, accompanied by little notes of thanks, inquiry, and admiration. Consider the packet, marked in Warrington's neat script: *"T's Letters, 1758–9."* Shall we open it and expose these tender secrets to public scrutiny? Those virgin words were whispered for one ear alone. Years later, the husband undoubtedly read them with sweet pangs of memory, recalling the fond lines addressed to his lover. It would be a sacrilege to display them publicly; kindly readers need only accept our assurance that the young lady's letters were modest and pure, and the gentleman's most respectful and tender.

In essence, we have revealed very little, yet in these final months, Mr. George Warrington had decided he had found the woman of women. She may not be the most beautiful—why look further than Cousin Flora, Coelia, or Ardelia, who are undeniably more striking? Yet her sweet face pleased him more than any other. She may lack supreme intellect, but her voice was the dearest and most pleasant to hear. In her company, he felt clever, eloquent, and wise; he possessed fine thoughts and spoke with such grace that it was no wonder he delighted in her presence. Regarding the young lady, we may assume—with gratitude that heaven has not taught me to despise women—that she possesses every attribute necessary to endow her young hero: wit, wisdom, beauty, valor, and virtue.

When George's letter arrived home, it created quite a stir in the circle gathered around Madam Esmond's fireside. He was in love and wished to marry! It was natural, and would keep him safe from harm. If he intended to unite with a well-bred Christian woman, Madam saw no objection.

"I knew they would be setting their caps at him," observed Mountain. "They imagine his wealth equals his estate. He hasn't mentioned whether the young lady has money. I fear she does not."

"People would certainly set their caps at him here," retorted Madam Esmond, casting a grim glance at her dependent. "And try to catch Mr. Esmond Warrington for their own daughters, who are no richer than Miss Lambert may be."

"Suppose your ladyship means me!" exclaimed Mountain. "My Fanny is poor, as you say; and 'tis kind of you to remind me of her poverty!"

"I said people would set their caps at him. If the cap fits you, *tant pis!* as my papa used to say."

"You think, madam, I am scheming to keep George for my daughter?"

"I thank you, on my word! A good opinion you seem to have of us after the years we have lived together!"

"My dear Mountain, I know you much better than to suppose you could ever fancy your daughter would be a suitable match for a gentleman of Mr. Esmond's rank and station," Madam replied with dignity. "Fanny Parker was as good as Molly Benson at school, and Mr. Mountain's daughter is as good as Mr. Lambert's!"

Mrs. Mountain cried out, "Then you did think of marrying her to my son!"

"I shall write to Mr. Esmond Warrington," said the mistress of Castlewood, "and express how sorry I am that you

Colonel James Wolfe, Harry's new commander, returned from America shortly after our Virginian had joined his regiment. Having previously served as Lieutenant-Colonel of Kingsley's forces, Wolfe had been granted command of a second battalion in recognition of his distinguished gallantry at Cape Breton. Harry approached his new leader with genuine respect and cordiality, mindful that the entire nation now held its breath in anticipation of Wolfe becoming a great general. Recent events in France had seen several officers of high reputation fail miserably under pressure; the Duke of Marlborough proved unworthy of his illustrious lineage, and even Lord George Sackville's genius was questioned before his conduct at Minden sealed his fate. Eager for military glory, the public and the Ministry alike sought a commander capable of satisfying their fervent desires. While Wolfe and Lambert were frequently occupied with business in London, their friendship flourished anew, much to Lambert's delight at witnessing his younger friend's rising fortune.

Away from duty, Harry never grew weary of listening to Wolfe recount the military campaigns of the preceding year. Wolfe possessed a heroic simplicity reminiscent of Nelson, speaking frankly of his actions without reserve. Though some refined gentlemen at St. James's might have scoffed at such candor, within our intimate circle, he found devoted admirers. The young General retained the romantic spirit of a boy, delighting deeply in music and poetry. On the eve of his death, he confessed he would have preferred composing Gray's *Elegy* to winning a battle. Given these literary inclinations, it is certain that George and Wolfe became close companions. Both were in love, both accepted lovers, and both desperate for happiness; surely they shared countless sentimental conversations that would have been fascinating to record had we access to accurate accounts. In a later letter, Warrington wrote:

> "I had the honor of knowing the famous General Wolfe and spending much time with him during his final stay in London. Our conversations were invariably engaging, and I admired his simplicity, frankness, and a glorious bravery that defined him. He was deeply in love and craved heaps of laurels to present to his mistress. 'If it be a sin to covet honour,' he used to say with Harry the Fifth—who was passionately fond of plays and poetry—'I am the most offending soul alive.' Surely, on his last day, he feasted sufficiently to satisfy the greediest appetite for glory. He hungered for it. To me, he seemed less like a soldier merely fulfilling his duty than a knight seeking dragons and giants. My own country has recently produced a chief of a vastly different order, possessing a contrary genius. I scarce know which to admire most: the Briton's chivalrous ardour or the Roman constancy of our great Virginian."

While Lambert's official duties kept him in London, his family remained content there. Mr. Warrington, seemingly satisfied with the rural tranquility of Southampton Row and the beauty of Bedford Gardens, showed little inclination to leave London permanently. He did visit Castlewood, staying briefly in the chamber his grandfather had praised and which Colonel Esmond had occupied as a boy, receiving a warm welcome from the family members present. Yet, undoubtedly, he preferred the company of a young woman in London who offered him pleasures far surpassing anything available in Lord Castlewood's circle. Although all the ladies were civil—and Lady Maria particularly gracious, enchanted by the tragedy George and Parson Sampson recited—the Chaplain's enthusiasm ultimately secured Warrington's play for the stage. While Mr. Johnson had urged Garrick at Drury Lane to produce it, Garrick had already committed to a tragedy by the author of *Douglas*. Consequently, *Carpezan* was taken up by Mr. Rich at Covent Garden.

On the night of the premiere, Mr. Warrington hosted an elegant gathering at the Bedford Head in Covent Garden, from which his friends proceeded en masse to the theatre. Only one or two remained behind with the young author at the coffee-house, where messengers periodically arrived to relay updates on the performance. The role of Carpezan was played by Barry; Shuter portrayed the old nobleman; Reddish excelled as Ulric; and the King of Bohemia was enacted by a young Dublin actor named Mr. Geoghegan, known on stage as Hagan, who performed with admirable grace and presence. Mrs. Woffington appeared slightly aged in the first act as the heroine, yet her murder in the fourth act terrified and delighted the audience. Miss Wayn sang the ballad intended for the king's page at the moment of the unfortunate wife's execution, earning universal acclaim. All agreed that Barry was terrifying and pathos-filled as Carpezan, particularly during the execution scene, while the youth and elegance of Hagan won general applause. Mr. Rich staged the piece with great elegance, though some doubted the propriety of introducing a favorite elephant—a staple of pantomime—to represent the Janissaries' march, causing one of Mr. Warrington's black servants to enter in Turkish attire while another sat in the footman's gallery, weeping and applauding uproariously at appropriate moments.

The execution of Sybilla marked the turning point of the drama. Once her head fell, George's friends exhaled in relief, and messenger after messenger rushed to the coffee-house to declare the tragedy a complete triumph. Amidst thunderous applause, Mr. Barry announced the play for repetition, revealing it as the debut dramatic effort of a young gentleman from Virginia.

How we wished to occupy the box where our friends sat, watching Theo's fluttering anxiety as the play's success hung in the balance, and observing the blushes and sparkling eyes that lit up when victory was assured. During the turmoil of the fourth act, Harry turned deadly pale—paler, Mrs. Lambert noted, than Barry with all his chalk. Yet, if Briareus could have clapped his hands, he could not have created more noise than Harry did at the conclusion. Mr. Wolfe and General Lambert cheered enthusiastically; Mrs. Lambert wept. Though Hetty remarked, "Why do you cry, mamma? You don't want any of them alive again; you know it serves them all right," the girl was genuinely as delighted as anyone present, including little Charley from the Chartreux, who had special permission from Dr. Crusius to attend, and Miss Lucy, brought specifically from boarding school for the occasion. Lord Castlewood and his sister, Lady Maria, were also there; the latter rose to compliment the actors on stage. Parson Sampson proved invaluable in the pit, leading the applause, presumably guided by Gumbo in the gallery to ensure he followed instructions.

A truly jovial supper ensued among Mr. Warrington's friends that night—far more spirited than Mr. Garrick's, whose *Agis* and dreary choruses met with poor success, forcing him to regret rejecting our young author's work in favor of Mr. Home's tragedy. Many celebrations occurred: Mr. Gumbo entertained several "shoulder-knot" gentlemen who supported his master's masterpiece; Mr. Henry Warrington hosted ten officers of his new regiment at the Star and Garter in Pall Mall, who had traveled expressly to back *Carpezan*; and finally, Mr. Warrington treated the three principal actors, our family party from the side box, Mr. Johnson, his clever friend Mr. Reynolds the painter, Lord Castlewood, Lady Maria, and others. Lady Maria sat beside the young actor who played the King. Mr. Warrington somehow managed to seat Miss Theo nearby, ensuring a pleasant evening for both. Great animation and cordiality prevailed. When toasts were proposed, Lady Maria cheerfully offered "The King of Hungary." That gentleman, full of eloquence, fire, and excellent manners both on and off stage, declared he had already died once that evening, hoping to die a hundred times more on the same field, yet vowed eternally to serve his humble mistress. Oh, if he possessed a real crown instead of his pasteboard diadem, what joy he would show laying it at her ladyship's feet! Neither Lord nor Mr. Esmond were overly pleased with the gentleman's excessive gallantry, which they rightly attributed to the liberal consumption of wine and punch. Theo and her sister, inexperienced in the world, were initially startled by Mr. Hagan's intense manner, but Lady Maria, more seasoned, took it all in good part. Late coaches were summoned to escort the ladies, after which some gentlemen returned to the supper room. The result was that *Carpezan* had to be carried out in a chair, the King of Hungary suffered a severe headache, and the Poet, despite delivering numerous speeches, was astonished when half a dozen guests appeared at his house the following morning, having been invited the night prior to dine again.

As he escorted Mrs. Lambert and her daughters into their carriage the previous night, all the ladies were flustered, delighted, and excited. Naturally, our gentleman accompanied them the next day to discuss the play, the audience, the actors, and the beauties of the piece repeatedly. Mrs. Lambert had heard warnings that theatrical women were dangerous company for young men and feared George might frequent the greenroom too much.

George smiled, assuring her he possessed a preventive against all greenroom temptations, fearing none. As he spoke, he looked into Theo's face as if those eyes held the amulet preserving him from peril.

"Why should he be afraid, mamma?" the maiden asked simply, unaware of danger or guile.

"No, my darling, I don't think he needs fear," the mother replied, kissing her.

"Do you suppose Mr. George would fall in love with that painted old creature who played the lead?" Miss Hetty queried, tossing

Human hearts recoil from the thought of parting; thus, I shall omit any description of Harry Warrington's sorrow as he prepares to leave his brother and friends. Are there not thousands of men in precisely the same predicament? Did not Mr. Wolfe have a mother to kiss—his brave father having passed away during his son's absence on the glorious campaign at Louisbourg—and a sweetheart to hold in a final embrace? Did not the sturdy Admiral Holmes, before sailing westward with his formidable squadron—the *Somerset*, the *Terrible*, the *Northumberland*, the *Royal William*, the *Trident*, the *Diana*, and the *Seahorse*—take leave of Mrs. Holmes and her daughters, with his own flag flying proudly from the *Dublin*? Was even Admiral Saunders, who departed the following day, immune to human feeling?

Away went William and his crew of jovial sailors, cutting through the tumbling waves, while poor Black-eyed Susan remained on shore, watching the vessel shrink until it vanished into the sunset. It dwindled toward the West. Night fell darkly over the ocean. They were gone, yet their hearts lingered at home for a while longer. In silence, with spirits inexpressibly soft and tender, how each man must have thought of those left behind! What a chorus of pitiful prayers rose to the Father, both at sea and on shore, on that night of departure: by the vacant bedside where a wife knelt in tears; around the hearth where mothers and children poured out their supplications; or on the deck, where the sailor gazed upward at the stars as his ship cleaved through the roaring midnight waters.

Tomorrow, the sun would rise upon ordinary life again, and we would resume our daily tasks of toil and duty. George accompanied his brother, staying with him at Portsmouth while they waited for a favorable wind. He shook Mr. Wolfe's hand, looked at his pale face one last time, and watched the ships depart amidst the clamor of bells and the thunder of cannon from the shore. The next day, George returned home to the business that is surely one of the world's most selfish and absorbing pursuits—a trade to which nearly every man of thirty has apprenticed himself by this time.

He felt a pang of sadness as he glanced into the lodgings at the little room Harry had occupied, seeing his half-burned papers still lying in the grate. Soon, however, he was on his way to Dean Street again, whispering words of comfort to his clinging sweetheart by the fitful firelight. She was exceedingly happy—oh, so happy! at his return, yet ashamed of her joy. Was it not heartless to be so glad when poor Hetty was so melancholy? Poor little Hetty! Indeed, it seemed selfish to rejoice while she suffered. It made one quite wretched to behold her distress. "Don't, sir!" Theo exclaimed. "Well, I ought to be wretched, and it is very, very wicked of me if I am not." One could understand her soft-hearted repentance. What she meant by "Don't" remains unclear; I had noted earlier that the room was dark and the fire burned fitfully—"Don't" was likely whispered during one of those shadowy moments.

Servants entered with supper and lights. The family arrived, and conversation became general. The destination of the fleet was now known throughout the realm. The force aboard was deemed sufficient to defeat the French in Canada, and under an officer of Wolfe's caliber, to correct the blunders and disasters of previous campaigns. He looked dreadfully ill, indeed, but possessed a great soul within a feeble body. Ministers and the nation pinned their highest hopes upon him.

After supper, as was customary, Mr. Lambert assembled his modest household, of whom George Warrington formed a significant part. As he prayed for all travelers by land and water, Theo and her sister knelt together. Thus, as the ship sped farther and farther into the West, these fond thoughts pursued it; the night passed, and the sun rose. Within a day or two, everyone returned to their books or usual work. As for George Warrington, that celebrated dramatist, he was already busy composing another piece.

When the tragedy of *Carpezan* had run some thirty or forty nights, other persons of genius took possession of the theater. There may have been those who marveled how the town could grow so fickle as to tire of such a masterpiece, unable to bear seeing actors in different costumes reciting others' verses. But George, of a skeptical turn of mind, accepted the fate of his tragedy with philosophical calm, pocketing the proceeds with quiet satisfaction. From Mr. Dodsley, the bookseller, he received the usual sum of one hundred pounds; from the theater manager, two hundred or more; and with such praise from critics and friends, he set to work preparing a new piece, hoping to achieve even greater success.

Over these studies and other charming occupations, months slipped away. What happy business! The happiest time of youth and life, when love is first spoken and returned; when dearest eyes shine daily with welcome, and fondest lips never tire of whispering sweet secrets; when the parting glance accompanying "Good night!" offers delightful promise of tomorrow; when the heart overflows with love and happiness, leaving something to spare for all the world; when days close with glad prayers and open with joyful hopes; when doubt seems cowardice, misfortune impossible, and poverty merely a sweet trial of constancy!

Theo's elders, thankfully recalling their own prime, sat softly by to witness this pretty comedy performed by their young people. In one of his later letters, dutifully written to his wife during a temporary absence, George recorded how he had looked up at the windows of the dear old house in Dean Street, wondering who sat in the chamber where he and Theo had been so happy.

Meanwhile, we can learn how time passed and what occupied our friends through excerpts from George's letters to his brother:

*'Why have you returned to rugged rocks, bleak shores, burning summers, and nipping winters, when you might have been cropping countless laurels in Germany? Kingsley's trees are returning covered with them, as Jack-a-Lent on May Day. Our six regiments did wonders; our cavalry would have done likewise had my Lord George Sackville allowed them. But when Prince Ferdinand shouted "Charge!", my lord either could not hear or could not translate the German word for "Forward"; consequently, we only beat the French, failing to utterly annihilate them as we might have, had Lord Granby or Mr. Warrington commanded. My lord has returned to town and is demanding a Court-Martial. He held his head high enough in prosperity; in misfortune, he displays such constancy of arrogance that one almost admires him. He appears to envy poor Mr. Byng, regarding the fact that he was not shot as a mere lack of respect toward him.*

*'The Duke has received notice to prepare for departure from this world of grandeur, victory, downfall, and disappointment. An attack of palsy has visited His Royal Highness, and pallid Death has just peered in at his door, saying, "I will call again." Tyrant as he was, this prince has been noble in disgrace, and no king has ever found a truer servant than ours has found in his son. Why do I favor the losing side and seem disposed to revolt against the winners? Your famous Mr. P----, your chief's patron and discoverer, I have heard speak in the House of Commons two or three times. I revolt against his magniloquence. I wish some little David would topple that swelling giant. His thoughts and language are perpetually affected. I prefer Barry's manner, though the other is the more awe-inspiring actor.*

*'Pocahontas is progressing well. Barry enjoys his part as Captain Smith; although he insists on wearing a red coat with blue facings and an epaulet, I fancy dressing him exactly like one of the portraits of Queen Elizabeth's gentlemen at Hampton Court: with a ruff, a square beard, and square shoes. "And Pocahontas—would you like her to be tattooed?" asked Uncle Lambert. Hagan's role as the warrior who loves her, and who, seeing her partiality for the captain, nobly rescues him from death, I trust will prove a hit. A strange fish is this Hagan: his mouth full of stage plays and rant, yet good, honest, and brave, if I am not mistaken. He is angry at having been cast recently as Sir O'Brallaghan in Mr. Macklin's new farce, *Love à la mode*. He claims he does not care to disgrace his tongue with imitations of that rascal's brogue. As if there were any need for imitation, when he possesses such an admirable twang of his own!*

*'Shall I tell you? Shall I hide the circumstance? Shall I hurt your feelings? Shall I set you in a rage of jealousy, causing you to request leave to return to Europe? Know, then, that though Cousin Carpezan is long since dead, Cousin Maria is forever appearing at the playhouse. Tom Spencer has spotted her night after night in the gallery, and she attends specifically on nights when Hagan performs. Quick, Burroughs, bring Mr. Warrington's boots and portmanteau! Order a chaise and four for Portsmouth immediately! The letter I burned one morning at breakfast (now that puss has such a prodigious way of running, I may let the cat out of the bag

Despite the past friction between Lord Castlewood and Harry, the two men remained cordial acquaintances. Their friendship was marked by a generous spirit; if George Warrington required financial aid, an introduction to the Court, or a seat in the House of Lords to witness a debate, his cousin was invariably ready to oblige. Lord Castlewood was a witty and agreeable companion, eager to advance his relative's interests so long as his own were not compromised. Indeed, he promised to use his influence to secure a position for Mr. Warrington, a gesture warmly embraced by the Virginian.

George's reluctance to return to America stemmed from more than mere sentimentality. While the idle existence of a planter might have suited him had it included independence, in Virginia he felt stifled—both the first and, in his view, the worst-treated subject of his mother's dominion. He dreaded the prospect of bringing his young bride back to a life defined by tedious subordination, domestic bickering, and the inevitable jealousies that threatened to sour their union. In contrast, London offered congenial society, the allure of potential distinction, and a freedom that poverty could not diminish. Thus, he clung to Lord Castlewood's offer of patronage, choosing to believe in the sincerity of a kinsman whose own circumstances were far from enviable.

Lord Castlewood proved remarkably gracious upon learning of George's engagement to Miss Lambert. He visited her parents, lavished praise upon the young couple, and conducted himself with such charm that the Lamberts, previously wary of his reputation, began to view him through a lens of charity. They convinced themselves that the world must be wicked indeed to malign such a man. Within their household, Lord Castlewood seemed transformed; he shed his worldliness at the door, becoming witty and lively until he departed. Perhaps he was genuinely better among them, or perhaps he simply found a temporary harbor from the storms of his usual life.

Mrs. Lambert, however, saw something deeper in his affection for her daughter. When she gently suggested that it was "not too late" for him to seek her hand, Lord Castlewood recoiled, interpreting her words as a plea for romance rather than repentance. With a melancholy grace, he confessed his inability to change his nature. "A man who learns languages late can never master them," he mused, comparing his situation to the acquisition of virtue. "Goodness cannot be learned at my age; I can only admire it in others." He painted himself as a man condemned by fate, a creature of selfish nature and harsh education, destined to remain on the wrong side of Lazarus. Yet, despite his self-deprecation, he held Mrs. Lambert's hand with pitying tenderness. Though he believed his redemption impossible, the lambert family, moved by his apparent sorrow and the kindness of his demeanor, continued to pray for him, unaware that his "good feelings" were fleeting and his capacity for genuine change limited.

With Harry away on service and George firmly engaged to Theo, Mrs. Lambert's matchmaking energies shifted toward her son, Jack. She envisioned a wealthy American bride for him, a plan that clashed with her husband's desire to keep his son in the army. Nevertheless, the social circle expanded. The Ladies of Castlewood became regular guests at the Lamberts', attending card parties and assemblies where high society mingled freely. Mrs. Lambert, finding acceptance and admiration for her daughters, welcomed these connections wholeheartedly.

Meanwhile, another suitor entered the scene: Mr. Van den Bosch, a wealthy merchant of Dutch extraction. Initially, he had been a figure of the lower commercial class, taking his daughter Lydia to modest outings in Islington and attending Baptist meetings. However, upon Lydia's return from school, her grandfather relocated them to an elegant estate in Bloomsbury. This move alienated their former neighbors—the merchants and tradesmen of Virginia Walk—and drew the attention of the elite. Soon, the old merchant abandoned his humble chapel for a pew in the prestigious Queen Square Church, living adjacent to Mr. George Warrington's residence.

This shift alarmed Mr. Draper, who warned George of the Van den Boschs' dubious origins. He revealed that the patriarch had amassed his fortune through risky land speculation, privateering, and the Guinea trade, and that his lineage was marred by scandal. Undeterred, the old gentleman confronted George directly. He dismissed Draper's accusations as slander, boasting of his immense wealth and expressing bewilderment that George preferred Miss Lambert to his own granddaughter, Lydia. He argued that while Lydia possessed beauty and fortune, George's choice of Theo was inexplicable.

Yet, beneath the transactional dialogue lay a touching dynamic between the grandfather and granddaughter. The old man, reflecting on his own youthful regrets and harsh treatment of his son, indulged Lydia completely. He confessed that he had moved to the fashionable quarter solely to please her, admitting that he cared little for fashion or theater unless it brought joy to his "little minx." Their interactions were filled with affectionate teasing and physical endearments, a stark contrast to the calculated maneuvering of the adults around them.

As the conversation wound down, a subtle tension arose. George recounted the encounter to Theo, omitting certain details of the grandfather's boasts regarding Lydia's inheritance. This omission suggests that while George maintained his engagement to Theo, the allure of the wealthy American heiress lingered in the background, leaving the outcome of these romantic intrigues uncertain. For now, however, the innocence of the young lovers stood firm against the encroaching complexities of the adult world.

Before we delve further into the adventures of Miss Lydia, we must first unravel a mystery hidden within Mr. Warrington's letter to his brother regarding Lady Maria Esmond. To the uninitiated, the notion seems baffling: how could decent people imagine that a woman of noble birth, ancient lineage, and mature judgment—who was ardently in love merely a dozen months prior—could suddenly forget herself? How could innocent youths conceive that she would fall for a man of low station, decades younger than herself, and wed him against the world's wishes? Technically, she did not marry "in the face" of the world, but rather behind its back, secured by the private rites of Parson Sampson in Mayfair.

But pause before condemning her entirely. Did her past affection for her young cousin preclude future love? Do men alone possess the right to change partners while women are scolded for seeking solace? Such judgments are crude and unphilosophical. Recall Hamlet's harsh critique of his mother's remarriage; surely, a widow needs someone to cling to once her first husband passes. History shows women capable of twisting around two men simultaneously. Why forbid marriage tables after funeral feasts? Just as one eats today, hunger returns tomorrow. Nature serves *à la russe*; we consume dishes sequentially. Like the cannibals of Polyphemus's cave, society devours us one by one—young, old, plump, lean, beautiful, or plain—with no escape. Witness Lady Ogresham! Last year she seized, devoured, and discarded poor Tom Kydd; now she holds Ned Suckling captive beneath her gaze, trembling and fascinated. Soon, faint squeaks of pity will rise, followed by silence.

Tragically, I suspected Maria Esmond had lost her heart many times before Harry Warrington claimed it. I fancied she would preserve her love through celibacy, bewailing her misfortunes. Yet, if I fell in love tomorrow, I would assume ownership of my beloved's heart—not as a tenant in a dilapidated flat shared by countless lovers, but as a rightful owner. We declare eternal unity, dismissing rivals like Sir Cresswell Cresswell. But Lady Maria of the last century possessed an ill-regulated mind. As worldly observers know, when an older flirt exhausts one passion, she seeks another. Flirting resembles drinking: once the brandy is gone, one turns to gin. If Maria found a successor for Harry Warrington, establishing a new sovereign in her heart, what could anyone expect? Her emotional territory resembled the Low Countries: accustomed to conquest and perpetually open to invasion.

Her current captor was none other than Mr. Geoghegan (or Hagan), the young actor famed in George's tragedy. With his thrilling voice, bright eyes, noble demeanor, and striking appearance in gilt armor and a large periwig, he enchanted her completely. Even Ariadne surrendered to Bacchus after Theseus departed. The Irishman was deeply moved by her favor. Though he might have preferred a younger lady, few could match her tenderness. She clung to him fiercely, retiring to his modest Westminster lodgings when their marriage became public knowledge, despite her furious relatives cutting ties.

News reached General Lambert at Whitehall, sparking merriment among his family. In those simpler times, jokes could be broad; a fine lady might laugh at Fielding's pages while weeping over Clarissa's letters. The General regaled his kin with waggery about the union, George's role in facilitating it, and Barry's anticipated jealousy. He joked that cousin Hagan hadn't chosen George as best man, suggesting names like "Carpezan" or "Sybilla" for the first child, born amidst theatrical drama. Perhaps they couldn't afford a coach, but a chariot with pasteboard dragons from Mr. Rich's theater would suffice. The baby might be christened in Macbeth's cauldron, with Harry and Harlequin as godparents.

"Why shouldn't she marry him if she loves him?" asked little Hetty innocently. "Why shouldn't he love her for being slightly older? My mother is older, yet you love her dearly. When you married my mother, sir, you mentioned poverty, yet you were happy, and no one mocked you!" Unaware of Lady Maria's past indiscretions, the child spoke purely from youthful naivety.

Had her family truly abandoned her? George recounted their fury: Lady Castlewood's mourning attire, Mr. Will's threat to tear the actor's ears, and Madame de Bernstein's explosive rage. "It insults the family," declared haughty Miss Hett. "Ladies of rank must be indignant at marrying such a man, though desertion is another matter."

"My dear child," her mother replied, "after Lady Maria's behavior, no respectable person visits her."

"What behavior, mama?"

"Never mind. Little girls shouldn't pry into such matters. Suffice it that I am shocked she ever returned here. No honest soul associates with her!"

"Aunt Lambert, then I must be whipped and sent to bed," George said mockingly. "Though I admit, earlier than I confessed, that I visited my cousin the player and his wife. They lived in filthy Westminster quarters, where the wretch supported not only his wife but also his elderly mother and a younger brother attending school. Despite Mr. Hagan's improvidence, I felt a genuine respect for him. Some marriages are simply reckless, aren't they, Theo?"

"If they marry spendthrifts like you," Theo retorted playfully.

"No, not the purse, sir," George smiled tenderly. "That was Theo's doing. Mine came empty. Mr. Rich is furious, fearing riots similar to those surrounding foreign dancers. The macaronis threaten violence, and my cousin Will vows to seize the actor's ears. Without his salary, this couple faces starvation."

"And you gave them aid, sir? Truly generous, George!" Theo admired him.

"It was your purse, dear Theo," he corrected.

"Mama, pray go visit them tomorrow!" Theo begged.

"If she refuses, I demand a divorce!" Papa exclaimed jokingly, adding, "Kiss me, you little wench—with Monsieur le Beau-Fils's permission."

"Monsieur mon beau fiddlestick, papa!" Miss Lambert laughed, complying with the order. This marked the first time George Esmond Warrington was dubbed a "fiddlestick."
