Anyone making an imprudent marriage endures familial scorn, pity, and wrath. Whether marrying a curate's daughter or a struggling barrister, the reaction is severe. Imagine the outrage faced by Lady Maria Hagan and her husband. First, consider old Mrs. Hagan, dutifully cared for by her son since his father's death. His earnings sustained his siblings and household comforts. Now, a frivolous lady intruded, sharing their meager provisions. Unless Hagan was spirited and the mother fearful, life would be uncomfortable. Taking a seat at such a small table was selfish, yet love often ignores consequences. Passionate individuals invite guests knowing full well the room cannot accommodate them, trusting that fewer will arrive. Intoxicated by romance, they neglect budgeting for inevitable butcher and fishmonger bills.

Lady Maria's presence strained resources, prompting complaints from the old mother-in-law. Yet, her outcry paled beside the scandal erupting in Kensington. Whispers filled the Princess's court, officers exchanged jests, printers published waggery, and prudes expressed outrage. Madame Bernstein suffered fits, fleeing to Tunbridge Wells. Lord Maria refused to see his sister, while the Countess mourned publicly. Days passed in excitement over the scandal until fresh news—a German victory, American uncertainties, a returning officer awaiting trial, or an Italian soprano—diverted public attention. Lady Maria faded into obscurity, eating hard-earned meals in her garret alongside the actor's family.

Mr. George Warrington continued his letter to Theo:

"My dearest little Theo, you wished to join Mama on this charitable mission, but I deemed Aunt Lambert's solitary visit preferable. Upon arrival, Cousin Hagan was absent. We found her ladyship in casual dress, hair untidy, playing cribbage with a neighbor from the second floor, while good Mrs. Hagan sat by the fire with punch and *The Whole Duty of Man*.

'Maria, your Maria once,' she whispered tearfully upon seeing us. Aunt Lambert offered immediate sympathy. While she comforted Lady Maria, I complimented the elder woman. As conversation lagged, we worked together to lighten the gloom. Discussing you, Wolfe, and war, we hoped you faced the French soon. We imagined you at Cape Breton, studying maps of Quebec and the St. Lawrence. Recalling your joyful expression upon my safe return from a minor skirmish, we prayed for Harry's similar success. Sharing our optimism, we discussed the campaign's conclusion and Captain Warrington's promotion. May our hopes fulfill swiftly.

How strange that the mettlesome one escapes conflict while I, a peaceful youth, find battles thrust upon me! What do you think of my latest entanglement? With no lesser figure than your old enemy, our kinsman, Mr. Will.

Who incited him remains unclear. Spencer, acting as seconds, suspects manipulation. His conduct has been unbearable. Frequently meeting at Mr. Van den Bosch's house—where his granddaughter, consigned to both of us by our mother, resides—we've clashed repeatedly. Perhaps showing spirit or doubting mine, Will displayed outright rudeness, contradicting me thrice before the family. Out of sheer spite, I maintained composure. Does he pursue Miss Lydia,

The petty altercation between George and his cousin proved to be the catalyst for a significant shift in social dynamics. George largely ceased his visits to Bloomsbury, finding Mr. Will's attentions increasingly assiduous and his behavior so outrageously conciliatory that George struggled merely to restrain his hands from striking his cousin. Now that both parties were legally bound to maintain the peace, Mr. Will's eagerness to avoid further conflict was palpable.

Miss Lydia, however, possessed a peculiar manner of receiving her friends. Six weeks prior, she had been madly jealous of George's apparent preference for another woman; now, she seized every opportunity to compliment Theo. "Miss Theo is such a quiet, gentle creature," Lyddy remarked with evident satisfaction. "I am certain George is simply the perfect husband for her. How fortunate that horrid quarrel was prevented!" She praised the timely intervention of the constables, noting the absurdity of hearing Mr. Esmond curse and swear about his thwarted duel. "But the arrival of the constables saved your valuable life, dear Mr. George," she said with a soft smile. "I am sure Miss Theo ought to bless them forever."

When George hesitated to attend dinner with Mr. Esmond, Lyddy offered kind, albeit tactless, reassurances. "You won't stop and meet Mr. Esmond today? You don't like his company? He can't do you any harm, and I am sure you will do him none." Such speeches, delivered by a young girl to a gentleman with such casual inadvertence in the presence of others, were unlikely to make Mr. Warrington eager for the society of the American lady.

News of George's intended duel with Mr. Esmond did not reach Dean Street immediately, as George wished to spare his hosts the disturbance. However, once the ladies became aware of the plot, a tremendous commotion ensued.

"You were actually going to take a fellow-creature's life, and you came to see us without a word!" Theo exclaimed, horrified. "Oh, George, it was shocking!"

"My dear," George pleaded, "he had insulted me and my brother. Could I allow him to call us both cowards while I sat by and said, 'Thank you'?"

The General sat nearby, looking grave. "You know you consider it a wicked, un-Christian practice, Papa," Theo reminded him, echoing her father's frequent admonitions that gentlemen should possess the courage to refuse duels.

"To refuse? Yes," Mr. Lambert agreed, still appearing glum. "It requires a prodigious strength of mind," added Jack Lambert, looking equally somber. "And if any man were to call me a coward, I suppose I should be apt to forget my orders."

"You see, brother Jack is with me!" cried George. "I must not oppose you, Mr. Warrington," Jack declared.

"Mr. Warrington!" George shouted, flushing crimson. "Would you, a clergyman, urge George to break the Commandments and commit murder, John?" Theo asked, aghast.

"I am the son of a soldier, sister," the young divine replied dryly. "Besides, Mr. Warrington has committed no murder yet. We shall soon be hearing from Canada; the great question of the supremacy of the two races must be settled there ere long!" Turning his back on George, he spoke with finality, leaving the younger man to stare at him in wonder.

Hetty, looking pale at Jack's startling remark, was excused from the room under a pretext arranged by her sister. George rose and followed the retreating girls to the door. Returning moments later, he exclaimed, "Great powers, gentlemen! On my honor, you seem to be granting me the credit of shirking this affair with Mr. Esmond!"

The clergyman and his father exchanged glances. "A man's nearest and dearest are always the first to insult him," George flashed out.

"You mean to say, 'Not guilty'?" the General cried, blessing his son. "I told thee so, Jack." He rubbed his eyes, blushed, and shook George's hand vigorously.

"Not guilty of what, in heaven's name?" Mr. Warrington demanded.

"Nay," the General said, dismissing the topic. "Mr. Jack here brought the story. Let him tell it. I believe 'tis a damned lie, with all my heart." Uttering this profanity, the General strode out of the room.

The Reverend J. Lambert looked remarkably foolish. "And what is this—this damned lie, sir—that someone has been telling about me?" George grinned at the young clergyman.

"To question the courage of any man is always an offense to him," Mr. Lambert stated solemnly. "I rejoice that yours has been belied."

"Who told the falsehood, sir, which you repeated?" Mr. Warrington bellowed. "I insist on the man's name!"

"You forget you are bound over to keep the peace," Jack warned.

"Curse the peace, sir! We can go and fight in Holland. Tell me the man's name, I say!"

"Fair and softly, Mr. Warrington!" the young parson interjected calmly. "My hearing is perfectly good. It was not a man who told me the story which I confessed to imparting to my father."

"What?" George asked, the truth suddenly dawning on him. "Was it that artful, wicked little vixen in Bloomsbury Square?"

"Vixen is not the word to apply to any young lady, George Warrington!" Lambert exclaimed indignantly. "Much less to the charming Miss Lydia. She is artful—the most innocent of Heaven's creatures! She is wicked—that angel! With unfeigned delight that the quarrel should be over, and with devout gratitude that blood of kinship should not be shed, she spoke in terms of the highest praise for your decision to decline this quarrel, expressing deep sympathy for your choice of the painful but necessary method to avert it."

"What method?" George demanded, stamping his foot.

"Why, laying an information, to be sure!" Jack said. Upon hearing this, George erupted in language far too violent to repeat here and highly uncomplimentary toward Miss Lydia.

"Don't utter such words, sir!" the parson cried, now taking his turn to be angry. "Do not insult, in my hearing, the most charming, the most innocent of her sex! If she has been mistaken in her information regarding you, doubting your willingness to commit what, after all, is a crime—for homicide is a crime of the most awful description—you, sir, have no right to blacken that angel's character with foul words. Innocent yourself, you should respect the most innocent, especially as she is the most lovely of women! Oh, George, are you to be my brother?"

"I hope to have that honor," George answered, smiling. He began to grasp the other's drift. "What, then, what—if 'tis too much bliss to be hoped for by sinful man—what if she should one day be your sister? Who could behold her charms without being subjugated? I own that I am a slave. I admit that those Latin sapphics in the September issue of the *Gentleman's Magazine*, beginning *Lydicae quondam cecinit venustae* (with an English translation by my friend Hickson of Corpus), were mine. I have told my mother what has passed between us, and Mrs. Lambert also believes that the most lovely of her sex has deigned to look favorably upon me. I have composed a letter; she has another. She intends to visit Miss Lydia's grandfather today to deliver it, bringing me the answer that will make me either the happiest or the most wretched of men!"

"It was during the unrestrained interchange of family conversation that I chanced to impart to my father the sentiments my dear girl expressed. Perhaps I spoke slightly of your courage, which I do not doubt—by Heaven, I do not doubt it. It may be that she has erred regarding you as well. It may be that the fiend of jealousy has been gnawing at my bosom, and—horrible suspicion!—that I thought my sister's lover found too much favor with her. I would have all my own. Ah, dear George, who knows his faults? I am as one distracted with passion. Confound it, sir! What right have you to laugh at me? I would have you to know that *risu inepto*..."

"What, have you two boys made it up?" the General cried, entering amidst a roar of laughter from George.

"I was giving my opinion to Mr. Warrington upon laughter, and upon his laughter in particular," Jack Lambert said, still fuming.

"George is bound over to keep the peace, Jack! Thou cannot fight him for two years; and between now and then, let us trust you will have made up your quarrel. Here is dinner, boys! We will drink absent friends, an end to the war, and no fighting outside the profession!"

George pleaded an engagement as an excuse to leave early. Jack soon followed suit. When George returned to his lodgings after handling some brief business, he found the young parson already in conference with a servant at Mr. Van den Bosch's door in Bloomsbury Square.

"His master and mistress left town yesterday," the servant reported.

"Poor Jack! And you had the decisive letter in your pocket?" George asked his future brother-in-law.

"Well, yes," Jack admitted, clutching the document. "

Looking across the fire toward my accustomed chair—the beloved partner of my hearth during the latter half of my life—I often find myself asking, for middle-aged gentlemen possess the distinct privilege of recycling their jokes, inquiries, and anecdotes: "Were two young people ever more foolish and imprudent than we were when we wed, in the year of the late King's death?"

My son, who has recently taken prodigious leaps in the heat of fox-hunting, tells me that he surveys the gaps and rivers he once crossed with terrifying ease, now filled with a profound sense of astonishment at his own foolhardiness in making such desperate ventures. Yet, there is no more eager sportsman in the two counties than Miles. He loves his amusement so passionately that he cares for nothing else. He has broken his collarbone and suffered a hundred tumbles (to his mother's eternal terror); but so has his father—perhaps thinking of a copy of verse or his speech at the Quarter Sessions—who was thrown over his old mare's head when she slipped on a stone while they dreamed along a park road at four miles an hour. Miles's reckless sport has been the delight of his life, just as my marriage has been the blessing of mine; and I never think of it but to thank Heaven.

Mind, I do not set up my worship as an example. I certainly do not tell all young folks, "Go and marry upon twopence a year"; otherwise, people would look very black at me at our vestry meetings. But my wife is known to be a desperate matchmaker. When Hodge and Susan appear in my justice room talking allowances, we urge them to spend their half-crown a week at home, add a little contribution of our own, and send for the vicar.

Now, when I pose a question to my dear oracle, I know exactly what the answer will be; hence, no doubt, the reason I so frequently consult her. I have but to wear a particular expression of face, and my Diana instantly takes her reflection from it. Suppose I say, "My dear, don't you think the moon was made of cream cheese tonight?" She will reply, "Well, Papa, it did look very much like cream cheese, indeed—you're nobody like you for droll similes." Or, suppose I say, "My love, Mr. Pitt's speech was very fine, but I don't think he equals what I remember of his father." "Nobody was equal to my Lord Chatham," says my wife. And then one of the girls cries out, "Why, I've often heard our Papa say Lord Chatham was a charlatan!" Upon which Mamma remarks, "How like she is to her Aunt Hetty!"

As for Miles, *Tros Tyriusve* is all one to him. He only reads the sporting announcements in the Norwich paper. So long as there is good scent, he does not care about the state of the country. I believe the rascal has never read my poems, much less my tragedies (for I mentioned *Pocahontas* to him the other day, and the dunce thought she was merely a river in Virginia). With respect to my Latin verses, how can he possibly understand them when I know he cannot construe Corderius? Why, this notebook lies publicly on the little table at my corner of the fireside, and anyone may read it who takes the trouble to lift my spectacles off the cover; yet Miles never has. I insert caricatures of Miles, jokes against him, in the loose pages—but he never knows nor heeds them.

Only once, in place of a neat drawing of mine in Chinese ink representing Miles asleep after dinner—a work my friend Bunbury would not disown—I found a rude picture of myself going over my mare Sultana's head, entitled "The Squire on Horseback, or Fish Out of Water." The fellow roared with laughter, and all the girls tittered when I came upon the page! My wife said she was never in such a fright as when I went to my book. But I can bear a joke against myself, and have heard many, though (strange to say, for one who has lived among some of the chief wits of the age) I never heard a truly good one in my life.

Never mind, Miles, though thou art not a wit, I love thee none the worse (there never was any love lost between two wits in a family). Though thou hast no great beauty, thy mother thinks thee as handsome as Apollo—or his Royal Highness the Prince of Wales, who was born in the very same year as thee. Indeed, she always finds Coates's picture of the Prince remarkably like her eldest boy and keeps the print in her dressing-room to this very day. *[Note, in a female hand: "My son is not a spendthrift, nor a breaker of women's hearts, as some gentlemen are; but that he was exceedingly like H.R.H. when they were both babies is most certain, the Duchess of Anester having herself remarked him in St. James's Park, where Gumbo and my poor Molly used often to take him for an airing. Th. W."]*

In that same year, with what different prospects! My Lord Esmond, Lord Castlewood's son, likewise appeared to adorn the world. At that time, My Lord C. and his humble servant had already come to a coolness, and heaven knows! my honest Miles's godmother brought no gold pap-boats to his christening! Matters have mended since, *laus Deo*—*laus Deo*, indeed! For I suspect neither Miles nor his father would ever have been able to accomplish much for themselves, or by their own wits.

Castlewood House has quite a different face now from that venerable one which it wore in the days of my youth, when it was covered with the wrinkles of time, the scars of old wars, and the cracks and blemishes which years had marked on its hoary features. I love best to remember it in its old shape, as I saw it when young Mr. George Warrington went down at the owner's invitation to be present at his Lordship's marriage with Miss Lydia Van den Bosch—"an American lady of noble family of Holland," as the county paper announced her ladyship to be. Then the towers stood as Warrington's grandfather, the Colonel (whom Madam Esmond would like to call her father, the Marquis), had seen them. The woods, thinned not a little to be sure, still stood; nay, some of the very same rooks may have cawed over them which the Colonel had seen sixty years prior. His picture hung in the hall which might have been his, had he not preferred love and gratitude to wealth and worldly honor.

Mr. George Esmond Warrington—that is, *Ego Met Ipse* who writes this page—as he walked the old place, pacing the long corridors, the smooth dew-spangled terraces, and the cool, darkling avenues, felt for a while as if he were one of Mr. Walpole's cavaliers with ruff, rapier, buff-coat, and gorget. He felt as if an Old Pretender or a Jesuit emissary in disguise might appear from behind any tall tree-trunk around the mansion or any antique carved cupboard within it. I had the strangest, saddest, pleasantest, old-world fancies as I walked the place; I imagined tragedies, intrigues, serenades, escalades, Oliver Cromwell's Roundheads battering the towers, or bluff Hal's Beefeaters pricking over the plain before the castle.

I was then courting a certain young lady (Madam, your ladyship's eyes had no need of spectacles then, and on the brow above them there was never a wrinkle or a silver hair). I remember writing a ream of romantic description, under my Lord Castlewood's franks, to the lady who never tired of reading my letters then. She says I only send her three lines now when I am away in London or elsewhere. "'Tis that I may not fatigue your old eyes, my dear!"

Mr. Warrington thought himself authorized to order a genteel new suit of clothes for my Lord's marriage. With Monsieur Gumbo in attendance, he made his appearance at Castlewood a few days before the ceremony. I may mention that it had become expedient to send my faithful Sady home on board a Virginia ship. A great inflammation attacking the throat and lungs, proving fatal in many cases in that year of Wolfe's expedition, had seized and well-nigh killed my poor lad, for whom his native air was pronounced to be the best cure. We parted with an abundance of tears, and Gumbo shed as many when his master went to Quebec; but he had attractions in this country and none for the military life, so he remained attached to my service.

We found Castlewood House full of friends, relations, and visitors. Lady Fanny was there upon compulsion, a sulky bridesmaid. Some of the virgins of the neighborhood also attended the young Countess. A bishop's widow herself, the Baroness Beatrix, brought a holy brother-in-law of the bench from London to tie the holy knot of matrimony between Eugene, Earl of Castlewood, and Lydia Van den Bosch, spinster. For some time before and after the nuptials, the old house in Hampshire wore an appearance of gaiety to which it had long been unaccustomed. The country families came gladly to pay their compliments to the newly married couple. The lady's wealth was the subject of everybody's talk, and no doubt did not decrease in the telling. Those naughty

It is a familiar trope from our favorite fairy tales that at every christening and wedding, someone inevitably feels slighted and vows vengeance. Thus, we need not marvel that Cousin Will cursed and raged with such energy upon hearing of his brother's engagement to the colonial heiress. Initially, Will fled the house in a fury, swearing he would never return. Yet, neither his family nor the swearer himself took his oaths seriously. This unrepentant prodigal returned after a day or two, unable to resist the allure of the marriage feast. He could not bear to resign his knife and fork at the Castlewood table. He came back to drink and eat in token of revenge, pledging the young bride in a bumper while muttering prayers for her perdition beneath his breath. During the chapel ceremony, as her father gave her away and my lord vowed to love, honor, and cherish her, Will offered responses of smothered maledictions.

Will was hardly the only grumbler regarding the union. Mr. Warrington heard no end of abuse directed at my lady and her grandfather from various quarters. The old gentleman's city friends, his legal adviser, the Dissenting clergyman who had welcomed them to England, and even poor Jack Lambert—the orthodox young divine whose eloquence Mr. Warrington had hoped would sway her privately—were all bitter against the "little lady's" treachery. Each had a story of being enslaved, encouraged, and ultimately jilted by the American. The lawyer, who possessed an exhaustive inventory of her properties, estates, money, slaves, and ships, was ready to swear that her entire account was a fabrication; that places like New York and Virginia did not exist, or at least that Mr. Van den Bosch held no lands there. He claimed there was no such thing as the Guinea trade, suggesting the negroes were merely "black falsehoods" invented by a wily old planter. The Dissenting pastor mourned over his "stray lamb"—or rather, lamented that such a wily, mischievous monster could even be called a lamb. Poor Jack Lambert ruefully confessed to his mother that he possessed a lock of black hair, which he bedewed with tears and addressed in quite unclerical terms. As for Mr. William Esmond, he indulged in shrieks and curses even under his sister-in-law's very nose, calling upon Acheron to witness that no such artful devil existed outside the region of Miss Lydia. He swore she was an infernal female Cerberus and invoked all the wrath of heaven and hell upon his swindling brother, who had cajoled him with fair words and filched his prize.

"Why," Mr. Warrington asked when Will expatiated on these grievances, "if the girl is such a she-devil as you describe, you are all the better for losing her. If she intends to deceive her husband and poison him, as you claim, how fortunate for you that you are not the victim! You ought to thank the gods, Will, rather than curse them, for robbing you of such a fury. There can be no greater revenge on Castlewood than allowing him her sole possession."

"All this was very well," Will Esmond replied, yet he justifiably noted that his brother was no less a scoundrel for cheating him out of the fortune he had risked his life to win.

George Warrington was perplexed as to how his cousin had endangered his precious existence—a fate for which a rope's end might have been a fitting conclusion—and Will, with utmost candor, explained how the "little Cerbera" had orchestrated their meeting, only for it to be interrupted by Sir John Fielding's men. He recounted how Lydia constantly accused George of cowardice for sneering at Will, and vice versa, leading George to flee and nearly die of fright during Braddock's expedition. "Deuce take me," Will exclaimed, "I was never more surprised than when you stood so coolly in Tottenham Court Fields, offering to wager that you would never come!"

Mr. Warrington laughed, thanking Will for this assessment. "Though," he added, "it was lucky the constables arrived, or you would have whittled your sword through my body another minute. Did you not see how clumsy I stood before you? You actually turned white and shook with anger!"

"Yes, curse me," Will retorted, turning red, "that is my way of showing rage. I was confoundedly angry with you, cousin! But now it is my brother I hate, and that little devil of a Countess—a countess! A pretty countess, indeed!" With another rumbling volley of oaths, he saluted the reigning member of his family.

"Well, cousin," George said, staring him oddly, "you let me off easily. I dare say I owe my life to you, or at least a whole waistcoat. I admire your forbearance and spirit. What a pity such courage is wasted as a mere court usher! You are a loss to His Majesty's army, positively!"

"I never know if you jest or are serious, Mr. Warrington," Will growled.

"Few gentlemen would dare joke with you, cousin, if they valued their lives or ears!" cried Mr. Warrington, who loved this grave method of dealing with his noble kinsman, watching with droll interest as Will choked back his curses, ground his teeth in fear of biting, and smothered his cowardly anger.

"You should moderate your expressions regarding the dear Countess and my lord your brother," Warrington continued. "Of you, they speak most tenderly. Her ladyship has told me everything."

"What everything?" cried Will, aghast.

"As much as women ever do tell, cousin. She admitted she thought you had been a little *épris* with her. What woman can help liking a man who admires her?"

"She hates you, and says you were wild about her, Mr. Warrington!" protested Mr. Esmond.

"Spretae injuria formae, cousin!" Warrington quoted.

"For me? What's for me?" asked the other.

"I never cared for her, and hence, perhaps, she does not love me. Do you not recall the case of the wife of the Captain of the Guard?"

"Which guard?" asked Will.

"My Lord Potiphar," said Warrington.

"Lord Who? My Lord Falmouth is Captain of the Yeomen of the Guard, and my Lord Berkeley of the Pensioners. My Lord Hobart had them before. Perhaps you haven't been in England long enough to know who is who, cousin!" remarked William.

Warrington clarified that he meant the Captain of the Guard for the King of Egypt, whose wife had persecuted Joseph for not returning her affection. Will countered that since Egypt was a confounded long way off, and if Lord Whatever-it-was's wife lied about him, it was simply the nature of his sex, whom he supposed were identical everywhere.

In truth, when paying his marriage visit to Castlewood, Mr. Warrington had heard the little Countess's version of the dispute between Will Esmond and herself. While differing in some respects from Will's ingenuous narrative, it was far from more authentic. The lady expressed grief at being deceived by her brother-in-law. She feared his life at court and town had eroded the high principles inherent to the Esmonds. She suggested Will's words were unreliable, that a loose life and financial troubles had made him mercenary, blunting his honor and impairing the high chivalrous courage "which we Esmonds... must always possess." She tossed her head proudly, asserting that she, her lord, and her cousin Harry possessed this quality.

"Oh, cousin George! Must I confess that I was led to doubt yours?" she continued. "Without which, a man of ancient and noble family like ours isn't worthy to be called a man! I shall try, George, as a Christian lady and the head of one of the first families in this kingdom and the whole world, to forgive my brother William for speaking ill of a member of our family, though a younger branch and by the female side, and making me for a moment doubt you. He did so. Perhaps he told me countless bad things you had said of me."

"I, my dear lady!" cried Mr. Warrington.

"Which he said you said of me, cousin, and I hope you didn't, and heartily pray you didn't; and I can afford to despise them. And he paid me his court, that's a fact; and so have others, and that I'm used to; and he might have prospered better than he did perhaps—for I did not know my dear lord, nor come to value his great and eminent qualities, as I do out of the fulness of this grateful heart now!—but, oh! I found William deficient in courage. No man lacking that can ever have the esteem of Lydia, Countess of Castlewood! He said 'twas you who lacked spirit, cousin, and angered me by telling me you were always abusing me. But I forgive you, George, that I do! And when I tell you that it was he who was afraid—the mean skunk!—and actually summoned the constables to prevent the match between you and him, you won't wonder why I wouldn't value a fellow like that—not that much!" snapping her little fingers. "I say, *noblesse oblige*, and a man of our family without courage, I don't care not this pinch of snuff for him—there,

Our relations at Castlewood kept us in their household until the new year commenced. After an absence of a fortnight—a span that felt like an eternity to the absurd and infatuated young man—I returned to the side of my beloved. Madame de Bernstein was relieved to depart from her father's home. Once we were away, she began to speak more freely. Regarding the intense confrontation between her and her niece, she offered no details. However, the old lady ceased speaking of forming *cette petite*, and when she did mention her, it was with a nervous laugh, devoid of genuine hostility toward the young Countess.

She remarked that her nephew, Eugene, was destined to be henpecked for the remainder of his days, observing that a touch of domestic order would benefit him immensely. She deemed the little, somewhat vulgar American gentleman a shrewd individual who would serve excellently as a steward. Concerning the Countess's mother, she noted she was likely a convict transported from England, having presumably endured hard labor in various prisons; yet, she argued this news need not be broadcast to the town crier. Ultimately, she posited that for certain types of individuals, lineage mattered little. "The young woman will be honest for her own sake now; she is shrewd and will learn English quickly," she declared. "And the name to which she has a right is great enough to secure her entry into any society. Whether one is the daughter of a grocer, a smuggler, or a slave-dealer—what does Mr. Van den Bosch's past profession matter? The Countess of Castlewood can afford to be anyone's daughter. As soon as my nephew produces her, it is our duty to stand by her."

Madame de Bernstein was deeply bound to her nephew by blood ties. Mr. Warrington hoped she would extend similar affection to his niece and spoke of his visits to Mr. Hagan and his wife, seeking her aunt's favor. Yet, the old lady remained obdurate regarding Lady Maria, begging that her name never be mentioned, and proceeded for two hours discussing everyone else. She recounted a series of anecdotes concerning her niece; since this book is open to all, including the young ladies and gentlemen of the family, I shall refrain from recording them. However, I must note that while the kind creature may have sinned, she was not alone in doing so, and if she repented, others should follow her example.

Hagan, after leaving the stage, lived an exemplary life, renowned for his elegance and eloquence in the pulpit. His wife adopted extreme religious views but commanded great respect within her sect. When I last saw her, she spoke of possessing a peculiar spiritual illumination, which I strongly suspected at the time resulted from the excessive consumption of alcohol. Nevertheless, I recall when she and her husband were kind to me and mine during times of need, when many a Pharisee would have turned away.

I have described how easily one could rise and fall in my fickle aunt's favor, noting how each of us brothers was alternately embraced and neglected. My turn of glory came following the success of my play. I was introduced to the town wits, held my place among them reasonably well, and was judged to be fairly well-bred by the macaronis and people of fashion. Had my purse been deeper, and had I chosen that path, I might have pursued a career among them. Instead, I loved a pair of kind eyes more than the brightest orbs of the Gunnings or Chudleighs, or all the painted beauties of the Ranelagh ring. Because I was fond of your mother, children, it was believed my tastes were low and deplored by my genteel family. Indeed, my godly Lady Warrington and my worldly Madame Bernstein laid their elderly heads together to lament my lifestyle.

"Why, with his name, he might marry anybody," said meek Religion, keeping one eye on Heaven and the other on the main chance. "I meddle with no man's affairs and admire genius," replied my uncle. "But it is a pity you consort with those poets and authors, and that sort of people, especially when you might have secured a lovely creature with a hundred thousand pounds, only to let her slip and pursue a country girl without a penny-piece."

"But if I had promised her, uncle?" I asked.

"Promise, promise! These things are matters of arrangement and prudence requiring a careful lookout," he retorted. "When you first committed yourself to little Miss Lambert, you had not yet seen the lovely American lady your mother wished you to marry, as a good mother naturally would. Your duty to your mother, nephew—your duty to the Fifth Commandment—would have warranted breaking with Miss L. and fulfilling your excellent mother's intentions regarding Miss... What was the Countess's Dutch name? Never mind. A name is nothing, but a plum, Master George, is something to look at! I have my dear little Miley at a dancing school with Miss Barwell, Nabob Barwell's daughter, and I do not disguise my wish that the children may develop an attachment enduring through their lives! I tell the Nabob so. We went from the House of Commons one dancing day and saw them. 'Twas beautiful to see the young things performing a minuet together! It brought tears to my eyes, for I have a feeling heart, George, and I love my boy!"

"But if I prefer Miss Lambert, uncle, with twopence to her fortune, to the Countess with her hundred thousand pounds?"

"Why then, sir, you have a singular taste, that's all," said the old gentleman, turning on his heel and leaving me.

I understood his vexation at my inability to view the world as he did. Neither did Aunt Bernstein approve of the engagement I had made or the family with whom I spent so much time. Their simple ways wore thin and perhaps annoyed the old woman of the world; she relished their company no more than a certain person (who is not quite as black as he is painted) enjoys holy water. The old lady chafed at my constant dallying at my sweetheart's lap. Having risen mightily in her favor, I began to fall again. Once more, Harry was the favorite, and his brother, heaven knows, was not jealous. He was now our family hero.

He wrote us brief letters from the seat of war where he was engaged. Madame Bernstein cared little initially about the letters or the writer, finding them simple and the facts narrated uninteresting. We had earlier learned in London of the action on the glorious first of August at Minden, where Wolfe's old regiment was one of the British six contributing to that famous victory. At that very hour, the young General lay in his bed, in sight of Quebec, struck down by fever, and perhaps also by rage and disappointment at the setback his troops had just suffered.

Arriving in the St. Lawrence in June, the fleet carrying Wolfe and his army landed them on the last day of the month on the Island of Orleans, opposite which rises the great cliff of Quebec. Following the great action in which his General fell, the dear brother who accompanied the chief wrote home one of his simple letters, describing his modest share in that glorious day. He added nothing to the many existing descriptions of the action of September 13th, except that, according to the testimony of a brother aide-de-camp standing beside him, the General never spoke at all after receiving his death wound. Thus, the phrase often attributed to the dying hero is no more authentic than an oration of Livy or Thucydides.

From his position on the island, lying in the great channel of the river north of the town, the General was ever hungrily watching for a chance to meet and attack his enemy. Above the city and below it, he landed—now here, now there; bent upon attacking wherever he saw an opening. 'Twas surely a prodigious fault on the part of the Marquis of Montcalm to accept a battle from Wolfe on equal terms, for the British General possessed no artillery. When we executed our famous scaling of the heights and reached the Plains of Abraham, we were certainly a little nearer the city, though as far removed from entering it as before. The game played between the brave chiefs of those two gallant little armies, lasting from July until Mr. Wolfe won the crowning hazard in September, must have been as fascinating a match as ever eager players engaged in.

On the very first night after landing (as my brother has narrated), the sport began. At midnight, the French sent a flaming squadron of fireships down upon the British vessels discharging their stores at Orleans. Our seamen thought it good sport to tow the fireships clear of the fleet and ground them on the shore, where they burned out. As soon as the French commander heard our ships had entered the river, he marched to Beauport, in advance of the city, and took up a strong position. When our stores and hospitals were established, our General crossed from his island to the left shore, drawing nearer to his enemy. He had the ships in the river behind him, but the entire countryside facing him was armed. The Indians in the forest seized our advanced parties as they tried to clear the area, murdering them with horrible tortures. The French were as savage as their Indian allies. The Montmorenci River rushed between Wolfe and the enemy. He could neither attack these nor the city behind them. Determined to find another point of vulnerability, the General passed around the town of Quebec and skirted the left shore beyond. Everywhere was guarded, as well as in his immediate front. Having run the gauntlet of the batteries up and down the river, he returned to his post at Montmorenci.

To the

Had your father, young folks, possessed the commonest share of prudence, not only would this chapter of his history never have been written, but you yourselves would never have appeared in the world to plague him in a hundred ways—to shout and laugh in the passages when he wants to be quiet at his books; to wake him when he is dozing after dinner, as a healthy country gentleman should; to mislay his spectacles for him, and steal away his newspaper when he wants to read it; to ruin him with tailors' bills, mantua-makers' bills, tutors' bills, as you all of you do; to break his rest of nights when you have the impudence to fall ill, and when he would sleep undisturbed but that your silly mother will never be quiet for half an hour; and when Joan can't sleep, what use, pray, is there in Darby putting on his nightcap? Every trifling ailment that any one of you has had has scared her so that I protest I have never been tranquil; and, were I not the most long-suffering creature in the world, I would have liked to be rid of the whole pack of you.

And now, forsooth, that you have grown out of childhood—long petticoats, chicken-pox, small-pox, whooping-cough, scarlet fever, and the other delectable accidents of puerile life—what must that unconscionable woman propose but to arrange the south rooms as a nursery for possible grandchildren, and set up the Captain with a wife, and make him marry early because *we* did! He is too fond, she says, of Brookes's and Goosetree's when he is in London. She has the perversity to hint that, though an entrée to Carlton House may be very pleasant, 'tis very dangerous for a young gentleman; and she would have Miles live away from temptation, and sow his wild oats, and marry, as we did. "Marry!" my dear creature, we had no business to marry at all! By the laws of common prudence and duty, I ought to have backed out of my little engagement with Miss Theo (who would have married somebody else), and taken a rich wife. Your Uncle John was a parson and couldn't fight, poor Charley was a boy at school, and your grandfather was too old a man to call me to account with sword and pistol. I repeat there never was a more foolish match in the world than ours, and our relations were perfectly right in being angry with us.

What are relations made for, indeed, but to be angry and find fault? When Hester marries, do you mind, Master George, to quarrel with her if she does not take a husband of your selecting. When George has got his living, after being senior wrangler and fellow of his college, Miss Hester, do you toss up your little nose at the young lady he shall fancy. As for you, my little Theo, I can't part with you. You must not quit your old father; for he likes you to play Haydn to him, and peel his walnuts after dinner.

[On the blank leaf opposite this paragraph is written, in a large, girlish hand: "I never intend to go.—THEODOSIA." "Nor I.—HESTER." They both married, as I see by the note in the family Bible—Miss Theodosia Warrington to Joseph Clinton, son of the Rev. Joseph Blake, and himself subsequently Master of Rodwell Regis Grammar School; and Miss Hester Mary, in 1804, to Captain F. Handyman, R.N.—ED.]

Whilst they had the blessing (forsooth!) of meeting, and billing and cooing every day, the two young people, your parents, went on in a fool's paradise, little heeding the world round about them, and all its tattling and meddling. Rinaldo was as brave a warrior as ever slew Turk, but you know he loved dangling in Armida's garden. Pray, my Lady Armida, what did you mean by flinging your spells over me in youth, so that not glory, not fashion, not gaming-tables, not the society of men of wit in whose way I fell, could keep me long from your apron-strings, or out of reach of your dear simple prattle? Pray, my dear, what used we to say to each other during those endless hours of meeting? I never went to sleep after dinner then. Which of us was so witty? Was it I or you? And how came it our conversations were so delightful? I remember that year I did not even care to go and see my Lord Ferrers tried and hung, when all the world was running after his lordship. The King of Prussia's capital was taken; had the Austrians and Russians been encamped round the Tower there could scarce have been more stir in London: yet Miss Theo and her young gentleman felt no inordinate emotion of pity or indignation. What to us was the fate of Leipzig or Berlin? The truth is, that dear old house in Dean Street was an enchanted garden of delights. I have been as idle since, but never as happy. Shall we order the postchaise, my dear, leave the children to keep house; and drive up to London and see if the old lodgings are still to be let? And you shall sit at your old place in the window, and wave a little handkerchief as I walk up the street. Say what we did was imprudent. Would we not do it over again? My good folks, if Venus had walked into the room and challenged the apple, I was so infatuated, I would have given it your mother. And had she had the choice, she would have preferred her humble servant in a threadbare coat to my Lord Clive with all his diamonds.

Once, to be sure, and for a brief time in that year, I had a notion of going on the highway in order to be caught and hung as my Lord Ferrers; or of joining the King of Prussia, and requesting some of his Majesty's enemies to knock my brains out; or of enlisting for the India service, and performing some desperate exploit which should end in my bodily destruction. Ah me! that was indeed a dreadful time! Your mother scarce dares speak of it now, save in a whisper of terror; or think of it—it was such cruel pain. She was unhappy years after on the anniversary of the day, until one of you was born on it.

Suppose we had been parted: what had come to us? What had my lot been without her? As I think of that possibility, the whole world is a blank. I do not say were we parted now. It has pleased God to give us thirty years of union. We have reached the autumn season. Our successors are appointed and ready; and that one of us who is first called away knows the survivor will follow ere long. But we were actually parted in our youth; and I tremble to think what might have been, had not a dearest friend brought us together.

Unknown to myself, and very likely meaning only my advantage, my relatives in England had chosen to write to Madam Esmond in Virginia, and represent what they were pleased to call the folly of the engagement I had contracted. Every one of them sang the same song: and I saw the letters, and burned the whole cursed pack of them years afterwards when my mother showed them to me at home in Virginia. Aunt Bernstein was forward with her advice. A young person, with no wonderful good looks, of no family, with no money—was ever such an imprudent connexion, and ought it not for dear George's sake to be broken off? She had several eligible matches in view for me. With my name and prospects, 'twas a shame I should throw myself away on this young lady; her sister ought to interpose—and so forth. My Lady Warrington must write, too, and in her peculiar manner. Her ladyship's letter was garnished with scripture texts. She dressed her worldliness out in phylacteries. She pointed out how I was living in an unworthy society of player-folks, and the like people, who she could not say were absolutely without religion (Heaven forbid!), but who were deplorably worldly. She would not say an artful woman had inveigled me for her daughter, having in vain tried to captivate my younger brother. She was far from saying any harm of the young woman I had selected; but at least this was certain, Miss L. had no fortune or expectations, and her parents might naturally be anxious to compromise me. She had taken counsel, etc. etc. She had sought for guidance where it was, etc. Feeling what her duty was, she had determined to speak.

Sir Miles, a man of excellent judgment in the affairs of this world (though he knew and sought a better), fully agreed with her in opinion, nay, desired her to write, and entreat her sister to interfere, that the ill-advised match should not take place. And who besides must put a little finger into the pie but the new Countess of Castlewood? She wrote a majestic letter to Madam Esmond, and stated, that having been placed by Providence at the head of the Esmond family, it was her duty to communicate with her kinswoman and warn her to break off this marriage. I believe the three women laid their heads together previously; and, packet after packet, sent off their warnings to the Virginian lady.

One raw April morning, as Corydon goes to pay his usual duty to Phillis, he finds, not his charmer with her dear smile as usual

Once the initial shock of our reunion subsided, the little maid, Hetty, launched immediately into her tirade. "So you have finally come to inquire after Theo, and you feel remorse that your neglect has rendered her so ill? Six weeks she has languished, and not a single word have you spoken concerning her! Truly, Mr. George, you are kind!"

"Kind?" Mr. Warrington gasped, incredulous. "Do you deem it kindness to attend her day and night for a year, only to vanish without a whisper?"

"My dear fellow," I replied, attempting to smooth things over, "do you recall the promise you gave my father?"

"A promise!" Hetty scoffed, rolling her eyes. "What a magnificent pledge, to sicken my darling and then, on a sudden, bid her 'Goodbye, Theo,' and stride away forever! Gentlemen make such vows merely to break them. I would not toy with a poor child's heart and abandon her if I were a man. What crime has she committed against you but to be foolish and excessively fond? By what divine right do you snatch her from us, only to desert her because an elderly American woman disapproves of her? She was content with us until you arrived. She adored her sister—there was never such a devoted sibling—until she met you. And now, simply because your mother believes a younger gentleman might suit her better, you intend to cast her aside!"

"Heavens, child!" I cried, exasperated by her stubborn logic. "Did I withdraw? Am I not barred from your household? Did not your father demand, on my honor, that I cease all attempts to see her?"

"Honor!" she retorted. "You are the men who claim superiority; it is we who must revere you! I declare, George Warrington, you belong in your schoolroom in Virginia still, tucked into bed by a black nurse, asking permission to step outside. Oh, George! I never imagined my sister was entrusting her heart to a man lacking the spine to stand by her, fleeing at the first obstacle! When Dr. Heberden mentioned attending to you, I resolved to visit, and you truly looked ill—I am glad to see it, perhaps fearing your mother. But I shall not inform Theo of your sickness. She refuses to break her solemn engagements to roam the world as if nothing occurred! That is the province of men, our superiors in courage and wisdom; to abandon an angel—an angel ten thousand times too pure for you—is what you term honor? Do not speak to me! I despise you all! Are we to worship and serve you? I care not for your wit, your tragedies, or your verses; they often seem dreadfully dull. I will not spend my nights copying your manuscripts nor waste hours gazing from a window hoping to catch you strolling with your hat cocked! If you are leaving, and God speed, give me back my sister! Return my darling of the old days, who loved us all until she saw you. You send her away because your mother seeks a richer match for you! Oh, brave gentleman! Marry whoever your mother selects, and let my dear perish here, forsaken!"

"Great heavens, Hetty!" I exclaimed, stunned by the coherence of her distress. "Is it I who wish to depart? Did I not offer to uphold my promise? Was it not your father who refused me and compelled me to swear never to seek her sight again? All I possess is my word and my honor!"

"Honor, indeed!" she snapped. "You keep your word to him and betray hers! A pretty sort of honor! If I were a man, I would show you quickly what I thought of your code! Only I forgot—you are bound to maintain the peace and must not... Oh, George, George! Do you not perceive the agony tearing me apart? I am distraught, scarcely knowing what I utter. You must not leave my darling. They do not realize at home; they assume otherwise, but I know her best, and she will die if you abandon her. Swear you will stay! Have pity on me, Mr. Warrington, and restore my dearest to me!"

Thus, the warm-hearted, frantic creature oscillated between rage and supplication, from scorn to tears. Was my little physician correct in diagnosing her state regarding her beloved patient? Was there no cure other than what Hetty demanded? Had not others endured the cruel pain of amputation, suffered the subsequent exhaustion and fever, lain near death expecting only the end, and yet recovered, limping onward through life? Perhaps, unless love is inherently selfish, ignoring the griefs and passions of others—or unless our own affection was so intense and unique that we believed no other lover could suffer as we did—in this passionate advocate for her sister, we might have witnessed proof that a loving heart could be struck by the disease of love, endure it silently, and eventually heal.

What had transpired in Hetty's own case? My sister and I, basking in our easy triumph and intimate chatter, had frequently discussed this matter. Egotists that we were, we perhaps extracted a secret zest and sense of security from her lesser fortune. It was akin to sitting by a fireside while winter howled outside, or walking beside the *maxi magno* while watching a ship toss at sea. We clung to one another more tightly, wrapped in our own bliss, viewing others' misfortunes with complacent pity.

Be the truth as it may. Granted that we might have been severed and, after a time, survived the separation—a notion my skeptical old age might concede. Yet, at that juncture, I was eager to share Hetty's terrors and apprehensions, willingly choosing to believe that the life dearest to me in the world would be sacrificed if torn from mine. Was I wrong? I would not assert that now. I may doubt myself (or perhaps I do not doubt), but of her, never; and Hetty found in me a willing partner in her alarms.

I considered imparting some of these fears to our doctor, but the good gentleman silenced me. "Hush," he said, with a comical expression of terror. "I must hear none of this. If two acquaintances chance to meet and converse in my patients' room, I cannot control myself; but as for matchmaking and love-making, I am your humble servant! What will the General do when he returns to town? He will have me dismissed from Montagu House as surely as I am a living doctor, and alive I wish to remain, my good sir!" With that, he skipped into his carriage, leaving me there to ponder.

"And you and Miss Hetty must have no further meetings here, mind you," he had warned earlier.

Oh, certainly not! We were gentlemen of honor, and so forth; our word was our bond. Moreover, to have seen Hetty was an inestimable boon, and how could we possibly be ungrateful? Refreshed by that drop of water, I fancied I could endure indefinitely. I walked away with Hetty toward Soho, never once considering arranging a new rendezvous. But the little emissary was more thoughtful. She asked if I intended to visit the Museum.

"Oh yes, sometimes, my dear," I replied. "But I am too wretched for reading now; I cannot discern what lies upon the page. I care not for my books. Even *Pocahontas* grows wearisome to me..."

"Nonsense!" she interrupted, stamping her tiny foot. "Why, I declare, George, you are stupider than Harry!"

"How do you mean, my dear child?" I asked.

"When do you go? You depart at three o'clock. You cross directly onto the road to Tottenham Court. You traverse the village and return via Green Lane, leading back toward the new hospital. You know you do! If you walk along there for a week, it cannot harm you. Good morning, sir! Please do not follow me any farther."

She dropped a curtsey, veiled her face, and departed.

That Green Lane, lying north of the new hospital, is now densely packed with houses. In my time, during the reign of the good King George II, it was a shabby rural exit from London, so perilous that city dwellers returning from their villas and country retreats at Hampstead and the outlying villages would travel in groups at night, escorted by waiters carrying lanterns to protect themselves from footpads prowling the town's edges. Hampstead and Highgate churches, each crowning its hill, formed the backdrop of the vista visible as one turned one's back on London. For one, two, or three days, Mr. George Warrington enjoyed gazing upon this landscape, walking back toward the new hospital.

Along the lane stood several small taverns. I recall one establishment selling cakes and beer, bearing the sign of the Protestant Hero, where a decent woman smiled at me on the third or fourth day, curtsying in her clean apron as she remarked, "It seems the lady does not come, sir! Your honor had best step in and take a can of my cool beer."

Finally, as I returned through Tottenham Road on the twenty-fifth of May—a day worthy of being marked with the whitest stone—a short distance beyond Mr. Whitefield's Tabernacle, I spotted a landau ahead. Upon the box-seat beside the driver sat my young friend Charley, who waved his hat and called out, "George! George!"

I rushed toward the carriage, my knees knocking together so violently

"You, Captain Miles Warrington, possess the rare honor of securing the favor of a lady—indeed, of several ladies simultaneously. Let us name the Duchess of Devonshire, Mrs. Crew, Mrs. Fitzherbert, the Queen of Prussia, the Goddess Venus herself, Mademoiselle Hillisberg of the Opera—it matters not whose affections you have captured. Now, if you win a lady's good graces, do you invariably retreat to the mess to recount every detail?"

"Not such a fool, Squire!" retorted the Captain, admiring his sideburns in the mirror. "Have you, Miss Theo, whispered every word spoken to Mr. Joe Blake, junior, in the shrubbery this morning?"

"Joe Blake, indeed!" cried Theo Junior. "And you, Mademoiselle? Did you reveal the contents of that scented note delivered under Sir Thomas's seal? Observe how crimson she turns! Red as the curtains, I swear! Well, Mademoiselle, we all harbor our secrets." (The Squire executed a particularly elegant French bow here.)

"No, Theo, there was nothing in the shrubbery save nuts, my child! No, Miles, my son, we do not disclose everything, even to the most indulgent of fathers—and if I recount what transpired in a landau on the Hampstead Road on the twenty-fifth of May, 1760, may Chevalier Ruspini extract every tooth from my skull!"

"Please, Papa!" urged Mama. "Or, since Jobson, who drove us, is now in your employ, perhaps you will summon him from the stables! I insist upon hearing the tale!"

"What is this mystery, then?" asked Mademoiselle, in her charming French accent, addressing my wife.

"Eh, ma fille!" the lady whispered. "Wouldst thou ask me what I said? I said 'Yes!'—behold, that is all I said."

Thus, it was my wife who had betrayed us, not I. This was the entirety of our exchange as the carriage, faster than I deemed prudent, galloped toward Hampstead and returned. Theo had not agreed to defy her honored parents—such a notion was unthinkable. Yet, we resolved to wed no other; nay, not even the Prince of Wales himself could command her hand. Her heart had been surrendered with her father's consent—nay, his direct order; it was not hers to reclaim. Such a benevolent father must eventually soften; and if George kept his promise—whether today, in twenty years, or in another world—she knew she would never break hers.

Hetty's face beamed with delight when, upon my departure, she observed Theo's countenance settling into a sweet tranquility. "All the physician's medicine has not wrought such improvement," the devoted sister remarked. After their act of disobedience, the girls departed home. I relinquished the position I had briefly occupied beside my dear invalid. Hetty skipped back into her seat, and Charley mounted to the box. He later confessed that the sermon he attended was exceedingly dull and obtuse; the little chap was too orthodox to appreciate dissenting preachers. Hetty was not the sole family member to notice her sister's transformed demeanor and elevated spirits. I was informed that upon their return, their mother embraced both, especially the invalid, with uncommon fervor.

"There is nothing comparable to a country ride," Aunt Lambert declared. "It has done her dear Theo wonders. She rode to Hampstead, did she? She must ride again tomorrow. Blessed be Heaven, Lord Wrotham's horses are at our disposal three or four times weekly, and the sweet child may enjoy their company!"

As for the notion that Mr. Warrington might have encountered the children during their drive, Aunt Lambert never once entertained it—or at least, never voiced such a suspicion. I leave it to the curious to conjecture whether Mrs. Lambert could possibly have imagined her daughter and her sweetheart reuniting. Do women assist one another in matters of romantic entanglement? Do they scheme, intrigue, devise small plans, spin little falsehoods, create amorous opportunities, dangle the rope-ladder, coax, wheedle, mystify the guardians or Abigails, and divert their attention while Strephon and Chloe whisper in the twilight or dash off to Gretna Green in a postchaise? My dear young friends, some individuals are thus constituted; and some kind souls, having loved tenderly and truly in their own time, remain forever disposed with kindness and tenderness toward their young successors as they commence similar games. Miss Prim is not among them. Should she learn of two youths attached to one another, she will sneer at them as fools or conjure up every conceivable evil. Possessing a hunchback herself, she seeks to bite everyone else's. I believe if she witnessed a pair of turtles cooing in a wood, she would cast her gaze downward or hurl a stone to frighten them; yet I speak, you see, young ladies, of your grandmother, Aunt Lambert, who was a veritable syllabub of human kindness. Moreover, regarding the affair currently under discussion, how am I to determine whether she possessed knowledge of it or not? Thus, all she tells Theo upon her return is, "My child, the country air has restored you completely, and I hope you will take another drive tomorrow, and another, and another, ad infinitum."

"Do you not think, Papa, that the ride has performed miracles for the child, and ought she not be compelled to breathe the fresh air?" Aunt Lambert inquired of the General upon his arrival for supper.

"Yes, certainly. If a coach-and-six benefits little Theo, she shall have it," Lambert replied. "Or he will drag the landau up Hampstead Hill himself if no horses are available;" and so the good man would willingly spend his guineas, his breath, or his blood to grant his child pleasure. He was charmed by his daughter's altered complexion; she ate a piece of chicken with relish; she drank a small measure of negus, which he prepared for her. Indeed, it seemed superior to the physician's finest remedies, which, God knows, had yielded little benefit thus far. Mama was gracious and happy. Hetty was radiant and laughing. It resembled an evening at home at Oakhurst. Never for months past, never since that fatal, cruel day—the one no one dared mention—had they spent such a delightful night.

However, if the other women chose to coax and cajole the good, simple father, Theo herself was too honest to sustain that sweet and fond delusion for long. When, for the third or fourth time, he returned to the delightful topic of his daughter's improved health and asked, "What has effected this? Is it the country air? Is it the Jesuit's bark? Is it the new medicine?"

"Cannot you divine, dear, what it is?" she said, placing a hand upon her father's with a tremor in her voice, though her eyes remained wide and bright.

"And what is it, my child?" asked the General.

"It is because I have seen him again, Papa!" she exclaimed.

The other two women turned pale, and Theo's own heart began to palpitate, her cheeks flushing white as she continued to gaze into her father's terrified visage. "It was not wrong to see him," she continued more quickly; "it would have been wrong not to inform you."

"Great God!" groaned the father, recoiling his hand, his countenance etched with such profound grief that Hetty rushed to her nearly swooning sister, clutching her to her bosom and crying out rapidly, "Theo knew nothing of it, sir! It was my doing—it was entirely my doing!"

Theo lay upon her sister's neck, kissing it twenty, fifty times.

"Women, women! Are you playing with my honor?" the father shrieked, bursting forth with a fierce exclamation. Aunt Lambert sobbed wildly, "Martin! Martin! Say not a word to her!" Again Hetty cried out, "Martin!" and staggered backward toward the wall, for Theo had fainted upon her shoulder.

I was taking my breakfast the following morning with whatever appetite I might muster when my door opened and my faithful black servant announced, "General Lambert." Instantly, by the General's expression, I perceived that the events of the previous day were known to him.

"Your accomplices did not confess," the General stated as soon as my servant had departed, "but sided with you against their father—a testament to how desirable clandestine meetings are. It was from Theo herself I learned that she had seen you."

"Accomplices, sir!" I replied (perhaps not unwilling to shift the focus from the core issue). "You know how fondly and dutifully your young people regard their father. If they oppose you in this instance, it must be because justice lies against you. A man of your stature cannot establish *sic volo, sic jubeo* as the sole law within his household!"

"Pshaw, George!" cried the General. "Though we are parted, God forbid I should wish for our love to cease. I received your promise that you would not seek to see her."

"Nor did I go to her, sir," I said, turning red, undoubtedly; for while this was truth, I admitted it was also a lie.

"You mean she was brought to you?" asked Theo's father, greatly agitated. "Will you hide behind Hester's petticoat? What a splendid defense for a gentleman!"

"Well, I shall not shield myself behind

Years later, while sorting through my mother's papers, I discovered a document neatly filed and labeled in her familiar, precise hand: "From London, April, 1760. My son's dreadful letter." When it finally became my possession, I burned it. I chose not to allow that tale of domestic sorrow and estrangement to remain among our family records, lest future Warringtons glance upon it with curiosity, or disobedient sons cite it as a warning of past familial rebellion. For similar reasons, I have also destroyed the reply my mother sent me during this season of tyranny, revolt, and irritation.

Blinded by the agony of separation from my mistress, and justifiably believing that Mrs. Esmond was the architect of the deepest grief and misery I had ever known, I wrote home to Virginia. My letter, though perhaps lacking in temperance, strove to maintain the utmost respect and reserve. I stated that I knew not her motives, yet I held her accountable for the misery of my future life, which she had wilfully marred. She had caused a rift between me and a virtuous, innocent young woman, whose hopes, health, and happiness were forever dimmed by her interference. The deed was done; I offered no judgment on the perpetrator, who answered only to God, but I did not hide from my mother that the injury inflicted upon me was mortal—that neither her life nor mine could repair it. The bond of my allegiance was severed; I could never again be the dutiful, respectful son she expected.

Lady Esmond replied with extraordinary dignity. Her correspondence was always elegant and refined. She uttered no reproach, merely referring the matter coldly to the "awful tribunal of God" for counsel. She humbly submitted her own conduct to divine scrutiny and asked if I, as a son, could equally answer for myself—could I demonstrate before the Great Judge that I had fulfilled my duty and honored my parents? *O popoi!* My grandfather often quoted Homer regarding how, in our troubles, we inevitably call the gods to witness. When pride, avarice, ambition, or the desire to dominate stir within us, do we not constantly plague Heaven to rule in our favor? During our great American quarrel, did we not both sides appeal to the skies for justice, sing Te Deums for victory, and confidently declare that righteousness would prevail? Was America right solely because she won? Then surely Poland was wrong because she lost? How easily I drift into these digressions about nations and history, while truly thinking of a little woman now gone, who appealed to Heaven with a thousand scriptural texts simply because her son desired a marriage she disliked. We plead, we curse, we kneel, we demand sentences according to law; yet the great course of the world moves on. We pant, strive, struggle, hate, rage, weep passionately, reconcile, race toward victory or defeat, pass away, and are succeeded by other little strugglers. Our days end, nights fall, and mornings rise that shine upon us no more.

Having composed my letter of revolt—a document of which I was perhaps slightly proud—I showed duplicates to Mr. Lambert. I wished him to understand the nature of my relationship with my mother and my determination that, regardless of future threats, I would never view my separation from Theo as voluntary. Whenever I could see her again, I would. My promise to her was *in secula seculorum*, binding at least as long as my life endured. I implied that Theo was similarly bound to me, and her poor father understood this fully. He might separate us; he might even administer poison, and the gentle, obedient creature might drink it and die—but the death or separation would be his doing. Let him answer for it. Though tender-hearted to a fault, unable to bear the thought of torturing any child—especially this one—he could not force the issue. We had tried to part; we could not. He had attempted to separate us, but it was beyond his power. The bars were raised, yet the young couple—the maid within and the knight without—continued to love each other. The wall stood high, but Pyramus and Thisbe whispered on either side. Amidst his grief and perplexity, Uncle Lambert possessed ample humor and recognized the sorry nature of his role. Light began to filter through the lime and rough plaster of the wall; the lovers were passing their hands through, then their heads. Indeed, it was the wall's finest business to retreat.

I forget the details of the stages and days that followed; nor does it greatly matter for the instruction of future ages. When my descendants encounter their own romantic entanglements, they will find their own solutions. I believe I ceased visiting Dean Street, as open-air riding was deemed most healthful for Miss Lambert. I acquired a fine horse and rode alongside her carriage. The elderly woman at Tottenham Court soon knew us both intimately, nodding and winking in the most friendly fashion as we passed. I suspect the old good woman was accustomed to taking an interest in young couples and had likely offered the hospitality of her roadside cottage to more than one pair.

The doctor and the country air wrought a prodigious cure upon Miss Lambert. Hetty always served as duenna, and occasionally, during my holidays, Master Charley rode my horse while I sat in the carriage. How much love-making Miss Hetty must have heard! With what exemplary patience she listened! I do not claim she resumed attending Methodist sermons, but certainly, whenever we traveled in a closed carriage, she would kindly and considerately peer out the window. Then, what a flood of letters! What constant running to and fro! Gumbo's bandy legs were perpetually on the move between my quarters and Dean Street; and, for my sake or hers, Mrs. Molly, the girl's maid, was endlessly returning answers from Bloomsbury. By the time autumn leaves began to pale, Miss Theo's roses were in full bloom again, and Doctor Heberden pronounced his cure complete. What else occurred during this blessed period? Mr. Warrington completed his great tragedy, *Pocahontas*. This time it was accepted by Mr. Garrick (having received favorable remarks from his friend Dr. Johnson), and my friend and cousin, Hagan, was engaged by the manager to play the hero, Captain Smith. Hagan's engagement was secured precisely when needed. I had assisted him and his family with means disproportionate to my power, particularly considering my feud with Madam Esmond. Her reply to my angry missive of April arrived towards autumn, written from Virginia with war for war and controlment for controlment. Yet these menaces frightened me little. My poor mother's thunder could not reach me; my conscience—or casuistry—supplied alternative interpretations for her scriptural texts, rendering her oracles weightless in scaring me from my purpose. I informed neither my new love nor any member of my maternal or paternal families—who had bitterly opposed my marriage—of how swiftly my new affections flourished. Why wrangle with them? It was better to seize the day (*carpere diem*) and its sweet loves and pleasures, leaving the railers to grumble and the elders to advise at their leisure.

Besides Madam Esmond, I must admit, in the frantic rage of my temporary separation, I sent notes of wondrous sarcasm to my Uncle Warrington, my Aunt Madame de Bernstein, and my Lord or Lady of Castlewood (I forget which individual), thanking them for the trouble they had taken in preventing the dearest happiness of my life, and promising corresponding gratitude from my obliged relative. Business brought the jovial Baronet and his family to London somewhat earlier than usual, and Madame de Bernstein was never sorry to return to Clarges Street and her social cards. I saw them. They found me perfectly well. Concluding the match was broken off, I did not choose to undeceive them. The Baroness took heart at my cheerfulness, making many sly jokes about my philosophy and my prudent behavior as a man of the world. As ever, she was bent on securing a rich match for me, and I fear I paid many compliments at her house to a wealthy young soap-boiler's daughter from Mile End, whom the worthy Baroness wished to place in my arms.

"You court her with infinite wit and esprit, my dear," my pleased kinswoman remarked, "but she does not understand half you say, and the other half, I think, frightens her. This *ton de persiflage* is very well in our society, but you must be sparing of it, my dear nephew, amongst these roturiers."

Miss Badge eventually married a young gentleman of royal dignity, though with shattered fortunes, from a neighboring island; and I trust Mrs. Mackshane has since pardoned my levity. There was another person at my aunt's house who did not comprehend my mockery nearly as well as Miss Badge herself: a lady who had witnessed the reign of James II and remained as worldly as ever in the time of King George. I loved to be in her company. If my little readers had access to this volume, I could recount a hundred stories of the great old figures she had known in the great old days—George I and his ladies, St. John and Marlborough, the reigning monarch and the late Prince of Wales, and the causes of their quarrel—but my modest muse sings only for boys and virgins. My dear son Miles cares nothing for court tales, or if he does, he possesses a fresh budget from Carlton House quite as scandalous as the worst of our old Baroness. No, my dear wife, there is no need to shake your powdered locks at me! Papa is not going to scandalize his nursery with old-world gossip nor bring a blush over our chaste bread

We, who had played such a pivotal role in the scandalous events of the morning, felt a treble sense of guilt when we witnessed the devastating impact our actions had on him—the man we loved and revered above all others. The shock inflicted upon this good man was strange, yet pitiful to watch, especially since we were the ones who had delivered the blow. His beloved child had deceived and defied him. I confess, my dear, that in retrospect, neither of us would—or could—repeat such a deed; it seemed as though his entire family had conspired against him. Oh, dear, kind friend and father! We know you have pardoned our transgression in that heavenly realm where you dwell among purified spirits who learned on earth the true arts of loving and forgiving. For that man, love and forgiveness were natural duties; beneficence flowed from him like sunlight, accompanied by a sweet, smiling humility. To wound either was to become savage and brutal, akin to torturing a child or striking a nursing mother. Once the deed was done, all of us, the guilty parties, cowered in shame before the man we had injured.

I shall skip over the scenes of forgiveness, reconciliation, shared worship, and the final separation when the good man departed for his government, sailing away before us and leaving me and Theo stranded on the shore. We stood there, hand in hand, horribly abashed, silent, and consumed by guilt. My wife did not join me until her father had left; during the interval between our marriage ceremony and his departure, she remained at home, occupying her usual spot beside her father and by her sister's side. He was as kind as ever, but the women were almost mute among themselves. Aunt Lambert, for once, was unkind and fretful; little Hetty was feverish and strange, muttering, "I wish we were gone. I wish we were gone." Although technically admitted to the house and forgiven, I slunk away during those final days, seeing my wife only briefly in the street or amidst her family. She was not truly mine until they were gone.

We traveled to Winchester and Hampton for what might be termed our wedding—a dismal affair. For a while, we felt utterly lonely, grieving as if we had buried our dear father or driven him to the grave through our undutifulness. I instructed Sampson to announce our marriage in the newspapers (my wife later hung her head in shame whenever the poor fellow mentioned it). I took Mrs. Warrington back to my old lodgings in Bloomsbury, where there was ample space for us, and our modest married life began.

I wrote a letter to my mother in Virginia, omitting all particulars except to state that since Mr. Lambert was departing for his government, I felt honor-bound to fulfill my promise to his dearest daughter. I also noted my intention to complete my studies for the Bar, qualifying myself for employment at home or in any colony. My good Mrs. Mountain replied on behalf of Madam Esmond, suggesting that for the sake of peace, my correspondence should be routed through her. This revelation plunged my relatives into a fury that was perfectly amusing to observe. When I visited my uncle's house in Hill Street, the butler's face, as he curtly announced, "Not at home," was a blank tragedy worthy of study by Garrick himself. With my poor little wife on my arm, we tripped away, laughing at the fellow's cold reception, only to encounter my lady in a street stoppage in her carriage.

I removed my hat and offered the lowest possible bow. Affectionately, I asked after my dear cousins. "I—I wonder you dare look me in the face!" Lady Warrington gasped. "Nay, don't deprive me of that precious privilege!" I replied cheerfully. "Move on, Peter," she screamed to her driver. "Your ladyship would not impale your own husband's flesh and blood!" I retorted. She rattled the glass of her carriage in a fury. I kissed my hand, removed my hat again, and performed another of my finest bows.

Shortly afterward, walking in Hyde Park with my dearest companion, I met my little cousin exercising on horseback with a groom trailing behind. As soon as he spotted us, he galloped up, the groom shouting, "Stop, Master Miles, stop!" "I am not to speak to my cousin," Miles declared, "but telling you to send my love to Harry is not speaking to you, is it? Is that my new cousin? I'm not told not to speak to her. I'm Miles, cousin, Sir Miles Warrington Baronet's son, and you are very pretty!" "Now, duee now, Master Miles," the groom interjected, tipping his hat to us, before trotting away laughing and glancing back over his shoulder.
