"You see how my relations have determined to treat me," I remarked to my partner. "As if I married you for your relations!" Theo laughed, her eyes beaming with joy and love. Ah, how happy we were! How brisk and pleasant the winter proved! How cozy the kettle by the fire (where the abashed Sampson occasionally came to stir the punch)! How delightful the nights at the theater, where our friends generously provided tickets, and where we eagerly anticipated our new play, *Pocahontas*, rivalling the successes of all former tragedies.

The fickle old aunt of Clarges Street, who had initially greeted me with a burst of scorn upon my arrival in London with my wife, quickly softened. As soon as she came to know Theo—who she had previously dismissed as an insignificant, country-faced chit—she fell utterly in love with her, inviting us to tea and supper every day whenever no other company was available. "As for company, my dears," she would say, "I don't ask for it. You are no longer *du monde*. Your marriage has put that entirely out of the question." Thus, she insisted we come to amuse her, entering and exiting via the back stairs. My wife was a fine lady enough to find this reception merely amusing. I must give credit to the Baroness's domestics; had we been duke and duchess, we could not have been treated with greater respect.

Madame de Bernstein delighted in my stories of Lady Warrington and the carriage incident. I acted out the scene for her, recounting anecdotes of the pious Baronet's lady and her daughters, which pleased the mischievous, lively old woman. The Dowager Countess of Castlewood, residing at Kensington, extended the kind of welcome gentle ladies typically offer their poorer relatives. We attended her gatherings once or twice, but losing more money at cards and spending too much on coach hire, we soon ceased these entertainments. Presumably, we were no more missed or regretted than other people swept out of the fashionable sphere by death, debt, or accident.

Theo did not regret this exclusion in the slightest. She had appeared at one of these gatherings adorned with small ornaments left by her mother, which the good lady valued highly. Yet, I thought her own white neck far prettier than those poor twinkling stones. There were dowagers whose wrinkled bones blazed with rubies and diamonds, surely wishing to exchange them for her modest parure of beauty and freshness. No one spoke to her—except Beau Lothair, a friend of Mr. Will's, who prowled about Bloomsbury afterward and even sent my wife a billet-doux. I met him in Covent Garden shortly after, promising to break his ugly face if I ever saw him near my lodgings, ensuring Madam Theo was molested no further.

The only relative to visit us was our poor Maria, along with her husband, Mr. Hagan, who frequently joined us for our homely dinners. Then there was friend Spencer from the Temple, who admired our Arcadian felicity and gently solicited our sympathy for his less fortunate loves. Twice or thrice, the famous Doctor Johnson came for a dish of Theo's tea. A dish? More like a pailful! "And a pail the best thing to feed him, sar!" exclaimed Mr. Gumbo indignantly. For the Doctor's appearance was unpleasant, and his linen was hardly white. He snorted, turned red, and sputtered while eating; he flung his food about and bellowed contradictions, annoying my Theo, whom he professed to admire greatly. Every time he saw her, he would declare, "Madam, you do not love me; I see by your manner you do not love me; though I admire you, and come here for your sake. Here is my friend Mr. Reynolds that shall paint you: he has no ceruse in his paint-box that is as brilliant as your complexion."

Mr. Reynolds, a most perfect and agreeable gentleman, would have painted my wife, but I knew his price and chose not to incur that expense. I wish I had done so now, for the sake of the children, that they might see what that face looked like some fifty years ago. To me, madam, 'tis the same now as ever; and your ladyship is always young! What annoyed Mrs. Warrington more than the Doctor's contradictions, sputterings, and dirty nails was an unfavorable opinion he formed of my new tragedy.

Hagan once proposed that the Doctor read some scenes from it after tea. "Nay, sir, conversation is better," the Doctor replied. "I can read for myself, or hear you at the theatre. I had rather hear Mrs. Warrington's artless prattle than your declamation of Mr. Warrington's decasyllables. Tell us about your household affairs, mad

The English public, unlike the inhabitants of Virginia who still cherish the memory of that simple and kindly creature, was not yet fully acquainted with the history of Pocahontas. Seizing this opportunity, Mr. Warrington, encouraged by his friends, composed a brief ballad concerning the Indian princess. This poem was printed in various magazines just prior to the premiere of the tragedy. Both Sampson and I deemed this maneuver to be remarkably astute and ingenious. "It serves as excellent ground-bait, sir," remarked the enthusiastic parson, "and you shall see the fish rise in multitudes on the great day!" Indeed, he and Spencer reported that the verses were discussed and admired in several coffee-houses within their hearing, often being mistakenly attributed to Mr. Mason, Mr. Cowper of the Temple, or even the renowned Mr. Gray. I suspect poor Sam himself circulated these rumors; had Shakespeare been named the author of the tragedy, he would undoubtedly have hailed *Pocahontas* as one of the Bard's finest achievements.

My connection with the legendary Captain John Smith began in childhood, within the walls of my grandfather's library. I recall sitting at the old man's knee, my favorite volume resting on my lap, as I spelled out the exploits of our Virginian hero. I delighted in reading of his travels, his sufferings, his captivities, and his escapes—not only across the American continent but throughout Europe. As I now reach for the familiar tome on the shelf in England, I feel transported back to my youth, surrounded by a flood of memories from my early home. My grandfather would paint vivid pictures for me of Smith battling the Turks on the Danube or being led forth by our Indian allies toward execution. Ah, what a terrifying spectacle was that engagement with the three Turkish champions! How I used to revel in the tale of his combat with Bonny Molgro, the final and most fearsome of the trio! What a formidable name, and with what a prodigious turban, scimitar, and flowing whiskers we would depict him! According to my cherished old book, after slaying and decapitating his first two foes, Smith faced Bonny Molgro. They clashed with their battle-axes, whose sharp edges occasionally left either rider with barely enough sense to hold onto their saddle. The Christian knight suffered a grievous wound, losing his axe, prompting a triumphant shout from the Turkish champion among the ramparts. Yet, through the readiness of his horse and his exceptional judgment and dexterity, Smith evaded further blows. Drawing his falchion, he struck the Turk beneath the ribs, piercing through his back and body. Though the enemy dismounted, he did not survive long, meeting the same fate as his predecessors. In reward for this feat, Duke Segismundus granted Smith three Turkish heads emblazoned on a shield for his coat of arms and an annual pension of three hundred ducats.

In my tragedy, disregarding time and place—a liberty poets alone possess—Smith performs similar feats of valor along the banks of our Potomac and James rivers. Our "ground-bait" verses ran as follows:

>
> Wearied arm and broken sword
> Wage in vain the desperate fight;
> Round him press the countless horde,
> He is but a single knight.
>
> Hark! a cry of triumph shrill
> Through the wilderness resounds,
> As, with twenty bleeding wounds,
> Sinks the warrior, fighting still.
>
> Now they heap the fatal pyre,
> And the torch of death they light;
> Ah! 'tis hard to die of fire!
> Who will shield the captive knight?
>
> Round the stake with fiendish cry
> Wheel and dance the savage crowd;
> Cold the victim's mien and proud,
> And his breast is bared to die.
>
> Who will shield the fearless heart?
> Who avert the murderous blade?
> From the throng, with sudden start,
> See, there springs an Indian maid.
>
> Quick she stands before the knight,
> Loose the chain, unbind the ring;
> I am daughter of the king,
> And I claim the Indian right!
>
> Dauntlessly aside she flings
> Lifted axe and thirsty knife;
> Fondly to his heart she clings,
> And her bosom guards his life!
>
> In the woods of Powhattan,
> Still 'tis told, by Indian fires,
> How a daughter of their sires
> Saved the captive Englishman.

There is no need for me to describe the full plot of my tragedy at length, as my children can retrieve it from the shelves at any time. Nor shall I hasten to read it again to my young ones, since Captain Miles and the parson both fell asleep last Christmas when, at my mother's request, I read aloud two acts. However, anyone with a moderate familiarity with plays and novels can easily construct a complete picture from the sketch above. The narrative features an Indian king, a loving princess, and her attendant, secretly enamored with the British captain's servant; a traitor lurking within the English fort; a valiant Indian warrior harboring an unhappy passion for Pocahontas; a medicine-man and priest of the Indians (excellently portrayed by Palmer), capable of every treason, stratagem, and crime, bent solely on the torture and death of the English prisoner. These elements, combined with the hazards of the wilderness, the war-dances and cries—which Gumbo mimicked with remarkable accuracy based on his experiences with Native Americans—and the arrival of the English fleet, alongside allusions to recent glorious victories in Canada and the British determination to rule and conquer America, seemed to many of us promising ingredients for success.

Yet, I must mention the ill omens that preceded the opening night. Difficulties arose from a peevish, jealous, and timid management, compounded by violent prejudice held against the piece in certain high quarters. No wonder, then, that *Pocahontas* failed to achieve victory. I scoff at the malignity of the critics who found fault with the performance. Pretty critics, indeed, who praised *Carpezan* as a masterpiece while sneering at a far superior and more elaborate work! I maintain that Hagan delivered his role so admirably that a certain actor and theater manager might well have envied him. Had it not been for the cabal orchestrated outside, the piece surely would have succeeded. Orders had explicitly been given that the play should fail, according to Sampson's declaration. "The house swarmed with Macs, by George," the honest fellow swore, "and they should have the galleries washed with brimstone!" He vowed that even Mr. Garrick himself would not have wished the piece to succeed, reaching a peak of fury during the grand scene in the second act. Here, Smith (played by poor Hagan), bound to the stake, was saved by Pocahontas. When the entire house thrilled with applause and sympathy, Sampson was beside himself with rage.

Any curious individual may consult the published tragedy—in the octavo form or the subsequent splendid quarto edition of my *Collected Works, and Poems Original and Translated*—to judge whether the scene lacks merit, or whether the verses are not elegant, the language rich and noble. One cause of the failure was my strict adherence to historical accuracy. I had carefully copied a figure of Sir Walter Raleigh from the Museum, complete with frill and beard; using lace donated by my dear Theo from her mother's collection, we dressed Hagan precisely according to this drawing, ensuring he looked impeccable. Similarly, I costumed Miss Pritchard as Pocahontas, drawing upon my own observations of indigenous attire from home. Can it be believed that the audience tittered when she first appeared? They eventually grew accustomed to it, but just as she rushed into the prisoner's arms, causing many to tearfully embrace the moment, a man in the pit shouted, "Bedad! here's the Belle Savage kissing the Saracen's Head!" This provoked an impudent roar of laughter that erupted in fitful explosions throughout the remainder of the performance. As the wit in Mr. Sheridan's *Critic* noted regarding morning guns, the playwrights were not content with one jest but insisted on firing two or three. Thus, this wretched tavern joke of the "Belle Savage"—ignored by the uninformed, who did not realize Pocahontas herself was the namesake of the tavern—ruined the mood. My friend in the pit repeated the insult ad nauseam, saluting every new character with a tavern name: the English governor, sporting a long beard, became the "Goat and Boots"; his lieutenant, Barker, whose face was notably broad, was dubbed the "Bull and Mouth," and so on. The curtain finally descended amidst a shrill storm of whistles and hisses, particularly targeting poor Hagan whenever he opened his mouth.

Sampson spotted Master Will in the green boxes with some of his pretty acquaintances, convinced the treacherous scoundrel was a ringleader in the conspiracy. "I would have flung him over into the pit," the faithful fellow declared (and Sampson possessed the courage to follow through), "but I saw two of Mr. Nadab's followers prowling the lobby and was forced to retreat." Consequently, the eggs we had hoped to sell at market were broken, and our poor hopes lay shattered before us!

Before the curtain rose, I peeked into the house from the stage and observed it was reasonably full, noting Mr. Johnson in the front

I have already laid out my current sentiments as an elderly gentleman concerning that hasty leap into matrimony, which I convinced my dearest partner to take when we were both barely out of our teens. As a man and a father—with a proper awareness of the necessity of mutton chops and the importance of settling the baker's bill, surrounded by a pack of rash children who might flee to Scotland tomorrow, citing my and my wife's example as justification for their impudence—I know I ought to be extremely cautious in recounting this early phase of the married life of George Warrington, Esquire, and Theodosia his wife. I should perhaps offer a *mea culpa*, adopt a demure demeanor, sit comfortably in my armchair, claim to be in a state of profound repentment, and present myself as a cautionary tale for imprudent, hot-headed youth.

Yet, truth be told, the married life regarding which my dear relatives prophesied so gloomily has utterly disappointed those prudent and respectable individuals. It has indeed had its trials; however, I can recall them without bitterness—its passionate sorrows, of which Time, by God's gracious ordinance, has served as the benign healer; its days of poverty, which we, who endured them, bore to the astonishment of our sympathetic relatives watching from afar; and its precious rewards and blessings, so immense that I scarce dare whisper them onto this page. To speak of them requires nothing less than awful reverence and prayer directed toward the One Ear to which the thanks of all mankind are offered. To marry without financial security is undoubtedly wrong, dangerous, and a transgression against our social codes; yet, do not tens of thousands of our fellow creatures commit this "crime" annually, trusting solely in Heaven, health, and labor? Do young people not enter marriage taking hope into account, nor dare to begin housekeeping until the cottage is fully furnished, the cellar and larder stocked, the cupboard brimming with silver, and the strongbox heavy with coin? The population of the world would cease to multiply if the laws regulating the genteel classes were made universal. Our gentry tremble at the precipice in their silk stockings and pumps, waiting years for a bridge or a gilded barge to carry them across; our poor do not fear to wet their bare feet, plant them in the stream, and trust to fate and strength to bear them over. Who would wish to consign his daughter to poverty? Who would advise his son to undertake the countless risks of impoverished married life, removing the beloved girl from comfort and competence, and subjecting her to debt, misery, privation, loneliness, sickness, and the hundred gloomy consequences of *res angusta domi* (narrow circumstances)? I gaze upon my own wife and beg her pardon for imposing a task so fraught with pain and peril upon one so gentle. I reflect on the trials she endured and am grateful for them, and for that unfailing love and constancy with which God blessed her and strengthened her to bear them all. Regarding the question of marriage, I cannot be considered a fair judge: my own union was so imprudent—and yet so happy—that I must not presume to offer counsel to the young. I have suffered poverty, but I rarely found it anything other than tolerable. Had I not undergone it, I never could have known the kindness of friends, the delight of gratitude, or the surprising joys and consolations that sometimes accompany a scanty meal and a narrow fire, cheering the long day's labor. At least this is certain concerning the lot of the decent poor: a great deal of superfluous pity is often wasted upon them. Good-natured fine folks, who occasionally stepped out of the sunshine of their wealth into narrow obscurity, were blinded, as it were, whereas we could see quite cheerfully and clearly. They stumbled over obstacles that meant nothing to us; they were surprised at the resignation with which we drank small beer and heartily gave thanks over such cold mutton.

My father-in-law, the good General, married his Molly when he was merely a subaltern in a foot regiment, possessing a purse scarce better filled than my own. They experienced their share of fortune's ups and downs. I suspect (though my wife will never confess this) that they married, as people could in their youth, without previously seeking permission from their parents. At any rate, so pleased were they with their own marital luck that they did not begrudge their children's, nor were they frightened by the prospect of minor hardships we might encounter in our married lives. I suppose that when I presented my financial statements to Mr. Lambert, I was anxious to deceive both of us. Believing me to be master of two thousand pounds, he departed for Jamaica quite at ease regarding his darling daughter's comfort and maintenance, at least for some years. After covering the expenses of his family's outfit, the worthy man left not much richer than his son-in-law; and a few trinkets, some lace from Aunt Lambert, and twenty new guineas in a purse crafted by her mother and sisters constituted Theo's marriage portion. However, in valuing my assets, I chose to count as a valid debt a sum my honored mother never acknowledged until the day she was called to pay the final debt of all. The sums I had disbursed for her, she argued, were spent on the improvement and maintenance of the estate destined for me upon her death. Any money she could spare was reserved for my poor brother, who possessed nothing. He would never have spent his own means had he not imagined himself the sole heir to the Virginian property—a notion the good lady emphasized in many letters—but for a half-hour accident of birth. He was now distinguishing himself in the service of his King and Country. Purchasing his promotion was, she supposed, his brother's duty! Once I had completed my bar studies and dramatic amusements, Madam Esmond informed me that I was welcome to return home and assume the position in our colony to which my birth entitled me. She communicated this statement to me more than once through Mountain, before news of my marriage had reached her.

There is no need to recall her expressions of maternal indignation upon learning of the step I had taken. Upon the pacification of Canada, my dear Harry requested leave of absence and dutifully visited Virginia. He wrote describing his reception at home and the splendid entertainments my mother arranged in honor of her son. Castlewood, which she had not inhabited since our departure for Europe, was reopened to our colonial friends; and the friend of Wolfe and the soldier of Quebec was received by all our acquaintances with every becoming honor. Some dismal quarrels, to be sure, ensued because my brother persisted in maintaining his friendship with Colonel Washington of Mount Vernon, whose praises Harry never grew tired of singing. Indeed, I grant the gentleman every virtue; and in the struggles that terminated so fatally for England a few years prior, I can admire as well as his warmest supporters General Washington's glorious constancy and success.

If these battles between Harry and our mother were frequent, as he described in his letters, I wondered why he should remain at home. One reason naturally suggested itself to my mind, which I scarcely liked to communicate to Mrs. Warrington; for we had both discussed our dear little Hetty's romantic attachment to my brother, wondering why he had never discovered it. Needless to say, my gentleman had found some young lady at home more to his taste than our dear Hester, thus accounting for his prolonged stay in Virginia.

Soon thereafter, a letter arrived containing not a full confession but an admission of this interesting fact. A person was described, unnamed—a Being of all beauty and perfection, typical of young ladies under similar circumstances. My wife asked to see the letter; I could not help showing it and handed it to her with a very sad face. To my surprise, she read it without exhibiting any corresponding sorrow.

"I have thought of this before, my love," I said. "I feel with you for your disappointment regarding poor Hetty."

"Ah! poor Hetty," said Theo, looking down at the carpet.

"It would never have worked," I remarked.

"No—they would not have been happy," sighed Theo.

"How strange he never should have found out her secret!" I continued.

She looked me full in the face with an odd expression. "Pray, what does that look mean?" I asked.

"Nothing, my dear—nothing! Only I am not surprised!" said Theo, blushing.

"What," I asked, "can there be another?"

"I am sure I never said so, George," the lady replied hurriedly. "But if Hetty has overcome her childish folly, ought we not all to be glad? Do you gentlemen suppose that you are the only ones to fall in love and eventually tire of it?"

"What!" I exclaimed, with a strange commotion within my mind. "Do you mean to tell me, Theo, that you ever cared for anyone but me?"

"Oh, George," she whimpered, "when I was at school, there was—one of the boys at Doctor Backhouse's school who sat in the loft next to us; and I thought he had lovely eyes, and I was so shocked when I recognized him behind the counter at Mr. Grigg's, the mercer's, when I went to buy a cloak for baby, and I wanted to tell you, my dear, but I didn't know how!"

I went to see this creature with the lovely eyes, having had my wife describe the fellow's dress to me. I observed a little bandy-legged wretch in a blue camlet coat, with his red hair tied with a dirty ribbon, about whom I forbore generously even to reproach my wife; nor will she ever know that I have looked at the fellow,

Little Miles arrived in this world mere days after the birth of the gracious Prince who now commands a regiment. Great illuminations lit the sky and cannons roared in salute to the Royal George's arrival; multitudes flocked to gaze upon him as he lay behind a gilt railing at the Palace, watched over by noble nurses. In stark contrast, few guards stood by the cradle of our little Prince. No courtiers, no faithful retainers paid homage to it, save for our trusty Gumbo and kind Molly, who loved and admired the little heir of my poverty with a loyalty that matched the deepest desires of our hearts.

Why was our boy not named George, like the other paragon just mentioned, and like his father? Instead, I gave him the name of a little scapegrace from my family—a name borne by many generations of Warringtons alike. Yet, my poor little Miles's love and kindness touched me profoundly at a time when affection from my own blood was scarce. Theo and I agreed then that our child should be named after that single, bright little friend of my paternal race.

We wrote to inform our royal parents of the auspicious event, and boldly inserted the child's birth notice in the *Daily Advertiser*, specifying the location as Church Street, Lambeth, where he was born.

"My dear," Aunt Bernstein replied in her letter to my announcement, "how could you point out to all the world that you live in such a trough as that in which you have buried yourself? I kiss the little mamma, and send a remembrance for the child."

This remembrance was a fine silk coverlet edged with lace fit for a prince. It was hardly practical—the cost of the lace alone would have served us far better—but Theo and Molly were utterly delighted. Consequently, my eldest son's cradle boasted a cover as fine as any nobleman's.

Good Dr. Heberden visited my wife several times to ensure everything proceeded smoothly. He recommended a local surgeon who took charge of her case; fortunately, my dear patient required little care beyond what our landlady and her trusted attendant could readily provide.

Soon, our humble precinct was once again adorned with the gilded spectacle of Lady Castlewood's carriage wheels. She arrived bearing a pot of jelly, which she believed Theo might enjoy—no doubt a delicacy previously served at one of her ladyship's grand banquets. She regaled us with tales of the court ceremonies and the splendor surrounding the birth of the august heir to the crown.

Our good Mr. Johnson happened to drop by on one of those days when Lady Countess's carriage blazed up to our little gate. He was visibly struck by her magnificence and offered her some bows that were more respectful than graceful. She addressed me as "cousin" with great affability and assisted in transferring the gift of jelly from her silver dish into our crockery pan with much benignity.

Dr. Heberden tasted the sweetmeat and pronounced it excellent.

"The great, sir," he remarked, "are fortunate in every way. They can engage the most skilled practitioners of the culinary arts and assemble the most amiable wits around their tables. If, as you suggest—and judging by the dish, your hypothesis is plausible—this sweetmeat has already graced His Lordship's table, it has done so in good company. It has quivered beneath the eyes of celebrated beauties, been tasted by ruby lips, and shared the attention of a distinguished assembly amidst fruits, tarts, and creams, which I make no doubt were equally delicious."

Thus speaking, the good doctor consumed a considerable portion of Lady Castlewood's benefaction. However, regarding the epithet "delicious," I must confess that my poor wife, after sampling the jelly, set it aside as unpalatable. Molly, throwing up her head, declared flatly that it was moldy.

At least, my boy enjoyed the privilege of having an Earl's daughter for his godmother. This honor was bestowed by his cousin, our poor Lady Maria, whose kindness and attention to both mother and infant were beyond praise. Having lost her own solitary chance for maternal happiness, she yearned over our child in a manner deeply moving to behold.

Captain Miles is indeed a magnificent gentleman, and his uniforms in the Prince's Hussars are as splendid as any worn by a soldier of fashion. Yet, we trust his heart is too good, and his nature too truly genteel, for him not to feel gratitude when he recalls that his own infant limbs were once dressed in some of the small garments prepared for the poor player's child.

Sampson christened him in the very chapel in Southwark where our marriage ceremony had taken place. Never were the words of the Prayer Book read more beautifully or impressively than by the celebrant of that service. There was an exception, however, at the conclusion, when his voice failed him, and he, along with the rest of the tiny congregation, found themselves wiping away tears.

"Mr. Garrick himself, sir," said Hagan afterward, "could not have read those words so nobly. I am certain no innocent soul ever entered the world accompanied by wishes and benedictions more tender and sincere."

Now, I have not yet explained how the Captain acquired his name of Miles. A couple of days prior to the christening, when it was still intended that our firstborn should bear his father's name, a light patter of horsehooves galloped up to our gate. Who should pull the bell but young Miles, our cousin? I feared he had disobeyed his parents on that undutiful journey.

"You know," he exclaimed breathlessly, "Cousin Harry gave me my little horse, and I simply cannot help liking you because you are so like Harry. Besides, everyone at home keeps talking about you, and it's a shame! So, I've brought my whistle and the coral beads my godmamma, Lady Suckling, gave me for your little boy. And since you're so poor, Cousin George, here is my gold moidore. It's worth ever so much, and it's no use to me anyway, because I'm not allowed to spend it, you know."

We lifted the boy up to Theo in her room (he climbed the stairs proudly in his little tramping boots, of which he was very fond). Theo kissed him warmly and thanked him; and that moidore has remained in her purse from that day forward.

Later, my mother, writing through her usual ambassador, informed me of her royal surprise and displeasure upon learning that my son had been christened Miles—a name unknown, at least, in the Esmond family. I chose not to explain the reason at the time. But when, in later years, I recounted to Madam Esmond how my boy received his name, I saw a tear roll down her wrinkled cheek. I learned subsequently that she had asked Gumbo countless questions about the boy who gave his name to our Miles—our *Miles Gloriosus* of Pall Mall, Valenciennes, Almack's, and Brighton.

How strange it seems, reflecting on our earliest days at home—when Harry and I were so notoriously undutiful to our tutor—that Mr. Esmond Warrington of Virginia should eventually rise to become "Bearleader" himself. My mother, when we reunited, could never bring herself to speak directly of this turbulent period of my life. Instead, she would allude vaguely to "that terrible time, my love, which I can't bear to think of," or "those dreadful years when there was difference between us." Even though my former pupil, a worthy and grateful man, shipped several barrels of the very liquor that built his great fortune to Jamestown for me, Madam Esmond referred to him only as "your friend in England" or "your wealthy Lambeth friend," never using his actual name. Furthermore, she never tasted a drop of his beer. We brewed our own at Warrington Manor, yet our excellent Mr. Foker faithfully shipped two butts of his finest ale to Ipswich annually. His son is a fashionable young sprig who married an earl's daughter, while the father remains a very worthy and kind gentleman. It was sheer luck in making his acquaintance that secured for me some of the most welcome guineas I ever possessed in my life.

Yet, it was not merely the sum of money that mattered to me, but the dignity and hope attached to the office of Governor—a post I assumed voluntarily. Mr. Foker's brewery, located near Pedlar's Acre in Lambeth (though the site has since changed), employed a surgeon who attended my wife during her confinement; this physician was a Bavarian originally named Voelker. I presume Mr. Lance, the surgeon, introduced the brewer to my name and history. The worthy doctor would smoke many a pipe of Virginia tobacco in my garden, developing a deep affection for my family. He brought his patron to my house. When Mr. Foker discovered I had a smattering of his language and could sing *"Prinz Eugen the noble Ritter"*—a song my grandfather brought home from the Marlborough wars—the German conceived a profound friendship for me. His lady placed her carriage and chair at Mrs. Warrington's disposal; his little daughter developed a prodigious fondness for our baby (and to do him justice, the Captain, who appears as ugly now as ever he wore a queue, was as beautiful as an infant): indeed, he was the very image of the Squire at thirty, according to everyone. Moreover, his son and heir, Master Foker, having been severely maltreated at Westminster School due to his father's trade as a brewer, begged if I would take charge of him. The parents agreed, paying me a generous sum for supervising his education.

Mr. Foker was a shrewd businessman, and since he and his family genuinely cared for me and mine, I laid all my financial affairs before him without reservation. Upon hearing my statement, he expressed delight, saying it augmented the respect and regard he already held for me. He laughed at our tales of aid from noble relatives—my aunt's coverlet, Lady Castlewood's mouldy jelly, Lady Warrington's contemptuous treatment—but he wept openly over the story of little Miles's moidore. Regarding Sampson and Hagan, he declared, "I vow, dey shall have so much beer als ever dey can drink." He sent his wife to call upon Lady Maria, treating her with the utmost respect and obsequiousness whenever she visited. It was with Mr. Foker that Lady Maria stayed when Hagan traveled to Dublin to complete his college terms; the good brewer's purse also supplied our friend's needs and outfitted him.

Once Mr. Foker fully understood my situation, he spoke of me with enthusiastic terms, as if my conduct displayed extraordinary virtue. I have mentioned how my mother saved money for Harry, leaving both of us in debt. Yet, when Harry spent money, he did so believing it to be his own. Madam Esmond could never comprehend that she was dealing harshly with me; the money was paid and gone, and that was the end of it. By the end of 1662, Harry sent over a considerable remittance for his promotion, reminding me that he was in my debt and urging me to draw on his agents if needed. He did not realize the magnitude of my need, nor how my small capital had been consumed.

To accept my brother's money would have delayed his promotion, so I naturally refrained from drawing on him, though I admit I was tempted. Similarly, knowing General Lambert's limited means, I hesitated to impoverish him by requesting supplies. These simple acts of forbearance, my worthy brewer considered, were instances of exalted virtue. Consequently, he wrote privately to my brother in America, lauding me and my wife as the most admirable humans, and called upon Madame de Bernstein—who never informed me of his visit, though I noticed she subsequently treated us with singular respect and gentleness, surprising me in someone I viewed as selfish and worldly. Later, I asked him how he gained access to the Baroness. He laughed: "De Baroness!" he said. "I knew de Baron when he was a waiter at Munich, and I was a brewer's apprentice." Perhaps our family had best avoid curiosity regarding our uncle the Baron.

Thus, the portion of my life that ought to have been most melancholy was rendered pleasant by numerous friends, happy circumstances, and strokes of fortunate chance. The bear I led was a docile little cub, dancing readily to my piping. It was far better to lead him than to loiter outside booksellers' shops or wait upon the whims of managers. During our exile, my wife and I spent many delightful evenings with these kind friends and benefactors. We were not devoid of intellectual enjoyment either; Mrs. Foker and Mrs. Warrington sang beautifully together, and sometimes, when in the mood, I read my own play *Pocahontas* to this friendly audience, in a manner Mr. Foker delighted to acknowledge was superior to Hagan's recitation.

Following the minor escapade of Miles Warrington, junior, I saw little of him and heard rarely of my paternal relatives. Sir Miles was assiduous at court (as I believe he would have been at Nero's), and I laughed one day when Mr. Foker told me he had heard on the Exchange that they intended to make my uncle a "Beer." "A Beer?" I asked in wonder. "Can't you understand the word when I say it?" replied the testy old gentleman. "Well, well, a Lord!" Sir Miles indeed was the obedient, humble servant of the Minister, whoever he might be. I was surprised he did not speak English with a Scottish accent during the First Duke's brief reign. I once saw him and his wife returning from court when Mrs. Claypool was presented to Her Majesty upon her marriage. I carried my little boy on my shoulder. My uncle and aunt stared resolutely at me from their gilded coach window; the footmen looked blank over their nosegays. Had I worn the Fairy's cap and been invisible, my father's brother could not have passed me with less notice.

We did not avail ourselves often of Lady Castlewood's peculiar invitation to drink tea and supper with her when no other company was present. Old Van den Bosch, however shrewd his intellect and great his skill in wealth-making, was not amusing in conversation, except to his daughter, who discussed household and city matters, bullion and bearer bonds, raising and selling livestock, with equal keenness and acumen. Neither was my Lord Castlewood frequently at home, nor greatly missed by his wife when absent, nor particularly at ease in the old father's company. The Countess confided all this to my wife in her simple manner. "Guess," she said, "my lord and father don't pull well together. Guess my lord is always wanting money, and father keeps the key of the box, which is quite right. If he could have his fingers on all our money, my lord would soon squander it, and then what becomes of our noble family? We pay everything, my dear (except gambling debts, which we won't tolerate). We pay cooks, horse dealers, wine merchants, tailors, and everyone—and lucky for them too—I reckon my lord wouldn't pay them! We always ensure he has a guinea in his pocket so he goes out like a real nobleman. What that man owes us, what he did before we arrived—gracious goodness only knows! Me and father do our best to make him respectable, but it's no easy job. Law! he'd melt the plate, only father keeps the key of the strong-room. And when we go to Castlewood, my father travels with me, armed, as do the others."

"Gracious heavens!" cried my wife, "does your ladyship mean to say you suspect your own husband of a desire to..."

"To what? Oh, no, nothing, of course! And I would trust our brother Will with untold money, wouldn't I? As much as I'd trust a cat with a cream-pot! Tell you, my dear, it's not all pleasure being a woman of rank and fashion. If I have bought a countess's coronet, I have paid a heavy price for it—that I have!"

Indeed, my Lord Castlewood paid a large price to free his estate from encumbrances, furnish his houses and stables, and discharge his debts. He became the slave of the little wife and her father. No wonder the old man's society was unpleasant to the poor victim, causing him to gladly slip away from his own fine house to feast at the club when he had

The era of my hardship had finally ended; I was no longer forced to wrestle with poverty. The death of my little kinsman shifted my worldly prospects entirely, making me the next heir to a substantial estate. With my uncle and aunt unlikely to produce further offspring, the succession seemed secure. "The woman is capable of committing any crime to disappoint you," Sampson had vowed regarding my aunt's potential interference, yet Lady Warrington proved guiltless of such treachery. Instead, crushed by the blow that had fallen upon them, she allowed her religious advisors to convince her that this tragedy was a divine chastisement requiring submission to the Heavenly Will. "While your son lived, your heart was turned away from the better world," her clergyman admonished her, noting her excessive focus on earthly status. "For your son's advantage, you desired rank and title. You sought and might have secured an earthly coronet. But what use is it now to one who has but a few years left on earth—what importance holds it compared to the heavenly crown for which you are now an assured candidate?"

The accident caused considerable sensation. Within the chapels of the enthusiastic sect toward which she increasingly leaned following her son's death, numerous sermons were delivered referencing the event. Far be it from me to judge the path taken by the bereaved mother, or to view with anything less than respect and sympathy any grieving soul seeking refuge from sin, sorrow, and disappointment. Lady Warrington even attempted a reconciliation with me. A year after her loss, while visiting London, she signaled her desire to meet, and I duly waited upon her. There, in her customary didactic manner, she delivered a homily on my station and hers. She marveled at Heaven's decree, lamenting how dreadfully her poor child's disobedience had been punished—a disobedience that resulted in my gain. (It transpired that my poor little man had defied orders and gone out hunting with his gun without his mother's knowledge.) She expressed hope that, should I eventually inherit the property—though the Warringtons were, thank Heaven, a long-lived lineage, save for my own father, whose life was cut short by the excesses of a poorly regulated youth—I would alter my current lifestyle. She prayed that I would sever ties with my unworthy associates, abandon the horrid theatre and its licentious patrons, and turn toward quarters offering only peace and the sacred duties she feared I had neglected. Her exhortations were laden with Scriptural language, which I choose not to replicate. Upon my departure, she presented me with a packet of sermons for Mrs. Warrington and a small book of hymns by Miss Dora, who later became a prominent figure in that society alongside her mother. After the dowager's death at Bath three years prior, Miss Dora married the young Mr. Juffles, a celebrated preacher.

Though the poor lady had forgiven me, she could never bear the sight of our boy. Following the loss of our second child, my aunt and her daughter eagerly flocked to the suffering mother, even inviting us to their home. However, my uncle was now almost daily in our house. He would sit for hours gazing at our boy, bringing him endless toys and sweets, begging that the child address him as "Godpapa." When we felt our own grief—which, even twenty-five years later, strikes me as acutely as on the day we first lost our little one—I understood how to commiserate with him. Yet my wife could pity him before experiencing the loss of her own child; my own heart, initially more selfish, learned true pity only through experience, leading to my reconciliation with my uncle via my little baby's coffin.

The poor man dispatched his coach to follow our humble funeral procession. Afterwards, he took out little Miles, who prattled incessantly, forgetting any sorrow in the delight of his new black clothes and the pleasures of a carriage ride. How the innocent chatter of the child stabbed the mother's heart! Could we ever wish for that wound to heal? I know her face so well that to this day I can discern moments when she thinks of that lost little one. It is not merely grief for a parting so long ago, but a communion with a beloved soul in Heaven.

Soon thereafter, we returned to our bright lodgings in Bloomsbury. My young bear, whom I could no longer lead, had formed a prodigious friendship with Charley and was sent to the Chartreux School, where his friend ensured he received no more beatings than were beneficial. Thanks undoubtedly to the excellence of his private tutor, he excelled and maintained a good standing. He loved the school so dearly that he declared, should he ever have a son, it would be sent to that same seminary.

I could no longer lead my bear for another reason: I had other business to attend to. Fully reconciled to us, I believe, for Mr. Miles's sake, my uncle—who was such an obsequious supporter of the Government that I wonder the Minister ever bestowed favors upon him, being perfectly sure of his vote—used his influence in behalf of his nephew and heir. Consequently, I had the honor of being gazetted as one of His Majesty's Commissioners for licensing hackney coaches, a post I fulfilled, I trust, with credit until a quarrel with the Minister (to be recounted in its proper place) deprived me of it. I also took my degree at the Temple and appeared in Westminster Hall in my gown and wig. Furthermore, this year, my good friend Mr. Foker having business in Paris, I accompanied him there. I was received *bras ouverts* by my dear American preserver, Monsieur de Florac, who introduced me to his noble family and the polished society of the capital, though I lacked the leisure to frequent them extensively. I possessed too much spirit to desert my kind patron Foker, whose acquaintances lay chiefly among the bourgeoisie, particularly Monsieur Santerre, a great brewer of Paris who has since distinguished himself in blood rather than beer. Mr. F. required my services as an interpreter, and I was too glad to command them, eager to repay some of the kindness he had shown me. Meanwhile, our ladies resided at Mr. Foker's new villa in Wimbledon, delighted by the "Parisian letters" I sent them through my distinguished friend Mr. Hume, then of the Embassy, which were subsequently published in a neat volume.

While I tranquilly discharged my minor official duties in London, those troubles commenced that were to culminate in the great separation between our colonies and the mother country. When Mr. Grenville proposed his stamp duties, I told my wife the bill would create immense discontent at home, for we were ever anxious to extract as much as possible from England while paying back as little as feasible. Yet, I certainly never anticipated the prodigious anger the scheme ignited. It was with us as with families or individuals: a pretext is given for a quarrel, while the real cause lies in long-standing bickerings and previous animosities. Many foolish exactions, petty tyrannies, the habitual insolence of Englishmen toward all foreigners and colonists, and the natural spirit of men outraged by our imperious domineering nature set Britain and her colonies to quarrel. The astonishing blunders of the system adopted in England brought the dispute to an issue, which I, for one, do not intend to deplore. Had I been in Virginia instead of London, 'tis very possible I would have sided with the province, perhaps driven into revolt by mere opposition to that resolute mistress of Castlewood, who might have pushed me into rebellion just as England drove the colonies. Was the Stamp Act the cause of the revolution? It was a tax no greater than that cheerfully paid in England. Ten years earlier, when the French threatened our territory and we implored succor from home, would the colonies have rebelled at the payment of this tax? Do not most people consider the tax-gatherer the natural enemy? Against the British in America were arrayed thousands of high-spirited and brave souls, yet thousands more found profit in the quarrel or harbored private reasons for joining it. I protest I do not know now whether my motives were selfish or patriotic, which side held the right, or whether both did not. I am certain we in England had nothing to do but fight the battle out; and having lost the game, I vow and believe that, after the initial natural soreness, the loser felt no rancor.

What prompted brother Hal to write home from Virginia, which he seemed exceedingly reluctant to leave, with such flaming patriotic letters? My kind, best brother was always led by someone: by me when we were together (he held such an idea of my wit and wisdom that if I said the day was fine, he would ponder the observation as though it were a saying of the Seven Sages), or by some other wiseacre when I was away. Who inspired these fiery epistles, this boisterous patriotism sent to us in London? "He is rebelling against Madam Esmond," I surmised. "He is led by some colonial person—perhaps by that lady," my wife hinted. Who "that lady" was, Hal never told us; indeed, he beseeched me never to allude to the delicate subject in my letters to him, writing, "for Madam wishes to see 'em all, and I wish to say nothing about you know what until the proper moment." No affection could be greater than that displayed in his letters. When he heard (from the informant I have mentioned) that amidst my own extreme straits I had retained no more than a hundred pounds from his aunt's legacy, he considered mortgaging the estate he had just purchased; he had more than

When my mother learned that I had accepted a position within the household, I suspect she was hardly delighted. Perhaps she withheld her financial support deliberately, hoping to starve me into submission and force my return to Virginia, where I would remain dependent upon her. Between us, until the day of her death, there was never a single explanation regarding the monetary dispute. She simply severed my allowance; I said not a word, yet managed to survive without her aid. I never heard her express remorse for her injustice, nor acknowledge her error, unless indirectly through Harry's private communications to me. In later years, when we met, her behavior softened greatly, accompanied by an unusual respect and affection toward my wife, Madam Esmond. Perhaps she intended for me to interpret this as a tacit admission of wrongdoing, though she offered no formal apology, nor did I demand one.

Harry was provided for—a fact I could not begrudge—so all of my mother's savings and frugal schemes ultimately benefited me, as her heir. Once, a few guineas would have been more valuable to me than hundreds arriving when I had no need. But when Madam Esmond and I finally reconciled, the era of necessity had long passed. I possessed abundance; I had no cause to scheme for ignoble savings or resent the doctor's fees. She could only add to my wealth. Undoubtedly, she suffered internally knowing that my children had gone hungry due to her pride, and that strangers had relieved the very needs her own heart had refused to address. Was she prouder than I? A gentle word of explanation between us might have secured a reconciliation years prior, but neither of us spoke.

When I commit a wrong and discover it later, I love to seek pardon—not merely to appease others, but to satisfy my own pride, effectively apologizing to myself for failing to meet my own standards. Consequently, I rarely debase myself to perform a mean act. Consider the men whose entire lives consist of lies, schemes, and subterfuges. What company do they keep when alone? Do they wear masks even in their privacy, deceiving their own consciences? If I choose to overlook an injury, it is likely not born of a Christian spirit of forgiveness, but because I can afford to remit the debt and disdain settling accounts. I have known a few rare souls in my life—perhaps ones I tested—who forgive without effort, growing naturally like a plant in fertile soil. I claim to know how to remit debts, but not how to truly forgive. I wonder if proud men are proud of their pride itself.

Thus, I showed no sign of submission toward my parent in Virginia, and we remained estranged for years, exchanging only occasional brief notes concerning births or trivialities. Following our initial troubles in America regarding the Stamp Act, similar tribulations befell me in London. Although I sided with the Tories in that controversy—and indeed, on the losing side of most debates—I believed firmly that the Imperial Government held the right to tax the colonies. Yet, at the height of the dispute, I published a sharp letter to a member of the House of Burgesses in Virginia. In it, I addressed the habitual insolence of the mother country with such freedom and expressed sentiments so offensive to those in power that I was stripped of my post as hackney-coach licenser. This terrified my uncle, who could never tolerate anyone in disgrace. He had developed a deep affection for my wife and my young son, but viewed me personally with a pitying contempt that always amused me. He possessed a natural scorn for poverty and a corresponding love for success and fortune. Any deviation from conventional thinking shocked him; truth-telling turned him pale.

Yet, despite the terrible blows I dealt him, he retained some warmth of heart and genuine love for kin. Spite of my actions, he continued to visit Theo and the child (and me, offering a mournful recognition upon meeting). Broken-hearted by my outspokenness, he refused to cease speaking to me as he had during our first rift, instead regarding me as a melancholy, lost creature beyond worldly help or hope. Never mind; I had to devise a new plan for life. Fortunately, the repayment of Harry's debt to me at this critical moment enabled me to sustain myself for months, perhaps years. "O strange fatuity of youth!" I often remark. How could we dare to be so poor and so undeterred?

At this time, His Majesty's royal uncle, the Duke of Cumberland, fell ill and died suddenly. Remarkably, his death altered my fortunes significantly. Poor Sir Miles Warrington never missed a court ceremony he could attend. He was present at every drawing-room, christening, ball, and funeral. Whether a prince or princess was ailing, his carriage waited at their door. From Leicester Fields to Carlton House, from Gunnersbury onward, nothing satisfied him now but attending the stout Duke's funeral. Standing bare-headed in the rain, he contracted a severe cold and throat inflammation. Before I had even heard of his illness, a lawyer visited my lodgings in Bloomsbury and hailed me as Sir George Warrington.

With party strife and fear of the future now behind us, we laid the deceased gentleman beside his young son in the family churchyard, where so many of his lineage rest. Little Miles and I were the chief mourners. An obsequious tenantry bowed and curtseyed before us, striving to conciliate my honor and worship. The dowager and her daughter soon retired to Bath, leaving me and my family to take possession of the house, of which I have been master for thirty years. "Be not too eager, O my son!" I advise. "Have but a little patience, and I too shall sleep beneath those yew trees, while the people toss their caps for Sir Miles."

The chronicles of a prosperous country life are easily and briefly recounted. The steward's books record rents paid and forgiven, crops raised and rotated, visitors received and their durations of stay, the pensioners my wife supported and how they were cared for, the year I served as sheriff, and the frequency of hound meetings. All these details reside in our house journals, available to any heir willing to consult them. We could not afford the magnificent mansion in Hill Street occupied by my predecessor, so we selected a smaller residence, in which we nonetheless expended considerable sums. We displayed less ostentation than my uncle, lacking the grand liveries, equipages, and silverware for which he was famed. However, our beer was stronger, and my wife's charities were perhaps more generous than those of the Dowager Lady Warrington. No doubt she believed there was no harm in "spoiling the Philistines"; for she charged us exorbitantly for the goods she left behind in our country house, and I submitted to most of her extortions with unutterable good humor. What value she placed on the potted plants in her greenhouses! What price she set on that dreadful old spinet in her drawing-room! And the framed pieces of worsted work created by the accomplished Dora and the lovely Flora—if they had been masterpieces of Titian or Van Dyck, surely the Dowager Lady could not have valued them higher. Despite paying so generously, and being, I may say without boasting, far kinder to the poor than she ever was, we initially carried the worst reputation in the county, surrounded by tales told to my discredit.

I hoped perhaps to inherit my uncle's seat in Parliament alongside his lands, but I discovered, somehow, that I was considered a person of dangerously radical opinions. I would not bribe voters, nor would I coerce my own tenants to support me in the 1668 election. A gentleman descended from Whitehall bearing a pocketbook full of banknotes approached me, revealing that I stood no chance against my competitor.

"Bon Dieu!" Now that we were secure financially, with obedient tenants bowing as we attended church, driving in our great family coach drawn by four fat horses to visit friends or neighboring towns, did we not often regret our former poverty and the dear little cottage at Lambeth, where Want constantly prowled the threshold? Did I not long to resume bear-leading, vowing that translating for booksellers was not such arduous drudgery? Upon returning to London, we made sentimental pilgrimages to our old haunts. My wife undoubtedly embraced every landlady we knew. You may be sure we invited all the friends of our youth to share the comforts of our new home. The Reverend Mr. Hagan and his lady visited us repeatedly. His appearance in the pulpit at B------, where he preached beautifully in our estimation, caused quite a scandal there. Sampson also came, another unfortunate Levite, welcomed as long as he chose to stay among us. Mr. Johnson spoke of visiting but delayed us once or twice. I suppose our house grew dull. I admit I would remain silent for days, fearing my moodiness often tried the sweetest-tempered woman in the world living with me. I cared little for field sports. Killing one partridge seemed indistinguishable from killing another; I marveled how men could spend days pursuing such slaughter. Their fox-hunting stories began at four o'clock, after the tablecloth was removed, and lasted until supper. I sat silent, listening; day after day, I fell asleep. No wonder I lacked popularity among my company.

What confession is this? Here was the storm passed, the rocks avoided, the ship safely in port, yet the sailor not entirely content? Was Susan, whom I sighed for during the voyage, not the beauty I expected to find? First, Susan and the entire family can inspect William's logbook themselves, so, madam, I shall not record my secrets here. No, Susan, I never kept secrets from you. I never

An appeal such as our mother's would have melted hearts far harder than ours; yet, despite her pleas, we delayed our journey to Virginia. We discussed hiring the entire cabin of the *Young Rachel* for the voyage, but nature intervened. Our youngest child fell ill, rendering the sea travel too perilous, and naturally, the mother could not be parted from her sickening offspring. Consequently, the *Young Rachel* sailed that year without us.

Another year brought further obstacles. First, my beloved wife suffered her initial attack of the gout—a condition that occupied me greatly at the time, though it eventually sharpened my wit and enlivened my spirits. Then came a sorrow far heavier: tragic news from Jamaica. Two years after establishing ourselves at the Manor, our dear General returned from his governorship. While he had accumulated worldly goods, he bore a loss for which no wealth could provide solace; indeed, he showed little desire to remain in the West Indies thereafter. My Theodore's poor mother—the most tender and affectionate friend I have ever known—had died abroad of fever. Her final regret was that she would not live to see her children and us prosper.

"She sees us, though we do not see her," her husband said softly, "and she thanks you, George, for having been good to her children."

We feared he would not long survive to join her. Love for her had been the sole purpose of his existence; to be away from her felt like ceasing to live. It was heartbreaking to watch the grieving widower sit among us. My wife, through her demeanor and countless gentle gestures, constantly reminded the bereaved father of his lost mother. We offered whatever comfort we could, but above all, little Hetty became his chief support. She had declined several advantageous matches in the island, and upon returning to England, the heir of Lord Wrotham threw himself at her feet. Yet, Hetty declared she preferred staying with her father. As long as he needed her, she desired no husband.

"Nay," we suggested when a suitor was proposed, "let the General stay six months with us at the Manor, and you can have him at Oakhurst for the other half-year."

Hetty firmly rejected this, stating her father could never endure Oakhurst again since her mother's passing. She would marry neither for a coronet nor for money. The General, observing this, noted gravely that the girl showed no desire to wed, perhaps due to an unspoken disappointment in her youth. Respecting her silence, we remained quiet as well.

Meanwhile, my brother Lambert had secured a college living near Winchester and a wife to adorn his parsonage. We professed a moderate liking for this lady, welcoming her warmly when she visited, though our affection was limited. Mrs. Jack, however, viewed Hetty's determined celibacy differently. She was a chatty woman, prone to speaking her mind freely and taking pride in inflicting pain upon her friends.

"My dear Sir George," she exclaimed cheerfully, "I have often told our dear Theo that I wouldn't have a pretty sister in my house to make tea for Jack while I was upstairs, or to hover in the kitchen or nursery, or to dress neatly when I was baking pies or watching the children. I trust Jack implicitly, of course! I'd like to see him look at another woman, truly! And so I feel about Jemima, though they rarely coincide in my house when I am upstairs—that I promise you! And thus I warned my sister Warrington."

"Am I to understand," the General asked sharply, "that you have done my Lady Warrington the favor of warning her against her sister, my daughter Miss Hester?"

"Yes, pa, of course!" cried Mrs. Jack, blushing and becoming voluble. "A duty is a duty, and a woman is a woman, and a man is a man, as I know very well. Don't tell me! He is a man. Every man is a man, with all his sanctified airs!"

"You yourself have a married sister, with whom you were staying when my son Jack first met you?" the General pressed.

"Yes, everyone knows that! I have been as good a mother to her children as anyone could wish!"

"And am I to gather from your conversation that your charms tempted your sister's husband?"

"Law, General! Who ever said such a thing?" Mrs. Jack cried, turning red.

"Do you not perceive, my dear madam, that it is you who have insinuated as much—not only regarding yourself, but concerning my two daughters?"

"Never, never, never, as I'm a Christian woman!" she retorted, offended. "It is cruel of you to say so. And I do believe a sister is best out of the house! And since Theo's time is approaching, I warn her, that is all."

"Have you discovered, my good madam, whether my poor Hetty has stolen any spoons?" the General countered. "When I came to breakfast this morning, my daughter was alone, yet there must have been a dozen pieces of silver on the table."

"Law, sir! Who accused the poor dear?" Mrs. Jack shrieked, flouncing toward the door in tears. "Did I ever accuse her? If I did, may I drop dead on this hearth-rug immediately! And I am not used to being spoken to thus. Me and Jack have both noticed it, and I have done my duty!" With that, she stormed out.

"And has the woman dared to tell you this, my child?" the General asked when Theo entered the tea-table, looking slightly pale.

"She tells me every day since she arrived," Theo replied sadly. "She enters my dressing-room saying, 'I wouldn't have a sister prowling about my nursery!' She visits my nursery with the same remark. Ah, how pleasant it is to have amiable and well-bred relatives, I say."

"Your poor mother was spared this woman," the General groaned.

"Our mother would have improved her," Theo added, kissing him.

"Yes, dear." Both rose for prayer.

Yet, loving one's relatives is not always easy, nor is living with neighbors always amusing. The following day, Jack Lambert's behavior confirmed his wife's version of events. He was sulky, though not dignified; angry, yet still hungry. He delivered a sermon to us that was utterly foolish. Meanwhile, little Miles, clad once more in sable, sat beside his grandfather, holding the kind old man's hand.

Should he stay to manage the house during our Virginian trip? The housekeeper must submit fully to Hetty's command. The butler's keys belonged to him, for Gumbo—who had happily married Mrs. Molly and populated a cottage in my park with numerous tawny Gumbos—was to accompany us to Virginia.

Under the care of our General and his daughter, we departed, traveling to London and then to Bristol. Our obliging agent there vowed prayers for our prosperity and claimed that children as beautiful as ours (for we now had an infant to join Miles) had never been seen aboard a ship before. The voyage was uneventful. How strange the sensation of landing at Richmond! A coach and a host of negroes awaited us. Nearby, a gentleman on horseback, accompanied by servants in our livery, dismounted and rushed forward to embrace us. Both of us were deeply charmed to see our dearest Hal. He rode with us to our mother's door. There she stood on the steps to welcome us, and Theo knelt to ask her blessing.

Harry rode in the coach only as far as the house, refusing to enter lest he "spoil the sport." "She sees me," he admitted, "and we are fairly good friends; but Fanny and she are best apart. There is no love lost between them, I can promise you. Come to the Tavern tomorrow, George, when you are free. And I shall have the honor of presenting her sister to Theo. It was only because I happened to be in town yesterday that I learned of the ship's arrival; I waited specifically to see you. I have sent a negro boy home to my wife, and she will arrive soon to pay her respects to my Lady Warrington." After this brief greeting, Harry leaped from the carriage, leaving us to greet our mother alone.

Though I had encountered fine company since departing, and though Theo and I had paid our respects to the King and Queen at St. James's, none compared in stateliness to the man who welcomed us. Raising my wife from her knees, he embraced her and led her into the house. It was a simple, wood-framed structure with a surrounding gallery, typical of Virginian homes; yet, if it had been a palace housing a little empress, our reception could not have been more courteous. Old Nathan still served as major-domo, surrounded by a dozen kind black faces grinning in welcome. Some whose names I knew as a child had grown into sturdy youths; others, once dark-haired, now wore snow-white hair; and some, born since my departure, peered curiously from doorways with large eyes and bare feet. "I'm little Sip, Master George!" cried one. "I'm Dinah, Sir George!" called another. "I'm Master Miles's boy!" shouted a small lad in new liveried boots. Before dusk, the entire household had found pretexts to pass before us, bowing and smiling.

That evening, my Lady Warrington hosted the local gentry with perfect grace and good humor. I shook hands with a few old acquaintances—or rather, old enemies, though I was no longer a naughty prodig

What a perverse law of fate seems to dictate that I should forever find myself in the minority? If a motion were brought forward to surrender this realm to the Pretender of Rome or the Grand Turk, submitting it to a new sovereign's religion, it would surely pass—except for my vote, which I would undoubtedly cast against it. Back home in Virginia, I found myself disagreeing with everyone, as was my custom. To the Patriots, I was branded a Tory; to the Tories, I was immediately labeled a dangerous Republican. The times were utterly out of joint. Oh, cursed spite! Within a year of arriving in Virginia, I longed to be back by the banks of the Waveney. Yet, the situation was so fraught with peril that I could not abandon my mother—a solitary woman—to face potential war and disaster. Neither would she leave the country at such a critical juncture, nor should a man of spirit desert his post.

At His Excellency's table, amidst the generous flow of claret, many of the well-disposed agreed on a specific course of action, raising countless brimming bumpers in a toast. No, it was *statue signum, signifer*! We Cavaliers would rally around it. At these moments, our Governor spoke with the courage of the bravest. Among all my acquaintances in Virginia, Madam Esmond stood out as the most consistent. Many of our gentlefolk had gathered in Williamsburg, proposing to treat Her Excellency, the Governor's lady, to a ball when news of the Boston Port Bill reached us. Immediately, the House of Burgesses adopted an indignant protest against Parliament's measure, decreeing a solemn day of fasting, humiliation, and prayer across the country to avert the calamity of civil war.

Meanwhile, the invitations to my Lady Dunmore had already been issued and accepted. The gentlemen resolved that their ball would proceed on the appointed evening, with sackcloth and ashes reserved for several days afterward.

"A ball!" exclaimed Madam Esmond. "I am to attend a gathering hosted by rebels who intend to publicly insult His Majesty a week hence! I would rather die!" She wrote to the stewards stating that, given the dangerous state of the country, she could not possibly attend.

Her surprise was immense the following time she rode abroad in her chair. She was greeted by a hundred voices, white and black alike, shouting, "Huzzah, Madam!" and "Heaven bless your ladyship!" They clearly interpreted her refusal to go to the ball as an act of supreme patriotism. To ensure there was no misunderstanding, she leaned out of the chair and cried out, "God save the King" as loudly as she could. The crowd echoed her, crying "God save the King." Indeed, everyone shouted "God save the King" in those days.

On the night of the entertainment, my poor Harry, serving as a Burgess and one of the hosts, donned his scarlet uniform of Wolfe's regiment—which he would so soon exchange for another color—and departed with Madam Fanny to the ball. My Lady Warrington and her humble servant, being strangers in the land and essentially English, were permitted by Madam to attend the assembly from which she, of course, absented herself. I had the honor of dancing a country dance with the lady of Mount Vernon, whom I found a most lively, pretty, and amiable partner. However, I must note that my wife's praises of her were received with a very grim acceptance by my mother when Lady Warrington recounted the evening's events. Could not Sir George Warrington have danced with My Lady Dunmore or her daughters, or anyone else but Mrs. Washington? To be sure, the Colonel thought so highly of himself and his wife that he undoubtedly considered her the grandest lady in the room, despite remembering him as a road surveyor earning a mere guinea a day! Well, indeed! There was no measuring the pride of these provincial upstarts. As for this gentleman, my Lord Dunmore's partiality for him had evidently turned his head.

Whether Mr. Washington possessed similar pride remains unknown to me; I only know that my good mother never could be induced to love him or anything associated with him. She was no less displeased with him for attending the ball than she was for his conduct three days later, when the day of fasting and humiliation arrived. He attended the service performed by our new clergyman. She invited Mr. Belman to dinner that day along with various colonial authorities. The clergyman excused himself. Madam Esmond tossed her head and said he might do as he pleased. She staged a magnificent dinner, lighting her house brightly while the rest of the city remained shrouded in darkness and gloom. She requested Mr. Hardy, one of His Excellency's aides-de-camp, to sing "God Save the King." The people listening from the street outside believed it was part of a religious service Madam was celebrating. Then, however, she called for "Britons, Strike Home!" The simple young gentleman, fresh from Europe, began to perform it, when suddenly a great yell erupted from the street. A large stone, hurled by a rebellious hand, crashed into the punch bowl before me, scattering its contents across our dining room.

My mother walked to the window, undaunted. I can still see her rigid little figure standing there, head thrown high, frilled arms outstretched, with the twinkling stars as her backdrop, singing in chorus, "Britons, strike home! Strike home!" The crowd before the palings shouted and roared, "Silence! For shame! Go back!" but she refused to retreat. "Fling more stones, if you dare!" cried the brave little lady. More might have followed, but some gentlemen emerging from the Raley Tavern intervened between her and the mob. "You mustn't insult a lady," said a voice I believe I recognized. "Hurrah, Colonel! Hurrah, Captain! God bless your honour!" cried the people in the street. Thus, the enemies were pacified.

My mother, insisting the disturbance was entirely over, attempted to have Mr. Hardy sing another song. He gave a sickly grin and replied, "I really did not like to sing to such accompaniments," and the concert for that evening concluded. Though I must admit, some scoundrels returned that night, frightening my poor wife almost out of her wits and smashing every single window in the front of our tenement. "Britons, Strike Home!" proved to be too much; Madam should have contented herself with "God Save the King."

Militia was drilled, bullets cast, and supplies of ammunition prepared. Cunning plans were devised to thwart royal ordinances. Yet, to be sure, "God Save the King" was the cry everywhere. In response to my objections against the gentleman-Patriots—"Why, you are scheming for separation; you are inviting the inevitable wrath of the greatest power on earth!"—the answer was invariably, "We mean no separation at all! We yield to no men in loyalty; we glory in the name of Britons," and so on, ad infinitum. Powder barrels were stacked in the cellar, the fuse laid, but Mr. Fawkes persisted in his dutiful petitions to King and Parliament, intending no harm whatsoever.

It is true that when I spoke of our country's power, I imagined she would exert it fully. I thought she would not expect to conquer three million fellow-Britons on their own soil with merely a few battalions, six generals from Bond Street, and a handful of mercenaries hired from Germany. As if we needed to insult the thirteen colonies alongside subduing them, we must attack them with these hordes of Hessians and murderers from Indian wigwams. Was our great quarrel not to be fought without foreign aid and these defenders? Ah! It is easy now, since we are worsted, to glance over the map of the vast empire torn from us and declare how we ought not to have lost it. Long Island should have annihilated Washington's army; he should never have emerged from Valley Forge except as a prisoner. The South was ours after the Battle of Camden, but for the inconceivable meddling of the Commander-in-Chief at New York, who paralyzed the efforts of the only capable British general who appeared during the war, sending him into that miserable dead-end at Yorktown, from which he could only emerge defeated and captured. Oh, for just one more week! One more day! One more hour of darkness or light!

Reading over our American campaigns from their unfortunate beginning to their inglorious end, now that we can see the enemy's movements and conditions as clearly as our own, I imagine we can discern how an advance or a march might have placed enemies in our power who lacked the means to resist, thereby changing the entire outcome of the struggle. But it was ordained by Heaven, and for the good, as we can now have no doubt, of both empires, that the great Western Republic should separate from us. The gallant soldiers who fought on her side, their indomitable and heroic chief above all, earned the glory of facing and overcoming not only veteran soldiers well-provisioned and hardened to war, but also wretchedness, cold, hunger, internal dissension, and treason within their own ranks. All must have collapsed under such pressure but for the pure, unquenchable flame of patriotism that burned eternally in the bosom of that heroic leader.

What constancy! What magnanimity! What surprising persistence against fortune! Washington before the enemy was no better nor braver than hundreds who fought with him or against him (who has not heard the repeated sneers against "Fabius" indulged by

In the days when America was still a possession of Britain, one of its primary functions served as a sanctuary for our nation's outcasts. Alongside the convicts and indentured servants we shipped across the Atlantic, we dispatched to these distant shores the "black sheep" of society—scapegraces, younger sons with no inheritance, and those driven away by debt, despair, and relentless bailiffs. Much as Captain Cook presented English fauna to the indigenous peoples of his newly charted islands, we took care to export samples of our domestic failures to the colonies, leaving them to struggle and multiply in the wilderness as best they could.

I confess I played a small role in this exodus regarding the worthy Mr. Hagan, husband of my kinswoman. When England offered him no livelihood, we gladly shipped him to Virginia, providing him with a colonial pulpit to occupy his hands and mind. In Virginia, he conducted himself as a brave and honest gentleman, fulfilling his duties to both his congregation and his King with thoroughness, thereby earning credit for my own patronage. My cousin, Madam Theo, always defended my decision, insisting that my actions were guided by the highest principles of morality and honor. Yet, if I had truly believed in his worthiness, would I have kept him at home? Would I have chosen him and his wife to serve our own parish? I fear not. While I never doubted Cousin Maria's sincerity in seeking redemption, I admit I felt a secret relief watching her work out her salvation in the untamed frontier. I acknowledge this as a flaw born of pride.

Twice, when I needed support most desperately, this same Maria offered me her sympathy and friendship. She endured her own trials with courage and soothed the sorrows of others with admirable devotion. And yet, despite her virtues, I and several of my kin looked down upon her. Oh, the shame of our arrogance!

Maria was not alone among our family in being banished to the American wilderness. After exhausting his ability to borrow, steal, and cheat within the bounds of polite society, the Honourable William Esquire was relocated to New York. His noble brother and royal master expressed a heartfelt desire to never see him again. When the troubles erupted, news reached us of William's exploits in his new home. Deceit and malice seemed to precede him wherever he traveled. Lord Dunmore informed me that Mr. William had boldly declared to the public that our estate of Castlewood belonged solely to his brother's pleasure; that his father, acting out of kindness toward Madam Esmond (Lord Dunmore's half-sister), had granted her the estate for life; and that William was currently negotiating with the current Lord Castlewood to purchase the remainder interest.

We possessed the original deed of gift locked safely in our strongroom at Castlewood, and it was duly registered in Williamsburg. On paper, we were secure.
