However, intent is often more powerful than law. Hal and I resolved that upon meeting Mr. William, we would demand confirmation of this fabrication. The reaction of Madam Esmond upon hearing such slander needs no elaboration.

"What!" she cried, her voice trembling. "My father, the Marquis of Esmond, was a liar, and I am a cheat?" She feared he intended to seize her son's property upon her death. She prepared letters to Lord Castlewood in England and even to His Majesty at St. James's, only to be stopped by my assurance that Mr. William's reputation for falsehood was notorious among all who knew him. We dared not hope he would suddenly become consistent enough to tell the truth.

Soon, we heard of him as a leading Loyalist in New York, rising rapidly from Captain to Major of a volunteer corps. They sent addresses to sympathetic figures throughout the colonies, proclaiming their readiness to die for the Mother Country. Unable to remain in a house where windows were effectively boarded up by the atmosphere of distrust, Madam Esmond abandoned our rented home in Williamsburg. My family retreated to Richmond, which was likewise deserted by the members of the dissolved Assembly. Captain Hal and his wife returned promptly to their plantation. I, however, torn between annoyance at the unfolding chaos and a sense of duty, split my time between my own household and that of our Governor, who eagerly sought my counsel and companionship.

Political divisions ran deep, yet no personal enmity existed between the factions. Even after the dissolution of our House of Assembly—which adjourned to a local tavern to hold that famous meeting where the idea of a continental congress was reportedly conceived—the staunchest opponents of the Governor remained his friends, shared his hospitality, and joined him in leisurely excursions. Once the session ended, the gentry dispersed to their counties to hold their own meetings. With assemblies abruptly dissolved in most other provinces as well, a consensus formed: a general congress must be convened. Philadelphia, the largest and most significant city on the continent, was chosen as the venue. These celebrated conferences began, serving merely as the angry prelude to war.

We still sang "God Save the King"; we still submitted humble petitions to the throne. Yet, fate intervened. I had ridden out to visit my brother Harry at Fanny's Mount earlier that day. His new plantation lay near ours, separated only by the Rappahannock and bordering the Mattaponey River. That afternoon, I rode out again. A groom informed me that Master Harry had departed toward Willis's Ordinary. Thinking nothing of it, I followed.

Not far from Willis's, emerging from the tree line onto a clearing, I encountered Captain Hal on horseback. Surrounding him were thirty or forty countrymen, armed with a motley array of weapons: pikes, scythes, fowling-pieces, and muskets. The Captain, flanked by two or three promising young officers, was drilling the men.

As I approached, a peculiar expression crossed Hal's face.

"Present arms!" he commanded. The makeshift army attempted the salute with varying degrees of success.

"Captain Cade," Hal announced, gesturing to me, "this is my brother, Sir George Warrington."

"As a relation of yours, Colonel," replied the man addressed as Captain, "the gentleman is welcome." He extended his hand.

"And—a true friend to Virginia," Hal added, his face flushing slightly.

"Yes, please God, gentlemen!" I exclaimed. The regiment responded with a hearty hurrah for the Colonel and his brother.

Once the drill concluded, the officers and men moved to Willis's Ordinary for refreshments. Colonel Hal declined to join them that afternoon. Instead, we trotted homeward together.

"So, Hal, the cat is out of the bag!" I remarked.

He shot me a hard look. "I suspect there are wilder cats in this bag than you imagine. It must come to this, George. Listen, you must not tell Madam."

"Good heavens!" I cried. "Do you mean that with fellows like those I saw yonder, you intend to make a stand against the greatest nation and finest army in the world?"

"I guess we'll get an awful whipping," Hal admitted grimly. "That is the fact. But then, George," he continued, flashing his characteristic sweet, kind smile, "we are young. Perhaps a few whippings will do us good. Won't it do us good, Dolly, you old slut?" He playfully touched the whip against an old mongrel dog trotting alongside him.

I refrained from pressing him further on the British side of the argument, the perspective I deemed superior. He had grown accustomed to dismissing my reasoning with, "All mighty well, brother, you speak as an Englishman and have cast your lot with your country, just as I have with mine." Against such logic, there is no defense; the dispute ultimately resolves itself in battle, where the stronger party prevails. Who held the right in the wars of the last century? The King or Parliament? History showed that whoever stood atop the hill was considered right, and generally proved more humane in victory than the Cavaliers might have been had they won. Consider, moreover, the horror of a Tory triumph in America: the imagined ropes, scaffolds, and noble heads laid low. It is a strange paradox, I confess: I stood on the Loyalist side, yet I wished for the Whigs to win.

My brother Hal, conversely, distinguished himself greatly with his regiment while maintaining absolute decorum toward the enemy. "The officers of the British army," he often stated, "are gentlemen. At least, I have not heard they have changed much since my time. There may be scoundrels and ruffians among the enemy's ranks; I dare say we could find similar characters among our own. Our business is to defeat His Majesty's forces, not to insult them; any rascal can hurl names."

It was because of a name given to my brother by Mr. Lee—one that many of his rough horsemen failed to comprehend—that Harry was frequently dubbed "Chevalier Baird" within the Continental Army. He was, indeed, a knight without fear or reproach.

When I asked, "What could such people as those you were drilling possibly do against the British army?" Hal answered with unshakeable confidence. "They can beat them, Mr. George. That is what they can do."

"Great heavens!" I protested. "Do you mean that with your company of Wolfe's, you would hesitate to attack five hundred of them?"

"With my company of the 67th, I would go anywhere," Hal replied firmly. "Agreed with you, I know more of soldiering than they do at this precise moment. But place me on that open ground where you found us, armed as

Once my visit to my brother concluded, and my wife and young child had safely returned to our maternal home in Richmond, I turned my attention to the Governor, who was residing at his country estate near Williamsburg. My purpose was to discuss the ominous preparations for war that were unfolding not merely within our province, but across the entire continent.

Gentlemen whose names history has since immortalized were being appointed as delegates to the General Congress convening in Philadelphia. In Massachusetts, the populace and royal troops stood face-to-face in open hostility. In Maryland and Pennsylvania, we naively believed a loyal spirit still prevailed. In the Carolinas and Georgia, the Mother Country could count on steadfast adherence, with the vast majority of inhabitants firmly behind the Crown. It was inconceivable to us that Virginia, the cradle of loyalty, would forsake its ancient allegiance. Though our provincial forces were scant, our gentry prided themselves on their lineage tracing back to the Cavaliers of old. Surrounding the Governor were swarms of loud, confident Loyalists, eager for the moment to draw their swords and sweep aside the "rascally rebels."

Naturally, in these gatherings, I was subjected to harsh criticism of my poor brother, Harry. Everyone agreed—perhaps justifiably—that his wife had led him toward these extreme anti-British views, which he had recently espoused. Furthermore, it was whispered that he was infatuated with the gentleman of Mount Vernon, whose radical opinions Harry invariably followed, pushing him daily deeper into a course of desperate resistance.

"This is your friend," the circle around His Excellency remarked, gesturing toward me. "This is the man you favored, who enjoyed your special confidence and repeatedly shared your hospitality!" Much of this was true. Yet, what some eager Loyalists termed treachery was, in reality, evidence of a profound desire by Mr. Washington and other gentlemen to remain faithful to the Crown. They sought to exhaust every final chance for reconciliation before risking the terrifying alternative of revolt and separation.

"Let traitors arm, and villains draw the parricidal sword!" cried the Loyalists. "We, at least, will remain faithful. The unconquerable power of England shall be exerted, and the misguided, ungrateful provinces punished until they return to obedience." With what fervor we toasted His Majesty's health after our banquets! We vowed to die in defense of his rights and welcomed the prospect of a prince from his Royal house returning to govern our ancient dominions.

In light of my own and my mother's unwavering loyalty, my brother's benighted conduct should surely be forgiven. Could it not be too late to secure him by offering a commanding post? Should I not intercede with the Governor, known for his influence over Harry? Thus, in our Williamsburg councils, we swung wildly between exaltation, triumph, fury against the rebels, anxious expectation of reinforcements, and deep-seated doubt and gloom.

I promised to plead with my brother and wrote to him, albeit with little hope, reiterating arguments I had used countless times in conversation. My mother also employed her authority, though I feared little gain from her efforts. True to her habits, she assailed him with Scripture verses supporting her view, threatening divine punishment for disobedience to constituted authorities. She cited the example of his elder brother and, I fear, hinted at his subservience to his wife—the weakest possible argument in such a debate. While she did not show me her letter, fearing I might criticize its tone, she displayed his reply. To my astonishment, Mr. Hal had countered her scriptural barrage with dozens of texts of his own, composing a response that was neat, temperate, and elegantly written—a feat I suspected even his wife could not have achieved. Indeed, he had enlisted Mr. Belman, the clergyman of New Richmond, a zealous Whig who preached and printed sermons against Hagan (a figure of our faction). In this intellectual skirmish, Belman undoubtedly emerged victorious.

My exhortations proved as futile as my mother's. Harry ignored my letters entirely. Pressed further by government friends, I wrote imprudently promising to visit him at Fanny's Mount within the week. Upon arrival, however, I found only my sister. She greeted me warmly but informed me that Harry had departed for the countryside, traveling far toward the Blue Mountains to inspect horses, with no indication of when—or if—he would return. At that moment, hope vanished.

"My dear," I said, "judging by the signs of the times, the fuse lit over these years must soon ignite. Harry is riding away. God knows to what end."

"The Lord prosper the righteous cause, Sir George," she replied.

"Amen, with all my heart. You and he speak as Americans; I as an Englishman. Tell him from me that should anything happen to our mother, I possess sufficient means in England for myself and mine. I shall resign all our lands here in Virginia to him."

"You don't mean that, George?" she exclaimed, her eyes brightening.

"Well, to be sure, it is but right and fair," she added shortly thereafter. "Why should you, the eldest by mere hours, hold everything—a palace and estates in England, this plantation here, the title, and children—while my poor Harry gets nothing? Yet, it is generous of you. Leastways, it is handsome and proper. I didn't expect it of you; and unlike your mother, you do not act thus. Give my love to sister Theo!" She offered me her cheek to kiss before I rode away.

With a woman like Fanny guiding him, how could I possibly hope to convert my brother? Disappointed in my mission, I returned to the Governor. We agreed it was time to arm earnestly and prepare for the inevitable shock. His Excellency and his court officials were agitated, nearly savage in their denunciation of the "wicked Whigs," shouting praises of Old England. Yet, beneath the rhetoric, they all hungered for the day when opposing factions would meet hand-to-hand, allowing them to ride down these "wicked Whigs."

Leaving the Governor, I accepted his thanks in council and pledged my utmost effort to raise a body of men to defend the Crown. Thus began the corps later known as the Westmoreland Defenders, of which I was honored to be appointed Colonel, tasked with leading them once they appeared in the field. Naturally, the announcement in the Government Gazette drew officers quickly, but recruits were slow to enlist and swift to vanish. Nevertheless, friend Hagan eagerly volunteered as chaplain. Madam Esmond presented our colors and traveled the countryside recruiting volunteers. Most diligent of all was my old tutor, little Mr. Dempster, a former Jacobite exile from Scotland who had fought for Prince Charles. He ventured specifically into the Carolinas, among the descendants of his banished comrades who still wore the white cockade of the Pretender, finding them fiercely loyal to the Crown.

Meanwhile, Harry's horse-hunting expedition carried him far beyond the Blue Mountains, eventually leading him to Annapolis, Baltimore, and finally Philadelphia, where a second General Congress was seated alongside our Virginian delegates from the previous year.

As the almanacs recorded, history unfolded rapidly. Lexington had fallen; the first shots of the war that would culminate in our nation's independence had been fired. We continued to protest our loyalty to His Majesty, declaring our resolve to "die or be free." Some twenty thousand loyal petitioners assembled around Boston with arms and cannons, seized from government stores. Benedict Arnold had launched his infamous career with the daring, burglary-like capture of two forts. Three generals from Bond Street, accompanied by a large reinforcement, sailed to assist General Gage in his precarious position at Boston. Soon, the armies clashed. Our British generals began their careers of conquest and pacification with the glorious blunder of Breed's Hill. Fortifying themselves, acknowledging they lacked the strength for another decisive victory, the two armies watched one another while Congress deliberated in Philadelphia on who should command the confederated colonial forces.

History records the fortunate choice that fell upon the nation. Among the Virginian regiments marching to join the new General-in-Chief was one commanded by Henry Esmond Warrington, Esq., formerly a Captain in His Majesty's service. Riding beside him was his little wife, whose bravery we would later hear much of. I rejoiced that she had already left Virginia; had she remained after her husband's departure, our mother would inevitably have joined the fray to fight alongside them. I was grateful, at least, that this terrifying episode of civil war was spared to our family's direct experience.

The rush of farmers and country folk flowed almost exclusively toward the new northern army. Our people felt flattered by the selection of a Virginian gentleman for the principal command. With a surge of wrath and fury, the provinces learned of the blood spilled at Lexington. Men shouted denunciations against the cruelty and wantonness of the "bloody British invader." The invader was merely doing his duty, met by armed men wishing to prevent him from seizing what was theirs. Yet, anger clouds judgment; the colonists had chosen their side. Driven by a natural spirit and ardor, they were determined to test their strength against the arrogant Mother Country. Breed's Hill became a symbolic mountain visible to all the American continent, crowned with Liberty, Victory, and Glory on its flaming summit.

These dreaded troops could be withstood by farmers and ploughmen; these famous officers could be outgeneraled by doctors, lawyers, and civilians! Granted that Britons could conquer the world, here were their children who could match—and conquer—their parents! Indeed, I cannot decide which deserves the palm, either for

In Which We Both Fight and Run Away

What utility is there in a Colonel without a regiment? The Governor and Council, who had staged such a grand display of gratitude for endowing me with one, were nowhere to be seen, hiding aboard their ships while committing occasional acts of piracy and arson on the shore. My Lord Dunmore's Black allies had already driven away those of his own blood; furthermore, we heard rumors that he had dispatched envoys to the Southern Indians, promising them vast numbers to come down and "tomahawk our people into good behavior."

"And these are to be our allies!" I remarked to my mother, exchanging ominous glances with her. The memory flashed back with ghastly clarity to that savage whose face had glared over mine, and whose knife had been at my throat until Florac struck him down on Braddock's Field. We put our house at Castlewood into as good a state of defense as we could devise; yet, in truth, it was the Red Men and the Blacks we feared far more than the Rebels. I had never witnessed my mother lose her courage except once, and that was when she recounted the details of our father's death during an Indian raid more than forty years prior. One night, seeing vague figures moving before our house, the good lady refused all persuasion that they were anything other than savages. She sank to her knees, crying out, "The Lord have mercy upon us! The Indians—the Indians!"

My Lord's Negro allies eventually vanished onto their ships or wherever they could find pay and plunder; however, the painted heroes from the South never materialized. Yet, I confess to looking at my mother's grey head, my wife's brown hair, and our little one's golden ringlets with a horrible pang of doubt, fearing they might become victims of this ruffian war. Indeed, it was we who fought with such weapons and enlisted these allies! Though I dare not, so to speak, set myself up as an interpreter of Providence and point out the special finger of Heaven—as many are wont to do—I would say that our employment of these Indians and the German mercenaries brought their own retribution upon us in this war. In the field, where the mercenaries were attacked by the Provincials, they yielded, and it was triumphing over them that so greatly raised the spirit of the Continental army. Moreover, the murder of one woman (Miss McCrea) by a half-dozen drunken Indians did more harm to the Royal cause than the loss of a battle or the destruction of entire regiments.

Once the Indian panic subsided, Madam Esmond's courage returned. She began to be seriously, and not unjustly, uneasy regarding the danger I ran myself, and the peril I brought upon others by remaining in Virginia.

"What harm can they do me?" she asked. "I am but a poor woman. If I have one son, a Colonel without a regiment, I have another with a couple of hundred Continentals behind him in Mr. Washington's camp. If the Royalists come, they will let me off for your sake; if the rebels appear, I shall have Harry's passport. I do not wish, sir, I do not like that your delicate wife and this dear little baby should be here, merely increasing the risk to all of us! We must remove them to Boston or New York. Do not talk about defending me! Who would think of hurting a poor, harmless, old woman? If the rebels come, I shall shelter behind Mrs. Fanny's petticoats, and I shall be much safer without you in the house than within it." She spoke partly because it was reasonable, but more so because she wished to get me and my family out of danger. Regardless of the threat, she felt determined to remain in the land where her father was buried and where she was born. She was living backward, so to speak. She had seen the new generation, blessed them, and bid them farewell. She belonged to the past, to old days and memories.

While we debated the Boston scheme, news arrived that the British had evacuated that unfortunate city altogether. They had never dared to attack Mr. Washington in his camp at Cambridge, even though he lay there for many months without powder, at our mercy. Instead, they waited until he procured ammunition, seized and fortified Dorchester Heights—which commanded the town—forcing the entire British army and colony to beat a retreat. That the King's troops won the Battle of Bunker's Hill is no more certain than that they beat the French at Blenheim; yet throughout the war, their chiefs seemed constantly afraid of assaulting entrenched Continentals afterward. Otherwise, why, from July to March, hesitate to strike an almost defenseless enemy? Why the hesitation at Long Island when the Continental army was in our grasp? Why that astonishing timidity—of Howe before Valley Forge, where the relics of a force starving, sickening, and in rags could scarcely man the lines they held before a great, victorious, and perfectly appointed army?

As the hopes and fears of the contending parties rose and fell, it was curious to observe the altered tone of the partisans on either side. When news of the evacuation of Boston reached us in the countryside, every little Whig in the neighborhood made his bow to Madam and advised her to submit speedily. She did not carry her loyalty quite so openly as before, nor did she flaunt her flag in the faces of the public, but she never swerved. Every night and morning in private, poor Hagan prayed for the Royal Family in our own household. On Sundays, any neighbors were welcome to attend the service, where my mother acted as a very emphatic clerk, delivering the prayer for the High Court of Parliament under our most religious and gracious King with great vigor. The brave Hagan was a parson without a living, just as I was a Militia Colonel without a regiment. Hagan had continued to pray stoutly for King George in Williamsburg long after His Excellency, our Governor, had fled. But on coming to church one Sunday to perform his duty, he found a corporal's guard at the church door telling him that the Committee of Safety had installed another divine in his place and requesting he keep a quiet tongue in his head. He told the men to "lead him before their chiefs" (our honest friend always loved tall words and tragic attitudes). Accordingly, he was marched through the streets to the Capitol, accompanied by a chorus of white and colored blackguards at the skirts of his gown. He had an interview with Messrs. Henry and the new State officers, confronting the robbers, as he termed them, in their den. Naturally, he intended to make an heroic speech before these gentlemen (he was one of many men who perhaps would have no objection to becoming martyrs so that they might be roasted *coram populo*, or tortured in a full house). However, Mr. Henry was determined to give him no such chance. After keeping Hagan three or four hours waiting in an anteroom in the company of negroes, when the worthy divine entered the new chief magistrate's room with an undaunted mien and began a prepared speech—"Sir, by what authority am I, a minister of the----"—"Mr. Hagan," interrupted the other, "I am too busy to listen to speeches. And as for King George, he has henceforth no more authority in this country than King Nebuchadnezzar. Mind you that, and hold your tongue, if you please! Stick to King John, sir, and King Macbeth; and if you will send round your benefit tickets, all the Assembly shall come and hear you. Did you ever see Mr. Hagan on the boards when you were in London, General?" And, so saying, Henry turned around upon Mr. Washington's second in command, General Lee, who had now come into Virginia upon State affairs. Our shamefaced good Hagan was hustled out of the room, reddening and almost crying with shame. Following this event, we decided that Hagan's ministrations were best confined to us in the country, removing the worthy pastor from his restive lambs in the city.

The selection of Virginians for the very highest civil and military appointments of the new government bribed and flattered many of our leading people. These individuals, otherwise—and but for the outrageous conduct of our government—might have remained faithful to the Crown and made good head against the rising rebellion. Yet, although we Loyalists were gagged and muzzled, although the Capitol was in the hands of the Whigs, and our vaunted levies of loyal recruits were mostly Falstaff's regiments, the faithful still kept intelligence with one another in the colony and with our neighbors. Although we did not rise, although we ran away, and although, in examinations before committees and justices, some of our frightened people gave themselves Republican airs and vowed perdition to kings and nobles, we knew each other pretty well. According as the chances were more or less favorable to us, and the master more or less hard, we concealed our colors, showed our colors, half-showed our colors, or outright apostatized for the nonce, crying, "Down with King George!" Our negroes bore about, from house to house, all sorts of messages and tokens. Endless underhand plots and schemes were engaged in by those who could not afford the light. Once the battle was over, the neutrals came and joined the winning side, shouting as loudly as the patriots. The runaways were not counted. Would any man tell me that the signers and ardent well-wishers of the Declaration of Independence were not in a minority of the nation, and that the minority did not win? We knew that a portion of the defeated army of Massachusetts was about to make an important expedition southward, upon the success of which the very greatest hopes were founded. I,

It has always struck me—though I speak humbly before the judgment of seasoned military men—that the earthworks of Breed's Hill served as the backbone of the Continental Army throughout our entire war. The slaughter inflicted upon us from behind those lines was so devastating, and the resolve of the enemy so unyielding, that the British commanders thereafter held a profound respect for American barricades. Indeed, if the foe were merely firing from behind a row of blankets, certain of our own generals hesitated to force the breach. At White Plains, when Washington's army stood on the brink of total annihilation, we later learned that our victorious troops were halted in their tracks by a mere barrier of cornstalks and straw.

Another chance was granted us, lasting through a harsh winter. During this season, the dwindling, starved, and disheartened forces of Congress lay helpless under our grasp, while the magnanimous Lord Howe left the infamous encampment of Valley Forge untouched. Instead, his great, brave, and impeccably equipped army fiddled, gambled, and feasted in the comfort of Philadelphia. By the order of his compatriots, triumphal arches were raised, medieval-style tournaments staged in mocking celebration, and beautiful ladies draped wreaths around the necks of this "clement" chief, offering fantastical mottos declaring his laurels immortal. Yet, why have my ungrateful countrymen in America never erected a statue to this general? For in their entire army, there was no officer who fought their battles better; no one who retrieved their errors with such adroitness; no one who ensured their defeats caused them so little harm. Most importantly, when fortune shifted and the stronger force inevitably gained the upper hand, who displayed such tireless tenderness, patience, and complacency in helping the defeated opponent rise again?

Ah! Recall the eighteen years prior, and the fiery young warrior England dispatched to fight her adversary across the American continent. Imagine him forever pacing the defenses behind which the enemy lay sheltered, sleepless and eager by day and night. Consumed by fierce wrath and longing, he never closed his eye, intent on watching, winding his track with an untiring scent that panted and hungered for blood and battle. He prowled through midnight forests or climbed silent precipices before dawn, until his great heart was nearly worn out, until finally the foe revealed himself. Then he sprang upon him, grappled with him, and, dying, slew him! Think of Wolfe at Quebec, and then listen to Howe's fiddles as he sat smiling among the dancers in Philadelphia.

A favorite scheme among our ministers at home and some of our American generals was to establish a communication between Canada and New York, hoping thereby to sever New England from the neighboring colonies, overpower them in detail, and force submission. Burgoyne was entrusted with this plan, setting forth from Quebec with confident promises of success. His march began in military splendor; the trumpets of his proclamations blew ahead, bidding the colonists remember the immense power of England and summoning the misguided rebels to lay down their arms. He brought a formidable English force, an army of German veterans, a dreadful band of Indian warriors, and a brilliant train of artillery. It was expected that the populace along his route would rally to the Royal cause.

Initially, the Continental force opposing him was small, and Washington's army was further weakened by the withdrawal of troops hurriedly sent to meet this Canadian invasion. This was the moment when Washington's diminished army should have been struck down; but a greater Power willed otherwise. Nor am I, for one, inclined to regret the ultimate termination of the war. Looking back over the game now, how clear the blunders appear on the losing side! From start to finish, we were perpetually arriving too late. Our supplies and reinforcements from home arrived too late. Our troops struggled, and our succor reached them too late. Our fleet appeared off Yorktown just too late, after Cornwallis had already surrendered. A way of escape was opened to Burgoyne, but he resolved upon retreat too late.

I have heard discomfited officers in later days claim infallibly how a different wind would have saved America for us; how we must have destroyed the French fleet but for a few tempests; how, once, twice, thrice, but for the fall of night, Washington and his army would have fallen into our power. Who has not, while reading history, speculated upon the "has-beens" and "might-have-beens" of the world? I take my tattered old map-book from the shelf and gaze upon the board where the great contest was played. I marvel at the curious chances that lost it. Putting aside any idle talk regarding the relative bravery of the two nations, I cannot help but see that we held the best cards, yet we lost the game.

I confess the sport possessed a considerable fascination for me, stirring my languid blood. My brother Hal, settled on his plantation in Virginia, was perfectly content with the pursuits he found there. The company of country neighbors sufficed him; he never tired of tending his crops and people, catching fish, shooting ducks, hunting in the woods, or enjoying his rubber and supper. Happy Hal, in his great barn-like house, beneath his roomy porches, with dogs lying at his feet; his friends, the Virginian Will Wimbles, staying at free quarters in his mansion; his negroes fat, lazy, and ragged; his shrewd little wife ruling over them and her husband—who always obeyed her implicitly while alive, and was quickly consoled when she died! I say "happy," though his lot would have been intolerable to me. Wife, friends, plantation, and town life in Richmond (which succeeded to the honor of being the capital when our Province became a State)—how happy is he whose foot fits the shoe Fortune provides!

My income was five times greater, my house in England larger, built of brick and faced with freestone. My wife—would I have changed her for any other woman in the world? My children—well, I am content with Lady Warrington's opinion of them. But with all these plums, peaches, and rich fruits pouring from Plenty's horn into my lap, I fear I have been but an ingrate. Hodge, my gatekeeper, who shares his bread and scrap of bacon with a family as large as my own, seems to enjoy his meal as much as I do, despite Mrs. Molly preparing her finest dishes and Mr. Gumbo uncorking the choicest bottles from the cellar. Alas! Sweetmeats have lost their flavor for me, though they may still delight my young ones in the nursery, and the perfume of claret grows stale upon old noses.

Our parson has preached many sermons to me, and perhaps I cared little for them when he first began. Do you remember, honest friend? (Sure he does, for he has retold the story over the bottle as often as he has delivered his sermons, and my Lady Warrington pretends she has never heard it.) I say, Joe Blake, you remember full well, with advantage, that October evening when we scrambled up an embrasure at Fort Clinton, and a clubbed musket would have dashed these valuable brains out, had not Joe's sword whipped my rebellious countryman through the gizzard. Joe wore a red coat in those days—the uniform of the brave Sixty-Third, whose leader, the bold Sill, fell pierced with many wounds beside him. He exchanged his red coat for black and my pulpit. His doctrines are sound, and his sermons short. We read the newspapers together over our wine. Not two months ago, we read our old friend Howe's glorious deed of the first of June. We were told how the noble Rawdon, who fought with us at Fort Clinton, had joined the Duke of York; and today, His Royal Highness is in full retreat before Pichegru, while he and my son Miles have taken Valenciennes for nothing! Ah, Parson! Would you not like to don your old Sixty-Third coat? (Though I doubt Mrs. Blake could ever make the buttons and buttonholes meet again over your large frame.)

The boys were acting a play with my militia sword. Oh, that I were young again, Mr. Blake! That I had not the gout in my toe! I would saddle Rocinante and ride back into the world, feel the pulses beat again, and play a little of life's glorious game! The last "hit" I witnessed was gallantly won by our side; though it is true that even in this party, the Americans won the rubber. Our people gained only the ground they stood on, capturing and destroying the guns, stores, and ships, while our efforts at rescue were too late to prevent the catastrophe looming over Burgoyne's unfortunate army.

After one of those inevitable delays that retarded our plans and weakened the blows our chiefs intended to deliver, an expedition was launched from New York at the close of September, 1777. Had it advanced merely a fortnight earlier, it might have saved the doomed force of Burgoyne. *Sed Dis aliter visum* (But Fate saw otherwise). The delay here was not Sir Henry Clinton's fault, who could not leave his city unprotected, but rather the winds and weather that delayed the arrival of reinforcements we had long awaited from England. The fleet bringing them carried long, fond letters from home, containing the very latest news of the children under the care of their good Aunt Hetty and their grandfather. The mother's heart yearned toward the absent young ones. She made me no reproaches; but I could read her importunities in her anxious eyes,

Need I describe, young folks, the sheer delight of returning home, and the radiant joy of a mother welcoming her entire brood beneath her protective wings? It was written plainly across her face and acknowledged with humble gratitude on her knees. While our house was spacious enough to accommodate everyone, Aunt Hetty refused to remain within its walls. She stated quite frankly that relinquishing her role as the primary caregiver to her eldest children—who had been under her care for nearly three years—inflicted a pain too severe to bear. She simply could not stand the thought of sharing her maternal duties and settling for a secondary position among them. Consequently, she and her father departed for a residence in Bury St. Edmunds, not far from us. There, in private, she indulged her eldest nephew and niece with undivided affection.

It was the year following our return that Mr. B, the Jamaican planter, passed away. In his will, he left Aunt Hetty half of his fortune. Only then did I learn, for the first time, of the worthy gentleman's deep infatuation with her during their time in Jamaica. Though she had initially refused his advances, he demonstrated his unwavering constancy by providing for her after his death. Heaven knows how much of Aunt Hetty's inheritance Monsieur Miles has already consumed! Consider the cost of his military commission and outfit, his ostentatious uniforms, his gambling debts, and his various small ventures in the Minories. Do you truly suppose, sirrah, that I am unaware of human nature? What is the price of taverns like Pall Mall, *petits soupers*, and even moderate gaming at the Cocoa-Tree? Can a gentleman sustain all such pleasures on the five hundred pounds per annum that I currently allow him? Aunt Hetty has firmly resolved to remain a spinster. "I made a vow never to marry until I could find a man as good as my dear father," she declared. "And I never did, Sir George. Not half as good, my dearest Theo. Let Sir George put that in his pipe and smoke it."

Yet, when the good General finally died—calm, full of years, and glad to depart—I believe it was my wife who shed the most tears. "I weep because I fear I did not love him enough," confessed this tender soul. In contrast, Hetty barely concealed her composure, at least outwardly and in front of others. However, she speaks of him constantly as if he were still alive, recalling his witty remarks and his gentle, kind manner toward his children. When she does so, she brightens, looking momentarily like a girl again. She sits cheerfully gazing at the memorial slab in church that records his name and virtues, and for once, she tells no lies.

I had occasionally fancied that my brother Hal, for whom Hetty harbored a youthful passion, always retained a hold on her heart. Ten years prior, when he visited us, I confided in him about this childish romance of Hetty's. I admitted, with some reservation, that I hoped he might ask her to replace Mrs. Fanny, who had recently passed away. Regarding Fanny, my wife (ever prone to viewing herself as a miserable sinner) frequently reproached herself, claiming she did not regret Fanny's loss sufficiently. Upon arriving, Hal was plunged in grief over Fanny's death, vowing that no such woman existed anywhere else in the world. Our dear old General, still living at the time, welcomed him into his home and provided lodging, just as he had in those happier early days. The women played the very same tunes Hal had heard as a boy at Oakhurst. Hearts were softened by these nostalgic memories. Harry never grew weary of pouring out his sorrows and recounting his wife's virtues to Hetty, and then turning to speak fondly of his beloved Aunt Lambert, whom he cherished above all else. One can imagine how welcome such praise was to the faithful old husband, whose thoughts of his wife seemed never to be absent for more than three waking minutes throughout the day.

General Hal later traveled to Paris as an American General Officer, dressed in the fashionable blue and yellow livery that Mr. Fox and other gentlemen had popularized here. He was highly regarded at Versailles, despite being presented by Monsieur le Marquis de Lafayette to the Most Christian King and Queen—a couple who held little affection for the Marquis. I believe a Marquise developed a crush on the Virginian General and would have married him immediately had he not resisted, fleeing instead back to England, Warrington, and Bury, particularly the latter, where locals listened to his tales of his late wife with endless patience and sympathy. As for us, having known the poor paragon personally, we were merely civil, lacking the same enthusiasm, and often puzzled by our children's questions regarding Uncle Hal's angelic wife.

The two Generals, myself, Captain Miles, and Parson Blake—who was knocked down at Monmouth the year after I left America to change his coat and accept my parish living—used to reenact the battles of the Revolution over a bottle of wine. The parson would often exclaim, "By Jupiter, General!" (having compromised the deity's name when laying aside his military attire), noting, "You are the Tory, and Sir George is the Whig! He is forever finding fault with our leaders, while you stand up for them endlessly. Last Sunday, when I prayed for the King, I heard you following me quite loudly."

"And so I do, Blake, with all my heart," replied Hal. "I cannot forget that I wore his coat."

"Ah, if Wolfe had lived twenty more years!" lamented Lambert.

"Ah, sir," cried Hal, "you should hear the General talk about him!"

"What General?" I asked, intending to vex him.

"My General," Hal responded, standing up and raising a glass. "His Excellency, General George Washington!"

"With all my heart!" I shouted, though the parson looked as though he disliked either the toast or the claret.

Hal never tired of speaking of his General. It was on an evening similar to this, amidst friendly conversation, that he recounted how he had actually fallen into disgrace with the very General he loved so dearly. Their conflict seemingly revolved around the aforementioned Monsieur le Marquis de Lafayette, who had played such a significant historical role and had so mysteriously vanished from the public eye. Hal's previous rank in our own service and his acknowledged gallantry during the war should have secured his promotion within the Continental Army. Yet, a young upstart like M. de Lafayette arrived and was promptly complimented by Congress with the rank of Major-General. With the boldness of an old soldier, Hal had expressed considerable contempt for certain appointments made by Congress, accusing unscrupulous officers of engaging in miserable intrigues and cabals driven solely by greed for promotion. Mr. Warrington, perhaps imitating the example of his now illustrious friend at Mount Vernon, chose to treat the war *en gentilhomme*. Of course, he accepted his pay, spending it on comforts and clothing for his men, but he declared rank to be of no earthly consequence, stating he would serve equally well as a colonel as in any higher grade. Undoubtedly, he added scathing remarks concerning certain General Officers of the Continental Army, their origins, and the reasons for their advancement. He was particularly incensed by the sudden promotion of the young French lad—the Marquis, as they affectionately called him—in the Republican army. By the way, he was a prodigious favorite of the Chief himself. There were not three officers in the entire Continental force (after the unfortunate capture and disgrace of the reckless Lee) who could speak the Marquis's language. Thus, Hal could judge the young Major-General more closely and familiarly than other gentlemen, including the Commander-in-Chief. Mr. Washington, however, good-naturedly chided friend Hal for his jealousy of the beardless commander of Auvergne. Washington himself was not displeased by the filial regard and profound veneration the enthusiastic young nobleman always displayed toward him. Moreover, Washington possessed excellent political reasons for treating the Marquis with friendship and favor.

As events later revealed, the Commander-in-Chief was most urgently pressing Colonel Warrington's promotion upon Congress. Compounding his difficulties before the enemy, he was entrenched at Valley Forge during this harsh winter, commanding five or six thousand men at most, almost entirely lacking firewood, blankets, food, or ammunition. Facing Sir William Howe's army—which was fully equipped and three times larger—he encountered not only military peril but also the cowardly distrust of Congress and insubordination and conspiracy among his own officers. During the terrible winter of 1777, when a single blow from the sluggish leader of the British forces could have ended the war, and doubt, confusion, and despair reigned in the opposing camp (except in one indomitable breast), my brother had an interview with the Chief. He has since described this event to me, and Hal could never speak of it without succumbing to the deepest emotion. Mr. Washington had won no such triumph as that achieved by the daredevil courage of Arnold and the elegant incompetence of Burgoyne, which benefited Gates and the northern army. Save for one or two minor engagements proving his daring bravery and ceaseless vigilance, General Washington faced defeat after defeat from an enemy superior in every aspect. Congress mistrusted him. Many officers in his own camp hated him. Those who had been disappointed in their ambitions, those caught in embezzlement, and those whose selfishness or incapacity his honest eyes had exposed—all were, more or less, in league against him. Gates was the figurehead toward whom the dissidents turned. Mr.
